## Section 3 — PanMixer: the published tool, dissected

Everything in this section is about one specific tool: **PanMixer**, published in *Nature Communications* (DOI 10.1038/s41467-026-77591-0, "Article in Press"), with source code released under the MIT licence.

Two sources, kept strictly apart:

- Where I quote a **design decision**, I take it from the **published** paper. Our copy of record is `paper/s41467-026-77591-0_reference.pdf` in this project's repository. An earlier **preprint** exists (`archive_docs/Blindenbach2026_PanMixer.pdf`); it is superseded and several of its numbers were revised, so I flag it whenever the two disagree and never quote it as current.
- Where I describe **a file, an array shape, or a line of logic**, I take it from the released code, pinned at commit `c182c38d5bc8bb6f00f4b0b101207c4a009ca045` in this project's `external/PanMixer` checkout.

Those two things are not always the same, and the subsection "Where the implementation departs from the paper" is entirely about where they diverge.

**A note on provenance, which matters more here than anywhere else in this primer.** Numbers in this section come from three different places, and I label every one:

| label | meaning | where it is recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **published** | printed in `paper/s41467-026-77591-0_reference.pdf` | that PDF |
| **measured in this project** | produced by us, by executing the pinned code or inspecting our rebuilt data | `docs/memory.md` (the permanent dated ledger), `docs/data.md` (the artifact registry), `docs/bugs.md` (defects), and the run logs under `logs/` |
| **estimate** | a rough figure with no line-by-line counting method | flagged in the sentence itself |

Anything not carrying one of those labels is either a definition or a statement about code you can read yourself at the cited path. I do not cite scratch or temporary files anywhere, because a primer is a thing you come back to and a temporary path does not survive.

**One structural warning before any symbol appears.** This document uses several Greek letters in more than one sense, because PanMixer's paper and our own proposal (`paper/Private_Genome_Path_Release.pdf`) independently chose the same letters for different things. The table below is the authoritative disambiguation for Section 3 and should have been in front of you from Section 1 onward. Read it now; I will re-gloss each symbol at its point of use as well.

| symbol | meaning **in this section** | meaning **elsewhere** | reminder |
|---|---|---|---|
| **η** (eta) | PanMixer's **utility loss** — how much the release degrades the data | our proposal's **η_τ** is a *tilt strength*, a completely different object introduced in Section 4 | η here costs; η_τ there steers |
| **ε** (epsilon) | PanMixer's **privacy risk**. Two flavours: **ε_j**, the per-block risk, and **ε_private**, an empirical threshold | Section 1's **ε** is a differential-privacy budget; our proposal's **ε_τ = 2·arctanh(τ)** is a likelihood-ratio bound | none of these are interchangeable |
| **ε as a mismatch rate** | in the HMM's emission model only, a per-anchor error probability of 10⁻⁴. I write it **μ_mis** from here on to stop the collision | — | renamed on purpose |
| **p, q** | at a biallelic site, **allele frequencies** (p = reference-allele frequency, q = 1 − p) | in Section 4, **p** and **q** are *input paths*; in the Li–Stephens transition formula they are *stay* and *switch probabilities* | three unrelated uses; always check the sentence |
| **r** | the Li–Stephens **recombination-rate constant, r = 1.26** (published, Fig. 6) | **r²** is the LD correlation statistic of Section 1 | *unrelated quantities that happen to share a letter* |
| **N_e** | **effective population size**, fixed at 10,000 (published) | — | defined in §A.3 |

The section has two parts. **Part A** is the idea, stated without any code. **Part B** is the tool as a data pipeline: every step, its inputs, what happens to them, its outputs, and what you should expect to see if it worked.

---

## Part A — The theory of PanMixer

### A.1 Who is being protected, and from what

PanMixer protects a **contributor** to the pangenome graph.

To unpack that, three definitions, in order.

A **pangenome graph** is a data structure that represents many human genomes at once. Instead of one linear string of DNA letters (a **reference genome** — a single agreed-upon sequence that everyone's coordinates are stated relative to, e.g. GRCh38), you have a graph whose **nodes** are stretches of sequence and whose **edges** connect stretches that occur consecutively in at least one real genome. Each contributing person's chromosome is then a **path** through that graph — the specific sequence of nodes their DNA actually spells out.

A person has two copies of each **autosome** (a non-sex chromosome), one inherited from each parent, so each person contributes **two paths per chromosome**. Each such single-parental-copy sequence is a **haplotype**. When you know which variants sit on which parental copy, the data is **phased**; when you only know the unordered pair, it is **unphased**, and the unordered pair is called a **genotype**.

PanMixer works on the **variant-centric** view of this graph rather than on nodes and edges directly. A **variant** is a site where individuals differ from the reference: a **SNP** (single-nucleotide polymorphism, one letter swapped), an **indel** (a short insertion or deletion), or a **structural variant** (a large insertion, deletion, inversion, and so on). Each possible sequence at a variant site is an **allele**. A site with exactly one alternate allele — two alleles in total — is **biallelic**; a site with two or more alternates is **multi-allelic**. The standard text format for "here are the variant sites and here is which allele each sample carries" is a **VCF** (Variant Call Format) file: one row per variant site, one column per sample, and the genotype field written as `0|1` — allele index 0 (the reference allele) on the first haplotype, allele index 1 (the first alternate allele) on the second, with `|` meaning phased. In this view a haplotype path is just a vector of allele indices, one per variant site.

Now the privacy problem. If you are one of the 44 people whose genomes were used to build the graph, then your two allele vectors are *literally published inside the released VCF*, under your sample name. Anyone who separately obtains a piece of your DNA — a discarded sample, a consumer-genetics upload, a clinical record — can check whether it matches. This is a **re-identification** or **linkage attack**: an attacker holds a database of named genomes and asks which row of the published data corresponds to which person. It works so well because rare variants are nearly unique fingerprints. Sharing a common variant with someone is uninformative, because a large fraction of the planet shares it; sharing three or four rare ones is essentially a signature.

It is worth knowing, as **standard background rather than anything measured here**, that the vulnerability is not limited to per-individual records. The result usually credited to **Homer and colleagues (2008)** showed that even *aggregate* allele frequencies — a table of "this fraction of the cohort carried the alternate allele at each site", with no individual rows at all — can be enough to tell whether a known individual was part of the pool. That result is a standard piece of field background; I state it as attribution, not as something this project verified, and a reader who wants to rely on it should read the original.

PanMixer's answer is: **rewrite the contributor's genotype column** so that it is no longer a faithful copy of the real person, while keeping the released graph scientifically useful. The release is still "the cohort VCF", with the same sites and the same 44 sample columns; only the target's column has been altered.

### A.2 Why LD blocks are the unit of action

PanMixer does not edit one variant at a time. It edits **LD blocks**.

**Linkage disequilibrium (LD)** is the statistical fact that nearby variants are not inherited independently. **Recombination** — the shuffling of the two parental chromosomes during the production of eggs and sperm — happens at particular rates along the chromosome, and the genetic distance over which it happens is measured in **centiMorgans (cM)**: 1 cM is the distance over which a recombination occurs in about 1% of meioses. Note that a centiMorgan is *not* a physical distance: the conversion between cM and base pairs varies along the chromosome, because recombination is concentrated in hotspots. Stretches of DNA shorter than the spacing between hotspots tend to be passed down intact, so the alleles inside them travel together as a unit. An **LD block** is a contiguous run of variants that behaves like such a unit.

This matters for privacy for a blunt reason: **if you edit one variant but leave its LD neighbours alone, you have not hidden anything.** The neighbours still spell out which allele you "really" had, because in this population that combination essentially always co-occurs. Editing must happen at the granularity at which the data is actually correlated, which is the block. It also matters for utility: a half-edited block is a haplotype no human has, which corrupts exactly the LD structure that downstream users want.

PanMixer computes block boundaries with **plink** (a long-established open-source genetics toolkit; its `--blocks` flag runs a confidence-interval LD-block caller) on a large external **reference panel** — a published collection of phased haplotypes from many people, used as a statistical stand-in for "the population". It then assigns every pangenome variant to whichever block interval contains its coordinate. Variants that fall outside all block intervals become **blocks of their own**, holding exactly one variant each.

A number to carry, **measured in this project** on our rebuilt chr21 (recorded in `docs/memory.md`, entry dated 2026-09-18): plink produced **14,137 LD blocks**, but the working block dictionary has **100,757 entries**, of which **89,087 — that is 88.4% of *entries*** — hold exactly one variant. Because singleton entries hold one variant each by definition, those 89,087 entries account for 89,087 of 340,824 variants, or **26.1% of *variants***. **These two denominators must never be confused**: "88.4%" is a statement about entries, and the corresponding statement about variants is "about 26%". Separately, **10.4% of entries** contain two or more **anchor** variants (defined in §A.3) and therefore take the HMM path, and those entries hold **71.4% of all variants**.

The shape to internalise: *most entries are trivial, but most of the genome lives in the non-trivial minority.*

### A.3 What an "obfuscation move" is

For each block, and for each of the target's two haplotypes, PanMixer constructs exactly **one candidate replacement**: a plausible alternative haplotype block, sampled from a model of the cohort. That candidate, together with the decision to install it, is an **obfuscation move**. With *k* blocks and 2 haplotypes there are *2k* possible moves, and each is a binary yes/no.

An **anchor** is a variant the tool treats as trusted enough to model explicitly — concretely, a pangenome variant that also appears in a chosen external callset. (A **callset** is the set of variants a particular method reported from a particular dataset.) Which callset supplies the anchors is exactly where the code and the paper part company; see departure (d).

How the candidate is produced depends on how much structure the block has:

- **Blocks with fewer than two anchors**: sample each variant's new allele independently from that site's **allele frequency** (AF) — the fraction of haplotypes in a reference population carrying that allele. For a genuine one-variant block this ignores nothing, because there is no internal LD structure to preserve. An earlier version of this primer offered that as the justification for the whole branch. **That is retracted**, because the branch is defined on **anchor** count, not variant count: a block holding **two or more variants** with 0 or 1 anchors also takes this route, and it does have internal LD that site-independent sampling destroys. The primer's own Step 14b names the case — "block has ≥2 variants but 0 anchors"; §A.9.1 counts them.
- **Blocks with two or more anchors**: sample from a **Li–Stephens** model. This is a **hidden Markov model (HMM)** — a probabilistic model with an unobserved ("hidden") state that evolves along a sequence and emits observations. In Li–Stephens the hidden state at each anchor is "which cohort haplotype am I currently copying from", the transition is "stay on the same donor, or switch to another (a recombination)", and the emission is "copy that donor's allele, with a small probability of mismatch". A haplotype produced this way is a **mosaic**: a patchwork of segments copied from different donors. The whole point of the mosaic is that it is a *new* combination — not any single real person's data — while still looking like a real human haplotype, because every piece of it came from one.

> **Key idea** — **the HMM is per block, and the chain never crosses a block boundary.** This is the single most important structural fact about PanMixer's sampler and it is easy to assume the opposite, so state it four ways. The chain runs **within one block**, over **that block's anchor positions** only. Each block starts from a **fresh uniform state** — the start distribution is re-drawn from scratch, uniformly over the donor haplotypes, every time a new block is entered — so nothing is carried over from the previous block. There is **no transition model between blocks** at all. And there is **no chain over all blocks**: no T-blocks × K-states chain, no backward pass and no forward-filtering-backward-sampling anywhere in the released repository (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, Correction 1 of 2026-09-18, which records this in those terms; the routing and the per-block chain construction are in `get_support_and_pmi.py` with `hmm.py`).
>
> Two consequences, and one correction to a reading that is easy to arrive at. The consequences: whatever LD spans a block boundary is **not modelled**, because the only thing carrying correlation across the chromosome is the block partition itself (§A.2); and the blocks are therefore sampled **independently of one another**, which is the assumption the knapsack's additive accounting also makes (§A.6). The correction: **a state is a donor haplotype**, one of the cohort's haplotypes, **never a variant and never an allele**. The **variants** (specifically, the anchors) are the chain's **positions** — the axis the chain walks along — and the **states** are the donors it may be copying from at each position. Our own across-block chain and backward sampler would be new work, not something inherited from PanMixer.

Which route a block takes has one measured consequence that is worth meeting now rather than 600 lines later, and it turns on a term the next callout uses, so the term is defined first.

> **Definition** — a **`CONFLICT` record** is one where `vg deconstruct` found that a sample had more than one graph path through the bubble and those paths disagreed, so it could not emit a single genotype and wrote `.|.` instead. The `INFO` field carries `CONFLICT=<sample>`. `VCFtoNP.py` keeps neither the tag nor the reason — it sees `.` and writes `-1` — so downstream, a conflict is indistinguishable from an assembly gap or from a variant that does not exist on that haplotype at all.

> **Caution** — "anchor" and "has population data" are different things. **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24): **10,460** non-anchor variants still receive real 1000G frequencies, so a zero-anchor block can be fully populated with real frequencies and *still* be sampled site-independently. The anchor set decides which route a block takes; it does not decide whether frequencies exist. And the route matters: **measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23), the 1,855 `CONFLICT` records occupy 1,634 blocks of which **1,469 are singletons** and only **1.8%** are HMM-path blocks against a **10.4%** baseline — so the records with the most unusual structure are overwhelmingly handled by the route that ignores LD.

The switch rate is where the population genetics enters. The published model (Fig. 6) sets

> **d = Δx · N_e · r**

Reading that in words: **d is the expected number of ancestral recombination events separating two neighbouring anchors**. It is the genetic distance **Δx** between them (in centiMorgans), scaled by how much ancestral shuffling the population has accumulated (**N_e**) and by a standard rate constant (**r = 1.26**, published). **N_e**, the **effective population size**, is the size of an idealised, randomly mating population that would show the same amount of genetic drift — and the same rate of shared ancestry — as the real one; it is much smaller than the census population because of past bottlenecks, and humans are conventionally modelled with **N_e = 10,000** (published). Again: this **r** is the recombination-rate constant and has nothing to do with the LD correlation **r²**.

From d, with **n** donor states available, the two transition probabilities are

> **stay: p = e^(−d/n) + (1 − e^(−d/n))/n**  
> **switch: q = (1 − e^(−d/n))/n**

In words: with probability e^(−d/n) no recombination occurred and we stay on the current donor; otherwise we re-draw a donor uniformly from all n, which lands back on the current donor 1/n of the time. (Here **p** and **q** are transition probabilities, *not* the allele frequencies p and q of §A.7 and Step 21, and *not* the input paths p and q of Section 4.) In PanMixer's sampler **n = 86**, because the target's own two haplotypes are excluded from the donor set. Note that the two lines sum correctly: p + (n−1)q = 1.

Each move buys some privacy — the block no longer reveals the target's real alleles — and costs some utility, because the cohort's allele frequencies and LD structure shift a little.

### A.4 The privacy quantity: PMI, and why it collapses to self-information

PanMixer's privacy number is called **PMI** — **pointwise mutual information** — both in the paper and in the code. Since the primer has so far used the abbreviation without defining it, here is the definition:

> **PMI(x, y) = log [ P(x, y) / (P(x)·P(y)) ]**

In words: **how much more often do x and y occur together than they would if they were independent?** If x and y are statistically independent, P(x,y) = P(x)P(y), the ratio is 1, and PMI = 0. If observing y makes x much more likely than chance, PMI is large and positive. It is the pointwise (per-outcome) version of mutual information, which is the average of PMI over the joint distribution.

PanMixer applies this to the pair (original path *h*, released path *h\**) and calls it privacy risk **ε**. The reasoning is direct: a released path that is statistically *dependent* on the original leaks information about the original; a released path that is *independent* of it leaks nothing. PMI(h, h\*) = 0 is the ideal.

The paper defines the joint probability blockwise over blocks (b₁, …, b_k), which is where the useful collapse comes from. Two cases, and this is the whole argument:

- For a block you **do not** obfuscate, the released block *is* the original block. So the joint probability equals the marginal, p(h_bj, h\*_bj) = p(h_bj), and the block contributes its full PMI.
- For a block you **do** obfuscate, PanMixer samples the replacement *independently of the original*. So the joint factorises, p(h_bj, h\*_bj) = p(h_bj)·p(h\*_bj), and PMI for that block is log 1 = 0.

Therefore obfuscating block *b_j* **removes** exactly the amount of risk that block was contributing, and the paper's Supplementary Lemma 2 gives that removed amount as

> **ε_j = −log p(h_bj)**

where p(h_bj) is the population probability of the target's *own* haplotype block. In words: **how surprising is the target's real block under a model of the general population?**

That quantity is the **self-information** (also called surprisal) of an event. The intuition is clean. If your block is the most common haplotype in the population, p is large, −log p is small, and disclosing it tells an attacker almost nothing — millions of people have it. If your block is one that essentially nobody else carries, p is tiny, −log p is large, and disclosing it identifies you. Because log is monotone, ranking blocks by ε_j is exactly ranking them by rarity. **Rarer blocks score higher and get obfuscated first.**

**Units.** Self-information is measured in **bits** when the logarithm is base 2 and in **nats** when it is the natural logarithm. PanMixer's code uses `np.log`, the natural log, so everything in this section is in **nats**. One nat ≈ 1.44 bits.

**Micro-example.** Suppose a block reduces to a single variant with two alleles, and the reference allele has frequency 0.9, the alternate 0.1. A target carrying the reference allele scores −log(0.9) = 0.105 nats. A target carrying the alternate scores −log(0.1) = 2.303 nats — about 22 times more. Now suppose the target carries a **novel** variant, one seen in no external database at all. Step 13 gives such a variant a frequency with a deliberately large denominator: 2 × (N_panel + N_pangenome). On our rebuilt chr21, using the **30x GRCh38 panel (3,202 samples)**, that denominator is 2 × (3,202 + 44) = **6,492**, so a variant carried on one haplotype scores −log(1/6492) = **8.78 nats**. (Had the shipped pipeline's **Phase 3 panel (2,504 samples)** been used, the denominator would be 2 × (2,504 + 44) = 5,096 and the score 8.54 nats. Always say which panel produced the number.) The optimizer will reach for that block long before it touches the common one.

For multi-anchor blocks, p(h_bj) is not a product of independent allele frequencies — that would double-count correlated variants. It is computed with the **forward algorithm** on the same Li–Stephens HMM. The forward algorithm is the standard dynamic program answering "what is the total probability that this HMM emits the sequence I observed", summing over all hidden state paths in O(T·K) time for T positions and K states, by carrying forward one number per state instead of enumerating K^T paths.

An earlier version of this paragraph ended there, with the sentence *"Using it here means a block whose alleles are individually uncommon but jointly a well-known haplotype gets a correctly modest score."* **That sentence claimed more than the code delivers and is retracted**; the reason is §A.4.2 below. Everything up to this point in §A.4 stands.

#### A.4.1 The exact formula, for a block with any number of variants

The question this subsection answers is the blunt one: **given a block that holds three variants, what number comes out?** The paper states ε_j = −log p(h_bj) and leaves p(h_bj) as "the population probability of the block". The released code computes it by one of two routes, chosen by a branch at `get_support_and_pmi.py:87`, and the route decides the arithmetic completely. Write **a_v** for the allele index the target actually carries at variant *v*, and **f_v(a)** for the frequency of allele *a* at variant *v* — one row of `allele_frequencies.npy`, built by the three-way priority of Step 13 and dissected in §A.9 below.

**Route F — the allele-frequency sum.** Taken when the block holds **exactly one** variant, or when it holds **two or more variants and zero anchors**:

> **ε_j = Σ_{v ∈ block} −log f_v(a_v)**

In words: **for every variant in the block, look up the frequency of the specific allele the target carries there, take minus its natural log, and add them all up.** This is the sum of per-site self-informations, and it treats the variants as statistically independent — which is exactly the assumption an LD block exists to deny, so the route is only strictly appropriate for the one-variant case. (§A.3 previously justified this route by saying a one-variant block has no internal LD to preserve. That justification covers only part of the branch; see §A.9.)

**Route H — the forward algorithm over the anchors.** Taken when the block holds **two or more variants and at least one anchor**:

> **ε_j = −log [ Σ_{i=1}^{K} α_T(i) ]**, where the chain runs over the block's **anchors only**

In words: **build a Li–Stephens HMM whose T positions are the block's anchor variants and whose K states are the cohort haplotypes, run the forward algorithm on the target's own anchor alleles, and take minus the log of the total probability it returns.** The chain is **per block** and starts from a fresh uniform state, with no transition model between blocks — see the **Key idea** in §A.3, which is worth re-reading here, because it is what makes ε_j a sum of independent per-block terms rather than one quantity over the chromosome. The code calls this as `−forward_algorithm(haplotype, block)`, once per strand. Concretely, in this implementation: T = the number of anchors in the block; K = **88** (the scorer's emission panel is `self.pangenome_haplotypes`, all 88 haplotypes — which is departure (c), and is why ε_j carries a **soft** ceiling near log 88 = **4.4773 nats** — soft, not hard, for the reason given under departure (c)); the per-anchor mismatch probability is **μ_mis = 10⁻⁴**; and the stay/switch transition probabilities are the pair given in §A.3 with **n = 86**. Every number in that list is **measured in this project** by reading the pinned code.

Two mechanical facts complete the definition, both **measured in this project** by reading the code:

- **A missing site contributes exactly 0.** The code executes `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`, so any variant where the target's allele is `-1` adds nothing to ε_j on that strand. A `-1` conflates **five** distinct conditions and all five are treated alike here (**measured in this project**, `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy`; Step 9 is where `-1` is introduced).
- **Both strands are scored separately.** The output is `subjects/{id}/pmi_blocks.npy`, shape `(num_blocks, 2)` float64; column *h* is the privacy value of obfuscating haplotype *h* of that block.

> **Worked example** — a three-variant block, every intermediate number carried. Take **block 1083** on our rebuilt chr21, **measured in this project** (`docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25). It holds exactly three variants: **two anchors**, at each of which the allele the target carries has frequency **0.934**, and **one non-anchor**, at which the allele the target carries has frequency **0.00385**. Run **Route F** on it first, because that is the arithmetic the formula above describes:
>
> | step | quantity | value (nats) |
> |---|---|---|
> | variant 1 (anchor) | −ln(0.934) | **0.06828** |
> | variant 2 (anchor) | −ln(0.934) | **0.06828** |
> | variant 3 (non-anchor) | −ln(0.00385) | **5.55968** |
> | sum of the two anchors | 2 × 0.06828 | **0.13656** |
> | **Route F total, recomputed here** | 0.13656 + 5.55968 | **5.69624** (derived) |
> | **Route F total, as measured** | — | **5.6956** (measured in this project) |
>
> The per-line values are **derived** from the measured allele frequencies; the total **5.6956 nats** is itself **measured in this project**. The two totals differ by 0.00064 nats because the two frequencies are recorded in `docs/bugs.md` rounded to three significant figures, so the recomputation cannot reproduce the measured value exactly. Do not read the last digit of either total as meaningful.
>
> Read the decomposition, because it is the whole point of §A.4.2. Of the recomputed **5.69624 nats**, the single non-anchor variant supplies **5.55968**, which is **97.6%** of the total (**derived**), and the two anchors together supply **0.13656**, which is **2.4%** (**derived**). The rare material in this block is not merely the largest term; it is essentially the only term.

> **Worked example** — the same machinery on a block that really does take Route H. **Block 1077**, **measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24): VCF rows **34816–34820**, POS **13,000,333 – 13,000,621**, **5 variants, all 5 of them anchors**, so the branch sends it to Route H. The target HG00438 carries alleles `[0,0,0,0,0]` on **both** strands. The forward pass therefore runs with T = 5 positions and K = 88 states — **440** α values in total (**derived**) — and returns a total probability whose negative log is
>
> **ε_j = 0.0711 nats per strand** (**measured in this project**).
>
> What does 0.0711 nats *mean*? Here is the cross-check that pins it down, and it is the single most useful sanity test in this section. **Measured in this project**: **82 of the 88** cohort haplotypes carry exactly the pattern `[0,0,0,0,0]` across those five variants. The surprisal of an event with probability 82/88 is −ln(82/88) = **0.0706 nats** (**derived**). The forward algorithm returned **0.0711**. The two agree to three decimal places, and the residual of 0.0005 is the size of the per-anchor additive term T·μ_mis = 5 × 10⁻⁴ (**derived**). Note the letter: departure (c) writes that same term as n·μ_mis with **n = the number of anchors in the block**, which is *not* the n = 86 donor states of §A.3. Here T = 5 anchors. So ε_j reads exactly as advertised: **the surprisal of the target's own block under a model of the cohort.** A block that 82 of 88 haplotypes share is worth almost nothing, and the number says so.
>
> For scale, **measured in this project** over **150 real Route-H blocks** for HG00438 on chr21 (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-18): ε_j runs from **0.071 to 4.508 nats** with a **median of 1.242**. Block 1077's 0.0711 sits at the very bottom of that measured range; it is about as unremarkable as a block gets. The top of the range is discussed as departure (c), where the soft ceiling near log 88 = 4.4773 nats is derived — soft because the measured maximum of 4.508 sits 0.031 nats above it, which is what the additive per-anchor term predicts.

> **Note** — block 1077 is also the block where PanMixer's sampler drew a replacement **identical** to the target's original, which is a separate finding with its own numbers at Step 14. It is mentioned here only so the two tellings do not look like two different blocks.

#### A.4.2 ε_j sums over ANCHOR alleles only, not over the block

This is the finding that changed our own design, so it is stated at length and framed carefully.

On Route H, `forward_algorithm` builds **both** its `observed` vector and its emission panel from `anchor_indices` alone (**measured in this project**, `docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25). A variant in the block that is not an anchor is therefore **not a position in the chain at all**. It has no emission, no transition into it and no transition out of it, and it contributes **nothing whatsoever** to p(h_bj). Its allele frequency may have been computed perfectly well by Step 13 and stored in `allele_frequencies.npy`; on Route H that row is simply never read.

> **Caution** — ε_j is not "the self-information of the block". It is **the self-information of the block's anchor alleles**. A small ε_j has two causes that the number itself cannot distinguish: the block genuinely is a common haplotype, or the rare material in it was never looked at.

The measured consequence, using the block already worked above:

> **Defect** — **block 1083**, **measured in this project** (`docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25). Route F over all three of its variants gives **5.6956 nats**, as computed term by term in §A.4.1. The block has two anchors, so the code takes Route H and returns **0.0349 nats**. That is a factor of **163×** (**derived**: 5.6956 / 0.0349 = 163.2). Read the 0.0349 as a probability and the mechanism is transparent: e^(−0.0349) = **0.966** (**derived**), i.e. the model reports that the target's anchor alleles at this block are about **96.6% likely** — essentially unsurprising, which is *true of the two anchors* at AF 0.934 each. Meanwhile the allele the scorer never looked at has a population frequency of **0.00385**, i.e. **0.385%**. The number is not nonsense; it is a correct answer to a narrower question than its name implies.

And the population-level version, which is what makes this structural rather than anecdotal:

| metric, over **400 random Route-H blocks** on chr21 | value |
|---|---|
| median ε_j as the code computes it (anchors only) | **1.079 nats** (measured in this project) |
| median all-variant allele-frequency sum for the same blocks | **2.769 nats** (measured in this project) |
| ratio of the two medians | **2.57×** (derived) |
| **share of blocks where the non-anchor rarity the scorer ignores is larger than the ε_j it reports** | **32%** (measured in this project) |

In roughly a third of Route-H blocks, the material the privacy score never examined is individually more identifying than everything the score did examine. That is the sentence that motivated dropping the anchor concept from our own v1 sampler (§A.8, and Section 4).

> **Note** — On framing, and this is not a formality. The paper defines ε_j over **the block** (§A.4 attributes the definition to the paper's Supplementary Lemma 2). The released code evaluates it over **the block's anchors**. The finding is therefore of the form *"this metric measures something narrower than its name suggests"* — and, equivalently, *"the released code differs from the published Methods"*. It is **not** a claim that the paper's reported results are wrong. We have not reproduced any of the paper's figures. ε_j enters the optimizer only through a **ranking** (§A.6). `docs/memory.md` makes that point explicitly about the *self-inclusion ceiling* — *"ε_j enters the LP only through a ranking, and we have not measured how much the selection changes when the ceiling is removed"* — and no measurement of how much the selection changes when the **anchor restriction** is removed is recorded in `docs/` either. So nothing above licenses a statement about their published frontier, and none is made.

#### A.4.3 Retraction: the `−log 0 = +inf` zeroing does not fire on this data

An earlier version of this primer — and of this project's own notes — carried the following argument. It appeared in this primer in Part B at Step 14b, which now carries its own retraction of it, and it is **withdrawn**.

**What was claimed.** That an allele whose frequency comes out exactly 0 gives −log 0 = +inf; that the code detects values above 10²⁰⁰ and sets them to **0**, the *minimum* rather than the maximum; and therefore that *"PanMixer assigns the most identifying sites on the chromosome a privacy score of exactly zero."*

**What is withdrawn.** The final step. The code path is real and is quoted accurately — `obfuscate.py:232-240` performs `pmi_subject[pmi_subject > 1e200] = 0`, and the pipeline copy carries a literal `#FIX ME` comment at `get_support_and_pmi.py:65-74` (both **measured in this project** by reading the pinned code). What is withdrawn is the claim that this path **runs**, and with it the conclusion drawn from it. The retraction is recorded in `docs/memory.md` as **Correction 7**, dated **2026-09-24**.

**What was measured.** Across all **319,092** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a called allele, the number with f_v = 0 is **zero** (**measured in this project**). The branch never executes on this data.

**Why the original reasoning failed.** It assumed that an allele private to the pangenome would have frequency 0 in the frequency table. It cannot, because the frequency table counts the target's own allele in every one of its three branches (§A.9 walks them): branches 2 and 3 count the pangenome haplotypes directly (`get_af.py:41-53,68-73`), and under our **30x GRCh38 panel** repin even branch 1 includes the target, because **39 of 44** HPRC donors — HG00438 among them — appear in that panel (**measured in this project**). On top of that, branch 3 pads the reference-allele count so the row sums to a **constructed** denominator of 2 × (3,202 + 44) = **6,492**, which floors the frequency of a one-haplotype novel allele at **1.54 × 10⁻⁴** (**derived**). A frequency that is floored cannot be zero. The reasoning treated a denominator as if it were the count of external carriers; it is not.

> **Defect** — the zeroing is nevertheless a real defect in the released code. It is **latent**, and its trigger is **panel-dependent**. Under the **shipped Phase 3 panel**, where **0 of 44** HPRC donors appear (**measured in this project**), a branch-1 variant carrying an allele that no 1000 Genomes sample carries *would* give f_v = 0 and *would* fire it. Which panel you run decides whether you ever see it. That is worth knowing about in its own right: a defect that does not reproduce is not a defect that is absent.

**What is true instead, and the mechanism matters.** The *conclusion* — that the most identifying record on the chromosome contributes zero privacy value — survives. That is the claim `docs/memory.md` makes in Correction 8, and it is made about that one record, not about a class. The *mechanism* is completely different, and substituting one for the other would leave the reader with a fixable bug in place of a design problem.

Take the extreme case, **measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-22 and Correction 8 of 2026-09-24). The site `grch38#chr21:14569980`, VCF row **51791**, is top level (`LV=0`), declares **90 alleles**, and has **88 distinct alleles carried across the cohort's 88 haplotypes — every haplotype unique**. Its REF is **257,439 bp** long. HG00438 carries alleles **89** and **88** there, and each is assigned f_v = **1.540 × 10⁻⁴**, worth **8.78 nats** of self-information. That sits at the top of the range the frequency table can produce at all, because 1/6,492 is the floor branch 3 imposes (**derived**; §A.9.3). For scale only — and it is a per-site number against a per-block one — the median ε_j over the 150 measured Route-H blocks of §A.4.1 is 1.242 nats.

**That 8.78 is never used.** The record is **not an anchor**: it does not appear in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset, so the test `r in align` is False. Its block is **block 1639**, which holds **92 records of which 82 are anchors** — far past the two-anchor threshold — so the block takes **Route H**, where the forward algorithm scores the **82 anchors** and ignores the other **10** records (**derived**: 92 − 82), this one among them. The most identifying record on chr21 contributes nothing to the privacy score because it is missing from an external SNP callset.

> **Key idea** — a zeroing bug is repaired by changing three lines. Non-anchor exclusion is repaired only by changing **what the chain is defined over**, which is a design change, not a patch. This is exactly why §A.8 exists and why our v1 sampler drops the anchor concept rather than fixing a clamp.

For completeness, and because it is easy to assume the story ends with "so the site was never touched": at **capacity 0.1** block 1639 **was** selected, and HG00438's alleles at row 51791 were rewritten on both strands from `[89, 88]` to `[35, 64]` (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23). Separately, in that same capacity-0.1 run the largest single allele rewrite anywhere on the chromosome was **257,485 bp** (**measured in this project**, same entry); the docs do not attribute that particular rewrite to this record, so do not assume it is this one. The record was rewritten for reasons that had nothing to do with its own rarity, because its own rarity was never in the objective. And the cost metric that priced the rewrite counts **records, not base pairs** (§A.5), so the size of it does not appear in the accounting either.

Two properties are worth naming now, because our own mechanism (Section 4 — the cohort-tilted τ-private path release set out in `paper/Private_Genome_Path_Release.pdf`) departs from both:

1. ε_j is a property of **the target's own data** evaluated under a population model. It is an *average-case information quantity about one observed value* — not a bound on any distribution the mechanism outputs.
2. ε_j is defined *per block*, and the total risk is the sum across blocks. Blocks you do not obfuscate contribute their full ε_j to the residual risk.

### A.5 The utility cost: one over the support

Each move's cost is

> **η_j = Σ_{v ∈ Δ} 1 / support(v)**

where **Δ** is the set of variants the move actually changes and **support(v)** is the number of haplotypes that have a *called* (non-missing) allele at variant v. In words: **for every variant this move rewrites, add one divided by how many haplotypes were actually observed at that variant; the sum is the move's cost.** (Reminder from the symbol table: this η is PanMixer's utility loss, not our proposal's tilt strength η_τ.)

Why 1/support? Because to a good approximation it is exactly the change in allele frequency you cause. Take a single variant with support *n* — that is, *n* haplotypes have a called allele there. Its allele frequency is (count of haplotypes carrying that allele)/n. If you flip one haplotype from reference to alternate, the numerator changes by 1, so the frequency changes by 1/n. The **L1 distance** (sum of absolute differences) between the before and after frequency vectors therefore changes by 1/n for the allele lost and 1/n for the allele gained. So η is, up to a constant factor of two, the induced L1 change in allele frequency, summed over the variants you touched.

Two consequences follow, and both are important:

- **Cost is flat in allele frequency.** Changing a rare variant costs the same 1/n as changing a common one. Privacy *value*, by contrast, scales steeply with rarity. So the value-per-unit-cost ratio is enormously better for rare blocks, and the asymmetry between a log and a constant does point the optimizer at rare material. **That is the behaviour the design intends, and an earlier version of this section stated it as the behaviour the implementation delivers. That stronger claim is retracted here**, because two measured effects cut across it in opposite directions, and both are consequences of the cost formula rather than of the optimizer. Going *down*: a move whose sampled replacement happens to equal the original has η_j = **exactly 0** on a block with more than one variant, and ε_j > 0, which is infinite value per unit cost, so the LP buys it ahead of every real edit — quantified in §A.6. Going *up*: where the target's own allele is missing, the score is zeroed while 1/support(v) *rises*, so a whole class of sites is worth zero privacy and costs more than the median record elsewhere, and the optimizer has essentially no reason to touch them — quantified in §A.10. What survives is the sign of the incentive. Whether the optimizer actually reaches the material most in need of protection is a separate question, and the two effects just named are measured cases where it does not.
- **Cost is roughly linear in the number of edits — counted in records, not in base pairs.** A block containing 200 variants of which 40 change costs about 40/n. An earlier version of this section added "so 'utility loss' and 'amount of the genome rewritten' track each other". **That is retracted**; it is false in base pairs, which is the reading a new reader will take. `1/support(v)` is charged **per record**, so a 257 kb allele and a 1 bp SNP cost exactly the same.

> **Defect** — the utility axis cannot see the size of what it changes. **Measured in this project** (`docs/bugs.md`, dated 2026-09-25; chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1, seed 123): **3,129,810 bp — 3.13 Mb, 6.7% of chr21** — was rewritten, across **222** changed alleles longer than 1 kb, the largest a single **257,485 bp** rewrite. The same run reports "**68,335 of 650,541 alleles changed (10.5%)**". Non-SNP records changed at a **higher** rate than SNPs (**23.3%** against **14.9%**). Both numbers are correct descriptions of different things; the megabases never enter the accounting. This matters for our own design because §4.13 proposes reusing this weight as `phi_t`, and reusing it inherits the blind spot. The full treatment is departure (h).

The paper calls the aggregate of these costs the **Weighted Path Edit Distance (WPED)**: the minimum total cost of the edits needed to turn the original path into the released path, by analogy with edit distance on strings.

**Now the case that decides most of what follows: what does this cost formula give for a move that changes nothing?**

Read the definition literally. The sum runs over **Δ**, the set of variants the move *actually changes*. Δ is not "the variants in the block" and it is not "the variants the sampler drew a value for" — it is the variants at which the drawn value **differs from what the target already had**. In the code that is written as a mask multiply: the per-record weights are multiplied by the boolean array `new != orig` before summing (`obfuscate.py:207-211,279-290`, **measured in this project** by reading the pinned checkout). If the drawn replacement is allele-for-allele identical to the original, every entry of that boolean array is false, and

> **Definition** — a **no-op move** is a move that is *selected* by the optimizer and *installed* by the stacker, but whose replacement block is identical, allele for allele, to the target's original block. Its Δ is empty. Its utility cost is η_j = **0.00000 exactly** on a block with more than one variant (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24; for the single-variant exception in the released code see the **Caution** below). Nothing about the released file differs from the input at that block.

There is nothing subtle about the arithmetic, and it is worth doing once on a real block so the number is concrete rather than notional.

> **Worked example** — block 1077 on our rebuilt chr21, target **HG00438**, cohort of 44 individuals = 88 haplotypes, capacity 0.1, seed 123. All figures **measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24) unless marked derived. The block is VCF rows **34816–34820**, spanning POS **13,000,333 – 13,000,621**: **5 variants, all 5 of them anchors**, so it takes the haplotype-model route. At every one of the five variants `support(v)` = **87 of 88** — note in passing that even a thoroughly ordinary common block has one haplotype uncalled at each site, which is the whole reason support is defined against non-missing entries. So each variant's weight is 1/87 = **0.011494** (derived). If the move rewrote all five it would cost 5 × 0.011494 = **0.05747** (derived; the same figure is recorded as measured). The target carries `[0,0,0,0,0]` on both strands. The sampler returned `[0,0,0,0,0]`. Changed variants: **0**. Therefore η_j = **0.00000**, and `xsol[625]` is **1 on both strands** — the move was taken — while the released alleles at rows 34816–34820 are byte-identical to the input.

**Why the zero matters, and it matters more than it looks.** The LP relaxation of a single-constraint knapsack is solved by sorting items on **value density** ε_j/η_j and taking them in that order until the budget is exhausted (§A.6 derives this). Density is where a zero weight becomes structural rather than incidental, and it splits the item set into three regimes that behave completely differently:

| property | ε_j > 0 and η_j > 0 | ε_j > 0 and η_j = 0 | ε_j = 0 and η_j > 0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| what the item is | an ordinary edit: some privacy bought with some utility | a **no-op move** — selected, installed, changes nothing | a site the score cannot credit, e.g. one where the target's own allele is missing |
| value density ε_j/η_j | finite | **infinite** | **zero** |
| where the greedy rule puts it | in the middle, ranked on density | **first, ahead of every real edit** | **last, behind every real edit** |
| what it consumes from the budget ΔU | its weight | **nothing** | its weight |
| does the budget ever exclude it | yes, if density is too low | **no — a free item is always affordable at every capacity** | it is excluded for lack of value, not for lack of budget |
| quantified where | throughout §A.6 | §A.6, the no-op measurement | §A.10 |

*(the three regimes are derived from the definitions of η_j and of the greedy rule; the measured instances of each are cited at the sections named in the last row)*

> **Key idea** — on a multi-variant block η_j is **honest**. It sums 1/support(v) over the variants that genuinely differ, so it reports zero cost for a move that did nothing and it reports real cost for a move that did something (the single-variant exception is in the **Caution** below). When §A.6 shows that the privacy-per-utility ratio is overstated, the error will be entirely in the numerator. Do not carry away the impression that the no-op moves inflate the utility axis; they do not. (η_j has a separate and unrelated limitation, which this subsection does not treat: it is charged **per record**, so it cannot see how many base pairs a rewrite touched — `docs/bugs.md`, and departure (h).)

> **Caution** — there is **one exception in the released code**, and it runs the other way. For a block containing a **single** variant, the precompute assigns that site's `utility_loss` to **both** haplotype columns **unconditionally**, without testing whether the resampled allele equalled the original (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned checkout; see Step 14d). So on a singleton block a no-op is charged its full weight, not zero. This is not a rare corner: **89,087 of the 100,757** block-dictionary entries on our rebuilt chr21 are singletons — **88.4%** (**measured in this project**). The honest summary is therefore: on a multi-variant block a no-op is free, and on a single-variant block it is paid for. Both behaviours are in the same file.

> **Open question** — `docs/` does **not** record how the measured no-op moves split between single-variant and multi-variant blocks, nor between the two routing branches of §A.10's table. Until that is measured, the exact amount of budget consumed by no-ops cannot be stated — only that on the multi-variant blocks it is zero. Nothing in §A.6 depends on the split; the privacy-side accounting there is measured directly.

### A.6 The knapsack and the LP relaxation

Now the optimization. You have *2k* items. Item *j* has a **value** ε_j (privacy removed) and a **weight** η_j (utility destroyed). You have a **budget** ΔU on total weight. You must choose a subset.

This is literally the **0–1 knapsack problem**: choose x ∈ {0,1}^{2k} to

> **maximize Σ_j ε_j x_j subject to Σ_j η_j x_j ≤ ΔU.**

In words: **take the most privacy you can buy without spending more utility than your budget allows.**

0–1 knapsack is NP-hard in general, and 2k here is on the order of 200,000 items per chromosome (twice the 100,757 block-dictionary entries measured on our rebuilt chr21). PanMixer therefore solves the **LP relaxation** — "LP" being **linear program**, an optimization with a linear objective and linear constraints, solvable in polynomial time. The relaxation replaces x_j ∈ {0,1} with x_j ∈ [0,1], i.e. it permits "take 37% of this move", and the solution is then rounded back to 0 or 1.

This is not a heuristic hack, and it is worth understanding why. The LP relaxation of a knapsack with a **single** capacity constraint is the *fractional* knapsack, whose optimum is obtained by the greedy rule: sort items by value density ε_j/η_j descending, take them in order until the budget runs out, and take a fraction of the one item that straddles the boundary. **At most one variable is fractional at the LP optimum.** So rounding perturbs the solution by at most one item out of roughly 200,000, and the LP optimum upper-bounds the true integer optimum. In practice the relaxation is essentially exact here, and the published paper reports that its results came from solutions the solver reported as optimal.

The dual framing — fix the privacy level and minimize utility loss — is the same LP with value and weight swapped and the inequality flipped, which is why the tool exposes `--fixed_param utility` and `--fixed_param privacy`.

**What the objective can and cannot see.** Everything above describes the optimizer as a piece of mathematics, and as mathematics it is sound: the LP is the right relaxation, it is near-exact, and the published paper reports solver-optimal solutions. The interesting question is a different one, and it is the question to hold while reading the rest of this section. The LP maximizes **Σ_j ε_j x_j**. It does not maximize privacy. It maximizes a *number the precompute handed it*, and that number was computed from the **target's original block alone** (§A.4), before any replacement was drawn and without ever comparing the two. Two consequences follow, and both have been measured.

**Blind spot 1 — the objective books credit for moves that change nothing.** ε_j is the surprisal of what the target *had*. It is not the difference between what the target had and what was released. So if the sampler's draw comes back identical to the original, the objective still books ε_j in full, at η_j = 0 (§A.5). The greedy density rule then takes that item **first**, because infinite density sorts ahead of everything, and it takes it at **every** capacity, because a free item never strains a budget.

> **Defect** — **measured in this project** on our rebuilt chr21, target **HG00438** (cohort 44 individuals / 88 haplotypes, 1000 Genomes 30x GRCh38 panel of 3,202 samples), **capacity 0.1, seed 123** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24; `docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25): of the **47,070** moves the optimizer selected, **14,526 changed nothing at all** — **30.9%** (derived from those two counts). The run's reported privacy total was `pmi_gain` = **115,315.84** nats, against a utility spend of **928.92** out of the constructed maximum **9,288.85**, i.e. **10.000%** of maximum utility loss (measured; this is an exact match to the published normalization). The framing matters and is a standing rule of this project: what is wrong here is that **the released code differs from the published Methods** — the paper describes a candidate constrained to differ from the original, and the pinned checkout contains no such constraint and no rejection loop (see Step 14c) — and that **`pmi_gain` measures something narrower than its name suggests**: it is *credit booked by the objective*, not *privacy delivered by the release*. We have not reproduced the paper's figures and nothing here says their published results are wrong.

> **Caution** — **two different denominators, and they must never be swapped.** **30.9% is the share of selected *moves*.** The share of the reported *privacy credit* those moves carry is **7.0%** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24). The 31% figure counts **moves** and is not the share of privacy credit, so a single combined figure — "privacy inflated by ≈31%" — would conflate the two, and **that phrasing must not be used**. `docs/memory.md` says so explicitly in the follow-up section of its 2026-09-24 entry `A-DAT-noop-moves`, headed *"the inflation is 7% of eps, not 31%"*. The correct sentence is a **pair**: *30.9% of selected moves change nothing, and those moves carry 7.0% of the reported privacy credit.* Anyone who writes one number for both has lost the finding.

**Why a third of the moves are only a fourteenth of the credit.** This looks like a contradiction and is not; it is a measurement, and the measurement also explains itself. The split was taken on a random **3,000-move sample** of the 47,070 (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24):

| metric | moves that changed something | moves that changed nothing |
|---|---|---|
| moves in the 3,000-move sample | **2,112** | **888** |
| share of the sample | 70.4% (derived) | **29.6%** (derived; the full-run share is 30.9%) |
| **median** ε_j, nats | **2.1904** | **0.3898** |
| **mean** ε_j, nats | **3.3103** | **0.5964** |
| total ε_j contributed by the group, nats | 2,112 × 3.3103 = **6,991.4** (derived) | 888 × 0.5964 = **529.6** (derived) |
| share of the sample's total ε_j | **93.0%** (derived) | **7.0%** (derived: 529.6 / 7,521.0 = 7.04%, matching the 7.0% measured directly) |

So the resolution is that no-op moves are **not** a random third of the selected moves. They are concentrated on the blocks that were worth the least in the first place: a factor of **5.6×** separates the two medians (2.1904 / 0.3898 = 5.62, derived) and **5.6×** separates the two means (3.3103 / 0.5964 = 5.55, derived). A third of the moves, each worth about a fifth of what a real move is worth, comes to about a fourteenth of the credit. The arithmetic is not a coincidence; it is the same two numbers read twice.

> **Note** — a consistency check on that sample, because a 3,000-of-47,070 subsample invites the question. The sample's mean ε_j over all 3,000 moves is 7,521.0 / 3,000 = **2.507** nats (derived). The whole run's mean is `pmi_gain` / moves = 115,315.84 / 47,070 = **2.450** nats (derived). Those agree to about 2%, which is what one wants from a random subsample of that size. Converting the share to absolute units: 7.0% of 115,315.84 is roughly **8,072 nats** of the reported privacy total that corresponds to no change in the released file (derived).

**And the mechanism behind the concentration is exactly the one you would guess, which is why it is worth pinning down on a real block.** A block gets a low ε_j precisely when the target's pattern there is one that lots of other people carry. But "lots of other people carry it" is also the condition under which a randomly drawn donor is likely to carry it — and the sampler's degeneracy means the replacement is essentially *one donor's alleles* — with the shipped transition constants, **300 of 300** randomly chosen haplotype-model blocks for HG00438 came back identical to a single donor haplotype (**measured in this project**; see departure (b) and Step 14c). So on a common block, the draw has a high chance of handing back the target's own pattern. Block 1077 is the worked case: **82 of 88** donors carry exactly the target's `[0,0,0,0,0]` pattern there (**measured in this project**) — a share of **93%** (derived: 82/88 = 0.932) — and the sampler picks its start donor uniformly among the non-target haplotypes (Step 14c), so a copied donor is very likely to carry that same pattern. And the same 82 explains the score: −log(82/88) = **0.0706** nats (derived), against the measured ε_j of **0.0711** nats per strand — which is also the cleanest available confirmation that ε_j reads as *the surprisal of the target's own block under the cohort model*, and nothing else.

**Which reported quantity moves, and in which direction.** This is the part worth being precise about, because the natural first reading of the defect gets it half wrong. The natural reading is: "the privacy credit is booked and the utility cost is booked too, so both sides of the ratio are corrupted." **The second half is false.** η_j for a no-op on a multi-variant block is **0.00000** exactly (§A.5), so those moves add nothing to the utility spend. Only the numerator gains. Putting the two together on the capacity-0.1 run:

| metric | as reported | with no-op credit removed |
|---|---|---|
| privacy numerator, nats | `pmi_gain` = **115,315.84** (measured) | 93.0% of that = **107,244** (derived) |
| utility spend | **928.92** (measured) | **928.92** — unchanged, because a no-op weighs nothing (derived) |
| privacy per unit utility | **124.14** (derived) | **115.45** (derived) |
| overstatement of the ratio | — | **1.075×** (derived) |

*(all four rows are the single run: chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1, seed 123)*

So the honest verdict on the ratio is: yes, the privacy-per-utility ratio is overstated, by about **7.5%** on this run — but through the numerator alone, and by a mechanism that compounds with the sampler degeneracy rather than being independent of it. The more often the sampler hands back the target's own alleles, the more free credit the LP books. The fix we adopt for our own work is stated in `docs/bugs.md` and is one line: **report the actual allele change, never `pmi_gain`, as the privacy numerator.**

**Blind spot 2 — the objective never saw some of the rarity in the block.** The value vector the LP sorts on is built by the precompute, and on the haplotype-model route that precompute scores the block's **anchors only** (§A.4.2 and §A.8 give the mechanism and the measurements; the underlying record is `docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25). The consequence for *this* section is specific: over **400 random haplotype-model-scored blocks on chr21, 32% contain ignored non-anchor material whose rarity exceeds the block's entire ε_j** (**measured in this project**), so the number the knapsack ranks on is systematically too small for exactly the blocks where a rewrite would matter most. Combined with the ceiling described at §A.4.1 — ε_j cannot exceed about log(2N) = **4.4773** nats (derived, with 2N = 88) plus a small additive term proportional to the number of anchors, because the scoring panel includes the target — the direction is consistent: the objective **systematically under-values the highest-risk blocks** (derived).

> **Open question** — how much does the *selection* actually change if those two effects are removed? **This has not been measured.** For the scoring-side distortion `docs/memory.md` says so explicitly: ε_j enters the LP only through a **ranking**, the selection change when the distortion is removed is unmeasured, and a ranking can be robust to a distortion that changes every value. Nothing in `docs/` measures it for the no-op effect either. So the honest statement is: the value vector is demonstrably narrower than its name suggests, and the effect on which moves get chosen — and therefore on any published frontier — is **unquantified here**. Do not infer one.

**One more property of the budget, because it interacts with all of the above.** Capacity is a fraction of the *constructed maximum* utility loss, not of anything intrinsic. At capacity 0.1 the run spends 10.000% of that maximum, as it should. At capacity **0.5** the same pipeline spends only **33.1%** of that maximum (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`). The knapsack **saturates**: every move with positive value already fits inside the budget, so raising capacity further buys nothing. Two readings follow. First, capacity is not a tight knob at the top end. Second, and more to the point here, the free moves of blind spot 1 are inside the budget at **every** capacity, so no choice of capacity can exclude them.

### A.7 What kind of guarantee this is — and what it is not

This is the single most important conceptual point in the section.

PanMixer's headline privacy number is **ε_private = 0.002** (**published**). The published definition: *the largest privacy risk value at which all obfuscated target individuals could no longer be linked.* (The superseded preprint gave 0.001 and defined it as the *smallest* such value; the published wording is the one to use.) It is found empirically, by running an actual linkage attack against an actual external genotype database at decreasing privacy-risk settings and observing where every target's **gap score** goes negative. Published equation (7) is

> **GapScore(g_target) = L(g_target, g\*\_target) − max over i ≠ target of L(g\_i, g\*\_target)**

where L is a rarity-weighted matching score between a database entry and the released obfuscated genome. In words: **how much better does the true person match the release than the best impostor in the database?** Positive gap score = the true person is still the best match = attack succeeded. Negative = someone else scores higher = attack failed.

**A caution carried forward from §2.4.1, repeated here because anyone re-implementing from this section alone would otherwise not know it exists.** Published equation (5) defines L over S, the set of loci at which the two genotypes are identical, with f_j that genotype's population frequency. A *positive* sum Σ_{j ∈ S} log f_j would be a negative number that gets *more* negative for rarer matches, which is the opposite of the "emphasizes rare allele matches" reading the surrounding text gives it — so the sign is load-bearing. **It is settled, and the paper has it right.** The equation is typeset with its minus sign, L = − Σ log f_j, on **PDF page 10** of `paper/s41467-026-77591-0_reference.pdf`, confirmed by rendering that page at **300 dpi** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). The apparent discrepancy this primer once carried as unresolved was an artefact of **text extraction dropping the glyph** — that PDF has no text layer at all — and not a defect in the paper. With the minus sign the equation agrees with the paper's own prose and with what the code computes.

So ε_private is:

- **empirical** — it is measured by running one attack, not derived;
- **attacker-specific** — a threshold against *this* scoring function and *this* attack;
- **database-specific and cohort-specific** — the paper says so explicitly: "ε_private is not universal as it depends on the specific graph, external genotype database, and target individuals";
- **an average-case statement about observed scores**, not a bound on a distribution.

At that threshold the published paper reports an **average utility loss of 0.28**, on a scale whose maximum is 1.

What ε_private is **not** is a worst-case distributional guarantee. In the privacy literature the gold standard is a statement of the form "for any two neighbouring inputs, the output distributions are close", which bounds *every possible attacker at once* — **differential privacy** is the canonical example, and **total variation distance** (the largest difference in probability that two distributions assign to any single event) is the natural way to measure "close". PanMixer makes no such claim, and structurally cannot: because unselected blocks are copied through **verbatim**, two inputs that differ in an unselected block produce outputs that differ there with certainty, so the two output distributions have **disjoint support** and total variation distance 1. That is not a flaw in PanMixer's execution of its own goal; it is a statement that PanMixer's goal is a different one. It is also the precise gap our own mechanism is designed to close, which is Section 4.

One clarification that has caused confusion elsewhere in this primer. PanMixer's mechanism **does** use randomness — the candidate for each block is a random draw — and that randomness is operationally load-bearing: if the seed leaked, an attacker could re-run the sampler on candidate inputs until the output matched. But no **formal** claim of the paper is *proved from* that randomness; ε_private is measured against realised outputs. Both statements are true and they are not in tension: *seed secrecy is necessary in practice; randomness is not what the guarantee rests on, because there is no distributional guarantee to rest.*


> **Question** — "ε_private = 0.002 — 0.002 of *what*? It has no units anywhere in this section. And which external genotype database was it measured against, and how many people are in it?"
>
> It is a setting on the paper's **privacy-risk axis** — the same axis on which the per-block quantity ε_j of §A.4 lives. ε_j itself is measured in **nats**, because the code takes `np.log`, the natural logarithm, everywhere it computes a self-information (measured in this project, reading `get_support_and_pmi.py`), and one nat is about 1.44 bits (derived). What this primer cannot tell you is how the published operating point is constructed from those per-block values: **the aggregation from the per-block ε_j values to the single reported risk number has not been established in this project**, so do not assume 0.002 is a quantity in nats, and do not read it as a probability or a percentage either. The honest statement is that it is a threshold on an axis the paper defines and we have not reconstructed.
>
> The database is the masked reference-panel array that `gap_score.py` loads — `1000g_*_masked_posref.npy`, built from the phased 1000 Genomes panel of Step 3 (measured in this project, reading the code). How many people are in it is exactly where the release becomes ambiguous, and the honest answer has to say so: the shipped pipeline downloads **1000 Genomes Phase 3, 2,504 samples** (measured in this project), while `constants.py:38` hardcodes `NUM_1000G_SUBJECTS = 3202`, the size of the **1000 Genomes 30x GRCh38** release, and the 30x attack-database files that `diploid_gap_score.py` prefers are produced by **no script in the released repository** (measured in this project; see departure (e)). So the file the published 0.002 was produced against cannot be identified from the release.
>
> Say the consequence precisely, because it is narrow: **we have not reproduced 0.002 and we are not claiming it is wrong.** What we can say is that the released code, followed as documented, does not identify the database its headline number refers to.

> **Question** — "You say PanMixer 'structurally cannot' make a worst-case claim because unselected blocks go through verbatim, so TV is 1. Can you show me the actual pair of inputs? Which block, what alleles, and what does the attacker literally do?"
>
> Yes, and the construction needs nothing exotic — any unselected block will do, and most blocks are unselected. At capacity 0.1 on chr21 for HG00438, **47,070 of 201,514** (block, strand) decisions were selected, so about **77%** of block-haplotypes are republished exactly as they arrived (measured in this project; the 201,514 figure is derived as 2 × 100,757 block entries).
>
> Pick one such block b. Construct two admissible inputs, p and q, that are **identical at every row of the chromosome except the rows of block b**, where p carries allele `0` at each row and q carries allele `1`. Because `stacker.py:37-70` begins from `new_variants = original_haplotypes.copy()` and overwrites only the rows of blocks the optimizer selected (measured in this project), the release derived from p carries `0` at those rows **with probability 1**, and the release derived from q carries `1` **with probability 1**. The two output distributions put their mass on disjoint sets of outputs.
>
> The attacker is one line long: *read the rows of block b in the released VCF; output whichever of p and q matches.* Its accuracy is **1**, not 0.5 plus a small advantage. Total variation distance between the two release distributions is therefore **1**, which is its maximum, and the bound (1 + TV)/2 of §1.3.5 gives an attacker-success ceiling of 1 — i.e. no ceiling at all. §4.12 works the same construction out at length.
>
> Two honest qualifications. First, this says nothing about whether the *specific* attack of Step 21 succeeds — it is a statement about what kind of claim is available, not a demonstration of a break. Second, it is not a failure of PanMixer at its own goal: its goal is an empirically located operating point for one attacker, and it reaches one.

Three more questions about that threshold, and the first is about a symbol rather than a measurement.

> **Question** — "The sign problem in equation (5). If the published score is negative and gets *more* negative for rarer matches, then a negative gap score might not mean the attack failed at all. Doesn't that flip the meaning of ε_private?"
>
> It would, if the sign were wrong in the **code**. It is not: `gap_score.py` scores a database entry by summing **−log(probability)** over the loci where that entry's genotype equals the released genotype (measured in this project, reading the code), which is a positive, rarity-weighted score that grows as matches get rarer. Under that arithmetic a positive gap score means the true person out-scores every impostor, and a negative one means somebody else out-scores them. The direction the section states is the direction the code computes.
>
> As for the paper, the question is now closed and this primer no longer says two things. The minus sign **is** typeset, on **PDF page 10** of `paper/s41467-026-77591-0_reference.pdf`, confirmed by rendering that page at **300 dpi** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26); with it, the equation agrees with the paper's own prose. Its apparent absence was an artefact of text extraction eating the glyph, and that PDF carries no text layer at all, so extraction was never evidence about it. So: **there is no sign discrepancy in the published paper**; there is a discrepancy between the paper and any extraction pipeline that drops the glyph, which matters only because implementing the sign backwards ranks every candidate in reverse.
>
> And the consequence for ε_private is a non-consequence, deliberately: we have not reproduced the published figure, so nothing here licenses a claim about it in either direction.

> **Question** — "If ε_private is empirical and attacker-specific, why report a single number for it at all? Isn't the honest output a curve of attack success against capacity?"
>
> The curve is what the experiment produces. `experiment_starter` (Step 16) takes the cross product of subjects and capacities, `gather_results` (Step 27) collects one `gap_score_all.json` per (subject, capacity), and the reported quantity per cell is `genotype_g_to_gstar` — the gap score (measured in this project, reading the code). Sweeping capacity therefore gives, for every target, a trajectory of gap scores. ε_private is the published **summary** of that surface: *the largest privacy risk value at which all obfuscated target individuals could no longer be linked* (quoted from the published paper).
>
> Why summarise at all: because a single operating point is what lets every other number be quoted comparably. The published average utility loss of **0.28** on a scale whose maximum is 1, the Wasserstein allele-frequency divergences of **0.006 / 0.006 / 0.005** for all alleles, SNPs and rare SNPs, and the LD divergence of **0.003** (all quoted from the published paper) are all reported *at* ε_private. Without a chosen point there is nothing to attach them to.
>
> The paper does not hide the cost of that choice — it states that ε_private "is not universal as it depends on the specific graph, external genotype database, and target individuals" (quoted from the published paper). For our own comparison the decision already taken is the stricter one: report **both frontiers**, run the attacks **per operating point and never pooled**, and never convert between the two systems' privacy parameters, because ε_pmi and τ are formally incomparable (project decisions recorded in `docs/plan.md`; see §4.11).

One last question here, and it is about what our own mechanism is *for* rather than about theirs.

> **Question** — "Is the fact that their guarantee is empirical the main thing our τ buys us, or is it the fact that it covers attackers we haven't thought of? Those feel like different sales pitches."
>
> They are different, and both are real, and it is worth keeping them apart because only one of them is about attackers.
>
> The first is **quantification over attackers**. ε_private is a threshold at which *one* scoring function, against *one* database, failed for every target. τ bounds the success of **every** attacker at once, including attackers nobody has written, because the bound is on the distance between output distributions and an attacker is just a test on the output (§1.3.5, §4.1).
>
> The second is **worst case versus average case over inputs**. ε_private is a statement about observed scores; τ is a statement about *every admissible pair of input paths*, so it does not weaken at the tails — which matters here because the tails are exactly where the identifying material lives. The measured shape of departure (c) is the cleanest illustration: the privacy scorer's ceiling barely moves a typical block (median ratio **1.03×** between the leave-one-out and shipped scores) while truncating the rarest by up to **17.4×** (measured in this project).
>
> The price of both is stated where it belongs and should not be discounted here: §4.10.4 shows the per-block tilt budget is thin, and **no value of τ has been run end to end in this project** (project status, `docs/plan.md`).

### A.8 Anchors reconsidered: what one actually is, and whether a design needs them

§A.3 defined an anchor in one sentence, and §A.4.2 has just shown that the definition decides what the privacy score can see. This subsection answers the question that follows: **why does the anchor concept exist at all, and what would be lost by deleting it?** The answer matters for us specifically, because our v1 sampler deletes it.

#### A.8.1 Which callset defines the set

> **Definition** — an **anchor** is a pangenome VCF record that **also appears in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset**, matched exactly on the triple (POS, REF, whole-ALT-string). The matching is done at `get_mappings.py:45-49` and the resulting mapping is loaded by `hmm.py` (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned code). Anchors are the positions that the per-block HMM uses as chain positions — both when it **scores** a block (Route H of §A.4.1) and when it **samples** a replacement (Step 14c).

Two cautions attach to that definition, and both are the kind of thing this reader has asked about by name.

- **It is not the paper's definition.** The published Methods define the anchor set as `V_1000G` (published page 8, lines 944–945) — variants shared with 1000 Genomes. The released code uses the PanGenie overlap instead, loaded from a pickle whose *name* says `thousand_g_alignments`. That is departure (d), and the mismatch has a bounded blast radius because PanGenie is also GRCh38, so the anchor join is a same-build join.
- **"Anchor" is not the same property as "has population frequency data".** These are two different sets built by two different joins, and conflating them produces a specific wrong prediction that §A.9 unpicks. **Measured in this project**: **10,460** non-anchor variants still receive real 1000 Genomes frequencies.

> **Question** — *"The PanGenie callset is 368 samples genotyped against the same graph. Is the target one of the 368? Because if it is, then the set of chain positions itself knows about the target, and that is the thing we keep saying our own method is not allowed to do."*
>
> The honest answer is that **this project has not measured it**. `docs/data.md` records the callset as `external/PanMixer/starting_data/PG.vcf.gz`, Zenodo 7669083 `grch38_all-samples_bi_all`, **368 samples** (**measured in this project**), and `docs/data.md` records the pangenome cohort as **44 individuals / 88 haplotypes** (**measured in this project**). The **intersection of those two sample lists is not recorded anywhere in `docs/`**, so no number for it may be written here. What would settle it is one command: read the `#CHROM` sample line of `PG.vcf.gz` and intersect it with the 44 names in `docs/data.md`.
>
> Two things are worth separating while that stays open. First, the anchor role is **positional**: an anchor contributes a *chain position*, and the alleles emitted at that position come from the pangenome matrix `X`, not from PanGenie. So even a target-containing callset would not put the target's PanGenie genotypes into the emission model. Second — and this is the part that matters for us — it would still make the *choice* of chain positions depend on the target, which is exactly the shape of the condition our own mechanism has to satisfy (the output menu fixed without looking at the target, §4.8).
