For **our** v1 this is moot, because v1 deletes the anchor concept and makes every variant a chain position, so no external callset is consulted at all (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, REPLACE item 1). It is a question about reading PanMixer's numbers, not about inheriting its hazard.

> **Question** — *"Matched exactly on POS, REF and the whole ALT string. At `chr21:14569980` the REF is 257,439 bp and the ALT field lists 90 alleles. Does a record like that have any chance of matching, or is 'not an anchor' basically guaranteed for anything big?"*
>
> For that specific record the answer is measured and it is no: row **51791** is **not an anchor**, because the test `r in align` is False (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md` Correction 8, 2026-09-24). And a whole-ALT-string equality test is indeed unforgiving — one extra allele anywhere in the list fails the join even when POS and REF agree.
>
> But the general rule you are reaching for does **not** hold, and the counts say so. **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25): of the **38,943** anchors that are not top-level SNPs, **25,507** are top-level **non-SNPs** — indels and larger records that matched anyway. Large records therefore do sometimes clear the join. What is **not measured** in this project is the match rate *as a function of* allele count or REF length, which is the number your question actually asks for. What would settle it: bucket the 340,824 records by `num_alleles.npy` and by REF length and compute the anchor rate per bucket. Until that exists, treat "big records rarely match" as an untested intuition rather than a finding.

#### A.8.2 Why a design that *infers* a chain wants restricted chain positions

The published rationale, **quoted from the published paper**, is about the count, not the identity:

> **Note** — quoted from the published paper: *"dependent on the number of top-level SNPs that exist inside the LD block. If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM, and we can synthesize more accurate new haplotype blocks. If only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM."*

The **≥2** part of that is not arbitrary and is worth saying out loud, because it is the kind of thing a reader is expected to supply and then wonders whether they supplied it correctly: **a Markov chain needs two positions in order to have a transition between them** (**derived**). With one position there is an emission and nothing else — no copying, no recombination, no mosaic. So a one-position "chain" is not an HMM in any useful sense, and PanMixer routes those blocks to the allele-frequency sampler instead.

The *identity* part — why restrict to top-level SNPs at all, rather than using every variant — has a real justification, and it is a data-quality one rather than a modelling one. **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25), by record class on chr21:

| metric | top-level SNPs (the paper's `V_SNPs`) | top-level non-SNPs | nested records (`LV ≥ 1`) |
|---|---|---|---|
| number of records | **253,000** | **56,335** | **31,489** |
| mean share of the 88 haplotypes missing (`-1`) | **2.25%** | **3.11%** | **22.53%** |
| records more than 50% missing | **421** | **722** | **3,515** |

Nested records are roughly **ten times** more often uncalled than top-level SNPs (**derived**). A chain position where a quarter of the donor panel has no allele is a position where the emission model has almost nothing to say, and where a `-1` forces the implementation into a policy decision it never declares. **That** is the real justification for restricting chain positions, and the consequence is measurable: mean missingness at PanMixer's anchors is **0.26%**, while mean missingness once **every** variant becomes a chain position — which is what our v1 does — rises to **11.31%**, with **7,555** positions more than 25% missing (all three **measured in this project**).

Two rationales that people reach for first do **not** survive measurement, and saying so saves the reader from inventing them:

- **It is not about compute.** Anchors number **273,475** against **340,824** total variants, only **1.25×** more positions if the restriction were lifted — **24.1 million** operations versus **30.0 million** at K = 88 (all **measured in this project**). Negligible.
- **It is not about multi-allelic emissions.** The median ALT count is **1** even for non-SNP records (**measured in this project**). The 90-allele tail at sites like `chr21:14569980` is real but rare: **7** sites on chr21 have ≥88 alleles, **155** have ≥44, **618** have ≥20.

> **Question** — *"2.25%, 3.11% and 22.53% missing — missing out of what? The 88 pangenome haplotypes, the 368 PanGenie samples, or the 3,202 people in the panel? And is the number an average over records or over haplotypes?"*
>
> Out of **the 88 pangenome haplotypes**, and it is a **mean over records within the class**. The quantity `docs/memory.md` records for 2026-09-25 is, in its own words, *"fraction of the 88 haplotypes with no called allele"* (**measured in this project**) — so for one record it is (number of the 88 haplotypes whose entry in `X` is `-1`) / 88, and the table's cell is the average of that fraction across all records in the class. Neither PanGenie nor the 3,202-sample panel enters this number at all; it is computed entirely from the pangenome allele matrix.
>
> The right unit to carry forward is therefore *"at a randomly chosen nested record, about 20 of the 88 donors have no allele"* — 22.53% × 88 = **19.8 haplotypes** (**derived**). That is the sentence that makes the emission argument concrete: an emission at such a position has a fifth of its state space saying nothing.

> **Question** — *"Wait. You just told me a Markov chain needs two positions to have a transition, and that's why PanMixer sends singleton blocks somewhere else. But our earlier spec put singletons through the same sampler as T = 1. Isn't that exactly the same objection, and haven't we just walked into it?"*
>
> No, and the reason is that the ≥2 rule is a statement about **inferring** a chain, not about **sampling** one. Take the two cases apart.
>
> A chain of length **T = 1** has one emission and **zero** transitions; the transition loop simply executes zero times. What comes out is a draw from the state distribution at that single position — for us, a tilted categorical draw over the donor haplotypes (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, REPLACE item 2, which uses exactly that phrase). That is a perfectly well-defined object. PanMixer arrives at the *same* kind of object — a single-position draw — but reaches it through a **second, different code path** (independent allele frequencies, Route F of §A.4.1), and it is the existence of two code paths that produces the threshold disagreement of §A.9.4.
>
> So the published sentence *"if only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM"* (**quoted from the published paper**) is true of a design that has to *estimate* copying structure from the observations inside the block — with one observation there is nothing to estimate. It is not true of a design that *copies donors*, because the donor panel is the same **K = 88** haplotypes at every position, whether there is one position or five hundred. Note the panel size, because it is a place the two settings diverge: our target is **external** to the cohort, so nothing is held out and `K` is 88 unconditionally. The `K = 2(N − 1) = 86` panel belongs to the **head-to-head arm alone**, where a cohort member stands in as target and externality has to be simulated (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`).
>
> One update to the gate that was written against this point. *"Singleton blocks provably traverse the same sampler with T = 1, and no allele-frequency branch exists in the source"* (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, exit gate 2) was written against a **block-structured** spec, where the worry was two code paths inside one design. Under the decided chain architecture — **model C**, one chain over the whole chromosome with the **individual variant** as the unit — the gate's first half has nothing left to test: there are no blocks, therefore no singleton blocks, no `T = 1` chains and no per-block `Z_b`. One forward pass, one backward sample (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The second half survives intact and is the part that mattered: **no allele-frequency branch exists in the source.**

#### A.8.3 How many variants qualify, and how many do not

| metric | the paper's anchor notion (`V_SNPs`: `LV=0` **and** SNP) | the released code's anchor set (PanGenie overlap) |
|---|---|---|
| records on chr21 | **253,000** | **273,475** |
| share of all 340,824 chr21 records | **74.2%** (derived) | **80.2%** (derived) |
| records **not** in this set | **87,824** (derived) | **67,349 = 19.8%** (derived) |
| overlap between the two notions | **234,532** (measured in this project) | **234,532** (measured in this project) |

*(Record counts and the overlap are **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25; the shares and complements are **derived** from them.)*

The interesting part is the asymmetry of the disagreement, all **measured in this project**:

- **38,943 anchors (14.2% of the anchor set) are not top-level SNPs**, and of those, **13,436 are nested records** — precisely the class the paper's own rationale excludes, and precisely the class with 22.53% missingness. The remaining **25,507** are top-level non-SNPs.
- **18,468 top-level SNPs (7.3% of them) are not anchors**, so they are excluded from every chain despite satisfying the published criterion exactly.

> **Caution** — the PanGenie overlap is a **proxy** for "this is a well-behaved, reliably-called position", and it is **neither necessary nor sufficient** for that property (**derived**). It admits 13,436 of the worst-behaved records and rejects 18,468 of the best-behaved ones. A design that wants low-missingness chain positions should test missingness, which is measurable, rather than membership in someone else's callset, which is a correlate.

> **Question** — *"74.2% and 80.2% — are those two percentages over the same denominator, or did the denominator move between columns? And if the overlap is 234,532, how many records are in at least one of the two notions, and how many are in neither?"*
>
> Same denominator in both columns: **340,824 chr21 records** (**measured in this project**). 253,000 / 340,824 = 74.2% and 273,475 / 340,824 = 80.2%, both **derived** from measured counts.
>
> The union follows by inclusion–exclusion, and it is worth having because it is the number that says how much of the chromosome either definition can reach:
>
> | metric, chr21 | value |
> |---|---|
> | in the paper's notion (`V_SNPs`) | **253,000** (measured in this project) |
> | in the released code's anchor set | **273,475** (measured in this project) |
> | in **both** | **234,532** (measured in this project) |
> | in **at least one** (253,000 + 273,475 − 234,532) | **291,943** (derived) |
> | that union as a share of 340,824 | **85.7%** (derived) |
> | in **neither** (340,824 − 291,943) | **48,881 = 14.3%** (derived) |
>
> So **48,881 chr21 records are excluded from every chain under either definition** (**derived**) — they are neither top-level SNPs nor PanGenie matches. That is the population of records whose rarity no version of the anchor idea can price, and it is the reason §A.4.2's blindness is not repaired by switching callsets.

> **Question** — *"The 18,468 top-level SNPs that get dropped — is that a risk we inherit or an opportunity for us? Because it sounds like free privacy signal lying on the floor."*
>
> Strictly, **neither**, and the reason is worth being precise about. v1 **deletes the anchor concept**: every one of the 340,824 chr21 variants becomes a chain position, so no variant is excluded for failing to appear in someone else's callset (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, REPLACE item 1). There is no set of 18,468 dropped records in our design to recover, because there is no dropping step. The 18,468 is therefore **evidence about the proxy's quality** — it is what makes the Caution above a measurement rather than an opinion — not a defect we carry forward.
>
> The early spec did inherit one thing unchanged here — the **plink LD block boundaries and the site-to-block assignment by position** — and the decided chain architecture has since dropped that too. Under **model C** the chain unit is the **individual variant**, so there are no LD blocks in the mechanism, no plink run, and **no external panel in the mechanism's input set at all**; the external panel survives only as the attack database in the evaluation (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). Blocks are kept only for the model-B fallback and for parity runs against PanMixer. So there is no inherited segmentation left to audit either. What does *not* go away is the cost of making every variant a chain position: it raises mean missingness at chain positions from **0.26%** to **11.31%** (**measured in this project**), which is the subject of §A.10.

#### A.8.4 The question this section exists to answer

> **Question** — *why not just consider every variant inside a block, rather than only the anchor-matching ones?*

The honest answer has two halves, and they point in opposite directions depending on what kind of sampler you are building.

**For a design that INFERS a chain, the restriction buys something.** PanMixer's HMM has to decide, position by position, which donor it is copying from, and it decides that from emissions. An emission at a position where 22.53% of donors have no allele is close to uninformative, and forces an undeclared missing-data policy at every such position. Restricting the chain to positions that an independent external callset has confirmed is a cheap way of buying a chain whose positions are almost always called — measurably so, 0.26% against 11.31%. The non-anchor variants inside a block are not thrown away by the sampler; they are **filled in afterwards** from the chosen donor or from allele frequencies. On PanMixer's own terms the restriction is a modelling shortcut with a defensible motivation, badly implemented via the wrong callset.

**For a design that COPIES DONORS rather than inferring a chain, the restriction buys nothing.** This is the crux, and it is worth stating slowly. If the released block is drawn as *a path through the donor panel* — pick a donor, take that donor's alleles, possibly switch donors partway — then every record in the block gets its allele from whichever donor is being copied at that point, and **whether the record appears in an external SNP callset is irrelevant to that assignment**. No external frequency is consulted, because no frequency is needed: the donor already has an allele there. A copying model does not need a position to be "well characterised" in order to copy it; it needs only the donor panel, which is the same `K` haplotypes at every position — `K = 88`, the full cohort, since our target is external and nothing is held out; `K = 86` only in the head-to-head arm, where a cohort member stands in as target (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The anchor requirement, in that setting, does no work at all — while the cost of keeping it is exactly the 163× case of §A.4.2 and the 32% of blocks where the ignored material outweighs the scored material.

There is one thing the restriction *was* doing that does not disappear when it is dropped, and pretending otherwise would be dishonest. Missingness does not go away; it moves. Making every variant a chain position raises mean missingness at chain positions from **0.26%** to **4.26%**, and adds a class that is far worse than either: the non-anchor records inside multi-variant blocks run **11.31%**, with **7,555** over 25% missing (measured in this project; per-class table in `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). What to do at a `-1` then becomes an explicit, declared choice rather than an implicit one — and that choice is an **open question in this project, not a decision**. The options named in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy` are **renormalise**, **wildcard**, **mismatch** and **restrict**, each with a different bias, and the reasons they differ come straight from the **five** distinct causes of a `-1` (**measured in this project**, `docs/terms.md`; Step 9 is where `-1` is introduced). PanMixer's anchor rule is, in effect, the **restrict** option, and it is being kept deliberately as a baseline **because it is the published behaviour**.

> **Open question** — which missing-data policy v1 adopts is **not settled**. Do not read this subsection as having chosen one. The options and the measured missingness that bears on them are carried in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy`; the decision is deferred.

> **Question** — *"Let me say that back to you and you tell me where I'm wrong. The anchor restriction exists to keep chain positions well-called; that reason is real if you are inferring a chain and empty if you are copying donors; the price of keeping it is the 163× block and the 32% of blocks where the ignored rarity is bigger than the score; and the one thing I actually have to decide is what happens at a −1. Do I have that right?"*
>
> Clause by clause.
>
> **"Exists to keep chain positions well-called" — right, but as a *proxy*, not as a test.** The data-quality motivation is real (nested records are 22.53% uncalled against 2.25% for top-level SNPs, **measured in this project**), and the implementation does not test that property; it tests membership in the PanGenie callset, which admits 13,436 nested records and rejects 18,468 qualifying top-level SNPs (**measured in this project**). So the motivation is sound and the instrument is a correlate.
>
> **"Real if you are inferring a chain, empty if you are copying donors" — right, and this is the crux of §A.8.4.** Add one qualifier: it is empty *for the assignment of alleles*. A copying model still has to decide what an emission does when the donor it is copying carries `-1`, which is precisely why the restriction's job does not vanish — it moves.
>
> **"The price is the 163× block and the 32%" — right, with one correction about *what* pays it.** Both numbers are prices paid by the **privacy score**, ε_j, not by the sampler. Block 1083 scores **0.0349** nats on Route H (**measured in this project**) against **5.6956** nats over all three variants (**measured in this project**), a factor of **163×** (**derived**, §A.4.2); and over 400 random Route-H blocks the ignored non-anchor rarity exceeds the reported ε_j in **32%** of them (**measured in this project**). What the *sampler* does with non-anchors is a separate thing: they are filled in afterwards from the chosen donor or from allele frequencies, so they are not left blank — they are simply never *valued*.
>
> **"The one thing I actually have to decide is what happens at a −1" — right, and it is deliberately still open.** The four named options in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy` are **RENORMALISE** (drop that donor from the state distribution at that position), **WILDCARD** (treat missing as matching whatever the target has, which makes poorly-assembled donors universally attractive), **MISMATCH** (treat it as a difference, which penalises assembly gaps as though they were genuine variation) and **RESTRICT** (exclude ill-behaved positions from the chain altogether, which is what the anchor rule does in effect). The evidence that bears on the choice is the missingness shift — 0.26% to 4.26%, with 7,555 positions over 25% missing (**measured in this project**) — and the fact that `-1` conflates **five** distinct conditions that do not want the same treatment (**measured in this project**, `docs/terms.md`). This primer does not choose among them and no number above should be read as arguing for one.

> **Question** — *"Hold on, these two things contradict each other. We're deleting the anchor concept, but we also said to keep 'do it the anchor way' as one of the missing-data options because it's the published behaviour. How can both be true?"*
>
> They are both true because the anchor rule appears in **two different roles**, and only one of them is deleted.
>
> **Role 1 — as the definition of which positions are in the chain.** This is the role §A.8.3 measured and found to be a bad proxy, and this is the role v1 deletes: every variant becomes a chain position, no external callset is consulted (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, REPLACE item 1).
>
> **Role 2 — as a way of handling missingness.** Excluding ill-behaved positions from the chain *is* a missing-data policy; it is the one `docs/terms.md` names **RESTRICT**. PanMixer's anchor rule is, in effect, an instance of it, and it is carried on the menu *because it is the published behaviour*, so that a v1 run has something published to be compared against (`docs/terms.md`, `missing_policy`, recorded 2026-09-25).
>
> So the position is: the anchor set is not v1's **default** definition of chain positions, and RESTRICT remains one of four **candidate** policies. Which of the four v1 adopts is not decided here — see the Open question above.

Two further pointers, so the thread is followable. First, dropping anchors is one of five replacements in the v1 sampler specification pinned on 2026-09-25 as `A-IMP-v1-sampler` in `docs/plan.md` (Section 4 gives the theory behind it); the other four are a single code path for every block with a singleton treated as a chain of length T = 1, exact tilted backward sampling in place of forward-only simulation, transition constants re-derived from the published Methods in centiMorgans, and no knapsack. Second, dropping anchors also removes the published rationale's own stated limitation — *"if only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM"* — because that is a limitation of **inferring** a chain, not of **copying** donors (**derived**).

### A.9 The allele-frequency route: which dataset supplies f_v, and a threshold that disagrees with itself

#### A.9.1 When a block is scored by frequencies rather than by the chain

Route F of §A.4.1 is taken when a block holds **exactly one** variant, or when it holds **two or more variants and zero anchors** (`get_support_and_pmi.py:87`, **measured in this project**). The first case dominates by count: **89,087 of the 100,757** block-dictionary entries on chr21 are singletons (**measured in this project**), which is **88.4% of entries** (**measured in this project**) but only **26.1% of variants** (**derived** from 89,087 / 340,824). Keep those two denominators apart.

The second case is a small residual. chr21 holds **11,670 multi-variant blocks** alongside the 89,087 singletons (**measured in this project**, `docs/plan.md`), and **10,954** blocks are scored by the chain route, i.e. have at least one anchor (**measured in this project**, §A.9.4). So the multi-variant **zero-anchor** blocks number **11,670 − 10,954 = 716**, which is **0.71% of entries** (**derived**). Be careful with a number you will meet elsewhere: the frequently quoted "10.4% of entries take the HMM path, holding 71.4% of variants" is measured against the **≥ 2**-anchor *sampling* threshold, not the **≥ 1**-anchor *scoring* threshold used here (see §A.9.4), so the residual it implies is a different set of blocks.

> **Question** — *"The 10,954 blocks scored by the chain route at ≥1 anchor — is that 10,954 out of the 11,670 multi-variant blocks, or out of all 100,757 entries? Because a singleton with one anchor in it would satisfy '≥1 anchor' too, and then the subtraction you just did is wrong."*
>
> Out of the **11,670 multi-variant blocks**, so the subtraction holds. The branch at `get_support_and_pmi.py:87` tests the **one-variant case first**: a block holding exactly one variant is sent to Route F *however many anchors it holds*, so a singleton never enters the 10,954 no matter what (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned code). Only after the one-variant case is excluded does the anchor count decide anything.
>
> That is what makes **11,670 − 10,954 = 716** (**derived**) a subtraction between two sets that are actually nested: both are drawn from the multi-variant blocks, and 10,954 is **measured in this project** (`docs/bugs.md`, 2026-09-25). If the 10,954 had been counted over all 100,757 entries the difference would be meaningless, and you were right to check. The three denominators in play in this subsection are **100,757** block-dictionary entries, **11,670** multi-variant blocks and **340,824** variants; every share above names which one it uses.

> **Question** — *"Fine, 716 blocks is 0.71% of entries. But how many *variants* do those 716 hold? A block can be fifty variants long, so 0.71% of entries could still be a lot of the chromosome. Say both denominators."*
>
> This project **has not measured it**. `docs/` carries the block *count* for the multi-variant zero-anchor set (derivable as 716, above) and carries no variant count, no size distribution and no mean block length for it. The same hole exists for the 452 blocks of §A.9.4 — `docs/bugs.md` records the count and nothing about their size. Asking the question in both denominators is exactly right, and the honest answer is that only one of the two exists.
>
> What would settle it: for each of the two sets, sum the block sizes out of the block dictionary and report both the entry share (out of 100,757) and the variant share (out of 340,824). Until that is run, no statement about how much of the chromosome these blocks cover may be written.

> **Open question** — there is **no measured distribution of ε_j for multi-variant zero-anchor blocks** in this project. The count of such blocks is derivable (716, above); how much privacy value they carry is not measured. Do not infer it.

#### A.9.2 The question, and why the obvious answer is wrong

> **Question** — *for the allele-frequency route, what dataset is the population frequency calculated from? Especially since, by definition, if there are no anchors then the frequency would be 1?*

The second clause contains a hidden premise worth exposing, because the premise is the whole confusion. It assumes that "no anchors" means "no external data about these variants", and therefore that the only population available for counting is the target itself — a population of one, in which the target's own allele has frequency 1 and self-information −log 1 = 0.

Both steps fail.

- **"No anchors" does not mean "no external data".** The anchor set is one join (pangenome ↔ PanGenie, on the full REF/ALT triple). The frequency table is built by a **different** procedure with a **different** priority order, and the two are not nested. **Measured in this project**: **10,460** non-anchor variants receive real 1000 Genomes frequencies. A block with **zero anchors** can be **fully populated with real external frequencies** and still be routed to the site-independent sampler, because the routing is decided by anchor count and the frequencies are not.
- **The frequency is never computed from the target alone.** Even in the worst case — a variant found in neither external source — the denominator is **constructed**, not counted. That construction is the direct answer to the puzzle, and it is worked below.

> **Question** — *"There is a version of my edge case that I think still works though. Suppose the target carries `-1` at every variant in a zero-anchor block. Then every term in the sum is zero, ε_j is zero, and the optimizer has no reason to touch it. Does that break the assumption, and does it ever actually happen?"*
>
> The first half is **correct as stated**, and it follows directly from the code rather than from an argument. On Route F the line `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0` sets the contribution of every uncalled site to exactly **0** (**measured in this project**, §A.4.1). A block in which the target is uncalled at every variant therefore books **ε_j = 0** on that strand, which is the minimum the score can take, and a move worth zero is a move the optimizer will never buy under any capacity. So yes: "the target has no data here" and "the target's data here is completely unremarkable" produce the **same** number, and ε_j cannot distinguish them. That is the same class of ambiguity as the Caution in §A.4.2, reached by a different route.
>
> The second half — does it happen on chr21 — is **not measured in this project**. What `docs/` does record is that HG00438 carries a called allele at **319,092** of the 340,824 chr21 records (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md` Correction 7), so **21,732** records are uncalled for it (**derived**). How those 21,732 distribute across the 100,757 block-dictionary entries is not recorded, so the number of all-missing blocks cannot be derived from it and must not be guessed. What would settle it: group the `-1` mask for the target by block id and count the blocks whose every entry is `-1`.

#### A.9.3 The three-way priority, in the order the code applies it

`get_af.py:27-78` decides, **per variant**, which of three branches supplies the counts. The file's own docstring gives the reasoning, **quoted from the code**: *"the 1000g phased dataset is the most reliable, the alignments dataset second, the pangenome least."* The output is `chr{N}/allele_frequencies.npy`, shape `(340824, 90)` float64 on our build, and **every row sums to 1** after the final division (both **measured in this project**; see Step 13).

| metric | branch 1 — variant maps into the 1000G phased panel | branch 2 — maps into PanGenie but not 1000G | branch 3 — novel (neither) |
|---|---|---|---|
| population the alleles are counted in | **the phased 1000 Genomes panel only** | **PanGenie, plus all 88 pangenome haplotypes** | **the 88 pangenome haplotypes only**, then padded |
| is the target counted in its own frequency? | **not** under the shipped Phase 3 panel; **yes** under our 30x repin, because 39 of 44 HPRC donors incl. HG00438 are in it | **yes** (`get_af.py:41-53`) | **yes** (`get_af.py:68-73`) |
| chr21 variants taking this branch | **258,610** | **25,325** | **56,889** |
| share of the 340,824 records | **75.9%** | **7.4%** | **16.7%** |
| median −log f_v where HG00438 carries a **non-REF** allele | **0.63 nats** | **0.43 nats** | **5.41 nats** |

*(Variant counts, shares and medians are all **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24. The branch logic and the target-inclusion line references are **measured in this project** by reading the pinned code.)*

The denominator in branch 3 is the answer to the puzzle:

> **Definition** — after counting the pangenome haplotypes, branch 3 pads the **reference-allele** count until the row sums to **`NOVEL_DENOMINATOR = 2 × (N_1000G + N_pangenome)`** — the same quantity Step 13 writes as `2 × (N_panel + N_pangenome)`. On our rebuilt chr21 with the **30x GRCh38 panel (3,202 samples)** and 44 pangenome individuals, that is 2 × (3,202 + 44) = **6,492** (**derived**). Under the shipped **Phase 3 panel (2,504 samples)** it would be 2 × (2,504 + 44) = **5,096** (**derived**). Always name the panel with the number.

> **Worked example** — a novel allele carried by exactly **one** of the 88 pangenome haplotypes, and by nothing in either external source. Naively you might count 1 carrier out of 1 observation and get f_v = 1, or count 1 out of 88 and get f_v = 0.0114. The code does neither. It puts the count of 1 over the constructed denominator **6,492**, giving
>
> **f_v = 1 / 6,492 = 1.54 × 10⁻⁴** (**derived**), and **−ln(1.54 × 10⁻⁴) = 8.78 nats** (**derived**).
>
> Under the Phase 3 panel the same allele would score **−ln(1/5,096) = 8.54 nats** (**derived**). So the answer to "wouldn't the frequency be 1?" is: **no — it is floored at about 1.5 × 10⁻⁴, and the floor is set by the panel size, not by how many carriers were seen.** The padding exists precisely to stop a novel allele from looking common because only its own carriers were counted; the reference allele absorbs all the padded probability mass.

There is a place where something close to "frequency 1" genuinely does appear, and it is the mirror image of what the question expected. Because the padding is dumped onto REF, at a novel site the **reference** allele ends up with a median frequency of **0.998** (**measured in this project**), and the target usually carries REF there. So **most novel sites contribute almost exactly 0 nats** to ε_j. The privacy signal from novel material is concentrated in the **11,760** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a **non-REF** allele at a novel variant (**measured in this project**) — which is where the 5.41-nat median in the table above comes from, an order of magnitude above branches 1 and 2.

> **Question** — *"Branch 3 counts the 88 pangenome haplotypes and then pads REF until the row sums to 6,492. That is adding thousands of imaginary people who all carry the reference allele. Is that a prior? Does it have a name, or is it just a fudge?"*
>
> It has a name: it is a **pseudo-count**, the standard device of adding fictitious observations to a count table before dividing — the same family as Laplace ("add-one") smoothing. What makes this instance unusual is that it is **asymmetric**: the added mass all goes to the **reference** allele rather than being spread evenly over the alleles.
>
> The size of it, with the panel named as it must be: after the 88 pangenome haplotypes are counted, the padding supplies **6,492 − 88 = 6,404** reference-allele observations under the **30x GRCh38 panel (3,202 samples)** (**derived**), or **5,096 − 88 = 5,008** under the shipped **Phase 3 panel (2,504 samples)** (**derived**). So roughly **98.6%** of the row's mass at a novel site is padding rather than observation on the 30x panel (**derived**: 6,404 / 6,492).
>
> Two consequences, and they point in opposite directions. It is **conservative for the privacy score**: the padding can only make a novel allele look *rarer*, never commoner, so it inflates ε_j rather than deflating it — and it is exactly why no `f_v` is ever 0 in branch 3, which is half of the retraction in §A.4.3. It is **not** conservative as a population model: it asserts a reference-allele frequency that was never observed, and at a novel site the reference allele comes out with a median frequency of **0.998** (**measured in this project**). Whether that is the right prior for a mechanism that is trying to price rarity is a modelling question this project has not taken a position on; v1 sidesteps it entirely, because **there is no allele-frequency branch in v1** (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`, REPLACE item 2).

> **Question** — *"That table of medians — 0.63, 0.43, 5.41 nats — is conditioned on the target carrying a non-REF allele. But most of the time he carries REF. What is the median in that case? Because otherwise the table is describing a minority of sites and reading like it describes all of them."*
>
> You are right that it is a conditional number, and the table's row label says so: *median −log f_v where HG00438 carries a **non-REF** allele*. For the REF-carrying case `docs/` records **one** number and not three. At novel (branch 3) sites the target usually carries REF and the median `f_v` there is **0.998** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24), which is **−ln(0.998) = 0.0020 nats** (**derived**) — arithmetically nothing.
>
> **No equivalent REF-carrying median is recorded for branch 1 or branch 2**, so do not infer one; a branch-1 REF allele at a common SNP could sit anywhere from near-zero up to a couple of nats and `docs/` does not pin it. What would settle it: recompute the same median over the REF-carrying sites, per branch.
>
> The consequence that *is* measured is the one that matters for reading the table: the privacy signal from novel material is concentrated in the **11,760** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a **non-REF** allele at a novel variant (**measured in this project**), out of **56,889** branch-3 variants in total (**measured in this project**) — so about **20.7%** of branch-3 sites (**derived**). The 5.41-nat median describes those; the other roughly four fifths contribute on the order of 0.002 nats each.

> **Caution** — in branches 2 and 3, and under our 30x repin in branch 1 as well, **the counts include the target**. A model that includes the individual it is scoring is not a population model in the sense ε_j needs. This is the same class of error as departure (c), in a less damaging place, and it is also the reason the `−log 0` branch never fires (§A.4.3). The scope of that caution for *us* is narrower than it first looks: under **model C** the mechanism reads no allele-frequency table at all (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`), and where the target is genuinely external nothing about that person could enter one anyway. The self-inclusion is live only in the **head-to-head arm**, where a cohort member stands in as target — and there it has to be corrected, not inherited.

#### A.9.4 The scoring and sampling routes use different thresholds

One more thing about the routing, and it is a defect rather than a subtlety, because the two conditions involved are **not complements of each other**.

- **Scoring** takes the chain route when the block has **at least one** anchor (`get_support_and_pmi.py:87`).
- **Sampling** takes the chain route only when the block has **at least two**: a block that is a singleton *or* has one anchor or fewer is sent to the frequency sampler instead (`get_support_and_pmi.py:159`).

Both **measured in this project** by reading the pinned code. So a block holding two or more variants and **exactly one** anchor is **scored by the forward algorithm** and **sampled from independent allele frequencies**. The value the optimizer books for that move was computed under a haplotype model; the replacement it actually installs was drawn under a site-independence model. Nothing in the pipeline notices.

| metric, chr21 | value |
|---|---|
| blocks **scored** by the chain route (anchors ≥ 1) | **10,954** (measured in this project) |
| blocks **sampled** by the chain route (anchors ≥ 2) | **10,502** (measured in this project) |
| blocks **scored one way and sampled the other** | **452** (measured in this project) |
| those 452 as a share of the 100,757 block-dictionary entries | **0.45%** (derived) |

> **Defect** — severity **MEDIUM**. The framing is the standing one: **the released code applies two different anchor thresholds in two adjacent functions**, where the published Methods describe one. It is a consistency defect in the code relative to the Methods, affecting 452 of 100,757 entries. It is **not** a claim about the paper's published numbers, which we have not reproduced. An earlier version of this primer noted the asymmetry at Step 14 and added *"nothing depends on this being wrong"*; the 452 is what turns that observation into a measured finding rather than a reading note. The full entry is departure (f).

> **Question** — *"452 out of 100,757 sounds tiny, but are those big blocks or small blocks? If the one-anchor-exactly blocks happen to be the long ones, 0.45% of entries could be a large share of the variants being rewritten under the wrong model."*
>
> This project **has not measured it**. `docs/bugs.md` (2026-09-25) records the three counts — 10,954 scored, 10,502 sampled, **452** in the gap — and **nothing about their size** (all three **measured in this project**). Note also that the defining condition, *"two or more variants and exactly one anchor"*, places no upper bound on block length: a block with fifty variants of which exactly one is an anchor lands in the 452 just as readily as a block with two. So the intuition that these must be small blocks has nothing supporting it.
>
> What would settle it: sum the block sizes over the 452 out of the block dictionary and report the variant share against the 340,824 denominator alongside the 0.45% entry share. Until then, **0.45% of entries** (**derived**, from 452 / 100,757) is the only share that may be quoted, and it may not be restated as a share of variants.

> **Question** — *"Which reported number actually moves because of those 452, and in which direction? Is the privacy inflated or the utility understated?"*
>
> **Not measured, and the direction is not derivable from what `docs/` holds.** What is known is the mechanism: for one of those 452 blocks, the value booked into the optimizer is the forward-algorithm ε_j over the block's single anchor (§A.4.1, Route H), while the replacement actually installed was drawn from independent allele frequencies at every variant in the block (`get_support_and_pmi.py:159`, **measured in this project**). The booked value and the installed move come from two different models. `docs/` records no recomputation of ε_j under the sampler's own model for those blocks, and no re-solve of the LP under a single consistent threshold, so **no statement about the size or the sign of the effect on any reported metric is available**.
>
> The framing stays the standing one: this is *the released code differs from the published Methods*, which describe one threshold. It is **not** a claim that the published numbers are wrong — we have not reproduced any of the paper's figures. What would settle the direction: re-solve the optimizer with one threshold and compare the selected set and the total booked ε against the shipped run. That is the same shape as the outstanding measurement for departure (c)'s ceiling, where `docs/memory.md` says plainly *"ε_j enters the LP only through a ranking, and we have not measured how much the selection changes when the ceiling is removed"*.

Our own design removes the question rather than picking a threshold, and under the decided chain architecture it removes the ground the question stood on as well. **Model C** is one chain over the whole chromosome with the **individual variant** as the unit (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). There is no anchor concept to set a threshold on, **no allele-frequency branch anywhere in the source**, and no block boundary for a second condition to disagree about: one forward pass, one backward sample, every variant a chain position. The earlier wording of the gate — "singleton blocks provably traverse the same sampler with T = 1" — was a guard against a two-code-path split inside a block-structured design, and with C there are no blocks and hence no singleton blocks for it to guard. Section 4 gives the theory.

### A.10 Missing genotypes: what a `-1` does to the score and to the cost

Both quantities the optimizer runs on — the value ε_j of §A.4 and the weight η_j of §A.5 — are defined over *called* genotypes. Neither definition says what to do when a genotype is absent. The code answers anyway, in two separate places, and the two answers point in opposite directions. This subsection follows a single missing entry through both, because the combination produces the worst cell in the whole value/weight table and it lands on a class of records that is not randomly distributed.

> **Definition** — a **missing entry** is a cell of the allele matrix holding **`-1`** instead of an allele index. Step 9 explains where it comes from: `VCFtoNP.py` reads only the `GT` subfield of each sample column, and writes `-1` either when the VCF wrote `.` in place of an allele or when the `GT` was haploid so there was no second entry. `X[i,s,h] = -1` therefore means *"this pipeline does not have an allele for haplotype h of sample i at record s"*, and nothing more specific than that. §1.1.7 introduces the `GT` field and the integers that index `REF, ALT[0], ALT[1], …`; a `.` is what stands in that slot when the caller has no allele to report.

> **Question** — *"when a `-1` matters, whose haplotype are we talking about — the target's, or a donor's? Those feel like two different problems."* They are two different problems, and that is exactly why this subsection needs two answers instead of one. The privacy mask keys on **the target's own entries and nothing else**: `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0` tests the target's own haplotype array (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned checkout), so a *donor's* `-1` zeroes nothing. The cost weight does the opposite: `support(v)` counts non-missing entries across **all 88 haplotypes** (`not_missing = np.sum(pangenome != -1, axis=(0,2))`, **measured in this project**), so a *donor's* uncalled entry raises the price of editing **the target**. One symbol, two unrelated consequences depending on whose row it sits in: the target's own `-1` destroys the value, and everybody else's `-1` inflates the cost. Answer 1 below is the first case and Answer 2 is the second.

> **Question** — *"every haplotype path runs along the GRCh38 backbone, so a top-level variant has to have an allele for everyone. Can a top-level record be missing at all?"* Yes, and the way it can is a good illustration of why one symbol for five causes is a problem. At a **top-level** record the *not-applicable* cause genuinely cannot arise — there is no parent allele to fail to carry, so every path passes through the bubble — but measured missingness at top level is not zero: **2.25%** mean missingness over the **253,000** top-level SNP records and **3.11%** over the **56,335** top-level non-SNP records (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25). What is left over at top level is assembly gaps and conflicts — cases where the haplotype does have an allele and this pipeline does not know it. At **nested** records the not-applicable cause does arise and dominates: **22.53%** mean missingness over the **31,489** nested records (measured), roughly ten times the top-level SNP rate, and **66.1%** of nested missingness is, among haplotypes that do have a parent call, perfectly determined by which parent allele they carry (measured, over the **5,735** nested records with any missing child call). So the mechanism in your question is right and the conclusion drawn from it is not.

**Answer 1: the privacy score assigns a missing entry exactly zero.** In `get_support_and_pmi.py` the per-site self-information array is masked wherever the target's own haplotype is uncalled — `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0` (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned checkout). So the block's ε_j receives **no contribution at all** from any site where the target's allele is missing. Read charitably this is defensible: you cannot leak an allele the file never published. Read mechanically it means the objective values that site at zero, and the objective is the only thing the LP maximizes.

> **Question** — *"is the zeroing per site or per block? If a five-variant block has one uncalled variant, does the block lose one variant's worth of score, or all of it?"* Per site. The mask zeroes entries of the **per-site** self-information array before the block's score is formed, so what is lost is that one site's contribution and not the block's (**measured in this project** by reading the pinned checkout; the route table at the end of this subsection says where each of the two branches is exposed). What matters is the direction, and it only goes one way: ε_j comes out smaller than it would otherwise be, so the value density ε_j/η_j is smaller, so §A.6's greedy rule ranks the block **lower**. An uncalled target allele can push a block **down** the list and can never push it up. In the limiting case where every variant in the block is uncalled for the target, ε_j is exactly **0** and the density is **0** — which is the third column of §A.5's three-regime table, reached from the value side rather than the weight side.

**Answer 2: the cost weight ignores a missing entry — and that makes the record *more* expensive, not less.** `support(v)` is computed as the number of **non-missing** haplotype entries at v: `not_missing = np.sum(pangenome != -1, axis=(0,2))` (**measured in this project**). Missing haplotypes are excluded from the count, so they are excluded from the *denominator* of the weight. The weight is 1/support(v). Drop entries from support and the weight goes **up**.

That is the whole mechanism, and it is worth doing the arithmetic on it once, because the two measured medians turn out to be exactly interpretable.

> **Worked example** — all figures **measured in this project** on our rebuilt chr21, cohort 44 individuals / 88 haplotypes (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23), except where marked derived. The median utility weight at a `CONFLICT` record is **0.01818**; elsewhere it is **0.01136**. Invert them: 1/0.01818 = **55.0** and 1/0.01136 = **88.0** (derived). So the median record outside the conflict set has support **88 of 88** — every haplotype called, nothing missing — while the median `CONFLICT` record has support **55 of 88**, i.e. **33 haplotypes uncalled**. That is consistent with the separately measured means: **29.4 of 88** haplotypes missing at a `CONFLICT` site against **3.6 of 88** elsewhere, which would give weights of 1/58.6 = **0.01706** and 1/84.4 = **0.01185** (derived — means rather than medians, hence the small difference). Either way the ratio is the same story: changing a `CONFLICT` record costs **1.60×** what changing the median record outside the conflict set costs (derived from the two medians: 0.01818 / 0.01136 = 1.600; the 1.60× figure is also recorded as measured).

> **Question** — *"so if `support(v)` counted carriage instead of callability, these records would be cheaper and the optimizer would start picking them. Is the fix for our own mechanism just fixing support?"* No, and the reason is worth carrying forward, because it is the difference between a bug fix and a policy. Suppose `support(v)` were redefined to count all **88** haplotypes regardless of callability. The median `CONFLICT` weight would fall from the measured **0.01818** to 1/88 = **0.01136** (derived), which is exactly the measured median weight outside the conflict set, and the **1.60×** penalty would vanish. But the **value** is still zero wherever the target's own allele is uncalled — that is Answer 1, and it is a separate line of code — so the density becomes 0 / 0.01136 = **0** (derived). Still zero density, still ranked behind every move with any value at all, still never selected. **A zero numerator is not repaired by shrinking the denominator.** Both halves have to change together, which is why the missing-data question at the end of this subsection is a modelling policy and not a patch.

Now put the two answers together on the same record.

> **Defect** — a `CONFLICT` record is worth **zero** privacy and costs **1.60×** the median record outside the conflict set, so its value density ε_j/η_j is **zero** and the greedy rule of §A.6 ranks it **behind every move with any value at all**. The optimizer therefore has essentially no reason to touch it at any capacity — and note from §A.6 that spare budget does not help, because a zero-value item adds nothing to the objective even when the knapsack saturates at capacity 0.5. The consequence recorded in `docs/memory.md` is that the knapsack *"will essentially never select them. They are released **VERBATIM**"* — what goes out is exactly what came in (derived there from the two measured weights). **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23): there are **1,855** `CONFLICT` records on chr21, **0.54%** of the 340,824 records. Framing, as always in this section: the finding is that the metric `1/support(v)` **measures something narrower than its name suggests** — support counts *callability*, not carriage — and that `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0` applies one rule to every cause of a missing entry alike. We have reproduced none of the paper's figures and assert nothing about them. The full entry is departure (j).

> **Note** — the term `CONFLICT` is defined where it is first used (see the Definition in §A.3). Restated in one line: `vg deconstruct` found a sample with more than one disagreeing graph path through the bubble, wrote `.|.`, and `VCFtoNP.py` turned that into a `-1` that carries no record of the reason.

> **Question** — *"hold on. Zero privacy and 1.60× cost is only true if the **target's** allele is one of the uncalled ones. A mean of 29.4 of 88 haplotypes are uncalled at a `CONFLICT` record, so most of them are called. Are you stating the worst cell as if it were the whole class?"* And then: *"'the knapsack will essentially never select them' — did we measure that, or did we infer it from the two weights?"*
>
> On the first — that is the right correction to make, and the **Defect** above should be read strictly: the zero applies at the sites where **HG00438's own** entry is `-1`. Where the target is called and other samples are not, the block keeps its ε_j and merely costs more — a bad cell, not the worst cell. Two measured facts bound how often the worst cell is reached. First, the mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes uncalled at a `CONFLICT` site leaves **58.6 of 88** called (derived), so being uncalled there is common but nowhere near universal. Second, the `INFO` tag `CONFLICT=<sample>` **names all 45 samples, at roughly 200–470 records each** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-22), so no one sample is the named conflicting sample at more than a few hundred of the **1,855** records. What `docs/` does **not** record is the split of those 1,855 into target-called and target-uncalled for HG00438. That is one intersection — the `CONFLICT` record rows against the target's own `-1` entries — and it is what would turn this subsection's worst cell from a class into a count.
>
> On the second — inferred, and the primer should not pretend otherwise. `docs/memory.md` derives the conclusion from two measured quantities — the zeroed score and the **1.60×** weight — plus the greedy density rule of §A.6. Nothing in `docs/` intersects the **1,855** `CONFLICT` record rows with the moves the run actually selected, on the **capacity-0.1, seed-123** run or any other. That is why the wording is "essentially never" and not "never", and the difference is not pedantry: a zero-value item is ranked last, and being ranked last is not the same as being excluded. The measurement that would settle it is cheap and we have not done it — take `xsol.npy` and the changed rows of `new_haplotypes.npy` from the capacity-0.1, seed-123 run, intersect them with the 1,855 record indices, and report how many were rewritten. Until that number exists, treat the pass-through as a strongly argued expectation rather than a count.

**Why "released verbatim" is worse than it sounds: the class is not random.** If conflicts were scattered uniformly, a 0.54% pass-through would be a rounding error. They are not. All figures **measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23):

- They concentrate in **nested** records. `CONFLICT` records are **37.5%** at nesting level `LV=1` against a chromosome-wide **8.2%** — a **4.6×** enrichment (derived) — and only **59.1%** are top-level against **90.8%** chromosome-wide.
- They are **more structural** than average: median size (longest of REF/ALT) **4 bp** with a mean of **602 bp** and a maximum of **311,406 bp**, and a median of **4 alleles per record** against 2 chromosome-wide, up to 81.
- They sit **alone**. The 1,855 records occupy **1,634 blocks**, of which **1,469 are singletons** — so most of them are the only variant in their block, and their block's fate is entirely their own.
- They are almost never on the haplotype-model route: only **1.8%** of `CONFLICT` records are in blocks that take the HMM path, against a chr21 baseline of **10.4%**. **98.2%** of them (derived) are handled by the per-variant allele-frequency sampler.

In other words the pass-through is concentrated exactly where the target's structure is unusual — larger, more multi-allelic, more deeply nested records than average — which is the opposite of where a privacy mechanism wants its blind spot to be. That inversion, not the 0.54%, is the finding.

> **Question** — *"a `CONFLICT` record is 4.6× enriched at `LV=1`, and we also said a nested variant can be not-applicable for a donor. Is `CONFLICT` just the not-applicable case wearing a different tag?"* No — they are two separate rows of Step 9's five-cause table, and a third candidate you might reasonably suspect has been tested and largely ruled out. *Not applicable* is decided by which parent allele a haplotype carries; **66.1%** of nested missingness is, among haplotypes with a parent call, perfectly determined by that (**measured in this project**). A *conflict* is a different event: one sample with more than one graph path through the bubble, and the paths disagree. The tempting third explanation — that conflicts arise when a repeat revisits a bubble — was cross-tabulated: of the **1,855** `CONFLICT` records, **1,844** show **no** revisiting traversal at all, only **37** records chr21-wide show any sign of one, and just **11** are both (all **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-22). The most plausible remaining cause on the record is assembly fragmentation — each HPRC haplotype is many contigs, and two contigs of one sample crossing the same region give that sample two paths — and `docs/` is explicit that this **cannot be determined from the VCF alone and has not been verified**. What `docs/` does not contain is a cross-tabulation of `CONFLICT` against not-applicable, so the overlap between those two causes is unmeasured; the `LV=1` enrichment is consistent with both of them living in nested records and settles nothing by itself.

> **Question** — *"0.54% is tiny. Is this a real problem or a thing we happened to find? And is it a risk or an opportunity for us?"* On PanMixer's own terms the share is small and the class is structurally selected, and the selection is the finding. For **our** mechanism the number that matters is not 0.54%. It is **11.31%**: drop anchors and make every variant a chain position, and mean missingness at chain positions rises from **0.26%** to **11.31%**, with **7,555** positions above 25% missing (all **measured in this project**) — a factor of **32** between the two measured figures (derived: 11.31 / 0.35 = 32.3). So the `CONFLICT` set is the small, visible, already-measured instance of a mechanism that is an order of magnitude more exposed in the design we have chosen, which makes it a diagnostic rather than a curiosity. Whether it is also an *opportunity* depends entirely on the missing-data policy below, and that is open: a policy that distinguishes the five causes would be something PanMixer does not have, but no such policy has been chosen here and none has been measured, so the primer claims no advantage from it.

Two questions about the released file itself follow, because "verbatim" is a claim about bytes and it is worth seeing what those bytes are.

> **Question** — *what does "released verbatim" literally look like in the output file, if the input was missing?* Step 19 answers it: the writer maps `-1` back to `.`, so the released record carries the same `.` the input did. Nothing was rewritten, nothing was filled in, and the release therefore reproduces **the target's own missingness pattern**, position for position. For a `CONFLICT` record that pattern is unusual by construction — a mean of 29.4 of 88 haplotypes uncalled — and it is published unchanged alongside everything that *was* rewritten.

> **Open question** — is a missingness pattern itself identifying? Nothing in `docs/` measures this, so the primer does not claim it. What `docs/` does establish is that the pattern is **not noise**: missingness is strongly structured by record class, with **2.25%** mean missingness at top-level SNPs, **3.11%** at top-level non-SNPs and **22.53%** at nested records — nested variants are uncalled roughly **ten times** as often (all **measured in this project**). A pattern that correlates that strongly with structure is at least a candidate quasi-identifier. Treat it as an unmeasured risk, not a finding.

**One `-1` collapses five different facts into one symbol.** This is the deeper problem behind both answers above, and it is why neither answer can be *right*. A missing entry can mean any of five distinct things — the variant does not exist on that haplotype at all; its parent call was itself missing; the donor's assembly does not cover the region; the caller found conflicting paths; or the VCF gave one allele where two were expected. **Step 9 tabulates all five with their measured shares** (the underlying record is `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25), and the essential point for this subsection is that all five are distinguishable *in the VCF* — by the `LV` and `PS` tags, by run length, by the `CONFLICT` tag, by `GT` arity — and **none** is distinguishable after conversion, because `VCFtoNP.py` keeps only `POS` and `GT`. So `pmi[hap == -1] = 0` and `1/support(v)` apply one rule to five situations that deserve different ones. For scale: **1,278,653** of the chr21 matrix entries are missing (**4.26%**), spread over **52,926** sites (**15.5%** of records) with at least one missing entry (**measured in this project**). The recommendation `docs/` records is to emit an auxiliary **reason array** beside the allele matrix at conversion time, on the grounds that every cause is recoverable at that moment and none is recoverable afterwards.

> **Question** — *"what would the REASON array actually hold — one code per entry? How big is that?"* And, in the same breath: *"then give me the breakdown of the 4.26% by cause."*
>
> On the layout — `docs/` records the recommendation and not the layout, so this is partly open on purpose, but the scale is fixable now. The allele matrix on chr21 is 44 samples × 340,824 records × 2 strands = **29,992,512** entries (derived from the measured shapes), of which **1,278,653** are missing — **4.26%** (**measured in this project**), so a cause code is meaningful for only about one entry in 24 (derived). That makes dense-versus-sparse a real choice, and `docs/` has not made it. What `docs/` does fix is the *timing* and the *inputs*: the array must be written by `VCFtoNP.py` itself, because every cause is derivable there — `LV` and `PS` separate not-applicable from inherited, run length separates assembly gaps, the `CONFLICT` tag names conflicts, and `GT` arity identifies the haploid case — and none of them is derivable afterwards, since the entire `INFO` column is discarded. The code set is not fixed either: whether "assembly gap" is decided per entry by run length or recorded as a run interval is not specified anywhere in `docs/`.
>
> On the breakdown — it cannot be given, and the obstacle is a denominator problem rather than a missing measurement — so this is worth reading slowly, because the five figures look comparable and are not. **66.1%** and **12.1%** are shares of *nested* missingness, over the **5,735** nested records that have any missing child call. The assembly-gap figure is **349 runs of ≥100 consecutive missing positions covering 448,283 entries**, out of **28,052** runs in total, whose median length is **1**, mean **20.0** and maximum **23,478**, with **54.4%** of runs being isolated single positions. `CONFLICT` is **1,855 records** with a mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes uncalled. The haploid-`GT` figure is **3.135% of sample `GT` fields**, measured over 150,000 chr21 records, alongside 5.581% of allele slots being an explicit `.` (all **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25). Four different units on four different denominators: shares of nested missingness, runs and entries, records, and `GT` fields. **They do not sum to 100% and must never be added.** A single breakdown over the **1,278,653** missing entries does not exist in `docs/` and this primer will not manufacture one — producing it is precisely what the REASON array would make possible.

**Why PanMixer mostly escapes this, and why we will not.** The anchor restriction (§A.8) has a side effect its stated rationale does not mention: it confines the haplotype model to positions that are almost always called. Mean missingness **at anchors is 0.26%**; make **every** variant a chain position and it is **11.31%**, with **7,555** positions above 25% missing (all **measured in this project**). So PanMixer does not solve the missing-data problem; the anchor rule **defers** it, and any design that drops anchors inherits it in full. That is a decision we owe an explicit answer to rather than a default. Four options are on the record in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy` — **renormalise**, **wildcard**, **mismatch** and **restrict**, the last being what PanMixer's anchor rule does in effect and kept deliberately as a baseline because it is the published behaviour — and `docs/terms.md` marks the choice **not yet decided**, flagging an undeclared default as a silent modelling choice. **That decision is open and is not made here.**

> **Question** — *"I said I was leaning renormalise. Does anything measured here argue against it?"* Yes — and something argues against each of the others too, which is why `docs/terms.md` records the choice as not yet decided rather than defaulted. What follows is the shape of the decision and the evidence on each side; it is not a recommendation, and the decision stays open. **For renormalise:** the not-applicable case is a donor that genuinely has no allele at that position, because the child bubble sits inside a parent allele the donor does not carry, and that case accounts for **66.1%** of nested missingness among haplotypes with a parent call (**measured in this project**). Dropping such a donor from the state distribution at that position is the modelling-correct move for that cause. **Against renormalise:** an assembly gap is a donor that almost certainly *has* an allele we merely do not know, and in bulk those are not rare — **349 runs of ≥100 consecutive missing positions covering 448,283 entries** (measured). Renormalising there penalises poorly assembled donors for a reason unrelated to genetics. **Against wildcard:** treating missing as matching whatever the target has makes the worst-assembled donors universally attractive, which is the same failure in the other direction. **Against mismatch:** it charges an assembly gap as though it were genuine variation. **Against restrict:** it is the published behaviour and therefore the one comparable baseline, but it is also the thing we decided to change. **A discriminator does exist in the data:** **54.4%** of missing runs are isolated single positions, consistent with the structural causes, while the long runs are contig breaks (measured), so run length separates those two cases — at conversion time, and only there. That is why `docs/` raises a fifth possibility beyond the four options, namely splitting the policy by cause, which the REASON array above would make possible. And one constraint that holds whichever way it goes: `docs/terms.md` records that a run is valid only against another run **under the same policy**, never across policies.

> **Question** — *"so low missingness at anchors is an argument for anchors that the paper never makes. Why not?"* The paper does give a rationale; it is a different one, and separating the two is the useful move here. The published Methods justify the branch by the number of **top-level SNPs** in the block — *"If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM, and we can synthesize more accurate new haplotype blocks. If only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM"* (**quoted from the published paper**) — and the ≥2 threshold is simply that a Markov chain needs two positions to have a transition between them. That is an argument about buildability, not about missing data. The missingness argument is **ours** and it is measured: **2.25%** mean missingness over **253,000** top-level SNP records, **3.11%** over **56,335** top-level non-SNP records, **22.53%** over **31,489** nested records (**measured in this project**), so restricting a copying model to top-level SNPs does have real support in the data. The sting is what the released code does with that: its anchor set is the **PanGenie overlap**, **273,475** records against the paper's **253,000** top-level SNPs, overlapping in **234,532**, so **38,943** anchors — **14.2%** — are not top-level SNPs at all, and **13,436** of those are **nested**, while **18,468** qualifying top-level SNPs (**7.3%**) are dropped (all **measured in this project**). The released code therefore admits as chain positions the very class the published rationale excludes. Framing as always: this is *the released code differing from the published Methods*, and we assert nothing about the published results.

Finally, a compact summary of the two routes, because the missing-data behaviour differs between them and the difference is easy to lose. Metrics are rows; the two routing branches are the columns.

| metric | haplotype-model (HMM) branch | per-variant allele-frequency branch |
|---|---|---|
| routing rule for the **score** | more than one variant in the block and anchors **≥ 1** (`get_support_and_pmi.py:87`) | a single-variant block, or **≥ 2** variants with **no** anchors |
| routing rule for the **sample** | anchors **≥ 2**, and more than one variant in the block (`get_support_and_pmi.py:159`) | a single-variant block, or **≤ 1** anchor |
| chr21 blocks **scored** this way | **10,954** (measured) | **89,803** (derived: 100,757 − 10,954) |
| chr21 blocks **sampled** this way | **10,502** (measured) | **90,255** (derived: 100,757 − 10,502) |
| blocks scored one way and sampled the other | **452** (measured) — they get an HMM-derived score for a replacement drawn from allele frequencies | the same 452 blocks, seen from the other side |
| what is **scored** | −forward_algorithm over the block, with the chain built from the **anchors only** | the sum of per-site −log f_v over the block's variants |
| what is **sampled** | one donor mosaic across the anchor positions; with the shipped transition constants the path tends to copy a single donor almost verbatim | one allele per variant, drawn **independently** from that variant's frequency vector |
| what the score **ignores** | every **non-anchor** variant in the block, however rare | nothing structurally — but any site where the target's own entry is `-1` contributes exactly 0 (`pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`) |
| what the cost weight **ignores** | uncalled entries, which are removed from `support(v)` and therefore raise 1/support(v) | identical — the weight is the same formula on both routes |
| how missing data reaches it | barely: mean missingness at the anchors its HMM steps over is **0.26%** (measured) | more: the records the anchor rule leaves out include **18,053** of the **31,489** nested records (derived: 31,489 measured nested records less the 13,436 measured nested anchors), and nested records carry **22.53%** mean missingness against 2.25% at top-level SNPs (measured) |
| where `CONFLICT` records land | **1.8%** of them (measured) | **98.2%** of them (derived), and **1,469** of their **1,634** blocks are singletons (measured) |
| measured consequence | the score ignores material rarer than the whole block's ε_j in **32%** of 400 randomly chosen such blocks (measured); worked case, block 1083, **0.0349** nats returned against **5.6956** from the all-variant sum (both measured) — **163×** (derived) | zero privacy value and **1.60×** cost at `CONFLICT` records (measured), so the knapsack essentially never selects them and the **1,855** records (measured) go out unchanged (derived) |
| consequence for our own mechanism | the anchors decision is Section 4's, and it cannot be taken without also taking the missing-data decision | dropping anchors raises chain-position missingness from 0.26% to **4.26%**, and the class it adds inside already-chained blocks runs **11.31%**, which is what makes `missing_policy` a required, undeclared-default-free choice |

> **Question** — *"two routing rules that differ by one anchor — the scorer at ≥1 and the sampler at ≥2. Why would anyone write two different thresholds?"* `docs/` does not say, and the primer will not invent a reason. What `docs/bugs.md` records (2026-09-25) is the two lines — `get_support_and_pmi.py:87` for scoring and `:159` for sampling — the measured consequence that **452** chr21 blocks are scored one way and sampled the other (**measured in this project**), and a severity of MEDIUM. It records **no rationale**, and the published Methods passage quoted above describes one threshold on top-level SNP count, not two. So the honest statement is the standing one: **the released code differs from the published Methods** here, and the effect is that 452 blocks carry an HMM-derived score for a replacement that was drawn from allele frequencies. Two things are also *not* measured and are worth naming: the **size distribution** of those 452 blocks, so whether they are large blocks carrying many variants or small ones is unknown — `docs/` has the count only — and whether their selection changes if one threshold is used. The v1 decision recorded in `docs/plan.md` dissolves the question rather than answering it: one sampler for every block, single-variant blocks as a chain of length 1.

> **Question** — *"the last row says the anchors decision can't be taken without the missing-data decision. Are those actually one decision?"* Coupled, not identical, and keeping them apart matters because one is settled and one is not. The anchors decision is **taken** — v1 drops the PanGenie-overlap criterion and makes every variant a chain position (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`). The `missing_policy` decision is **open** (`docs/terms.md`). The coupling is a single measured pair of numbers: chain-position mean missingness goes from **0.26%** to **11.31%**, with **7,555** positions above 25% missing (**measured in this project**). So dropping anchors *creates* the missing-data problem at a scale where it can no longer be ignored, but it does not choose among the four options for handling it — which is why `docs/terms.md` calls an undeclared default here a silent modelling choice. One asymmetry to hold on to: **restrict** is the only one of the four that feeds back into the first decision, because it removes positions from the chain and therefore changes the **number of chain positions** — and how many chain positions there should be is a decision this project has deliberately deferred, so this subsection states the coupling and stops there. The other three options change what the emission does at a position that stays in the chain, and leave the count alone.

### In plain words — Section 3, Part A

PanMixer is for people whose genomes are *inside* the published pangenome. Their data sits in the released file under their own name, and rare variants make that data a fingerprint. PanMixer chops the genome into LD blocks — runs of variants that are inherited together and therefore have to be edited together or not at all — and for each block it prepares one plausible substitute block, sampled either from population allele frequencies (for blocks with fewer than two anchors) or from a Li–Stephens HMM that stitches together pieces of other cohort members' haplotypes (for blocks with two or more). Each substitution is a "move". A move's *value* is how surprising the target's real block was under a population model — though, as §A.4.2 shows, what the code actually measures is how surprising the block's *anchor* alleles were, which is narrower — so rare blocks are worth a lot and common blocks almost nothing; a move's *cost* is one over the number of haplotypes observed at each variant it changes, summed up, which is essentially the allele-frequency distortion it inflicts. Because value grows with rarity while cost does not, the incentive points at identifying rare material — though two effects measured in this project blunt it: a move whose replacement happens to match the original costs nothing on a multi-variant block and is therefore bought first even though it changes nothing (at capacity 0.1 on our one measured run, 30.9% of selected moves, carrying 7.0% of the booked privacy credit), and a `CONFLICT` record, where the target's own allele is uncalled, scores zero while costing 1.60× the median record outside the conflict set, so such records are bought last or essentially never and go out unchanged. Neither effect inflates the cost side of the ledger, which correctly reports zero for a move that changed nothing. Choosing which moves to take under a utility budget is a 0–1 knapsack problem, solved by its linear-programming relaxation, which for a single-constraint knapsack is near-exact — at most one item comes out fractional. The resulting privacy claim is empirical: ε_private = 0.002 is the largest privacy-risk setting at which a specific linkage attack against a specific database failed for every target. It is a measured threshold against one attacker, not a worst-case bound against all attackers.

Four things about that value number deserve to be in the plain-words version, because they change what it means. First, the arithmetic depends on the block's shape: a block with one variant, or with several variants and no anchor among them, is scored by adding up minus-the-log of each allele's population frequency; a block with several variants and at least one anchor is scored by running the forward algorithm over the block's **anchors only** and taking minus the log of the total probability it returns. Second, that phrase "anchors only" is load-bearing rather than incidental. An anchor is just a variant that also appears in an external SNP callset — PanGenie's, in the released code, though the paper says 1000 Genomes — and any variant in the block that is not one of those is not a position in the chain and contributes nothing to the score, no matter how rare it is. On one measured three-variant block the two routes differ by a factor of **163**, and across 400 measured blocks the rare material the score ignores is individually larger than the score itself in **32%** of cases. So the number is a correct answer to a narrower question than its name suggests: it is the surprisal of the block's anchor alleles, not of the block. We have not reproduced the paper's figures, and none of this says their results are wrong; it says the released code evaluates the paper's definition over a smaller set than the definition names.

Third, an earlier version of this section explained the same conclusion by a different and wrong route — that a frequency of exactly zero produces an infinite score which the code then floors to zero, so the most identifying alleles are scored as worthless. **That explanation is retracted.** The clamp is real in the code but it never runs on this data: of **319,092** chr21 sites where the target carries a called allele, **zero** have frequency zero, because the frequency table counts the target's own allele in every branch — in branches 2 and 3 always, and in branch 1 as well under our **30x GRCh38 panel (3,202 samples)** repin, which contains 39 of the 44 pangenome donors — and pads novel variants against a constructed denominator of **6,492** on that panel, rather than against the number of carriers. Novel alleles are therefore floored at about **1.5 × 10⁻⁴** on that panel, worth **8.78 nats** — never zero. The most identifying record on chr21 does score zero, but by **exclusion, not by clamping**: it is not in the external callset, so it is not an anchor, so the forward algorithm never looks at it.
