The distinction matters because a clamp is a three-line fix and an exclusion is a design choice. Fourth and last, the same anchor count is used with two different thresholds — a block is *scored* by the chain route at one anchor but only *sampled* by it at two — so **452** measured chr21 blocks are scored under one model and rewritten under another. Our own mechanism removes all of this by deleting the anchor concept and putting every block, singletons included, through a single sampler; the reasoning is in §A.8 and the specification is in Section 4.

And one thing the two quantities cannot see at all, because it is not in either definition. Both ε_j and η_j are computed over *called* genotypes, and neither says what to do when the target's own allele is missing. The code answers in two places and the two answers pull apart: the privacy score is set to exactly zero at a missing site, while the cost weight 1/support(v) *rises*, because support counts only haplotypes that have a called allele. Zero value at above-average cost is the one cell of the knapsack's table that is never bought, so a class of records — **1,855** `CONFLICT` records on chr21, **0.54%** of them, enriched **4.6×** in nested variants and mostly alone in their own block — goes out exactly as it came in. §A.10 works that through, and it also states plainly that the single symbol `-1` conflates **five** distinct situations that deserve different treatment, and that the choice of what to do about them in our own mechanism is deliberately still open.


> **Question** — "This summary uses ε_j, η_j and ε_private in four sentences. Can the plain-words section carry the symbol glosses too, so I can read it standalone?"
>
> It should, and here they are in the form to keep: **ε_j** is one block's privacy value — the surprisal, in nats, of *the target's own original block* under the cohort model, so a block nobody else carries scores high and a block everybody carries scores near zero. **η_j** is that same block's utility cost — the sum of 1/support(v) over the variants the candidate would actually change, where support(v) is the number of haplotypes with any called allele at v, not the number carrying a particular allele. **ε_private = 0.002** (quoted from the published paper) is not a per-block quantity at all: it is the operating point of the whole sweep, the largest residual privacy-risk setting at which the linkage attack failed for every target.
>
> Two glosses to repeat every time, because they are the two readings you have had to correct twice already. A **state** in the Li–Stephens HMM is a **donor haplotype**, one of the 88 (measured in this project), never a variant and never an allele. And a **block** is a dictionary entry in `blocks_dict.json`, of which there are **100,757** on chr21 (measured in this project), not a plink interval, of which there are **14,137** (measured in this project).

> **Question** — "Can the plain-words close end on one named block carried all the way through — score, cost, selected or not?"
>
> Yes, and this is the block to use, because everything about it is measured. **Block 1077** holds five variants at rows 34816–34820, POS 13,000,333–13,000,621, and all five are anchors, so it takes the HMM branch. The target HG00438 carries `[0,0,0,0,0]` on both strands. Its privacy value is **ε_j = 0.0711 nats** per strand — small, because **82 of 88** cohort haplotypes carry that same pattern, and −log(82/88) = 0.0706, which matches the scorer's output and confirms that ε_j reads as the surprisal of the target's block under the cohort. Every variant has support **87 of 88**, so each would cost 1/87 = **0.01149**, and changing all five would cost **η_j = 0.05747**. The sampled replacement came back `[0,0,0,0,0]` — identical to the target — so nothing changed and the realised cost was **η_j = 0.00000**. The optimizer took it anyway: at capacity 0.1, `xsol[625]` is 1 on both strands and the released alleles are unchanged. (All measured in this project except the two arithmetic checks, which are derived.)
>
> That single block carries the whole Part A story: a value computed from the target's own data, a cost computed only from what changes, and a selection rule that will happily buy a positive value at zero cost.

---

## Part B — PanMixer as a data pipeline

The pipeline splits cleanly in two. **Preprocessing** (`starting_data/run_get_starting_data_pipeline.sh`) runs once per pangenome and per target and is the expensive part. The repository's README says so in as many words, at line 78: *"These commands take about 1–2 days to run end-to-end."* **Experiment runtime** (`main.py`) is cheap and is run many times at different budgets.

Numbers below labelled "chr21" are **measured in this project** on our rebuilt chr21 unless stated otherwise, and are recorded in `docs/memory.md` (entries dated 2026-09-18), `docs/data.md` and `docs/bugs.md`, with run logs under `logs/` (`pm_chr21.log`, `pm_obf.log`, `pm_prep1.log`). Array shapes are given because they are the fastest way to check whether a step did what it should.

**Which panel.** This matters throughout. The shipped pipeline downloads **1000 Genomes Phase 3** — 2,504 samples, **GRCh37** coordinates. This project **substituted** the **1000 Genomes 30x GRCh38 panel** — 3,202 samples, 1,002,753 chr21 records — by re-pointing the hardcoded filename slot, so that the pinned checkout runs unmodified on build-correct data. Every panel-derived number below says which panel produced it.

**Vocabulary used from here on, glossed once.** A **read** is a short DNA fragment output by a sequencing machine (typically 100–300 bp). **Alignment** is finding the place in a reference sequence that a read most closely matches, allowing a few mismatches. An **assembly** is a genome sequence reconstructed from sequencing fragments directly, rather than by aligning them to a reference. A **contig** is a named contiguous reference sequence — for a human reference, usually one chromosome; the contig name is the key that every coordinate is relative to, which is why a naming-convention mismatch breaks joins. **`vg`** (short for "variation graph") is the standard open-source toolkit for building, indexing and aligning against pangenome graphs. **bcftools** is the standard command-line toolkit for filtering, subsetting and indexing VCFs. **SLURM** is the job scheduler used on academic compute clusters; a SLURM "array job" launches many independent copies of the same script with different indices. A variant is **polymorphic** in a panel when both alleles actually occur in that panel; a site where everyone is identical carries no information and is dropped. **kb** = 1,000 base pairs, **Mb** = 1,000,000. **30x** means each base was covered by about 30 sequencing reads on average — deeper, and therefore more accurate, than the roughly 4x of the older Phase 3 release.

There is also a single-file standalone, `tools/panmixer/obfuscate.py`. Its docstring reads: *"Runs the full pipeline: PMI/utility computation -> optimize -> stack -> VCF output."* It runs the **full per-subject pipeline in one process** for one subject and one chromosome, and it **requires `blocks_dict.json` and `allele_frequencies.npy` as pre-existing inputs** — it consumes the preprocessing products rather than producing them, and it replaces the experiment-directory machinery with command-line arguments. **Measured in this project**: 68 s wall-clock and 1.7 GB peak resident memory for one subject on chr21 (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-18; log `logs/pm_obf.log`). For calibration, the **published** Table 1 reports **5 min / 14 GB** for the longest-running chromosome on HPRC v1 with 44 subjects, all 22 chromosomes run in parallel. Those are not the same measurement — ours is chr21, theirs is the longest chromosome — but scaling chr21 to chr2 by record count gives roughly 6.2 min / 9.3 GB, which is the same order.


> **Question** — "The preamble says we substituted the 30x GRCh38 panel 'by re-pointing the hardcoded filename slot'. So every measured number in Part B comes out of a modified checkout. Is the checkout still pinned at `c182c38d`? What exactly did we change?"
>
> The checkout is unmodified and still pinned at `c182c38d5bc8bb6f00f4b0b101207c4a009ca045`. Nothing in the Python or the shell scripts was edited. Two things outside the code changed. First, the **file occupying the `1000g_phased.vcf.gz` slot** was replaced: the shipped script downloads 1000 Genomes Phase 3 (2,504 samples, GRCh37 coordinates) and that file was moved aside under a new name and the 30x GRCh38 panel (3,202 samples, 1,002,752 unique (POS, REF, ALT) records on chr21) put in its place (measured in this project; `docs/data.md` records both files). Second, a **blocking local `sbatch` shim** (`scripts/sbatch`) was written so the pipeline's SLURM array submissions run to completion on one machine, again without editing the pipeline.
>
> Both deviations are visible and reversible — the original file is still there under its own name — and both are recorded, which is the point: a substituted input is a stated deviation, whereas an edited source file would make every downstream number unattributable. Every panel-derived number in Part B names which panel produced it, for the same reason.

> **Question** — "68 s / 1.7 GB for us versus 5 min / 14 GB published, and then you say those are not the same measurement. Then why are they in the same paragraph?"
>
> Because an order-of-magnitude check is worth having and is not worth mistaking for a reproduction. Ours is **68 s wall-clock and 1.7 GB peak resident memory for one subject on chr21** (measured in this project; log `logs/pm_obf.log`). Theirs is **5 min and 14 GB for the longest-running chromosome**, with 44 subjects and all 22 chromosomes run in parallel (quoted from the published paper, Table 1). Scaling chr21 to chr2 by record count is a factor of about 5.47, giving roughly **6.2 min / 9.3 GB** (derived), which lands in the same regime.
>
> What that licenses is narrow: the installation runs, at a plausible cost, on the right scale of data. It is not a reproduction of any published figure, and **this project has reproduced no published result** (project status, `docs/memory.md`). "Correct installation" here means *we run their code as shipped*, not *the code implements the published method* — the divergences between the released code and the published Methods stand, and they are the departures section.

### Step 1 — Download the pangenome VCF
**File:** `starting_data/scripts/get_pangenomes.sh`
**Input:** a URL on the Human Pangenomics AWS Open Data S3 bucket.
**What happens:** `wget` fetches `hprc-v1.0-pggb.grch38.1-22+X.vcf.gz` and its index. This is the HPRC v1.0 PGGB graph deconstructed to a VCF with `vg deconstruct -a` against a GRCh38 backbone. `vg deconstruct` walks the graph's bubbles and writes each out as a VCF record; the `-a` flag retains **nested** variants — a variant occurring inside the span of a larger one, e.g. a SNP inside a long insertion — rather than collapsing them.
**Output:** `starting_data/pangenome.vcf.gz` (+ `.tbi` index).
**Expect:** contig names of the form `grch38#chr1` … `grch38#chrX` (the graph's PanSN path-naming convention, not bare `chr1`), and **45 samples** in the `#CHROM` header line — 44 HPRC individuals plus `chm13`.
**Why it matters:** this file *is* the thing being protected. Every downstream array is indexed by its row order.


> **Question** — "`vg deconstruct -a` retains nested variants. Is that the flag that produces `chr21:14569980` with its 2,464 children? What would that same site look like without `-a`?"
>
> `-a` is why the children exist as rows at all. At `grch38#chr21:14569980` — graph bubble `>102270111>102277685`, VCF row **51791**, nesting level `LV=0` — the cohort's 88 haplotypes carry **88 distinct alleles** out of **90 declared**, the REF allele is **257,439 bp** long, and the single VCF line is **27,983,949 bytes**, about 28 MB. Beneath it sit **2,464 nested child records** tagged with that bubble as their parent (`PS`), so one region of the chromosome accounts for **2,485 rows — 0.7% of chr21's 340,824** (all measured in this project).
>
> Without `-a` you would get the top-level record and none of the 2,464 children, and chromosome-wide you would lose the **9.2%** of chr21 records that sit at `LV ≥ 1` (measured in this project: `LV=0` 90.8%, `LV=1` 8.2%, `LV=2` 0.9%, `LV=3` 0.1%, `LV=4` 10 records).
>
> The part worth noticing is that `-a` does **not** replace the top-level record with its children — it emits **both**. It has to. One of that site's alleles, allele 58, walks **10,008 node steps over 5,014 distinct nodes**, revisiting about 4,994 of them: the whole region is tandem-duplicated on that haplotype (measured in this project). No set of independent per-site substitutions can encode "this entire region appears twice", and a VCF allele is a flat string with no syntax for "this allele is that combination of child variants" (derived). So the parent survives, the children survive, and any per-row sum counts that region twice.

> **Question** — "45 samples in the header, 44 individuals plus `chm13`. Which 44? Are they the same 44 as the published paper's cohort?"
>
> The sample list is recorded in `docs/data.md` for this exact file, and the count agrees with the paper: the published version reports the cohort as **44 individuals** (quoted from the published paper, page 1 line 20, and "n = 44" in the Fig. 2 caption and Table 1), which is 45 VCF samples minus `chm13`, giving **88 haplotypes** (measured in this project). The superseded preprint said 47; that discrepancy is preprint-only and the published paper does **not** disagree with its own data.
>
> One donor matters by name throughout Part B: **HG00438**, the target for every measured chr21 run quoted here.

> **Question** — "'Every downstream array is indexed by its row order.' So what happens if anybody ever re-sorts this VCF, or re-runs `vg deconstruct` and gets a different order out? Is there a check anywhere, or does it just quietly produce wrong answers?"
>
> Row order is not a convenience here, it is the **join key for the entire pipeline**, so the question is the right one to ask at this step. Every later stage addresses data by row index and nothing else: `X[i, s, h]` addresses site *s* by its row number (Step 9); `pangenome_positions.npy` is a parallel array whose *n*-th entry is meaningful only if it lines up with the *n*-th row; the mapping pickles of Step 10 are literally `row index → row index`; and the block dictionary (Step 12), the optimizer's solution vector `xsol` (Step 17) and the stacker (Step 18) all key on row index. A row-order change that is not propagated to every one of those artifacts does not raise an error anywhere — it silently re-labels which variant is which.
>
> There **is** one guard, and it is worth knowing where it lives because it is the only one this primer can name: **Step 11** (`build_biallelic_snp_mask.py`) asserts that record *i*'s `POS` equals `positions[i]`. Re-sort the VCF without regenerating `pangenome_positions.npy` and that assertion fires loudly rather than letting the mask drift. Two honest limits on how much comfort to take from it. First, it compares `POS` only, not the full `(POS, REF, full ALT)` key that Step 10's strict mapping uses, so it is a **shift detector** rather than an identity check. Second, it sits at Step 11, so it catches drift introduced *before* it and says nothing about drift introduced after; **whether any later stage revalidates the invariant has not been audited in this project**, and naming that as unchecked is more useful to you than a guess either way.
>
> The same invariant becomes load-bearing for us the moment we opt into their evaluators, which is a **comparison-arm** decision rather than a property of the mechanism. If the sampler **optionally** emits `new_haplotypes` so that PanMixer's attack evaluators run unmodified (`docs/terms.md`, `new_haplotypes`: comparison-arm only, and not a requirement of the mechanism), that array is addressed by row index too. Borrowing their evaluators means borrowing their row-order contract, so the canary belongs in the comparison arm's plumbing as much as in theirs — the same reflex as departure (a)'s one-line mapping-length check.

### Step 2 — Download the PanGenie callset
**File:** `starting_data/scripts/get_pangenie_alignments.sh`
**Input:** Zenodo record 7669083, file `grch38_all-samples_bi_all.vcf.gz`.
**Output:** `starting_data/PG.vcf.gz` (+ `.tbi`), saved under the short name `PG`.
**Expect:** GRCh38 coordinates, bare `chr21`-style contig names, and **368 samples** (**measured in this project**, recorded in `docs/data.md`'s row for `external/PanMixer/starting_data/PG.vcf.gz`); chr21 has **384,720 records**.
**Why it matters:** the paper uses PanGenie frequencies for structural and nested variants that a SNP panel does not contain. In the released code this file also, unexpectedly, defines the HMM's anchor set — see departure (d).


> **Question** — "368 PanGenie samples. Do any of them overlap the 44 HPRC individuals? If the target is inside the PanGenie callset then the anchor set and any PanGenie-derived frequency already knows about the target."
>
> The inference is right and the measurement does not exist. **This project has not measured the overlap between the 368 PanGenie samples and the 44 HPRC individuals**; do not assume either way from the sample counts.
>
> What is measured is the related thing, and it is uncomfortable for us rather than for them. Branch 2 of the allele-frequency table (Step 13) counts a variant's alleles from **PanGenie plus all pangenome haplotypes**, and branch 3 counts from the pangenome alone — so at those **25,325** and **56,889** chr21 variants respectively the target's own alleles are already inside the frequencies used to score it (measured in this project). Separately, our own 30x repin put **39 of 44** HPRC donors — including `HG00438` — into the panel that branch 1 counts from, against **0 of 44** under the shipped Phase 3 panel (measured in this project). That break was introduced by this project, and its blast radius is the **head-to-head arm**, where `HG00438` stands in as target and externality has to be simulated; the deployed mechanism's target is external to every panel, so constraint 1 holds there by construction. Discussed at §4.4 and departure (c).

> **Question** — "Why does a *callset* get to define the anchor set at all? A callset is genotypes called against the graph — isn't that circular?"
>
> The paper's intent and the code's behaviour are two different things here, and the gap is departure (d).
>
> The published Methods define the anchor set as `V_1000G` (quoted from the published paper, page 8 lines 944-945) — a subset of the graph's variants that also appear in an external, independently genotyped reference panel, the idea being that the copying model should step over positions somebody else has already called reliably. The released code does not implement that. An anchor is a pangenome record that also appears in the **PanGenie `bi_all` callset**, matched exactly on (POS, REF, whole-ALT-string) (measured in this project, `get_mappings.py:45-49`), and `hmm.py` never opens the real 1000G map at all.
>
> Whether that is circular in the damaging sense is a fair question and the measured answer is "it is a proxy that is neither necessary nor sufficient". The paper's own rationale is about **top-level SNPs**; on chr21 there are **253,000** of those, against **273,475** code anchors, with **234,532** in both sets. So the implementation admits **38,943 records (14.2% of anchors)** that are not top-level SNPs — **13,436** of them nested, precisely the class the paper's rationale excludes — while dropping **18,468 (7.3%)** of the top-level SNPs that qualify (all measured in this project). Which anchor set is better is open and this project has not answered it; what v1 decided instead is in §A.8, in departure (d) and in §4.13.

### Step 3 — Download the phased reference panel
**File:** `starting_data/scripts/get_1000g_phased.sh`
**Input, as shipped:** `https://ftp.1000genomes.ebi.ac.uk/vol1/ftp/release/20130502/ALL.chr{N}.phase3_shapeit2_mvncall_integrated_v5b.20130502.genotypes.vcf.gz`.
**Output:** `starting_data/chr{N}/1000g_phased.vcf.gz`.
**Expect, as shipped:** 1000 Genomes **Phase 3**, 2,504 samples, phased, **GRCh37 coordinates**. This is the build-mismatch problem — see departure (a). **In our rebuilt chr21 this filename slot instead holds the 30x GRCh38 panel: 1,002,753 records, 3,202 samples**, array shape `(3202, 1002753, 2)`. The original GRCh37 file is retained beside it, renamed, so the deviation is visible and reversible (`docs/data.md`).
**Why it matters:** this panel supplies the reference haplotypes used for LD block boundaries, for common-SNP allele frequencies, and as the attack database in the evaluation. Three load-bearing roles, one file.


> **Question** — "'Three load-bearing roles, one file' — LD boundaries, allele frequencies, and the attack database. Shouldn't those be three separate configurable inputs? Isn't using the same panel as both our frequency model and the attacker's database handing the attacker our own model?"
>
> They should be three inputs, and separating them is the recommendation for anyone reusing this pipeline: the block-boundary panel, the frequency panel and the attack panel are three independent choices that happen to be satisfied by one file in the released pipeline. For **our** mechanism the separation goes further than that, and it is already settled. Under **model C** the chain unit is the individual variant, so there is no block-boundary panel and no frequency table in the mechanism at all — **no external panel in the mechanism's input set** (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The external panel survives in exactly one role, the **attack database** in the evaluation, where cohort overlap must be removed before it is loaded.
>
> The reason it matters is that the three roles pull in opposite directions. As the **attack database**, a denser panel makes a **strictly stronger attacker** — more candidates to out-score the target, and more sites the attack can use — so the choice of panel moves the privacy axis itself, which is the mechanism behind departure (e). As the **frequency model**, a denser panel makes rarity estimates better. As the **block-boundary source**, the panel decides where every boundary falls, and therefore what T is.
>
> The overlap you are pointing at is real but is not the fatal shape it first looks like: the attacker is *assumed* to know the frequency model anyway (§2.2.4, Kerckhoffs-style reasoning), so sharing a panel gives away nothing the threat model was protecting. The genuine hazard is the opposite one — the panel containing the **target** — which is the next question.

> **Question** — "2,504 Phase 3 samples versus 3,202 in the 30x release. Are the 2,504 a subset of the 3,202, or are they different people sequenced differently?"
>
> `docs/` records the two releases as two files with two builds and two sample counts — Phase 3 at **2,504 samples, GRCh37**, 1,105,538 chr21 records; 30x at **3,202 samples, GRCh38**, 1,002,752 unique (POS, REF, ALT) chr21 records (measured in this project) — and it does **not** record a sample-level comparison of the two lists. So this primer will not tell you whether one is a subset of the other, and you should not infer it from the counts.
>
> The overlap that has been measured is the one that bites: **39 of the 44 HPRC donors, including the target HG00438, are present in the 30x panel**, against **0 of 44** in Phase 3 (measured in this project). That single fact is why the panel substitution is not privacy-neutral, and it is the subject of the next question.

> **Question** — "If the 30x release contains most of the HPRC individuals, then for *PanMixer's* setting the target is in the attack database. But for *our* setting the target is external and might still be in the panel. Is that our version of the same error?"
>
> Not quite, and prising the two settings apart is the whole answer. **In the deployed mechanism the target is external to everything** — not in `G`, not in `D`, not in any external panel, and not in the attack database — so there is no route by which a panel could come to contain it, and nothing to check. **In the benchmark it is a different story**, because the head-to-head has no external target available and a cohort member has to stand in. There the error is real and self-inflicted. `get_af.py` branch 1 draws its counts from the 1000G panel alone, and a code comment asserts *"None of the pangenome subjects appear in 1000g_phased"* — true for Phase 3, where **0 of 44** HPRC donors appear, and **false** for the 30x panel we substituted, where **39 of 44** do, `HG00438` among them (measured in this project). So our rebuilt `allele_frequencies.npy` includes the stand-in's own alleles in the frequencies used to score it, at the **258,610** chr21 variants that take branch 1 (measured in this project).
>
> For PanMixer this is a stronger version of a self-inclusion their code already has in branches 2 and 3 and in the forward panel (departure (c)). **For our benchmark it breaks the externality that arm exists to simulate** — a break this project introduced while fixing a different problem. There is no visible symptom: the frequencies look entirely normal. Standing constraint 1 itself is not at risk in the deployed mechanism, where it holds by construction, and under **model C** that mechanism reads no allele-frequency table at all.
>
> The fix is specific, and it is a **comparison-arm** fix: any leave-one-out cohort must drop the stand-in from the **1000G panel** as well as from the pangenome. Recomputing only the donor panel — which is what PanMixer does — is not a leave-one-out cohort. A proper one recomputes the donor panel, the allele frequencies, the support counts, the site list **and** the block assignment without that individual (project decision, `docs/terms.md`). `loo_cohort` is a concept the mechanism itself never needs: with a genuinely external target there is nothing to hold out, so `K = 88` unconditionally.
>
> One side effect worth knowing, since it explains a retraction elsewhere: because the target's allele is now always counted, no site comes out at frequency zero, which is why the `−log 0` branch at Step 14b never fires on our data (**0 of 319,092** sites, measured in this project).

### Step 4 — Remove chrX
**File:** `scripts/remove_X.sbatch`
**Input:** `pangenome.vcf.gz`.
**What happens:** `bcftools view --regions grch38#chr1,…,grch38#chr22`.
**Output:** `pangenome_no_X.vcf.gz`.
**Expect:** identical sample set, autosomes only.
**Why it matters:** humans have 23 chromosome **pairs**, i.e. **24 distinct chromosome sequences** — 22 autosomes plus X and Y. (For scale: a **haploid** human genome is about 3.1 billion base pairs, and a diploid cell contains about twice that, since it carries two copies of each autosome.) X has sex-dependent ploidy — one copy in males, two in females — which breaks the uniform "every sample has exactly two haplotypes" assumption that every later array depends on. Dropping it is the cheapest way to keep that invariant.


> **Question** — "We drop chrX because ploidy varies. But our target is one external person whose sex we know. Could we keep chrX for them, or does the cohort's mixed ploidy break the donor panel? And what about chrY and the pseudoautosomal regions?"
>
> The obstruction is in the **donor panel**, not in the target. The data model is an array `X[num_samples, num_sites, 2]` (Step 9): every sample is assumed to have exactly two allele slots at every site. On chrX the cohort's 44 donors do not all have two, so the array's third dimension stops meaning "the two haplotypes of this person" and K — the number of donor haplotypes the chain may copy from — stops being a single number for the whole chromosome. That is a statement about the panel, so knowing the target's sex does not rescue it; you would need a per-position state set, which is the same machinery the `missing_policy` RENORMALISE option needs (§4.13).
>
> chrY does not arise for this file. The pangenome VCF is `hprc-v1.0-pggb.grch38.1-22+X.vcf.gz` — chromosomes 1 to 22 plus X, per the filename, with no Y — so the pseudoautosomal question has nothing to attach to here. It would have to be reopened for any release that covered Y.

### Step 5 — Remove the chm13 sample
**File:** `scripts/remove_chm13.sbatch`
**Input:** `pangenome_no_X.vcf.gz`.
**What happens:** `bcftools view -s ^chm13`.
**Output:** `pangenome_no_X_no_chm13.vcf.gz`.
**Expect:** **44 samples = 88 haplotypes.**
**Why it matters:** **CHM13 is human.** It is a human cell line derived from a **complete hydatidiform mole** — an abnormal human conception in which all chromosomes come from the father, so the resulting cell line is effectively homozygous throughout. That homozygosity is precisely what makes it an excellent substrate for building a reference assembly, and CHM13 is used here as a **reference assembly**, not as a cohort donor whose privacy is at stake. It should not be described as non-human. Leaving it in the cohort would pollute the population model with a sample that is not a typical individual and is not a person to protect. The published paper reports the cohort as **44 individuals**, matching this count exactly. (The superseded preprint said 47; that discrepancy is preprint-only.)


> **Question** — "If CHM13 is dropped from the VCF, is it also dropped from the *graph*? And if the backbone is GRCh38, what is allele `0` — GRCh38's base or CHM13's?"
>
> Two different objects, and only one of them is touched. `bcftools view -s ^chm13` removes a **sample column from a VCF**. The graph is untouched: the PGGB graph was built from the assemblies it was built from, and nothing in this pipeline modifies graph topology at any point — the release itself is the cohort VCF with one column rewritten (Step 19).
>
> Allele `0` is the **REF column of a VCF deconstructed against the GRCh38 backbone**, so it is GRCh38's base, not CHM13's (derived from what `vg deconstruct` does, Step 1). Allele indices `1, 2, 3…` are the first, second, third ALT **as listed in that row** — which is why a row's allele numbering is meaningless outside that row.
>
> One residue of this step is worth knowing because it shows up later: `view -s ^chm13` is run **without** `--trim-alt-alleles`, so alleles that only CHM13 carried stay declared while carried by nobody. At the hypervariable site the allele-count field `AC` for allele 1 is **0** for exactly that reason (measured in this project), which is also why the site declares **90** alleles while the cohort carries **88** distinct ones.

### Step 6 — Split per chromosome
**File:** `scripts/split_data.sbatch`
**Input:** the 44-sample pangenome and `PG.vcf.gz`.
**Output:** `chr{N}/pangenome.vcf.gz` and `chr{N}/PG.vcf.gz`, each indexed.
**Expect:** chr21 pangenome has **340,824 records** (**measured in this project**, `docs/data.md`).
**Why it matters:** every later stage is embarrassingly parallel across chromosomes, and the paper notes the optimization is done per chromosome for efficiency. Note the different region syntax for the two files (`grch38#chr21` versus `chr21`) — a standing reminder that these datasets do not share a contig-naming convention.


> **Question** — "Everything here is per chromosome — blocks, budget, sampling. But the guarantee we want in Section 4 is stated over a path. Is a path a chromosome? Is τ per chromosome or per genome? That feels like the T question again."
>
> It is the same question, and it is a reserved decision, so this answer draws the line rather than crossing it.
>
> For PanMixer the answer is per chromosome by construction: blocks are computed per chromosome, the optimizer recomputes its own per-chromosome denominator (Step 17), and `gather_results` sums privacy and utility across chromosomes afterwards (Step 27). That sum is only meaningful if blocks are independent, which is an assumption and not a measurement.
>
> For us the scope algebra is settled, and the decided chain architecture has simplified it further. The **product of per-block tilted samplers equals the global tilted distribution**, provided the baseline factorises over blocks, the per-block weights `beta_t` sum to 1 across all T blocks, and every `phi_t` lies in [0, 1] (project argument recorded in `docs/plan.md`). Theorem 1 needs nothing more, so per-block now and genome-wide later is a **scope choice, not a weaker guarantee**. Under **model C** the argument is not needed at all: there is **one** chain over the whole chromosome, so Theorem 1 applies to the whole path directly (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The factorisation result survives as what would license the model-B fallback.
>
> What does not go away, under any of them, is the catch the same condition exposes: the weights must sum to 1 over the whole path, so each position's share shrinks as the path lengthens. With uniform weights on chr21's **T = 100,757** blocks each block would get `beta_t = 1/T`, and §4.10.4 works through what that leaves. Under C the weights are `beta_v = w_v / Σ_u w_u` rather than uniform, and the **total** tilt `exp(eta_tau)` is **invariant in `T`** — more positions each move less, and they compose to the same total — but the **per-position** tilt still thins: it sits only **13.5×** above the float32 noise floor under C, against **45.7×** under B (**derived**), which is why float64 stored alphas are load-bearing rather than a preference.
>
> What is **not** decided is what a chain position should be at all. That is open question 8, raised on 2026-09-22 and explicitly deferred to your PI, and it is not settled here.

> **Question** — "Two contig naming conventions in one pipeline, `grch38#chr21` and bare `chr21`. Has anyone checked that a region query with the wrong one returns *nothing* rather than silently returning a partial file?"
>
> The expected failure mode is that a `bcftools` region query naming a contig the file does not contain returns an **empty result rather than an error**, which is why the two conventions are a standing hazard rather than a nuisance. This project has not recorded a test of that specific behaviour, so treat it as the thing to check, not as a measurement.
>
> The reason to take it seriously is that the pipeline already contains one silent coordinate failure of exactly this family, and it was not caught by anything erroring: under the build mismatch of departure (a), `pangenome_mask.npy` comes out tiny, `ld_loss` returns `(0.0, 0)`, and every `af_loss` MAF stratum is `0/0` — all reported as legitimate values (measured in this project). The canary that catches it is one line: print `len(pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased)` after `get_mappings`; near-zero confirms the mismatch.

### Step 7 — Count possible alleles per site
**File:** `scripts/src/get_possible_alleles.py`
**Input:** `chr{N}/pangenome.vcf.gz`.
**What happens:** for each record, `len(ALT.split(',')) + 1` — the number of comma-separated alternates, plus one for the reference.
**Output:** `chr{N}/num_alleles.npy`, shape `(340824,)`, dtype int64.
**Expect:** every entry ≥ 2; many entries > 2, because the graph is deeply multi-allelic.
**Why it matters:** it bounds the allele-index space and is used as an assertion in the allele-frequency step — if frequency mass ever lands on an allele index beyond what the site declares, two datasets have been mis-joined and the assertion fires immediately rather than silently corrupting the model.


> **Question** — "Shape (340,824,) with 'many entries > 2' — what is the maximum, and is it the hypervariable site? The frequency array is (340,824, 90), so is 90 the maximum?"
>
> Yes to both, and the two facts are the same fact. The widest row on chr21 declares **90 alleles**, and it is `grch38#chr21:14569980`, VCF row **51791** — the site where the cohort's 88 haplotypes carry **88 distinct alleles**, i.e. every haplotype is unique. `allele_frequencies.npy` is `(340824, 90)` because its width is set by the **worst site on the chromosome**; every other row is REF-padded out to 90 columns and normalised so it sums to 1 (all measured in this project).
>
> That site is not a freak. On chr21 there are **7** sites with at least 88 alleles, **155** with at least 44, and **618** with at least 20 (measured in this project). The 90-column array is the cheapest possible signal that this class of site exists, which is a good reason to read Step 7's output rather than skip it.

> **Question** — "This step only exists to feed an assertion. Do we keep it?"
>
> Keep it. It costs one pass over the VCF and it is the only cheap check that the numpy arrays and the VCF are still row-aligned — every entry must be at least 2, and `num_alleles[i]` must bound the largest allele index seen at row i. The failure it catches is a **mis-join**, and mis-joins in this pipeline are silent by default: the integration seam `new_haplotypes.npy` is **positional**, so a row-order mismatch scores the wrong variants rather than raising (project note, `docs/terms.md`). An assertion that fires loudly is worth more here than the step costs.

### Step 8 — Compute LD blocks with plink
**File:** `scripts/get_blocks.sbatch`
**Input:** `chr{N}/1000g_phased.vcf.gz`.
**What happens:** `plink --vcf … --make-bed` converts to plink's binary genotype format (`.bed` genotypes, `.bim` variants, `.fam` samples), then `plink --blocks no-pheno-req` runs plink's confidence-interval block-detection procedure and writes the block intervals.
**Output:** `chr{N}/blocks.blocks` and `chr{N}/blocks.blocks.det` (a whitespace table with `BP1`, `BP2` start/end base-pair coordinates per block).
**Expect:** **14,137 blocks** on our rebuilt chr21 from the **30x GRCh38 panel** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-18).
**Why it matters:** this defines the unit of editing. Note that the boundaries are derived from the *reference panel*, not from the pangenome cohort — 44 people is far too few to estimate LD structure reliably, so using an external panel of thousands is the right call. It also means the panel's genome build determines where the boundaries land, which is why departure (a) reaches into this step.


> **Question** — "What does plink's confidence-interval procedure actually *do*? What is the confidence interval on, and which variants does it use — all of them, or only common SNPs?"
>
> `plink --blocks` implements a confidence-interval rule: for each pair of nearby variants it estimates D′ — the normalised linkage-disequilibrium coefficient, D divided by its maximum possible value given the two allele frequencies — puts a **confidence interval** on that estimate, and then classifies the pair as being in strong linkage, in strong historical recombination, or uninformative according to where that interval sits. A block is a maximal run of variants in which enough of the pairs are classified "strong LD" relative to the "strong recombination" pairs. The confidence interval is therefore on **D′ for a pair of variants**, and its whole purpose is to stop a small sample from declaring strong linkage on the strength of a few chromosomes.
>
> plink also applies its own minor-allele-frequency and completeness thresholds before it will use a variant. **This project has not verified which thresholds the shipped invocation ends up using**, and no such number is recorded, so do not quote one — it is a real gap, and it is worth closing for anyone reading PanMixer's own numbers, because those thresholds decide which panel variants get a vote on where **their** block edges fall. It is not a gap our mechanism inherits: under **model C** the chain unit is the individual variant, so the mechanism runs no plink and has no block edges (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The thresholds matter to us only through the model-B fallback and through parity runs against PanMixer.

> **Question** — "The boundaries come from the panel's variants, which are not our variants. So our block *edges* sit at positions that may not be anywhere near one of our variants. Does that make the edges effectively arbitrary with respect to the data we are editing?"
>
> Take the point in its strong form: plink never sees the pangenome at all. It reads the reference panel, computes intervals between **panel** variants in **panel** coordinates, and writes `BP1`/`BP2`. Step 12 then bins pangenome positions into whatever intervals came out. Nothing in that chain consulted a single pangenome variant.
>
> The measured consequence is larger than "the edges are a bit off". plink produced **14,137** intervals on chr21, and the block dictionary the mechanism actually operates on has **100,757 entries**, of which **89,087** are singletons — **88.4% of entries**, which is **26.1% of the 340,824 variants** (measured in this project). Most of that expansion is variants that fell **outside every interval** and were therefore given a block of their own. So for a large share of the chromosome the panel did not place a boundary near the variant; it said nothing about the variant at all, and the pipeline turned silence into a one-variant block.
>
> Read that as a modelling claim and it is a strong one: a one-variant block asserts that the variant is **independent of its neighbours**. Step 12's text does not separate "no LD partners" from "the panel had nothing to say", and this project has not separated them either — the two sources of singletons are not individually counted.
>
> A related number that is commonly wanted here and that we do **not** have: how many panel samples you need before LD estimates are trustworthy. No figure for that is recorded in this project, and 44 being too few is stated as a rationale, not measured. Do not invent a threshold.

> **Question** — "The early spec inherited these blocks unchanged. If the boundaries are panel- and build-dependent, are we inheriting a decision we have not audited?"
>
> The question has been answered by removing its subject, and the wording matters, so here it is unambiguously. The pinned specification did inherit the plink LD block boundaries *and* the by-position site-to-block assignment — with the proviso that the boundaries had to be the ones computed on the **build-matched** panel, our 30x GRCh38 rebuild, not the shipped GRCh37 Phase 3 run, whose coordinates do not correspond to the graph at all (**1,025** exact matches against **258,610**, a **252×** gap, measured in this project; departure (a)). That proviso still governs anywhere blocks are used at all. But the decided chain architecture, **model C**, makes the **individual variant** the chain unit, so the mechanism has no LD blocks, runs no plink, and takes **no external panel as an input at all** (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). Blocks survive for the model-B fallback and for parity runs against PanMixer, and the external panel survives as the attack database in the evaluation.
>
> What made the inheritance worth auditing is worth keeping in view, because it is exactly what C removes. `T` used to be the number of **blocks**, so the panel's thresholds and build decided `T`, and `T` decided the per-block weight — with uniform weights, `beta_t = 1/T` at T = 100,757 on chr21 (measured in this project), the arithmetic §4.10.4 confronts. Inheriting the segmentation therefore inherited a budget allocation from an external dataset's LD structure. Under C nothing external sets `T`: it is simply the number of chain positions, which is the number of variants. And the weights are no longer uniform in any case — `beta_v = w_v / Σ_u w_u` with `w_v = 1/support(v)`, additive and bounded in [0,1] by construction, with no per-block normaliser (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The total tilt `exp(eta_tau)` is **invariant in `T`**: more positions each move less, and they compose to the same total. What C does cost is numerical headroom — the per-position tilt sits only **13.5×** above the float32 noise floor, against **45.7×** under model B (**derived**), which is why float64 stored alphas are load-bearing rather than a preference. The snarl-tree alternative — which would make the 257 kb hypervariable region **one block instead of 370 overlapping ones** and make blocks disjoint by construction (measured and derived in this project) — was an answer to the segmentation question; C dissolves the question instead.

### Step 9 — Convert VCFs to numpy
**File:** `tools/common/VCFtoNP.py`
**Input:** the three per-chromosome VCFs. `pangenome` and `1000g_phased` are read as phased (`|`), `PG` as unphased (`/`).
**What happens:** two passes — count rows and samples, then fill the array.
**Output, and this is the layout everything else assumes:**

> **Definition** — the **allele matrix**, written `X`. Shape `X[num_samples, num_sites, 2]`, dtype `int16`.
> `X[i, s, h]` = the **allele index** carried by sample *i*, at site *s*, on haplotype *h* ∈ {0,1}.
> `0` = the reference allele. `1, 2, 3, …` = the first, second, third… ALT allele **as listed in that VCF row**.
> **`-1` = missing.** Not "reference", not "zero", not "absent from the graph" — *unknown to this array*. It arrives here from **five** different situations — four of them properties of the data upstream, the fifth introduced by this converter itself — and the array cannot tell them apart. See the table immediately after this step's "Why it matters" paragraph.
> Two are worth naming right here, because they are the ones a reader guesses: the VCF wrote a literal `.` for that allele, **or** the VCF gave a **haploid** genotype — one allele where the format allows two — and the converter put `-1` in strand 1 to fill the gap it was not given. ⚠ The second turns out **not to occur on chr21** (all separator-less `GT` fields are `.`; measured 2026-09-28), so the table below lists five candidate causes of which **four are live here**.

Plus `*_positions.npy`, shape `(num_sites,)` int64, holding the base-pair POS column; and `*_subjects.npy`, the sample names in column order.
**Expect, chr21 (measured in this project):** `pangenome.npy` `(44, 340824, 2)` int16; `PG.npy` `(368, 384720, 2)`; `1000g_phased.npy` `(3202, 1002753, 2)` in our 30x GRCh38 rebuild.
**Why it matters:** VCF parsing is slow and everything downstream is array arithmetic. **The `-1` is load-bearing**: it is *not* the reference allele, and treating it as 0 would silently invent data — turning "we do not know" into "this person matches the reference", which is exactly the kind of error that inflates both frequency estimates and apparent matches. Every later step masks on `!= -1` before counting.

**Exactly two things on a VCF line survive this step.** This is worth stating as narrowly as the code states it, because the consequence is the most important single point in Part B. In `tools/common/VCFtoNP.py` the parser keeps **`fields[1]`** — the `POS` column — and, for each sample *i*, **`fields[9+i].split(':')[0]`** — the `GT` subfield of that sample's genotype column, i.e. the text before the first colon (**measured in this project**, from the pinned checkout `external/PanMixer` at commit `c182c38d5bc8bb6f00f4b0b101207c4a009ca045`).

Everything else on the line is read and thrown away. In particular:

- the **entire `INFO` column** — which is where `LV` (the snarl-tree level), `PS` (the parent bubble id), `CONFLICT` (the list of samples whose graph paths disagreed here) and `AT` (the allele traversals through the graph) all live;
- **`REF`** and **`ALT`**, so the array knows an allele's *index* but not one base of its *sequence*, and therefore not its length;
- the record **`ID`**.

> **Key idea** — after Step 9 the matrix holds, per site and per haplotype, a small integer whose meaning is "the *n*th allele **as that VCF row happened to list them**". The row's identity as a piece of DNA — where it sits in the snarl tree, how long it is, whether the caller was confident — is not in the array and cannot be recovered from it. Every step from here to Step 19 is arithmetic over those integers.

**The consequence: five causes, one indistinguishable value.** A `-1` can arise in five distinct ways. They mean genuinely different things — one of them means *the DNA does not exist on that chromosome*, another means *we could not see it*, another means *the caller saw two incompatible answers* — and they call for opposite handling. Every one of them is identifiable **at this moment**, from something present on the very line the converter is parsing: in four cases a field it reads and throws away, and in the fifth the shape of the one field it keeps. None of them is recoverable afterwards.

| cause of `-1` | what it actually means | how common (all **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`) | the field that identifies it | does that field survive Step 9? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. not applicable** | the record is a **nested** child bubble sitting inside a parent allele that this haplotype does not carry, so the DNA the child describes **is not on that chromosome at all** | **66.1%** of nested missingness, over a denominator of **5,735** nested chr21 records that have any missing child call. Among haplotypes that *do* have a parent call it is **perfectly determined** by which parent allele they carry | `LV` and `PS` in `INFO` (the child's level and its parent's bubble id) | **no** |
| **2. inherited** | the **parent** record's call was itself missing, so the child could not be resolved either | **12.1%** of nested missingness, same **5,735**-record denominator | `LV` and `PS`, plus the parent row's own genotype | **no** |
| **3. assembly gap** | the donor's assembly simply does not cover this stretch of the chromosome. The allele almost certainly **exists**; we do not know it | **349 runs** of ≥ 100 consecutive missing positions, covering **448,283** matrix entries. Across all 44 subjects on strand 0 there are **28,052** runs in total, median length **1**, mean **20.0**, maximum **23,478**; **54.4%** of runs are isolated single positions, i.e. structural rather than gap-shaped | **run length** in the missingness pattern itself — a gap is long and contiguous, a not-applicable is a single position | **no** (the array keeps the `-1`s, so the run structure is technically still visible, but nothing downstream looks at it) |
| **4. conflict** | the sample had **multiple graph paths that disagreed** here, so `vg` refused to choose and wrote a missing allele in both slots | **1,855** chr21 records carry a `CONFLICT` tag = **0.54%** of 340,824 records; at such a site a mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes are missing, against **3.6 of 88** elsewhere | the `CONFLICT` tag in `INFO`, which **names the samples** | **no** |
| ~~**5. haploid genotype**~~ ⚠ **DOES NOT OCCUR ON THIS DATA** | the VCF writes **one** allele where the format allows two, and the converter puts `-1` in strand 1. This would be a case where *nothing is missing in the data* — but **measured 2026-09-28**, every such field on chr21 is an explicit `.`, so it carries no allele either | **3.135%** of sample `GT` fields on chr21 lack a separator, over a denominator of **150,000** chr21 records; **5.581%** of allele slots hold an explicit `.` (both **measured in this project**, 2026-09-25). ⚠ Scanning all **14,996,256** chr21 `GT` fields, **237,594 (1.584%)** lack a `|` and those 237,594 fields have **exactly ONE distinct value: `.`** — so there are **ZERO true haploid genotypes** and this branch only ever fires on a missing call (**measured in this project**, 2026-09-28) | the **arity of the `GT` string** | moot — the case is informationally identical to `.\|.` on this data |

**For scale, the totals (measured in this project):** **1,278,653** of the chr21 matrix's entries are `-1`, i.e. **4.26%**; **52,926** sites — **15.5%** — have at least one missing entry. So this is not a rare corner. ⚠ **Retraction (2026-09-28).** An earlier version of this passage said of the last row that *"3.135% of genotype fields become missing for a reason that is not a property of the data at all"* — a conversion decision rather than a fact about the data. That is **wrong on this data**: all **237,594** separator-less chr21 `GT` fields hold exactly `.`, so the converter is handed no allele to lose and records "unknown" correctly. The hazard is real for any OTHER input VCF — a converter handed a genuinely haploid `GT` could record "haploid, one allele known" and this one would not — so the row is kept as a warning rather than deleted. **On chr21 there are FOUR causes, not five.**

> **Caution** — the loss is not that five causes are collapsed into one *label*. It is that they are collapsed into one *value*, and that value is then used as though it meant the third one. Two downstream consumers read `-1` and both of them get it wrong for four of the five causes. `support(v)` (see §A.5 and Step 14a) counts **non-missing haplotype entries**, so a not-applicable and an assembly gap both shrink the support and both raise `1/support(v)` identically. And the privacy scorer zeroes the score wherever the target's own entry is `-1` (`pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`, **measured in this project** in `get_support_and_pmi.py`), so all five causes contribute **exactly 0** nats.

> **Question** — *"Row 3 of that table says the run structure is 'technically still visible' in the array. So could we recover the assembly gaps after the fact and skip the reason array, at least for that one cause?"* — **partly, and only for that one cause.** Run length is the one discriminator of the five that is a property of the **matrix itself** rather than of a field the converter dropped: the `-1`s are still in the array, so the runs are still in the array. The signal is real and measured — on strand 0 across all 44 subjects there are **28,052** runs of consecutive missing positions, median **1**, mean **20.0**, maximum **23,478**, of which **54.4%** are isolated single positions and **349** runs of ≥ 100 consecutive positions cover **448,283** entries (all **measured in this project**). A long run is a contig break; an isolated position is not. Three things stop it being a substitute for recording the cause. First, **there is no classification threshold in `docs/`**: the ≥ 100 cut above is the length at which runs were *counted*, not a validated decision rule, and **how accurately any threshold would classify a run has not been measured in this project** — what would settle it is a labelled comparison against `LV`/`PS` and `CONFLICT` on the same records, which means reading the `INFO` column anyway. Second, run length cannot separate cause 1 from cause 2 or cause 5 at all, because all three routinely produce runs of length **1**. Third, nothing downstream reads the run structure, so using it would mean adding a pass that does. The other four causes are gone outright.

> **Question** — *"Why is the reason array a prerequisite? Why not just pick a `missing_policy` now and move on?"* — because the policy is **not decidable from the array as it stands**, and the amount of data it governs changes by about **16×** (**derived**) between the two chain designs: mean missingness at a chain position is **0.26%** under PanMixer's anchors and **11.31%** under v1, where every variant is a chain position, with **7,555** positions over 25% missing (all **measured in this project**). The four options recorded in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy` each fit one cause and misfit another. **RENORMALISE** — drop that donor from the state distribution at that position — is right for a not-applicable, which is **66.1%** of nested missingness (**measured in this project**), and wrong for an assembly gap, where the donor almost certainly carries an allele we merely cannot see. **WILDCARD** makes a poorly assembled donor universally attractive. **MISMATCH** charges an assembly gap as though it were genuine variation. **RESTRICT** excludes ill-behaved positions from the chain, which is what the anchor rule does in effect, and is kept as a baseline precisely because it is the **published** behaviour. **This decision is deliberately reserved and this primer does not make it** — the ledger records the leaning as RENORMALISE with nothing decided. What would settle it is a reason array plus a measured comparison of the options on the same data, and `docs/memory.md` records the further possibility of **splitting the policy by cause**, which is only available if the cause survives conversion. That is why the array is a prerequisite: it is what makes any of the four expressible at all.

**A worked example of cause 1**, because "how could a donor not have an allele at a variant?" is the natural objection and the answer differs by record class.

> **Worked example** — **for a top-level variant it cannot happen.** Every haplotype path in this graph runs along the **GRCh38 backbone**, so a top-level (`LV=0`) record has an allele for all 88 haplotypes by construction. The **2.25%** missingness measured at top-level SNPs (**measured in this project**) is assembly gaps and conflicts, not structure.
>
> **For a nested variant it happens routinely.** Take this measured pair on chr21 (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`):
>
> - **Parent:** row **37624**, `POS` **13,205,133**. `REF` is **13 bp** long; it has five `ALT`s, of lengths **15, 1, 17, 11 and 13 bp**. So allele **2** — the second `ALT`, the 1 bp one — is a **deletion** of essentially the whole 13 bp region.
> - **Child:** row **37625**, `POS` **13,205,136** — three bases *inside* the parent's span.
> - **87 of 88** haplotypes carry parent allele 0, 1, 3, 4 or 5, and every one of them has a called allele at the child.
> - **One** haplotype carries parent allele **2**. On that chromosome the sequence the child variant describes **has been deleted**. There is no allele to report, because there is no DNA to report it about.
>
> VCF has **no syntax for "not applicable"**. Its only way to say anything here is `.`, which also means "unknown". So the format collapses two different statements before the converter even starts, and the converter then discards `LV` and `PS`, which are the only fields that would let a reader tell which statement was meant.

**Why the conflict row is the worst of the five**, and where the reader will meet it again. Conflicts land on exactly the records a privacy release most needs to touch, and they land there with the arithmetic pointing the wrong way.

> **Defect** — this is a *"the metric measures something narrower than its name suggests"* finding, not a claim about PanMixer's published numbers. The `CONFLICT` tag **names the samples** whose paths disagreed, and a mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes are missing at such a record against **3.6 of 88** elsewhere (**measured in this project**) — so it is not every haplotype. But wherever the target is one of the named samples, its own entry is `-1`, and the privacy value of editing it is then **exactly 0** nats. At the same record the support is smaller, so the cost of editing it goes **up**: median utility weight **0.01818** at `CONFLICT` sites against **0.01136** elsewhere, i.e. **1.60× more expensive** (**measured in this project**; the ratio is **derived**). Zero value at above-average weight is the worst cell of the knapsack's value/weight table, so the optimizer of §A.6 and Step 17 will **essentially never select such a record — it is released verbatim.**
>
> And they are not spread uniformly. Against a chr21 baseline of **90.8% `LV=0` / 8.2% `LV=1`**, `CONFLICT` records are **59.1% `LV=0` / 37.5% `LV=1`** — a **4.6× enrichment** in nested records — with median size **4 bp** (mean 602, max 311,406) and a median of **4 alleles** per record against 2 chromosome-wide (all **measured in this project**). The **1,855** records occupy **1,634** blocks, **1,469** of them singletons, and only **1.8%** of *those blocks* are HMM-path blocks, against a chr21 baseline of **10.4% of blocks** (**measured in this project** — note the denominator: this is a share of the 1,634 conflict-occupied blocks, not of the 1,855 records). So the leak is concentrated precisely where the target's local structure is unusual, and it is handled by the independent per-variant sampler rather than by the haplotype model.

> **Question** — *"Zero privacy value at 1.60× the cost. Which reported number does that move, and in which direction?"* — **none of the reported numbers move, and that is exactly why it is worth flagging.** Both axes are per block and both are summed over the blocks the optimizer **selected**, so a block that is never selected contributes to neither: it does not inflate the reported privacy risk and it does not consume any utility budget. The quantity that moves is the **leak**, and no metric in the suite is pointed at it. The evaluation barely sees these records either — of the **1,855** `CONFLICT` records, the `af_loss` / `ld_loss` mask retains **37** and the gap-score attack after the biallelic-SNP mask of Step 11 retains **49** (**measured in this project**). **How much of the target's total identifiability sits in those released-verbatim records has not been measured in this project**; what would settle it is summing the target's own per-site self-information over just those **1,855** records and comparing it against the chromosome total. Do not confuse this with the separate **7.0% of ε** inflation contributed by no-op moves (**measured in this project**) — that is a different mechanism on a different axis. Framing, as always: this is the released code and our own measurements of it, not a claim that the published figures are wrong.

> **Open question** — what *causes* a conflict is **not established**. Assembly fragmentation is named in `docs/memory.md` as the most plausible candidate and explicitly recorded as **unverified** — it cannot be determined from the VCF alone. One hypothesis *was* tested and does not explain it: repeats that revisit a bubble. On chr21, **37** records have a revisiting traversal in their own `AT` and **1,855** carry a `CONFLICT` tag, but only **11** are both; **1,844** conflict **without** any revisit (**measured in this project**). `CONFLICT` also names **all 45 samples**, roughly **200–470 records each**, which is far too widespread for tandem duplications to be the mechanism. One caveat `docs/memory.md` records against its own test: a record's `AT` lists traversals of **its own** bubble only, so it cannot reveal that a sample passed through that bubble twice — that is visible only at the **enclosing parent**. The cross-tabulation therefore **understates** the link for child records inside a duplicated region. The revisit mechanism is real; what is ruled out is its being the *dominant* cause, not its existing.

**What this produced as a recommendation for our own preprocessing.** One line, and it is in `docs/plan.md` as a prerequisite of the v1 spec rather than as a nice-to-have:

> **Key idea** — **emit an auxiliary reason array beside the allele matrix, at conversion time.** Not a policy, not a fix, just a record: one array row-aligned and slot-aligned to `X[i, s, h]`, holding a small integer code per entry — 0 for "called", then one code for each of the five causes above. It is cheap **now**, because the converter is already holding the `INFO` string and the `GT` string on the same line it is writing the `-1` into. It is **unrecoverable later**, because by the time any downstream step could want it, the only fields that distinguish the causes have been dropped. *Cheap now, unrecoverable afterwards.*
>
> The reason it is a prerequisite and not a refinement: our `missing_policy` decision — four options, **RENORMALISE**, **WILDCARD**, **MISMATCH** and **RESTRICT**, recorded in `docs/terms.md` under `missing_policy` and **still open**, with RENORMALISE the leaning choice and nothing decided — is **only expressible if the causes are distinguishable**. Dropping a donor from the state distribution is right for a not-applicable and wrong for an assembly gap, where the donor almost certainly *has* an allele we merely cannot see; treating missing as a wildcard match makes poorly-assembled donors universally attractive; treating it as a mismatch penalises bad assemblies as though they were real variation. Without a reason array every one of those becomes the same undeclared default. `docs/memory.md` records the further suggestion of **splitting the policy by cause**, which is only possible if the cause survives.

> **Note** — the scale of what the policy has to cover changes under v1, and the change is large. The mean missingness at the chain positions PanMixer's HMM actually steps over — anchors inside blocks holding two or more anchors — is **0.26%**; across **every** variant, which is what v1 makes a chain position, it is **4.26%**, with **30,800** records over 25% missing. Worst is the class v1 adds *inside* blocks PanMixer already chains — the non-anchor records of multi-variant blocks — at **11.31%**, with **7,555** over 25% missing (all measured in this project; the per-class table is in `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). **Each of those means is over a different set of positions; none of them is quotable without naming its set.** Restricting to anchors *is*, in effect, a missingness policy: it is the RESTRICT option, and it is kept deliberately as a baseline because it is the **published** behaviour.

**Three more questions you may be about to ask, answered here.**

> **Question** — *"`PG` is read as unphased and the other two as phased. If PanGenie genotypes are unphased, how can they supply haplotype anchors?"* — because the anchor role is **positional, not allelic**. An anchor is a *site* that PanMixer is willing to treat as a chain position; the alleles that actually populate the chain come from the **pangenome** matrix, not from PanGenie. PanGenie is used only as a membership test — "does this (POS, REF, whole-ALT-string) appear in the `bi_all` callset?" — so its phasing never enters the model. See §A.3, §A.8.1 and departure (d) for what that membership test is and is not a proxy for.

> **Question** — *"A haploid genotype writes `-1` into strand 1. On an autosome, when is a genotype haploid at all?"* — **not** because of sex chromosomes: `chrX` was already removed at Step 4, so every record reaching this point is autosomal and every sample should have two paths. The measured rate is nonetheless **3.135%** of chr21 sample `GT` fields (**measured in this project**). **What upstream condition makes `vg deconstruct` emit a single allele on an autosome is not recorded in `docs/` and is not established here** — do not accept a plausible story for it, including one from this primer. What *is* established is the converter's behaviour and its asymmetry: the filled slot is always **strand 0** and the `-1` always **strand 1** (**measured in this project**), so the two haplotype columns are not exchangeable in the way everything downstream implicitly assumes (**derived**).

> **Question** — *"Can we just add up the five shares and see which cause dominates?"* — **no, and this is a gap we owe rather than an answer.** The five figures above have **five different denominators**: 66.1% and 12.1% are shares of missingness at *nested records* (of 5,735); 3.135% is a share of *`GT` fields* (of 150,000 records); 349 runs / 448,283 entries is an *absolute count*; 1,855 is a *record count*. **There is no table in `docs/` apportioning the 1,278,653 missing chr21 entries across the causes**, and the **2.25%** top-level figure has not been split into gaps versus conflicts either. Both are measurements this project has not made. Do not let the percentages above be added.

### Step 10 — Compute variant mappings between datasets
**File:** `scripts/src/get_mappings.py`
**Input:** the three VCFs and their `_positions.npy`.
**What happens:** builds, for each VCF, a dictionary keyed on the triple **(POS, REF, full ALT string)** → row index. Then intersects: for every pangenome key present in a target VCF, record pangenome-row → target-row. It additionally builds a **relaxed** mapping keyed only on (POS, REF), taking the first match.
**Output:** `pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased.pickle` (to the phased panel), `pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments.pickle` (to PanGenie — note the name; see departure (d)), the paired index arrays `pangenome_mask.npy` / `thousand_g_phased_mask.npy`, and the `_posref` relaxed variants.
**Expect (measured in this project, `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18 and `docs/data.md`):** on our rebuilt chr21 against the **same-build 30x GRCh38 panel**, **258,610** strict matches and **268,851** relaxed — 75.88% of the 340,824 pangenome records. Against the panel the released script actually downloads (**Phase 3, GRCh37**), the identical computation yields **1,025** strict and **2,176** relaxed — 0.30%. A **252× gap**. That single number is how you diagnose departure (a), and an independent `bcftools` comparison predicted the same figure, so two routes agree.
**Why it matters:** these mappings are the join keys for everything downstream — which pangenome sites have trustworthy external frequencies, which sites are scorable by the attack, and which sites count as anchors.
**Pedagogical note:** the strict key requires exact match on the *entire* ALT string. In a deeply multi-allelic graph VCF, two callsets that discovered the same SNP but a different set of neighbouring alternates produce different ALT strings and will **not** match. That is why the relaxed (POS, REF) key exists as a fallback, and it is also why the residual ~24% of non-matching records under the correct build should not be assumed to be a problem: the pangenome carries graph-only and structural variants that a SNV/indel/SV panel simply does not have. That residual has not been characterised here and should not be assumed benign either — it is an open question.


> **Question** — "Two mappings, strict and relaxed. Which one does the *anchor* set use, and which one does the frequency table use? Those are different decisions."
>
> They are, and the file names actively mislead, so name the consumers explicitly:
>
> | consumer | mapping it reads | what that mapping actually joins |
> |---|---|---|
> | the anchor set (`hmm.py`) | `pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments.pickle` | **PanGenie** `PG.vcf.gz`, not 1000 Genomes — the name is bound to the wrong dataset at `get_mappings.py:28` |
> | the allele-frequency table (`get_af.py` branch 1) | the 1000G strict `(POS, REF, full ALT)` map | the phased reference panel of Step 3 |
> | `af_loss` / `ld_loss` site sets | `pangenome_mask.npy`, from the strict 1000G map | the phased reference panel |
> | the attack database | the masked posref arrays, from the relaxed `(POS, REF)` map | the phased reference panel |
>
> (All read from the code, measured in this project.) The `alignments` misnomer is the whole content of departure (d), and it has one consequence worth holding on to: because PanGenie is **GRCh38**, the per-block HMM-versus-frequency **routing decision is not degraded** by the build mismatch. What the mismatch does corrupt is the block **boundaries**, the **attack database**, and the `af_loss`/`ld_loss` **site sets** (measured in this project; an earlier draft of this claim said the routing was also degraded and that was retracted as Correction 5).

> **Question** — "The relaxed key takes the *first* match on (POS, REF). At a site with 90 alleles the first match is essentially arbitrary. Doesn't that attach a frequency from the wrong allele?"
>
> It can, and the hazard is exactly as you describe: `(POS, REF)` is not a key at a multi-allelic record, so "first match" resolves a genuine ambiguity by accident of file order. In a graph VCF this is not a corner case — the median ALT count is 1 even for non-SNP records, but the tail runs to **90 declared alleles at one chr21 site** (measured in this project).
>
> **Whether it ever actually attaches a frequency from the wrong allele, and how often, has not been measured in this project.** That is a real gap, listed as one, and there is no number to quote. What can be said without measurement is which consumers are exposed: the relaxed map feeds the masked attack-database arrays and the gap score's site set, not branch 1 of the frequency table, which uses the strict `(POS, REF, full ALT)` key (measured in this project).

> **Question** — "The residual ~24% that don't match even under the correct build. Can we measure what they actually *are* before we decide they're fine? 'The pangenome carries graph-only and structural variants' is an argument, not a measurement."
>
> Agreed, and `docs/` says so in as many words: the residual "is expected … but has not been characterised and should not be assumed benign" (project decision, `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18, carried as open question 1 in `docs/plan.md`). So treat the sentence in the step above as a hypothesis with no measurement behind it.
>
> Two things *are* nailed down, and they are worth separating. First the size. Under the build-matched 30x GRCh38 panel, **258,610 of 340,824** chr21 pangenome records match strictly and **268,851** match on the relaxed key — **75.88%** — against **1,025** strict and **2,176** relaxed under the Phase 3 GRCh37 panel the released script actually downloads, **0.30%**, the **252×** gap that diagnoses departure (a) (all measured in this project). The residual is therefore **340,824 − 258,610 = 82,214 records, 24.12%** (derived).
>
> Second, that residual is not an unaccounted set in the *bookkeeping* sense, only in the *structural* sense. It is exactly the union of the two fallback branches of the frequency table (Step 13): branch 2, matched in PanGenie but not 1000G, **25,325 records (7.4%)**, and branch 3, novel in both, **56,889 records (16.7%)** — and 25,325 + 56,889 = 82,214, which closes the accounting exactly (measured in this project; the sum is derived). What nobody has done is ask *what kind of variant* those 82,214 rows are.
>
> That measurement is cheap and would be a one-pass join on arrays this project already has: classify the 82,214 non-matching rows by nesting level, by SNP-versus-indel-versus-structural, and by REF length, against the chromosome-wide baselines already measured here — **`LV=0` 90.8%, `LV=1` 8.2%, `LV=2` 0.9%, `LV=3` 0.1%, `LV=4` 10 records** (measured in this project). No new download and no new panel is needed. Until it exists, there is no number to put on the composition of the residual and this primer will not supply one.
>
> The reason it matters lands on three reported quantities at once. A record outside the strict 1000G map is outside `pangenome_mask.npy`, so the **gap-score attack cannot score it** (Step 21); it is outside the `af_loss` and `ld_loss` site sets, which are built from the same strict mask — **258,610 sites** (measured in this project) — so the two published distortion metrics **cannot see it** (Steps 23, 24); and it is the material branch 3 scores as **rarest** when it carries signal at all — median self-information **5.41 nats** *at sites where the target HG00438 carries a non-REF allele*, against **0.63** for branch 1 and **0.43** for branch 2 under the same condition, with the caveat that at most novel sites the target carries REF (median `f_v` **0.998**) so most of them contribute nearly nothing, and the signal sits in the **11,760** novel sites where it does carry a non-REF allele (all measured in this project). In the sanctioned framing: the attack and both distortion metrics measure something narrower than their names suggest, and the 82,214-record residual is the boundary of that narrowness. Whether the material inside it is harmless is the open question; we have not reproduced any published figure and nothing here says otherwise.

### Step 11 — Build the biallelic SNP mask
**File:** `scripts/src/build_biallelic_snp_mask.py`
**Input:** `chr{N}/pangenome.vcf.gz`, `pangenome_positions.npy`.
**What happens:** marks `True` where the record has exactly one ALT and both REF and ALT are a single base in {A, C, G, T}. It asserts that record *i*'s POS equals `positions[i]`, so any row-order drift is caught loudly rather than silently.
**Output:** `chr{N}/biallelic_snp_mask.npy`, shape `(340824,)`, dtype bool.
**Expect, chr21 (measured in this project):** **274,458 biallelic SNPs, 80.5%** of records — exactly, as the `True` count of `biallelic_snp_mask.npy` (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26, which logs it as 274,458 of 340,824 = **80.53%**). Keep it distinct from two neighbouring counts: **275,648** records are single-base, so **1,190** are multi-allelic SNP records and do not enter the mask; and **238,052** is a different quantity again — the sites the gap-score attack keeps after this mask *and* the 1000G match — which must never be quoted as the mask count. Every figure in this paragraph is **measured in this project**.
**Why it matters:** the linkage attack's scoring model is a biallelic **Hardy–Weinberg** genotype model, which only makes sense on biallelic sites.

> **Definition** — **Hardy–Weinberg**, and the entire empirical privacy claim rests on it. Let **p** be the reference-allele frequency at a biallelic site and **q = 1 − p** the alternate-allele frequency. If a person's two chromosome copies were drawn independently from the population, then
>
> **P(0/0) = p²,  P(0/1) = 2pq,  P(1/1) = q².**
>
> (The factor 2 on the heterozygote is because there are two ways to get one of each.) This is **Hardy–Weinberg equilibrium**: the assumption that alleles pair at random. It is what turns an *allele* frequency, which is what the panel gives you, into a *genotype* frequency, which is what the attacker's score needs. It fails in real populations under inbreeding, population structure and selection, so it is an approximation — but a standard and generally serviceable one. Reminder: **this p and q are allele frequencies**, not the stay/switch probabilities of §A.3 and not the input paths of Section 4.


> **Question** — "80.5% of records are biallelic SNPs and the attack only scores those. So the whole empirical privacy claim is blind to 19.5% of records — and those are the indels and the hypervariable sites, which are the most identifying ones. Isn't that the same defect as the non-anchor blindness, just in the attack instead of the score?"
>
> Structurally it is the same shape, and the sanctioned way to say it is that **the metric measures something narrower than its name suggests**. The mask marks **274,458 records — 80.5% of 340,824** (measured in this project; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26), and the linkage attack of Step 21 restricts to those *and* to sites polymorphic in the attack panel, so everything outside the mask is invisible to the measured privacy claim.
>
> The full accounting of what each stage sees, measured on chr21 at capacity 0.1 for HG00438, makes the asymmetry concrete:
>
> | stage | records seen | of which non-SNP | of which `CONFLICT` |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | the mechanism (privacy score, knapsack, stacker) | **340,824 (100%)** | 65,176 | 1,855 |
> | `af_loss` / `ld_loss` (strict 1000G mask) | 258,610 (75.9%) | 21,550 | 37 |
> | gap score (posref mask) | 268,851 (78.9%) | 29,965 | 110 |
> | gap score after the biallelic-SNP mask | 238,052 (69.8%) | **0** | 49 |
> | read mapping (`bcftools view -v snps`) | 288,020 (84.5%) | mixed records kept | 1,167 |
>
> (All measured in this project.)
