Read the first row against the last four: **the mechanism sees everything and every metric filters.** Note the direction carefully, because the opposite claim was made in an earlier draft and is wrong: indel and structural records are **not** excluded from the mechanism — they are scored, selected and rewritten at a **higher** rate than SNPs, **23.3%** against **14.9%** on that run (measured in this project). It is the evaluation that is narrow, not the mechanism.

The model in that box is an assumption, and it is the kind of assumption worth interrogating before anything is built on it.

> **Question** — "Hardy–Weinberg assumes alleles pair at random. HPRC was assembled to be maximally diverse across populations. Is HWE even approximately true in a 44-person panel chosen that way?"
>
> The model is not applied to the 44. The genotype probabilities p², 2pq, q² are computed from the **attack panel's** allele frequencies — the phased reference panel of Step 3, 2,504 samples as shipped or 3,202 in our rebuild (measured in this project) — and used to price a match against a database entry. The 44-person cohort supplies the released genotype being matched, not the frequencies.
>
> That does not make the caveat vanish, it relocates it. A multi-population panel is precisely the case where HWE fails, because population structure makes alleles co-occur non-randomly; the box at this step already names structure, inbreeding and selection as the three standard failure modes. The honest reading is that HWE here is a **scoring convention that makes rare matches expensive**, not a claim about the biology of the panel.

> **Question** — "Work the box for me on one site: p = 0.9, q = 0.1. What are the three genotype probabilities, and which one does the attacker score against?"
>
> With p = 0.9 for the reference allele and q = 0.1 for the alternate: **P(0/0) = p² = 0.81**, **P(0/1) = 2pq = 0.18**, **P(1/1) = q² = 0.01** (derived; they sum to 1, which is the check).
>
> The attacker scores the genotype that the **released** record shows, and only at loci where the database entry's genotype **equals** it. So if the release shows `0/1` at this site and a candidate also has `0/1`, that locus contributes **−log(0.18) = 1.7148 nats**. If both show `1/1` it contributes **−log(0.01) = 4.6052 nats**. If both show `0/0` it contributes **−log(0.81) = 0.2107 nats** (all derived). Matching on the homozygous-rare genotype is worth about **22 times** as much as matching on the homozygous-common one, which is the entire point of the weighting: a handful of rare agreements identifies a person, and thousands of common agreements do not.

### Step 12 — Refine plink blocks into the block dictionary
**File:** `scripts/src/get_blocks_simple.py`
**Input:** `blocks.blocks.det` and `pangenome_positions.npy`.
**What happens:** reads the (BP1, BP2) intervals, drops degenerate ones where start == end, sorts by start, and for every pangenome variant position uses a binary search (`np.searchsorted`) to find the interval with the greatest start ≤ position; the variant is *in* that block if it is also ≤ that interval's end. Variants inside no interval each receive **their own unique block id**.
**Output:** `chr{N}/simple_blocks_idx.npy` (shape `(340824,)`, one block id per variant) and `chr{N}/blocks_dict.json`, a JSON map `block_id → (list_of_positions, list_of_row_indices)`.
**Expect, chr21 (measured in this project, `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18):** **100,757 dictionary entries** from only 14,137 plink blocks. Of those entries, **89,087 — 88.4% of entries — hold exactly one variant**, and since each holds one variant they account for 89,087 of 340,824 variants, i.e. **26.1% of variants**. Say which denominator, every time. (Most of these singleton entries arise because the variant fell outside every plink interval and was given a block of its own; an interval that happens to contain exactly one pangenome variant would also count as one. We have not separated the two sources, and the distinction does not affect anything downstream.) Separately, **10.4% of entries contain ≥2 anchors** and therefore take the HMM path, and those entries hold **71.4% of all variants**.
**Why it matters:** this is the index structure the whole mechanism iterates over. Internalise its shape: *most entries are trivial, but most of the genome lives in the non-trivial minority.* Note also the ordering convention — `block[0]` is **positions** (base pairs) and `block[1]` is **row indices**. Confusing these two is the root of departure (b).

**Nothing is ever split — and that is the problem.** The assignment rule in this step is one binary search on **one number**. `np.searchsorted` takes the variant's **start position** and nothing else; the variant lands in the interval with the greatest start ≤ that position, provided the position is also ≤ that interval's end. A record's `REF` length never enters the calculation, and it could not: Step 9 discarded `REF`, so by this point in the pipeline the array does not know how long any record is.

So a record is assigned **wholly to exactly one block, by its start coordinate alone**, even when the sequence it describes runs across many blocks. Nothing is cut in half; no block holds "the tail end of a variant" as a separate object. In that narrow sense it is true that a block never contains part of a variant.

> **Caution** — that sentence is **pedantically true and unhelpfully so**, and an earlier version of this material leaned on it. What it hides is the thing that actually matters: **a parent record can sit in one block while the child records inside its span sit in other blocks.** The children are separate rows in the VCF, with their own `POS` values, and this step assigns each of them independently. So the *DNA* of one variant is absolutely represented across several blocks — as several rows. The assignment is clean; the **correspondence between a block and the region a variant covers is not.**

**The extreme measured case.** The hypervariable chr21 site at `grch38#chr21:14569980`, graph bubble `>102270111>102277685`, is a top-level (`LV=0`) record at VCF row **51791** with a `REF` of **257,439 bp** and 89 `ALT`s ranging **257,124 – 514,768 bp**. Its span crosses **370** overlapping plink blocks — a segmentation taken from the snarl tree instead would make it **1** — while the record itself is assigned to exactly one: **block 1639**, which holds **92 records with 82 anchors** and therefore takes the HMM branch. The same region also carries **2,464 nested child records**, giving **2,485** matrix rows for one locus, **0.7%** of chr21's 340,824 rows (all **measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md` and `docs/plan.md`).

> **Question** — *"Block 1639 holds 92 records with 82 anchors, so it takes the HMM branch. Does the 257 kb record itself get to be one of the chain's positions?"* — **no. It is not an anchor** (`r in align` is `False`, **measured in this project**), and the forward algorithm on the HMM branch steps over a block's **anchors only** (see §A.4.1 and Step 14b), so the chain walks straight past the largest record on the chromosome. Its frequency is computed anyway and is not degenerate: HG00438 carries alleles **89** and **88** there, each with `f_v` = **1.540e-4**, i.e. **8.78 nats** of self-information apiece — the most identifying record on chr21 — and **that value is never read** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md` Correction 8; the same point is worked at Step 13). Note carefully what does *not* follow. The record is not thereby left alone: Step 18's stacker overwrites **every** record index in a selected block, so measured on our HG00438 chr21 run at capacity **0.1** this record **was** rewritten on both strands, alleles **[89, 88] → [35, 64]** (**measured in this project**). So the failure is not that the record escapes editing — it is that **the edit earned no privacy credit, because the record's own rarity never entered the decision.** This is a *"the metric measures something narrower than its name suggests"* finding about the released code, not a claim about the published results.

> **Question** — *"If the utility weight is per record, does rewriting 257 kb cost the same as flipping one base?"* — **in the accounting, essentially yes.** The cost of an edit is `η_j` = the sum of `1/support(v)` over the variants that changed, and `support(v)` counts **non-missing haplotype entries at that record** (see §A.5 and Step 14a) — a record's *length* appears nowhere in it. Measured on the same HG00438 chr21 run at capacity **0.1**: **3,129,810 bp — 3.13 Mb, 6.7% of the chromosome — was rewritten**, across **222** changed alleles longer than 1 kb, the largest single rewrite being **257,485 bp**, while the run is reported as **68,335 of 650,541** allele slots changed, **10.5%** (all **measured in this project**, `docs/bugs.md` and `docs/memory.md`). Because the weight is per record, those 3.13 Mb never enter the accounting on either axis, and **no metric in the evaluation suite is sensitive to them** (**measured in this project**). For our own mechanism this is an open item, not a solved one: whether `φ_t` should be **length-weighted** rather than per-variant is recorded as undecided in `docs/plan.md`, with this 257 kb block named as the reason it was raised. Nothing is measured about our mechanism, which does not exist yet. The full entry is departure (h).

**The share of records involved**, with its denominator stated and its limits stated. Taking each record's span as `[POS, POS + len(REF) − 1]` over all 340,824 chr21 records (all **measured in this project**):

| measure | value |
|---|---|
| records whose `POS` lies inside an **earlier record's span** | **31,487 = 9.2%** — which matches the `LV ≥ 1` nesting fraction exactly |
| …inside a record whose `REF` is ≥ 10 kb | **19,211 = 5.6%** |
| …inside a record whose `REF` is ≥ 100 kb | **4,608 = 1.4%** |
| blocks containing ≥ 1 such overlapped record | **15,540 of 100,757 = 15.4%** |
| variants living in an affected block | **78,937 = 23.2%** |
| HMM-path blocks (≥ 2 anchors) affected | **1,229 of 10,502 = 11.7%** |

The verdict recorded in `docs/memory.md`, quoted because the wording is the finding: *"not documentable as an edge case. Roughly a tenth of records and a quarter of variants-by-block are involved, so block disjointness — which both the knapsack's additive accounting and our FFBS chain assume — fails broadly, not locally."*

> **Caution** — read that **9.2%** for exactly what it measures: **records nested inside another record's span.** It is **not** a count of records whose span crosses a plink block **boundary**, which is a different and larger question. **That count has not been measured in this project.** Do not repurpose 9.2% as if it were the boundary-crossing rate; if you need the boundary rate, it is a measurement we owe. The same is true of a second count: **how many blocks contain both a parent record and one of its own children is unmeasured.**

> **Question** — *"Then can you measure the boundary-crossing rate? That feels like the number this passage actually needs."* — **it is not measured, and asking for it is the right instinct.** What would settle it: take each record's span as `[POS, POS + len(REF) − 1]` — the same definition that produced the **9.2%** figure above — and for each of the **340,824** chr21 records ask whether that span's end runs past the end of the block interval its `POS` landed in. Two reasons it is a fresh measurement and not a re-slice of the table above. It needs `REF` **lengths**, which live in the VCF and are **not** in the array, because Step 9 discarded `REF` — so it must be run against `pangenome.vcf.gz`, not against `simple_blocks_idx.npy`. And it needs a decision about singletons: a variant that fell outside every plink interval was given a block id of its own, so that "block" has no meaningful end, and **88.4% of the 100,757 dictionary entries** are of that kind (**measured in this project**). A second count is missing for the same reason: **how many blocks contain both a parent record and one of its own children is unmeasured.** Until both exist, the honest statement is the one above — nesting is broad, and the boundary rate is unknown.

**What actually breaks: the one assumption a block is supposed to encode.** A block is not a fact about the genome. It is a claim: *everything inside this interval is linked; everything across the boundary is independent.* That claim is the whole reason blocks exist here, and the reason is stronger than it looks — **PanMixer never loads a genetic map.** Searching the pinned checkout, every `recomb` hit is an output filename for the RECOMB conference, and the only genetic-map reference is the unwired `starting_data/scripts/get_genetic_maps.sh`, which has a broken shebang (`!/bin/bash`) and is referenced only by `.gitignore` (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`). Its transition "distance" is a **VCF row index**, not centiMorgans (see departure (b)). With no recombination map anywhere in the pipeline, the LD blocks have to carry the recombination information **structurally**: the per-block HMM treats everything inside a block as one linked unit and everything across blocks as independent. **Blocks are a discretised stand-in for a distance-dependent transition model.**

Now put the nesting measurement against that claim. When a parent record sits in block *b* and its children sit in blocks *b+1, b+2, …*, the mechanism asserts that the parent is independent of its own children — of the same DNA, described a second time at finer resolution. That is the most strongly correlated pair of rows the matrix can contain: the correlation is not statistical, it is definitional. The block structure encodes independence exactly where dependence is total. And it happens the other way too: because a nested variant and its parent are **both rows in the matrix**, any per-row sum over a block **counts the same region twice** (**derived**), so the additive accounting of §A.5 and §A.6 double-counts inside affected blocks.

> **Question** — *"If a parent and its child are both rows, then any per-row sum over a block counts that region twice. Is that only their problem, or is it ours too?"* — **ours too, and `docs/plan.md` records it as an open design item rather than a solved one**, in almost those words: how to avoid double-counting the top-level record against its **2,464** nested children (**measured in this project**) in `u_path` and in any privacy measurement we report. Two related items sit beside it in the same list, and it is worth seeing that all three are the same locus viewed from different angles. Whether `φ_t` should be length-weighted is one (see the callout at the extreme measured case above). The other is the segmentation itself, and that one has since been settled: the chain architecture is **model C** — one chain over the whole chromosome, with the **individual variant** as the unit — so chain positions no longer come from plink intervals at all (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). Note carefully what that does and does not fix. It removes the *block* from the picture, so there is no longer a per-block sum to inflate, and it removes the choice between plink intervals and snarl-derived segments as a live design question. It does **not** remove the parent/child double-count, because a top-level record and its nested children are still separate rows of the pangenome matrix and therefore still separate chain positions. The snarl-tree option was the one that would have dissolved the double-count outright, by making positions disjoint by construction; C does not do that, so the double-count remains open on its own terms, over the **15.4% of blocks** and **23.2% of variants** measured above. **The size of the double-count — how much any reported sum is inflated by it — is not measured in `docs/`**, so there is no number to put on either side of that trade yet.

> **Question** — *"So does a block boundary have to be drawn around the parent, or do the children get their own blocks?"* — **the children get their own, and nobody chose that.** Three facts compose to produce it, and it is worth separating them.
>
> 1. **plink never sees the pangenome.** Step 8 runs `plink --blocks` on the **reference panel's** variants. The boundaries are a property of the panel — its build, its sample set, its own variant positions — computed with no knowledge that a 44-person pangenome exists. So there is no mechanism by which a boundary *could* be drawn around a pangenome parent record: the procedure that draws boundaries has never been shown that record.
> 2. **This step then bins pangenome positions into whatever intervals came out**, one variant at a time, by `POS` alone. The parent is one variant. Each child is another. No step relates them, because `LV` and `PS` — the only fields that say "this row is inside that row" — were discarded at Step 9.
> 3. **Therefore parent and children are assigned independently.** A child three bases downstream of its parent will usually land in the parent's block; a child 200 kb downstream, inside a 257 kb parent, will not. Which happens is decided by the panel's boundary positions, which is to say by an external dataset's LD structure.
>
> **What that means for a chain.** The per-block HMM of Step 14 builds a chain over the positions inside one block. A parent and a distant child of its own therefore end up as positions in **two different chains**, each sampled independently, with no term anywhere in either model expressing that one is contained in the other. And within a single chain containing both, they appear as two independent emissions. Neither the scoring branch nor the sampling branch has a representation for containment. There is no code to inspect here, because there is no code: **PanMixer does not model the nesting at all**, and the honest answer to "how does PanMixer handle this?" is that it does not.

**And then what does the released file actually say?** This is where the two threads meet, and the answer is more mechanical — and more unsettling — than the question suggests. Step 18's stacker does `new_variants = original_haplotypes.copy()` and then, for every selected `(block, strand)`, overwrites **the record indices in that block** from the presampled candidate (**measured in this project**, `stacker.py:37-70`). The output is `new_haplotypes.npy` of shape `(n_sites, 2)` — **one allele index per record**.

> **Defect** — **there is no sequence-assembly step and no coherence check anywhere in the pipeline.** A long parent record and the records inside its span are decided **independently**: if the parent's block is not selected the parent keeps the target's **true** allele, while children in a selected block **are replaced**. The released VCF then **asserts both at once** — this haplotype carries the parent's original 257 kb allele, *and* carries replacement alleles at child records inside it. Whether those per-row assignments jointly correspond to any walk through the graph is **never computed, never checked, and never asked** (**derived**, from `docs/memory.md`). Note the framing: this is what the released code does, not a claim that the paper's reported numbers are wrong — we have not reproduced their figures.
>
> So the answer to "do we keep the whole original long variant and tack the changed block on the end, or do we cycle through and change only the part in the changed block?" is **neither**. Nothing is tacked on and nothing is spliced, because the release is not a sequence — it is one allele index per row, written back into the VCF's genotype column at Step 19. The incoherence is not resolved in favour of one answer; it is simply present in the file, for a downstream tool to trip over.

**Two more questions this step tends to raise.**

> **Question** — *"88.4% of entries are singletons, and you say most of those are variants that fell outside every plink interval. That is not 'this variant has no LD partners', it is 'the panel had nothing to say about it'. Are we treating ignorance as independence?"* — **yes, and the primer should say so plainly.** The two sources of singleton — *an interval containing exactly one pangenome variant* and *a variant inside no interval at all, given a block id of its own* — are not separated in any measurement this project has made, and the text above says as much. They are different claims: the first is "the panel says these are unlinked", the second is "the panel never mentioned this position". Both become a **T = 1 chain**, and a T = 1 chain is a positive assertion of independence. Under the shipped tool this affects little, because a singleton block takes the allele-frequency branch anyway. Under **v1**, which routes every block through one sampler with singletons as **T = 1** (`docs/plan.md`), it becomes a modelling choice we are making, so it needs to be a declared one.

> **Question** — *"'10.4% of entries contain ≥ 2 anchors and hold 71.4% of all variants' — 10.4% of the 100,757 entries and 71.4% of the 340,824 variants? Say both denominators."* — correct on both, and yes: **always both.** The percentages in this step run over two different populations and the gap between them is more than threefold. **88.4%** is a share of **dictionary entries** (89,087 of 100,757); those same singletons are **26.1%** of **variants** (89,087 of 340,824, **derived**; the same pair is worked in §2.4.4). **10.4%** is a share of entries; the **71.4%** those entries hold is a share of variants. Writing "88.4% of variants are singletons" is wrong by more than 3× and contradicts the 71.4% figure in the same paragraph. For v1 the same structure is stated as **11,670** multi-variant blocks plus **89,087** singletons = **100,757** chains covering all **340,824** variants (**measured in this project**, `docs/plan.md`).

### Step 13 — Compute allele frequencies
**File:** `scripts/src/get_af.py`
**Input:** `pangenome.npy`, `1000g_phased.npy`, `PG.npy`, both mapping pickles, `num_alleles.npy`.
**What happens:** for each pangenome site *i*, a three-way priority, documented in the file's own docstring as "the 1000g phased dataset is the most reliable, the alignments dataset second, the pangenome least":
1. if *i* maps into the phased panel, count alleles **from the panel only**;
2. else if *i* maps into PanGenie, count from PanGenie **and** from the pangenome;
3. else (a **novel** variant, in neither), count from the pangenome only, then pad the reference-allele count so the row sums to `2 × (N_panel + N_pangenome)`.

Then divide each row by its own sum.
**Output:** `chr{N}/allele_frequencies.npy`, shape `(340824, max_allele + 1)` float64 — `(340824, 90)` in our build, i.e. up to 90 distinct alleles at a single site.
**Expect:** every row sums to 1; the assertion that no probability mass sits beyond `num_alleles[i]` holds for every site.
**Why it matters:** this array *is* the population model. Case 3 deserves attention: a variant that nobody outside the graph has ever reported is given a frequency with a *large* denominator — on our 30x GRCh38 rebuild, 2 × (3,202 + 44) = **6,492**; on the shipped Phase 3 panel it would be 2 × (2,504 + 44) = 5,096 — so it comes out very rare, so its −log is very large, so the optimizer prioritises it. That padding is deliberate and conservative, and on the **allele-frequency branch** it does exactly what it is meant to. An earlier version of this primer generalised it into "the mechanism by which novel variants and under-represented populations get protected first". **That generalisation is retracted**; the correction is recorded in `docs/memory.md` as Correction 8.

> **Defect** — a generous frequency only buys protection if the score is ever **read**. On the **HMM branch** (§A.3, Step 14b) the forward algorithm walks the block's **anchors only**, so a record that is not an anchor has its `−log f_v` computed here and then discarded there. This is a *"the metric measures something narrower than its name suggests"* finding about the released code, not a claim about the published results.
>
> **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, Correction 8): the 257 kb hypervariable record at row **51791** gives HG00438 two alleles each of frequency **1.540e-4**, i.e. **8.78 nats** apiece — the most identifying record on chr21. **That value is never used.** The record is not an anchor, and its block **1639** holds **92 records with 82 anchors**, so the block takes the HMM branch. And any edit that does reach the record is incidental rather than earned: Step 18's stacker overwrites **every** record index in a selected block, and measured on our HG00438 chr21 run at capacity **0.1** this record **was** rewritten on both strands, alleles **[89, 88] → [35, 64]** (**measured in this project**) — **8.78 nats** of identifiability edited away without a single nat of it appearing in the score that chose to edit it. This is a **different** failure from the `−log 0` zeroing discussed at Step 14b, and the difference matters: that zeroing branch **does not fire on our data at all** — of the **319,092** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a called allele, **zero** have `f_v = 0` (**measured in this project**), so it is a latent, panel-dependent defect. The non-anchor exclusion described here **does** fire, on the most identifying record on the chromosome.

**Caution:** in cases 2 and 3 the counts **include the target**. A model that includes the person it is scoring is not a population model in the sense §A.4 needs (see departure (c) for the same error in a more damaging place). Read that as a caution about **their** construction and about our **head-to-head arm**, where a cohort member stands in as target. It is not a hazard the mechanism inherits: under model C there is no allele-frequency table in the mechanism, and our target is external to every dataset that could be counted.

**The three branches in code order, with their measured sizes.** The priority above is a strict `if / elif / else` in `get_af.py:27-78`, evaluated per variant, and the ordering is by the file's own stated notion of **reliability** — not by relevance to the object being released. Configurations across the columns, properties down the rows:

| property | **branch 1** — variant matches the 1000G map | **branch 2** — matches PanGenie, not 1000G | **branch 3** — novel (neither) |
|---|---|---|---|
| where the counts come from | the **1000G panel only** | **PanGenie** plus **all 88 pangenome haplotypes** | the **pangenome only**, then the `REF` count padded up to `NOVEL_DENOMINATOR` |
| chr21 variants taking this branch (**measured in this project**) | **258,610** | **25,325** | **56,889** |
| share of 340,824 records (**derived**) | **75.9%** | **7.4%** | **16.7%** |
| is the **target's own allele** counted? | **not** under Phase 3 (0 of 44 HPRC donors in the panel); **yes** under our 30x GRCh38 repin (39 of 44, including HG00438) | **yes**, via the pangenome counts (`get_af.py:41-53`) | **yes**, via the pangenome counts (`get_af.py:68-73`) |
| median `−log f_v` where HG00438 carries a **non-`REF`** allele (**measured in this project**) | **0.63 nats** | **0.43 nats** | **5.41 nats** |
| what the branch is sensitive to | the panel's build — see departure (a) | PanGenie's callset membership | nothing external at all |

> **Question** — *"Branch 1 counts from the panel only, and you told me the 30x repin put 39 of 44 HPRC donors into the panel. So branch 1 includes the target. Is that departure (c) wearing a different hat, and did we do it to ourselves?"* — **yes to both, and the second half is the part that matters.** It is the same class of error as departure (c) — counting the target in the population the target is being scored against — turning up in a third place (the third is the support counts of Step 14a). **Measured in this project:** **0 of 44** HPRC donors appear in the shipped Phase 3 panel, so for PanMixer the self-inclusion is confined to branches 2 and 3, exactly as the `**Caution**` at case 3 says; **39 of 44**, including `HG00438` — the cohort member our head-to-head arm uses as a stand-in target — appear in the 30x GRCh38 panel we substituted, so under our repin it reaches **all three** branches. That breaks the externality the head-to-head arm exists to simulate, and **we introduced it** by repinning the panel — it is not inherited from PanMixer. It is not a hazard to the deployed mechanism, where the target is external to `G`, `D` and every panel and standing constraint 1 therefore holds by construction, and where under model C no allele-frequency table is read at all. The direction of the error never varies: including a person in the population makes that person look **less** surprising, so the score comes out too small. **The magnitude has not been measured for the frequency table** — only for departure (c) — so do not carry a number across. The full statement and the required fix are in the `Caution` at the end of this step.

**The novel denominator, worked.** `NOVEL_DENOMINATOR = 2 × (N_1000G + N_pangenome)`. On our 30x GRCh38 rebuild that is `2 × (3,202 + 44)` = **6,492** (**derived**); under the shipped Phase 3 panel it would be `2 × (2,504 + 44)` = **5,096**. Always say which panel. A novel allele carried by exactly **one** haplotype therefore gets `f_v = 1 / 6,492` = **1.54e-4** — **not 1** — whose self-information is `−log(1/6,492)` = **8.78 nats** (both **derived**; **8.54 nats** under Phase 3). The padding exists to stop a novel allele from looking *common* merely because the only people counted were its own carriers: the `REF` allele absorbs all the remaining mass.

> **Question** — *"Case 3 pads the reference count so the row sums to 2 × (N_panel + N_pangenome). Is that a prior? It looks like adding fake reference-carrying people. Does it have a name?"* — "adding fake reference-carrying people" is a fair description of what the arithmetic does, and it behaves like a conservative **pseudo-count**, the standard device of adding fictitious observations to a count table before dividing. What makes this instance unusual is that it is **asymmetric**: all the added mass goes to the **reference** allele rather than being spread over the alleles. Its effect is deliberate and one-directional: it forces a variant nobody outside the graph has reported to be scored as **rare**, which makes its `−log f_v` large, which makes the optimizer of §A.6 want it. The `docs/` ledger records it as padding rather than as a named statistical smoothing scheme, and no smoothing scheme is named in the code, so do not attach a textbook name beyond the generic one. §A.9.3 works the size of it: on the 30x panel roughly **98.6%** of a novel row's mass is padding rather than observation (**derived**).

**A caveat about the branch-3 medians that is easy to misread.** The **5.41 nats** in the table is conditioned on the target carrying a **non-`REF`** allele. At novel sites the target usually carries `REF`, with a median `f_v` of **0.998**, so **most novel sites contribute approximately 0 nats** (**measured in this project**). The signal is concentrated in the novel sites where the target carries a non-`REF` allele, of which there are **11,760** on chr21 (**measured in this project**). One number is the size of the effect where it exists; the other is how often it exists.

> **Question** — *"Branch 3's median is 5.41 nats against 0.63 and 0.43. Does that mean the optimizer spends most of its budget on novel variants?"* — **you cannot read that off these three numbers, and we have not measured it.** Three separate reasons, and they stack. First, the medians are **per site** and conditioned on the target carrying a non-`REF` allele, whereas the optimizer buys **blocks** and scores them with `ε_j`, a per-block quantity (see §A.4) — a block with one high-scoring novel site and forty ordinary ones is not a high-scoring block. Second, a branch-3 value only reaches `ε_j` at all when its block takes the **allele-frequency branch**; in a block that takes the HMM branch, a non-anchor's `−log f_v` is computed here and discarded there (see the `Defect` above), and a branch-3 variant is **always** a non-anchor (see the next callout) — so the largest per-site scores in the table are precisely the ones most at risk of never being read (**derived**). Third, and simply: **whether the blocks the optimizer selects are enriched for branch-3 sites has not been measured in this project.** What is measured is the size of the population — **56,889** branch-3 variants (**measured in this project**), **16.7%** of the 340,824 records (**derived**), of which **11,760** are sites where HG00438 carries a non-`REF` allele (**measured in this project**). What would settle the question is cross-tabulating the selected blocks from one run against the branch each of their variants took.

**"No anchors" does not mean "frequency 1".** This deserves its own paragraph because the two words sound related and are not.

> **Definition** — **"is an anchor"** and **"has population frequency data"** are two independent properties of a variant. An **anchor** is a pangenome record that also appears in the **PanGenie `bi_all`** callset, matched exactly on (`POS`, `REF`, whole-`ALT`-string) — it is a membership test that decides whether a site may be an **HMM chain position** (see §A.3, §A.8.1, departure (d)). **Having a frequency** is decided by the table above, whose first branch keys on the **1000G** map, a different dataset entirely.
>
> **Measured in this project:** **10,460 non-anchor variants still receive 1000G frequencies.** So a block with **zero anchors** can be fully populated with real external frequencies, and its `ε_j` is a genuine sum of per-site self-information over real population data, not a degenerate value. Nothing anywhere in this pipeline sets a frequency to 1 because a site is not an anchor; a frequency of 1 would require the allele to be the only one observed in whichever population the branch counted, which is a statement about the data and not about anchor status.

> **Question** — *"Can a variant be an anchor and novel at the same time? If the anchor set and branch 3 both consult the pangenome, that combination sounds like it ought to be possible."* — **no, never, and it falls straight out of the code.** Branch 3 fires only when the record is in **neither** mapping — the guard is literally `if i not in mappings_pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased and i not in mappings_pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments` (**measured in this project**, `get_af.py:27-78`). The anchor test consults **the same pickle** as branch 2, `pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments.pickle`, which despite its name is the **PanGenie** map, not a 1000G one (see Step 10 and departure (d)). So every anchor is in that mapping by definition, and therefore every anchor takes **branch 1 or branch 2** and can **never** receive the padded `NOVEL_DENOMINATOR` (**derived**). The implication runs one way only: **anchor ⇒ not novel**, but **not novel ⇏ anchor** — the **10,460** non-anchor variants above are the counterexample to the converse (**measured in this project**). Worth holding onto, because it is the hinge for the two failure modes in this step: the **56,889** branch-3 variants (**measured in this project**; **16.7%** of the 340,824 records, **derived**) carry the largest per-site scores and are guaranteed non-anchors, so their scores survive into `ε_j` only in blocks that take the allele-frequency branch (**derived**).

The remaining questions about this step are all about the priority order itself — why it is ordered that way, and what happens when its first branch stops firing.

> **Question** — *"Why is the panel the most reliable source, when the pangenome is the thing being released and the panel does not even contain most of these variants?"* — the file's own docstring gives the rationale verbatim: *"the 1000g phased dataset is the most reliable, the alignments dataset second, the pangenome least"*. The honest counter is that this is a priority by **reliability**, not by **relevance**: the panel has thousands of samples and a mature callset, so its frequencies are better estimated — but it fails to match roughly **24%** of pangenome records even under the correct build (**derived**, as the complement of the **75.88%** strict match rate **measured in this project** at Step 10), and what it is missing is expected to be exactly the graph-only and structural material that makes this a pangenome in the first place. "Expected to be" is doing work in that sentence: **that residual has not been characterised in this project and should not be assumed benign** — it is an open question. So the ordering gives its best estimates to its best-behaved sites, which is not wrong; it is just worth knowing which way it points.

> **Question** — *"If the frequency table is built from a build-mismatched panel, branch 1 almost never fires and nearly everything falls to branch 2 or 3. Does the shipped run's frequency table become effectively 'pangenome-only'?"* — yes, and the mechanism is precisely departure (a). Branch 1 fires only where the (`POS`, `REF`, `ALT`) join succeeded at Step 10. **Measured in this project:** that join yields **258,610** matches against the same-build 30x GRCh38 panel and **1,025** against the Phase 3 GRCh37 panel the released script downloads — a **252× gap** (**derived**). With 1,025 matches, branch 1 covers about **0.30%** of records and essentially every variant falls to branch 2 or branch 3 — both of which count the pangenome, and branch 3 counts nothing else. Nothing errors when this happens; the frequencies simply become a 44-person estimate wearing a 2,504-person denominator. Framing, as always: this is the released pipeline as documented, and we have **not** reproduced the paper's figures.

**The scored individual is counted in its own frequencies, and our repin made it worse.** The `**Caution**` at case 3 above says the counts include the target in cases 2 and 3. Two things must be added to that, and the second is ours.

> **Caution** — the code comment at branch 1 asserts *"None of the pangenome subjects appear in 1000g_phased"*. That is **true for Phase 3** — **0 of 44** HPRC donors overlap — and **false for the panel we substituted**: **39 of 44** HPRC donors are in the 30x GRCh38 panel, **including `HG00438`, the individual our benchmark uses as a stand-in target** (both **measured in this project**). So our rebuilt `allele_frequencies.npy` includes that individual's own alleles in the frequencies used to score it, on **all three** branches rather than two. For PanMixer that would be a stronger version of an existing self-inclusion. For the **head-to-head arm** — the one place a cohort member has to impersonate an external target — it breaks the externality that arm exists to simulate, and it is a break **we introduced** by repinning the panel. It is **not** a defect of the deployed mechanism, where the target is external to `G`, `D` and every panel, so standing constraint 1 holds by construction; and under **model C** the mechanism reads no allele-frequency table at all (project decision, `docs/plan.md`). It has **no visible symptom** — the frequencies look entirely normal. The required fix, in the comparison arm, is that any leave-one-out cohort must drop the stand-in from the **1000G panel** as well as from the pangenome. (One side effect worth knowing, because it explains a measurement elsewhere: with the target always counted, its allele's frequency is never 0 — **0 of 319,092** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a called allele have `f_v = 0`, **measured in this project** — which is why the `#FIX ME` zeroing branch discussed at Step 14b never fires on our data.)

> **Key idea** — counting the target in the population it is being scored against is a **leave-one-out** problem, and it is the *same* problem in **three** places in this tool, which is why it is worth naming as a class rather than as three bugs. It is here, in the frequency table (branches 2 and 3 always, branch 1 under our repin). It is in the **support counts** of Step 14a (`obfuscate.py:208`). And it is in the forward algorithm's own **emission panel** (`obfuscate.py:130,132`), which is departure (c). The direction of the error never varies: including a person in the population makes that person look **less** surprising, so every privacy score comes out **too small**.
>
> The size of it has been measured for the third of those three — see departure (c) for the full numbers, including the **78.1 nat** leave-one-out maximum and the **17.4×** worst understatement on ceiling-bound blocks. **The equivalent measurement has not been made for the frequency table or for the support counts**; we know the direction, not the magnitude, and should not imply otherwise.
>
> **What this means for us, concretely.** The whole leave-one-out question is **comparison-arm only**: it arises solely because the head-to-head has to make a cohort member impersonate an external target. The deployed mechanism has nothing to hold out — `K = 88`, and no panel, frequency table or support count ever saw the person being protected. Within that arm the correction must be applied in **both** halves, or the comparison measures the correction instead of the mechanisms. The standing recommendation in `docs/plan.md` is to report **both arms leave-one-out** *and* PanMixer's **as-published** numbers side by side, so the cost of the correction is visible rather than silently absorbed into one arm. Note also the hard limit on how far leave-one-out can be taken on PanMixer's data at all: the PGGB graph **topology** was built from all HPRC assemblies, so even a full leave-one-out only **approximates** our external-target threat model there. Never claim full externality on their data.

> **Open question** — one number a reader will want here does **not** exist. There is **no measured distribution of `ε_j` for multi-variant, zero-anchor blocks** — the branch where `ε_j` really is a plain sum of per-site `−log f_v` over whatever the table above supplied. The *count* of such blocks is derivable, at **716** chr21 blocks or **0.71% of the 100,757 entries** (§A.9.1), but how many **variants** they hold and how much privacy value they carry are both unmeasured. If you want to know what the allele-frequency branch scores look like in aggregate, that is a measurement this project has not made.

### Step 14 — Precompute per-block privacy, utility, and the sampled replacement
**Files:** `scripts/src/get_support_and_pmi.py` with `scripts/src/hmm.py`. This is the heart of the tool. Run as a SLURM array of 968 tasks = 44 subjects × 22 chromosomes.
**Input:** `pangenome.npy`, `allele_frequencies.npy`, `blocks_dict.json`, `pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments.pickle`, and a subject id.

It does four things.

#### Step 14a — The utility vector

In code, `not_missing = np.sum(pangenome != -1, axis=(0,2))`. In words: **for each site, count how many of the 88 haplotype slots have a called allele** — the sum runs over the sample axis and the haplotype axis, leaving one number per site. Then `utility_loss[s] = 1/not_missing[s]` where that is positive, else 0. Output `utility_loss.npy`, shape `(340824,)`. This is the `1/support(v)` of §A.5. Note precisely what is counted: **haplotypes with any called allele**, not haplotypes carrying a particular allele.

#### Step 14b — Per-block privacy (`pmi_blocks`)

First a per-site self-information. The code one-hot encodes the target's allele and multiplies by `−log(AF)`; in words, **for each site, pick out the −log frequency of the specific allele the target carries at that site**. Missing sites get 0. Then per block:
- block has exactly 1 variant → sum the per-site values;
- block has ≥2 variants but **0 anchors** → sum the per-site values;
- otherwise → `−forward_algorithm(haplotype, block)` for each of the two haplotypes.

Output `subjects/{id}/pmi_blocks.npy`, shape `(num_blocks, 2)` float64. Column *h* is the privacy value of obfuscating haplotype *h* of that block.

**Worth noticing while reading — and it is worth more than a note, because the two branch tests are not the same test.** Step 14b decides which blocks get an HMM-derived privacy score. Step 14c, further down the same file, decides which blocks get an HMM-drawn *replacement*. Those are two separate `if` statements with two different conditions, and reading them as one condition is the commonest way to misread this file. Here they are side by side.

> **Definition** — *anchor*, restated because the condition turns on it: a pangenome VCF record that also appears in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset, matched exactly on `(POS, REF, ALT)` (`get_mappings.py:45-49`). Measured in this project: **273,475** of chr21's **340,824** records are anchors. See §A.3 and §A.8.1 for the definition and departure (d) for why the set is PanGenie rather than the 1000 Genomes set the published Methods name.

| what the code tests | where | the branch taken when the block meets it |
|---|---|---|
| **Scoring** (`pmi_blocks`): the block has **≥ 2 variants** and **at least one** anchor | `get_support_and_pmi.py:87` | `−forward_algorithm(...)` over the block's anchors |
| **Sampling** (`new_haplotypes`): the block has **≥ 2 variants** and **two or more** anchors | `get_support_and_pmi.py:159` | `sample_block_prior`, the Li–Stephens chain over the block's anchors |

Both conditions are as the released code tests them, on the two line numbers given (measured in this project; `docs/bugs.md`, dated 2026-09-25). What `docs/` records is the **condition and the line number**, not the literal source expression, so the conditions are stated above in words rather than quoted as code. Note what the scoring row requires that a careless reading drops: **two or more variants**. A block holding exactly one variant is scored from allele frequencies however many anchors it contains (Step 14b, first bullet).

**They are not complements, and here is the table that shows it.** Write out every shape a block can have and mark which branch each side takes.

| block shape | scored by | sampled by | agree? |
|---|---|---|---|
| exactly 1 variant | allele frequencies (Step 14b, first bullet) | allele frequencies | yes |
| ≥ 2 variants, **0** anchors | allele frequencies (Step 14b, second bullet) | allele frequencies | yes |
| ≥ 2 variants, **exactly 1** anchor | **the HMM** | **allele frequencies** | **no** |
| ≥ 2 variants, ≥ 2 anchors | the HMM | the HMM | yes |

The third row is the whole finding. A block with exactly one anchor is **priced** by the Li–Stephens forward algorithm and **filled** by a site-independent draw from allele frequencies. The price is computed for a kind of replacement the block never receives.

> **Defect** — the scoring and sampling branches disagree about the anchor threshold. **Measured in this project** on chr21 (`docs/bugs.md`, dated 2026-09-25): **10,954** blocks are scored by the HMM and **10,502** are sampled by the HMM, so **452 blocks** are scored one way and sampled the other. Severity is recorded as **MEDIUM**. An earlier version of this note said "nothing depends on this being wrong"; **that is retracted** — it is now a registered departure with a measured blast radius, written up in full as departure (f). **Why that reasoning failed:** it assumed the two guards were complements of each other, so that whichever branch a block took, it took on both sides. They are not complements. For a block with two or more variants and **exactly one** anchor, the booked privacy score is a **forward-algorithm value** over that single anchor while the released alleles come from an **independent allele-frequency draw** at every variant in the block — so the score and the release are answers computed under two different models, and something does depend on it: for those 452 blocks, no statement of the form "this block's privacy score reflects the haplotype structure of what we released" is true. Note the shape of the finding: the published Methods describe **one** threshold — *"dependent on the number of top-level SNPs that exist inside the LD block. If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM"* (quoted from the published paper, via `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25) — and the released code tests two different ones on two different lines. This is a released-code-versus-published-Methods finding. It is not a claim that the paper's reported results are wrong, and we have reproduced none of them.

> **Question** — *"Why is there an allele-frequency branch at all? If the HMM is the better model, why not run it on every block and delete the other path?"* Because the HMM as PanMixer defines it is a chain **over the block's anchors** (§A.3), so its positions come from the anchor set rather than from the block: a block with no anchors gives the chain nothing to step over, and the allele-frequency draw is the fallback for it. The only rationale the published Methods offer is the one quoted in the Defect above — *"dependent on the number of top-level SNPs that exist inside the LD block. If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM"* (quoted from the published paper, via `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25) — which is a claim about how many positions are enough, and it is the **sampling** threshold. It does not explain the scoring side's ≥ 1, and `docs/` records no rationale anywhere for the two thresholds differing; no such reason has been found in the paper or in the code's own comments. But note where your question really lands: the branch exists **because anchors exist**. It is the escape hatch for blocks the anchor set leaves empty. Remove the anchor concept and there is nothing left for it to catch — which is exactly the route v1 takes (see the Note that closes this passage; and §A.3, §A.8 and departure (d) for why anchors were introduced in the first place, which is the question to answer before deleting them).

Two arithmetic observations, both **derived**, that make those three numbers easier to hold.

- **10,954 − 10,502 = 452** exactly, and the subtraction is meaningful rather than coincidental *because* of the two conditions. "≥ 2 variants and ≥ 2 anchors" is strictly stricter than "≥ 2 variants and ≥ 1 anchor", so every block the HMM samples is a block the HMM also scores: the sampled-HMM set is a **subset** of the scored-HMM set. Containment follows from the conditions; the subtraction then simply counts the gap, and the gap is exactly the `≥ 2 variants, exactly 1 anchor` row above.
- The "**10.4% of entries** on the HMM path, holding **71.4% of variants**" figure that §A.2 and §1.1.4 both quote is defined at **≥ 2 anchors**, which is the *sampling* threshold: **10,502 / 100,757 = 10.42%** (derived). The scoring side is larger: **10,954 / 100,757 = 10.87%** (derived). When you see "the HMM path" in this document it means the sampling side unless the sentence says otherwise.

> **Question** — *"How many blocks on our chr21 fall in that gap, and are they big or small blocks?"* The count is the **452** above (measured in this project). The **size distribution of those 452 blocks has not been measured**, so nothing can be said here about whether they are variant-rich or variant-poor. Neither has the downstream effect: we do not know how many of the 452 actually produced a different released allele than they would have under a consistent threshold. Both are gaps in `docs/`, not omissions from this paragraph.

> **Question** — *"The first row of that table says singleton blocks agree. But Step 14d charges a singleton's utility cost **unconditionally**, whether or not the redrawn allele differed, while a multi-variant block is charged only at the sites that actually differ. Isn't that a second disagreement, hiding inside the row you marked 'yes'?"* Yes — and it is a different disagreement from the one above. For a singleton the **routing** agrees; the **charging** does not. The table compares which mechanism prices the block and which fills it; it says nothing about how the bill is written, and Step 14d writes it two ways. Two things follow, and both are **derived** from the charging rules rather than measured.
> - A singleton is charged `1/support(v)` even when the draw came back identical, so its `η_j` is at or above the honest cost of what it changed, and its value-per-unit-cost — the only quantity the LP ranks on (§A.6) — is at or below its honest ratio. A multi-variant block is never over-charged this way. So the two conventions bias the knapsack in **opposite** directions on the two block shapes: against singletons, in favour of multi-variant blocks.
> - The free lunch of Step 17 therefore cannot arise on a singleton at all. A singleton whose site has any called allele anywhere in the cohort carries a strictly positive `1/support(v)`, hence `η_j > 0`; and a site with no called allele at all gives both `utility_loss = 0` and a zeroed per-site privacy value, so there is no credit to book either. Every move charged **exactly** `η_j = 0` while carrying `ε_j > 0` must be a multi-variant block — which means the **14,526 of 47,070** no-op moves measured at capacity 0.1 (measured in this project) all sit in the multi-variant population. `docs/` does not break those 14,526 down by block shape, so read this as a consequence of the rules, not as a measurement.
>
> What has not been established either way is whether the charging bias changes the LP's **selection**. The affected population is large — **89,087 of 100,757** entries are singletons, holding **26.1%** of the 340,824 variants (measured in this project) — but the run has not been re-solved under a single consistent charging rule, and that re-solve is what would settle it.

> **Note** — what this means for our own design. The question is removed rather than answered, and the decided chain architecture removes it more completely than the earlier spec did. That spec routed **every** block through **one** sampler, with a single-variant block treated as a chain of length `T = 1` rather than as a special case, and **no allele-frequency branch existing in the source at all** (`A-IMP-v1-sampler` REPLACE item 2, `docs/plan.md`). Under **model C** there are no blocks to route: one chain over the whole chromosome, the **individual variant** as the unit, no singleton case, no `T = 1` chains and no per-block `Z_b` (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). Exit gate 2's surviving half is the part that still tests something — **no allele-frequency branch may exist in the source**. A defect that arises from two `if` statements having to agree is best fixed by deleting one of them; C deletes both.

**A second thing worth noticing**, because an earlier draft of this primer got it wrong and the retraction is instructive. An allele whose frequency comes out exactly 0 gives `−log 0 = +inf`. The code detects values above 10²⁰⁰ and sets them to **0** — the minimum — rather than the maximum, and the pipeline copy carries a literal `#FIX ME` comment at that line (`get_support_and_pmi.py:65-74`). An earlier version of this section concluded from that code path that "PanMixer assigns the most identifying sites on the chromosome a privacy score of exactly zero". **That was wrong as a statement about what runs, and is retracted**; the retraction is recorded in `docs/memory.md` as Correction 7, and the full argument is §A.4.3.

**Measured in this project** across all **319,092** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a called allele: **zero** have `f_v = 0`, so the `#FIX ME` branch **never fires** on our data. The reason is that the target's own allele is counted in every branch of the frequency table of Step 13 — through the pangenome counts in branches 2 and 3, and, under our **30x GRCh38 panel** repin, through the panel itself in branch 1, because **39 of 44** HPRC donors including HG00438 appear in that panel.

> **Defect** — the zeroing is **latent** and its trigger is **panel-dependent**. Under the shipped **Phase 3 panel**, where **0 of 44** HPRC donors appear (measured in this project), a branch-1 variant carrying an allele no 1000 Genomes sample carries would give `f_v = 0` and would fire the branch. So the defect is real in the released code and simply does not reach on the panel we substituted. Which panel you run decides whether you ever see it.

The separate question — whether the most identifying records on the chromosome really do contribute nothing — has a different and more interesting answer, and it is **yes, by a completely different mechanism**: non-anchor exclusion, not clamping. See §A.4.3 for the worked case and departure (i) for the full entry.

#### Step 14c — Sample a replacement for every block, both haplotypes

Start from an array filled with −1.
- Singleton blocks, or blocks with ≤1 anchor: draw each variant's new allele independently from that site's normalised allele-frequency row.
- Otherwise: `sample_block_prior` walks the Li–Stephens chain over the block's anchors, picking a start donor uniformly among the **86** non-target haplotypes and then stepping donor to donor; anchor sites take the chosen donor's allele; non-anchor sites are meant to inherit from the donor chosen at the nearest anchor.

Output `subjects/{id}/new_haplotypes.npy`, shape `(340824, 2)`. **Only one candidate is generated per block per haplotype** — this is not a menu the optimizer chooses from, it is a single take-it-or-leave-it offer. The script raises `RuntimeError("OBSFUCATION FAILED")` if the candidate is byte-identical to the original everywhere.

Two consequences of "one unconditional draw", both of which matter later. First, the draw is made **without comparing to the original** — there is no rejection loop forcing the candidate to differ, despite the paper describing such a constraint. Second, therefore, a move can be selected, charged a privacy credit ε_j > 0, and change nothing at all, at zero utility cost. That is a free lunch the LP will happily take.

**Measured in this project** on chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1, seed 123 (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24): of **47,070** selected moves, **14,526 changed nothing — 30.9%**.

> **Caution** — two different denominators, and they must not be swapped. **30.9% is the share of selected *moves*.** The share of the reported privacy total those moves carry is **7.0%**, not 31%; an earlier reading of this finding quoted 31% as the inflation of the privacy total, and the follow-up in `docs/memory.md` (2026-09-24) corrects it. The correct sentence is the pair: *30.9% of selected moves change nothing, and those moves carry 7.0% of the reported privacy credit.* The full accounting, including why 30.9% of moves carry only 7.0% of the credit, is in §A.6; the departures entry is (g), and the worked block is the trace after Step 19.

#### Step 14d — Per-block utility cost (`support_blocks`)

For a multi-variant block: sum `utility_loss[s]` over the sites where the candidate **actually differs** from the original, separately per haplotype. For a single-variant block it assigns that site's `utility_loss` to both columns unconditionally — i.e. a singleton's cost is charged whether or not the resampled allele happened to equal the original.
Output `subjects/{id}/support_blocks.npy`, shape `(num_blocks, 2)` float64.

**Expect if all is well:** `pmi_blocks` and `support_blocks` have identical shapes and are elementwise non-negative (the optimizer asserts both). `new_haplotypes.npy` differs from the original at a healthy fraction of called sites.
**Why it matters:** after this step the biology is gone. What remains is two arrays of numbers — a value vector and a weight vector — and a pre-computed answer for every move. Everything downstream is arithmetic.

### Step 15 — Total utility normaliser
**File:** `scripts/src/get_total_utility_loss.py`
**Input:** every subject's `utility_loss.npy`, across all 22 chromosomes.
**What happens:** per subject, sums `2 × Σ utility_loss` — the factor 2 because both haplotypes can be edited — and writes one number per subject.
**Output:** `starting_data/utility_loss.json`.
**Why it matters, and a point of genuine confusion the code forces us to state plainly.** Step 15 produces a *genome-wide* per-subject total, but **Step 17's optimizer recomputes its own denominator from the single chromosome it is solving**: `max_utility_loss = 2 × Σ utility_loss` over that chromosome's sites only. So the capacity budget as executed is **per chromosome**, and the genome-wide JSON is a reporting aggregate rather than the constraint the LP sees. Concretely, our chr21 denominator is 9,288.85 — a chr21 number, not a genome number.

**Name discipline, because "maximum utility loss" means two different things.** Call Step 15/17's `2 × Σ utility_loss` the **budget denominator**. Call the paper's *"Removed"* baseline — where the target individual is excluded from the graph entirely, which the published text describes as "the maximum possible utility loss for a given individual" — the **removal baseline**. They are not the same quantity, and the budget denominator is not even achievable: `support_blocks` only charges sites that actually *change*, so total achievable loss is strictly less than the denominator. **This is exactly why the knapsack in Step 17 saturates below its nominal budget**, and that causal link is the thing to carry, not the numbers.


> **Question** — "Two things are called 'maximum utility loss': the budget denominator and the removal baseline. Which one does the paper's utility axis use, and is the published average utility loss of 0.28 on the same scale as the capacity I pass on the command line?"
>
> Keep the two names apart and never let them touch. The **budget denominator** is what this step computes — `2 × Σ_v utility_loss[v]`, i.e. twice the sum of 1/support over every site, which on our chr21 rebuild for HG00438 is **9,288.85** (measured in this project). `capacity` is a fraction of that: at capacity 0.1 the optimizer spent **928.92**, exactly **10.000%** of it (measured in this project). The **removal baseline** is a different object entirely — the paper's "Removed" arm, in which the target is excluded from the graph completely, reported as Wasserstein **0.027** for allele frequencies and **0.017** for LD (quoted from the published paper).
>
> The published **average utility loss of 0.28**, on a scale whose maximum is 1 (quoted from the published paper), is reported at ε_private. **Whether its denominator is the same construction as the capacity denominator has not been established in this project**, and the two are easy to conflate precisely because both are normalised to 1. So: do not read 0.28 as "capacity 0.28", and do not read capacity 0.1 as "utility loss 0.1 on the paper's axis" either. If that correspondence matters for the head-to-head it has to be established, not assumed.

> **Question** — "The denominator is not achievable, so capacity 1.0 would not actually rewrite everything. What capacity *does* correspond to 'rewrite every block'?"
>
> None of them, and that is structural rather than a tuning problem. At capacity **0.5** the optimizer spends only **3,077.60 of the 9,288.85** denominator — **33.1%** — across **177,865** selected moves (all **measured in this project**, and exact; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). An earlier version of this answer said the move count and the absolute spend at capacity 0.5 were not recorded in this project; **that is withdrawn** — they were measured in a session and quoted without being logged, and they are logged now. The knapsack **saturated**: once every move with positive value has been bought, there is nothing left to spend the budget on, so raising capacity further changes nothing.
>
> Why some moves are never worth buying is the interesting half. A move's value is ε_j, and ε_j is **zero** wherever the target's own haplotype is missing, because `get_support_and_pmi.py` zeroes the score there (`pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`, measured in this project). Those same sites cost **more** than average, because support counts only non-missing entries — at `CONFLICT` records the median utility weight is **0.01818** against **0.01136** elsewhere, **1.60×** more expensive (measured in this project). Zero value at above-average cost is the one cell of the table a knapsack will never buy at any budget, so those records are released **verbatim** (§A.10).
>
> Where between 0.1 and 0.5 the budget stops binding has **not been measured in this project**; we have the two endpoints and nothing in between.

### Step 16 — `experiment_starter`
**File:** `tools/common/experiment_starter.py`
**Input:** a capacities file (one float per line) and a subjects file (one sample name per line).
**What happens:** finds the next free `experiments/exp_N/`, takes the cross product subjects × capacities into a row table, attaches demographic columns, and pre-creates `data/chr{1..22}/{row_id}/` plus `data/all/{row_id}/`.
**Output:** `experiments/exp_N/data.csv` and the directory skeleton.
**Expect:** one row per (subject, capacity) pair; baseline modes instead write a single sentinel capacity (0.0 unedited, 1.0 empty, −1.0 remove-unique).
**Why it matters:** `row_id` is the primary key for the entire run — every later stage is addressed by `(chromosome, row_id)`.


> **Question** — "Say we run the head-to-head on their harness. Does a τ sweep fit into it as just another capacities file, or does the harness assume the capacity means utility?"
>
> Mostly the former, with one named change. The harness is generic in the sense that matters: `row_id` is the primary key, every later stage is addressed by `(chromosome, row_id)`, and the row table is just the cross product of subjects and levels with demographic columns attached (measured in this project, reading the code). A τ sweep is the same shape of object as a capacity sweep.
>
> What the harness does assume is the *meaning* of the level column: three values are sentinels for baseline modes — 0.0, 1.0 and −1.0 — and `capacity` is consumed downstream as a fraction of a utility denominator (Step 15). So substituting τ for capacity is a **harness change to name explicitly in the v1 specification**, not a free reinterpretation: the column means something different, the sentinels do not transfer (τ = 0 is a real operating point for us, not a baseline flag), and the reporting contract requires the stamp to carry graph-plus-cohort identity, τ, and utility identity (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).
>
> The reason this is a small change rather than a large one is the shape of the release object, **provided we choose to emit it in PanMixer's format**. If the sampler also writes `new_haplotypes.npy` — the same `(n_sites, 2)` int16 array the stacker emits — every attack and utility evaluator downstream runs unmodified, effectively a third stacker strategy. That emission is **comparison-arm only and optional**. It was once pinned as exit gate 5 of the v1 spec; it is no longer a gate of the mechanism at all, but one option for one comparison, and it now lives under `A-EVL-headtohead` (project decision, `docs/plan.md`). Emitting it buys the **attack** evaluators; it does not make PanMixer's cohort-level utility metrics applicable to our release object (`docs/terms.md`, `new_haplotypes`).

### Step 17 — The optimizer
**File:** `tools/panmixer/optimizer.py`
**Input:** for one (chromosome, row_id): `support_blocks.npy` (weights), `pmi_blocks.npy` (values), `utility_loss.npy`, and the row's capacity.
**What happens:** computes `max_utility_loss = 2 × Σ utility_loss` for that chromosome and `target = capacity × max_utility_loss`. Flattens the two `(num_blocks, 2)` arrays to length 2k, builds a **GLOP** model — GLOP is Google's linear-programming solver, reached through the OR-Tools library — with one **continuous** variable per move in [0,1], objective Σ value·x maximised, one constraint Σ weight·x ≤ target. Solves, exits with an error unless the solver reports `OPTIMAL`, rounds the solution to integers, reshapes.
**Output:** `xsol.npy`, shape `(num_blocks, 2)` int, and `optimizer_stats.json`.
**Expect (measured in this project on HG00438 / chr21, 30x GRCh38 rebuild; `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18, logs `logs/pm_obf.log`):**
- capacity 0.1 → **47,070 moves**, utility loss **928.92 / 9,288.85 = 10.000%** — the constraint is exactly tight, as it must be when the budget binds;
- capacity 0.5 → **177,865 moves**, utility loss **3,077.60 / 9,288.85 = 33.1%** — the knapsack **saturates below its budget**, because once every move with positive value has been taken there is nothing left to buy. This is the Step 15 point made concrete: the budget denominator is not reachable. Both figures on this line are **measured in this project** and exact, from the optimizer's own log line (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26; log `logs/pm_obf.log`, sentinel `logs/pm_obf.status` = 0), as are the two on the capacity-0.1 line above. The same run's stacker reports **106,549 of 649,242 alleles changed = 16.41%** at capacity 0.5, against 68,335 of 650,541 = 10.50% at capacity 0.1 (**measured in this project**); the allele denominators differ slightly between the two runs and the log does not say why.
- Same seed → byte-identical `new_haplotypes.npy` and `xsol.npy`; different seed → different output; **zero** allele-bound violations.

**What the move count does not tell you.** The number to carry away from this step is **47,070 moves at capacity 0.1** (measured in this project), and the natural reading of it — forty-seven thousand edits were installed — is wrong by nearly a third.

> **Defect** — the LP books privacy credit for moves that change nothing. **Measured in this project** on chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1, seed 123 (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24): of the **47,070** selected moves, **14,526 changed nothing at all — 30.9%**. The mechanism is the one already named at Step 14c: the candidate draw never compares itself to the original, so a sampled replacement can come back identical, and when it does `η_j` is **exactly 0.00000** while `ε_j` stays positive. Value over weight is then unbounded, so the LP takes such a move ahead of every real edit. The full accounting is in §A.6, in `docs/bugs.md` and in `docs/memory.md` (2026-09-24), and the departures entry is (g); the worked block is the *Worked trace* section after Step 19. This bullet is the pointer; the Caution below is the one thing you must not get backwards.

> **Caution** — **two denominators, and swapping them restates a figure the ledger has already corrected.** **30.9% is the share of selected *moves*.** The share of the reported privacy *total* those moves carry is **7.0%** (measured in this project at capacity 0.1, on a 3,000-move sample of the 47,070 selected). The two differ because no-ops concentrate on **low**-`ε_j` blocks — common patterns that many donors share, so a randomly drawn donor often carries the target's own alleles: on that sample the median `ε_j` is **0.3898** for moves that changed nothing against **2.1904** for moves that changed something (both measured in this project at capacity 0.1). Never write "privacy is inflated by 31%" — the correction from 31% to 7.0% is recorded in `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24.

> **Question** — *"7.0% comes off a 3,000-move sample of 47,070. How do I know 3,000 is enough — and does the sample even reproduce the 30.9%?"* It reproduces it closely, and that internal check is the only one the ledger supports. The sample splits **2,112** moves that changed something against **888** that changed nothing (both measured in this project, capacity 0.1; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24), and **888 / 3,000 = 29.6%** (derived) against the **30.9%** measured over all 47,070 selected moves — within about 1.3 percentage points. What `docs/` does **not** record is any dispersion for the 7.0% itself: no interval, no repeat draw, no per-stratum breakdown. And note what kind of quantity it is — a ratio of two sums of `ε_j`, computed on the subsample — which means the thing that would settle it is not a larger sample but the **exact** computation: sum `ε_j` over all **14,526** no-op moves and over all **47,070** selected moves. That is arithmetic on arrays this project already has and has not run. Until it is run, quote 7.0% as measured on the sample it was measured on, and never as an exact share.

Note carefully what is **not** corrupted, because it is the half of the ratio a reader will assume is broken. `η_j` sums `1/support(v)` **only over variants that actually differ** (Step 14d), so a no-op is charged **0.00000** and the constraint the LP satisfies is an honest one. The `928.92 / 9,288.85 = 10.000%` figure above is therefore exactly what it says it is. What inflates is the **objective** — the privacy numerator — and hence the **privacy-per-utility ratio** computed from this run's own reported numbers. The `pmi_gain` this run reported was **115,315.84** (measured in this project at capacity 0.1). Two things that do *not* follow, and the project's standing rule forbids writing either: this says nothing about the paper's published frontier, which we have not reproduced, and it does not license a claim about how the *selection* would change, which has not been measured. For our own reporting the rule that does follow is in `docs/bugs.md`: report **actual allele change** as the privacy numerator, never `pmi_gain`.

> **Question** — *"Capacity 0.1 is exactly tight at 10.000% and capacity 0.5 saturates at 33.1%. Where between them does it stop being tight?"* **Not measured.** Only those two capacities have been run here (measured in this project), and `docs/` holds nothing between them. What can be said without measurement is *why* saturation happens and that it is structural rather than numerical: the budget denominator `2 × Σ utility_loss` charges every site as if it changed, while `support_blocks` charges only sites that actually differ (Step 15), so total achievable loss is strictly less than the denominator. Once every move with positive value has been bought there is nothing left to spend on, and the constraint stops binding. The crossover capacity is the real ceiling on how much this method can ever change, and finding it is a measurement we have not made.

> **Question** — *"At most one variable is fractional at the LP optimum (§A.6), and the code rounds. Up or down? Up overspends the budget; down leaves privacy on the table."* The rounding **direction is not recorded in `docs/`** and this primer will not guess it. What is recorded: the solver is required to report `OPTIMAL` or the run aborts, the solution is rounded to integers, and the resulting utility loss was measured at **928.92 of 9,288.85 = 10.000%** — tight to the precision reported, which is consistent with the perturbation being at most one item out of roughly 200,000 decisions. Reading the rounding line out of `optimizer.py` and recording it is an open task.

Two operational properties of this step close it out: what the seed buys an attacker, and one baseline that cannot be run at all.

> **Question** — *"Same seed gives byte-identical output. If the seed leaks, can an attacker replay the sampler on candidate inputs?"* Operationally yes, and this is why §A.7 separates operational seed secrecy from formal reliance on randomness. **Measured in this project:** the same seed reproduces `new_haplotypes.npy` and `xsol.npy` **byte-identically** (seeds 123 and 456 compared); a different seed produces different output. `--seed` is an ordinary command-line argument of `obfuscate.py`, and the released artefact of Step 19 is a VCF that carries no provenance field for the run — but "not written into the file" is not the same as "secret", and nothing in the design makes the guarantee survive the seed becoming known. For PanMixer the seed is load-bearing in a way a formal bound would not permit. Our own mechanism has the same operational exposure, and its formal position differs only in what there is to lose: the bound of §4.7 is a statement about the mechanism's output **distribution**, so it is taken over the randomness of the draw and says nothing at all about an attacker who can reproduce that draw. Seed secrecy is an operational requirement in both designs; what differs is that in ours there is a theorem to void.

> **Note** — one thing this step cannot do, for later. `optimizer.py:293` references an undefined `pmi_gain`, so the **random baseline cannot run**, and `stacker.py:140-141` raises `NotImplementedError` for the `to_random` strategy it advertises (both measured in this project; `docs/bugs.md`). If a comparison needs an unguided-draw arm, it has to be written, not switched on.

**Why it matters:** this is the only place the privacy/utility trade-off is actually decided. Everything before it prices the options; everything after it executes the decision.

### Step 18 — The stacker
**File:** `tools/panmixer/stacker.py`, strategy `to_best`.
**Input:** `xsol.npy`, `new_haplotypes.npy` (the precomputed candidates), `pangenome.npy`, `blocks_dict.json`.
**What happens, in three lines of array arithmetic.** The whole step is this, and it is worth being concrete because "stacking" sounds like assembly and no assembly happens.

1. `new_variants = original_haplotypes.copy()` — start from the target's **true** haplotypes, all 340,824 records, both strands.
2. For every `(block index i, haplotype j)` with `xsol[i,j] == 1`, look up that block's **record indices** — the `block[1]` list of Step 12, which is a list of *row numbers into the matrix* — and overwrite `new_variants` at exactly those rows on strand `j` with the corresponding entries of the precomputed candidate from Step 14c.
3. Write the array out.

That is the entire release rule (measured in this project, read from `external/PanMixer/tools/panmixer/stacker.py:37-70`). Two consequences follow immediately and both matter more than the code length suggests.

> **Key idea** — **an unselected block is released byte-identical to the input.** Not "approximately unchanged", not "unchanged up to rounding" — the bytes at those rows were never touched, because step 1 copied them and step 2 never addressed them. This is the sentence §A.7 and §4.12 both turn on: if some blocks are published verbatim, then two candidate inputs that differ only in an unselected block produce releases with **disjoint supports** and total variation distance 1 — and therefore, by the identity §4.12 spells out, an attacker who reads that block and identifies the input with certainty. No setting of `capacity` repairs it, because the repair would have to be "select every block", and Step 17 measured that the knapsack **saturates** at **33.1%** of the budget at capacity 0.5 (measured in this project) — there is no capacity at which every block is bought.

> **Open question** — **what the release *is* when a record's span crosses a block boundary.** `new_haplotypes.npy` is shape `(340824, 2)`: **one allele index per record**. Because step 2 addresses **record indices**, a long parent record and the records positionally inside its span are decided **independently**. If the parent's block is not selected the parent keeps the target's **true** allele, while children sitting inside a selected block are replaced — and the released VCF asserts both at once. There is **no sequence assembly step and no coherence check**: whether the resulting set of allele indices jointly corresponds to a walk through the graph is **never computed, checked, or asked** (measured in this project, `stacker.py:37-70`). This is not a corner case. **Measured in this project** on all 340,824 chr21 records (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-22 follow-up): **31,487 records (9.2%)** have their `POS` inside an earlier record's span, and **15,540 of 100,757 blocks (15.4%)** contain at least one such record.
