How often the incoherence actually materialises in a run — i.e. how often the two blocks involved differ in selection status — is **not measured**. For our own mechanism the corresponding requirement is that `output_support` contain only valid traversals, which is open question 6 in `docs/plan.md`.

> **Question** — *"Give me the actual rows. Take that 257 kb parent record and one of its children, `xsol` picks the child's block and not the parent's — what does `new_haplotypes.npy` literally hold at those two rows afterwards?"* The concrete instance is the hypervariable site at `grch38#chr21:14569980`: one top-level record with a **257,439 bp** REF, **2,464** nested child records inside its span, **2,485** matrix rows for the region in total, and — because block occupancy is decided by `POS` alone (Step 12) — those rows spread over **370** separate blocks (all measured in this project; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-22). Now take the parent's row *r* and one child's row *c*, with the child's block selected and the parent's not. After Step 18, row *r* holds the target's **true** allele index, untouched, and row *c* holds the candidate's allele index for that child. Step 19 then writes both into the target's column on their own two VCF data lines, `g0|g1` each. Nothing anywhere compares them. The released file therefore asserts the target's original 257 kb allele **and** a replaced allele at a position sitting inside that same 257 kb, and whether both can be true of one walk through the graph is never computed (`stacker.py:37-70`, measured in this project). What is **not** measured is how often it actually bites: `docs/` records the 370-block spread and the chromosome-wide overlap figures — **31,487 records (9.2%)** positionally inside an earlier record's span, **15,540 of 100,757 blocks (15.4%)** containing at least one — but not whether any of those 370 blocks differed in selection status in this run. Counting the splits against the run's own `xsol` is the measurement that would turn a structural hazard into a rate.

**Output:** `exp_N/data/chr{N}/{row_id}/new_haplotypes.npy`, shape `(340824, 2)`, plus `stacked.json`.

**Expect (measured in this project, HG00438 / chr21 / capacity 0.1, seed 123):** **68,335 of 650,541** called alleles changed. Two derived readings of that pair, because both get misquoted. The target has `340,824 × 2 = 681,648` allele slots (derived); **650,541** of them carry a called allele, so about 4.6% are `-1` (derived). Step 9 names two routes to a `-1` — the VCF wrote `.`, or the genotype was haploid — and its five-cause table separates **five** distinct conditions that all arrive as the same `-1`. And `68,335 / 650,541 = 10.50%` (derived), so roughly **89.5%** of the target's called allele slots come through the release **untouched**.

> **Question** — *"68,335 of 650,541 is 10.50% and the capacity was 0.1. Are those the same 10%, or a coincidence?"* **Different quantities that happen to land close.** The capacity constrains a **weighted sum**: `Σ η_j x_j ≤ capacity × 2 Σ utility_loss`, measured at **928.92 / 9,288.85 = 10.000%** at capacity 0.1 (measured in this project). The 10.50% is an **unweighted count of allele slots**: 68,335 / 650,541 (derived). One is a sum of `1/support(v)` terms over changed variants, the other is a tally of changed rows. They land within half a percentage point of each other on this run and there is no reason they must; do not use either as a check on the other.

**Why it matters — and this is where the accounting gap lives.** This step fixes PanMixer's guarantee structure for good, per the Key idea above. It is also the step after which the only description of what was done is a **count of records**, and that is not the same thing as a description of how much of the genome changed.

> **Defect** — **the utility accounting cannot see the size of what it changes.** `1/support(v)` is charged **per record** (§A.5), so a 257 kb allele and a 1 bp SNP cost exactly the same. **Measured in this project** on this run (`docs/bugs.md`, dated 2026-09-25; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23): the quantity of sequence actually rewritten was **3,129,810 bp — 3.13 Mb, 6.7% of chr21** — across **222** changed alleles longer than 1 kb, the largest a single **257,485 bp** rewrite. What the tool reports for the same run is *"68,335 of 650,541 alleles changed (10.5%)"*. Both statements are correct descriptions of different things, and **no metric in the suite is sensitive to the megabases**. A per-record count cannot see it in principle: the metric's unit is the row, and the row does not carry a length. Severity is recorded as **HIGH for interpreting any published utility number**. This is a metric measuring something narrower than its name suggests; it is not a claim that the paper's figures are wrong, and we have reproduced none of them. The full entry is departure (h).

**Which records absorb that 3.13 Mb — and the assumption to unlearn before reading the next table.**

> **Caution** — **the mechanism is not restricted to SNPs.** It is natural to assume PanMixer leaves indels and structural variants alone, since its evaluation is so SNP-centred, and an answer given during this project's working session said exactly that. **That answer was wrong and is retracted** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23, Finding 5). Indels and structural variants are **scored, selected and rewritten**, and **measured in this project** on this run (chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1) they are rewritten at a **higher** rate than SNPs: **15,206** non-SNP records changed, a **23.3%** rate, against **14.9%** for SNPs. **The mechanism sees everything; only the evaluation filters.** That one sentence is the thing to remember.

Here is the filtering, stage by stage (all **measured in this project**, chr21, HG00438, capacity 0.1; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23). Read it as: the first row is what was changed, and every row below it is what some metric was allowed to look at.

| stage | records it sees | of 340,824 | non-SNP records included | `CONFLICT` records included |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the mechanism (Step 14 + Step 17 + Step 18) | **340,824** | **100%** | **65,176** | **1,855** |
| `af_loss` / `ld_loss` (Steps 23–24, strict 1000G mask) | 258,610 | 75.9% | 21,550 | 37 |
| gap score (Step 21, posref mask) | 268,851 | 78.9% | 29,965 | 110 |
| gap score after the biallelic-SNP mask | 238,052 | 69.8% | **0** | 49 |
| read mapping (Step 26, `bcftools view -v snps`) | 288,020 | 84.5% | mixed records kept | 1,167 |

The bottom four rows are all narrower than the top one, and they are narrowest exactly where the edits are largest. The biggest rewrites by base pairs land on non-SNP records; the gap score's biallelic path sees **zero** of those, and the read-mapping graph is built after `bcftools view -v snps` drops **52,804** indel and structural records. One correction to carry with that last row, because an intermediate figure used while investigating was wrong: `bcftools view -v snps` **keeps mixed multi-allelic records**, and `CONFLICT` records are enriched in those (median 4 alleles per record, measured in this project), so **1,167 of 1,855** conflicts **survive** the read-mapping filter — **37% are dropped, not the ~84% an earlier draft of that investigation claimed** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23 correction).

> **Question** — *"The `CONFLICT` column runs 1,855 at the top of that table and 1,167 at the bottom. But a `CONFLICT` record is missing data for the target — what does the mechanism even do with one, and does it ever get edited?"* Almost certainly not edited, and for a reason that stacks both of the per-move scores in the same direction. A `CONFLICT` record is one where `vg` found that sample several graph paths that disagreed, so it wrote `.` for the genotype, which `VCFtoNP.py` stores as `-1` (Step 9). Then, all **measured in this project** on chr21 (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-23): there are **1,855** such records (**0.54%** of 340,824), with a mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes missing at one of them against **3.6 of 88** elsewhere; the privacy score is **zeroed** wherever the target's own haplotype is `-1` (`get_support_and_pmi.py`, `pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`); and the utility weight *rises*, because `support(v)` counts called entries, giving a median `1/support(v)` of **0.01818** at `CONFLICT` sites against **0.01136** elsewhere — **1.60×** more expensive to change. Zero value at higher cost is the one combination the knapsack of Step 17 never buys, so these records are released **verbatim**, precisely where the target's own structure is most unusual. Note the status of that "never": `docs/` records the two scores and the consequence, but **not a count of how many `CONFLICT` records the LP actually selected in this run**, so it is a consequence of the pricing rather than a measurement. For our own mechanism this stops being a scoring question and becomes the `missing_policy` question, and that decision is **reserved, not made**. `docs/terms.md` records the options: **RENORMALISE** (drop that donor from the state distribution at that position), **WILDCARD** (treat missing as matching whatever the target has, which makes poorly assembled donors universally attractive), **MISMATCH** (treat it as a difference, which penalises assembly gaps as though they were real variation), and **RESTRICT** (exclude ill-behaved positions from the chain altogether, which is what PanMixer's anchor rule does in effect and is worth keeping as a baseline precisely because it is the published behaviour). The recorded leaning is RENORMALISE and no decision has been taken. The evidence that makes this load-bearing rather than a detail, and it cuts both ways: missingness at chain positions barely matters for anchors at **0.26%** mean, and matters a great deal once every variant is a position at **11.31%** mean with **7,555** positions over 25% missing (measured in this project). The prerequisite `docs/plan.md` names is the auxiliary REASON array, because the five causes of `-1` (Step 9; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-25) arrive at the mechanism indistinguishable from one another. §A.10 is the full treatment.

> **Note** — the consequence for us, since §4.13 builds **both** of our utility ingredients out of `1/support(v)`. Under the decided chain architecture, **model C**, a chain position is a single variant, and the pair collapses pleasantly: `φ_v` is an **indicator** — 1 if the drawn donor carries the target's allele at `v`, else 0 — and `β_v = w_v / Σ_u w_u` with `w_v = 1/support(v)`. Bounded in [0,1] and additive by construction, with **no per-block normaliser** (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). That is weight-proportional, not uniform — but the weight it is proportional to is still **per record**, so the blind spot is inherited whole: a 257 kb allele and a 1 bp allele with the same `support(v)` carry the same `w_v`, while differing enormously in how much sequence the release got right. Whether the weight should instead be **length-weighted** is listed as undecided in `docs/plan.md` (open question 7, part ii). Do not assume either; and note that the `β` weights must still sum to 1 whichever is chosen, because that normalisation is what keeps `u_path` in [0,1].

**The alternative stacker strategies**, for completeness and because two of them are the baselines every comparison needs: `to_empty` blanks the subject's column entirely — the strongest baseline, and the paper's *"Removed"* arm; `to_unedited` passes the original through — the null baseline; `to_random` is advertised as an unguided draw but raises `NotImplementedError` at `stacker.py:140-141` (measured in this project).

> **Question** — *"`to_empty` is the strongest baseline. Should our `τ = 0` point be compared against `to_empty`, since `τ = 0` means the output ignores the target entirely?"* **Yes, and they are the same idea reached from two directions.** At `τ = 0` the tilt factor `exp(η_τ · u(p,y))` is identically 1, so `Q_p = R_D` for every input `p` — the release is a draw from the public cohort model that never consulted the target. `to_empty` removes the target's contribution instead of ignoring it. Both are "the release carries no information about this person", so they are the right floor to line up against each other. The caution that goes with it: neither floor is *useless* on **cohort-level** utility axes. A pure-prior draw is a real cohort haplotype, so on any measure of "does this look like a plausible member of the population" it scores near-perfectly while carrying **zero** information about the target. Be precise about which measures those are on our side. `af_loss` and `ld_loss` are PanMixer's **cohort-distortion** metrics: they score the change a release induces in the *cohort's* allele frequencies and LD, which is a well-posed question when the release is the cohort VCF with one column rewritten. Our release is a standalone path and never edits the cohort, so those two are **not defined for our release object** and are **not metrics this project computes** (`docs/terms.md`). The hazard is real all the same on the axes that do apply — external read mapping among them — which is why `target_fidelity` is a mandatory axis in any comparison (`docs/terms.md`), and why our maximal-privacy point would otherwise look like a free lunch. See §4.6 and §4.13.

### Step 19 — Write the VCF
**File:** `tools/common/convert_2_vcf.py`
**Input:** the stacked `new_haplotypes.npy` and the original `chr{N}/pangenome.vcf.gz` as a template.
**What happens:** streams the template line by line; on the header it locates the target's column index; on each data line it replaces that one field with `g0|g1`, writing `.` for −1. Any other sample field lacking a `|` is rewritten to `.|.`. For the `to_empty` baseline it drops the column entirely. It asserts that the number of data lines equals the number of genotype rows. Then `bgzip` (block-gzip, which keeps the file randomly seekable) and `bcftools index`.
**Output:** `new_haplotypes.vcf.gz` (+ index).
**Expect:** same site count, same sample list, one column's contents different.
**Why it matters:** **this is the released artefact.** Note exactly what it means: the release is the *whole cohort VCF*, with one person's column rewritten in place. The graph topology, the sites, and the other 43 people are untouched. This is the precise sense in which PanMixer "modifies the pangenome" — it does not modify the graph.


> **Question** — "'Any other sample field lacking a `|` is rewritten to `.|.`' — that changes the *other 43 people's* data in the released file. How many fields does that hit, and does it mean the release is lossy for everybody, not just the target?"
>
> The rewrite is real and it is measured, at least on one axis: **2.38% of sample fields lack a `|`** across 200,000 chr21 records (measured in this project), and **zero records lose all support** as a result — so the release is lossy for other samples at the level of individual fields, not at the level of whole records. That measurement was made while chasing the read-mapping baseline offset, where it was judged at most a minor contributor.
>
> The more consequential version of the same mechanism is the `CONFLICT` chain, and it deserves to be stated here because this is the step where it becomes published missingness. At a `CONFLICT` record `vg` writes a sample's genotype as `.|.`; `VCFtoNP` stores that as **−1**; and this step writes **−1** back out as `.`. On chr21 there are **1,855 `CONFLICT` records (0.54%)**, at which a mean of **29.4 of 88** haplotypes are missing against **3.6 of 88** elsewhere (measured in this project).
>
> Now combine that with the pricing. The privacy score is **zeroed** wherever the target's haplotype is −1, and the utility weight **rises** there because support counts only non-missing entries — median **0.01818** against **0.01136**, **1.60×** more expensive (measured in this project). Zero value, above-average cost: the knapsack essentially never selects those records, so **they are released verbatim**. And they are not uniformly distributed — `CONFLICT` records are enriched **4.6×** at nesting level `LV=1` (37.5% against a chromosome-wide 8.2%, measured in this project). The leak is concentrated exactly where the target's structure is unusual. §A.10 is the full treatment.

> **Question** — "If the release is the cohort VCF with one column rewritten, and someone holds two releases at two capacities, can they diff them to recover the original?"
>
> A diff of two capacities does not hand back the original column, but it does hand over the **selection pattern**: every row where the two releases differ is a row in a block that was selected at one capacity and not the other. Since selection ranks blocks by ε_j — the surprisal of *the target's own* block — the selection vector is a **rarity fingerprint of the target**, which is information about the private input that neither release was supposed to reveal. §4.12 makes that argument in full.
>
> More generally, multiple releases of one genome are outside every guarantee on offer here. For us, one release per genome is condition 4 of §4.8 — an **operational rule, not a theorem** — and the only composition statement available is the loose subadditive bound k·τ for k releases (project status, `docs/plan.md`). It is also why the head-to-head's frontier is treated strictly as an evaluation device: a frontier of k points is k correlated releases of the same genome, and the attacks are therefore run **per operating point and never pooled** (project decision).

### Worked trace — block 1077, from raw records to released VCF

Every branch, threshold and defect above has now been named. None of them has been *instantiated*. This section takes one real numbered block from our own chr21 run and walks it through Step 6 to Step 19 with the actual numbers, because a mechanism you cannot walk a single object through is a mechanism you do not yet have.

**Which block, and why this one.** The trace below is **block 1077**, chosen because it is the block for which `docs/memory.md` records the whole chain of stages — the record range, the anchor count, the branch, `ε_j`, the support weight, the sampled replacement, the selection decision and the released alleles. All figures are **measured in this project** on HG00438, chr21, the 30x GRCh38 rebuild, capacity 0.1, seed 123, unless marked **derived** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24). Block 1077 is also, usefully, a *no-op*: it is selected on both strands, so it accounts for **two** of the 14,526 selected moves that changed nothing, and it demonstrates the Step 17 defect concretely rather than abstractly. It does **not** demonstrate ignored non-anchor rarity, because all of its variants are anchors — for that, a second and less completely documented block follows at the end.

| stage | what the code does | block 1077 | origin |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Steps 6 and 9** — the records exist | the per-chromosome VCF split of Step 6, converted to the `(44, 340824, 2)` matrix at Step 9 | rows **34816–34820** of `chr21/pangenome.npy`, i.e. **5 records**, spanning POS **13,000,333 – 13,000,621** | measured |
| **Step 12** — block assignment | each record assigned by its `POS` to the plink interval containing it | all 5 records land in one dictionary entry: `block[1] = [34816…34820]` | measured |
| **anchor count** | how many of the 5 records are in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset | **5 of 5 are anchors** | measured |
| **Step 14b routing** | HMM when the block has ≥ 2 variants and ≥ 1 anchor | **the HMM branch** (5 variants, 5 anchors) | derived from the measured anchor count |
| **Step 14b score** | `−forward_algorithm(haplotype, anchors)` per strand | `ε_j` = **0.0711 nats** on each strand | measured |
| rarity ignored by the scorer | non-anchor variants contribute nothing | **none — there are 0 non-anchor variants in this block** | derived |
| **Step 14a weight** | `utility_loss[s] = 1 / not_missing[s]` | `support(v)` = **87 of 88** at every one of the 5 sites → `1/support` = **0.01149** each | measured / derived |
| **Step 14c routing** | allele frequencies for a singleton block or a block with ≤ 1 anchor; otherwise the HMM | 5 variants and 5 anchors, so **the HMM sampler** | derived from the measured anchor count |
| **Step 14c draw** | `sample_block_prior` walks the chain over the anchors, from a uniform start among the **86** non-target donors | returns `[0,0,0,0,0]` — **identical to the target's own block** | measured |
| **Step 14d cost** | sum `utility_loss[s]` over sites that actually **differ** | **0 sites differ** → `η_j` = **0.00000** (it would be **0.05747** had all five changed) | measured / derived |
| **Step 17 decision** | LP maximises `Σ ε_j x_j` subject to `Σ η_j x_j ≤ target` | **selected on both strands** — `ε_j > 0` at `η_j = 0` is unbounded value per unit cost, so the LP takes it unconditionally. The ledger records the confirmation as `xsol[625] == 1` on both strands | measured |
| **Step 18 write** | overwrite rows 34816–34820 on both strands with the candidate | rows overwritten — **with the values they already held** | measured |
| **Step 19 release** | write the target's two allele indices per record into its VCF column, phased | each of the five records carries the reference allele on both strands for HG00438 — **exactly what the input VCF carried** | derived from the measured released alleles |

**Now the prose, stage by stage, because the table compresses several things that deserve a sentence each.**

**The block.** Five consecutive records, rows 34816 to 34820 of the 340,824-row chr21 matrix, occupying 289 bp of reference coordinate between POS 13,000,333 and 13,000,621 (the span is **derived** from the two measured endpoints). All five are anchors, so all five are positions the HMM will use. HG00438 carries allele index **0** — the reference allele — at all five, on both strands. (`docs/memory.md` records the POS *range*; it does not record the five individual positions, so the table gives the range.)

**Why it goes to the HMM twice over.** Step 14b sends it to the forward algorithm because it has more than one variant and at least one anchor. Step 14c sends it to `sample_block_prior` because it is neither a singleton block nor a block with one anchor or fewer — it fails the allele-frequency guard on both of that guard's clauses. So this block is one of the **10,502** that are both scored and sampled by the HMM, not one of the **452** that split. The two branch decisions agree here; the point of the earlier table is that they need not.

**Where `ε_j = 0.0711` comes from — and how far a hand calculation can get.** The natural question at this point is what `ε_j` even *is* for a block holding several variants rather than one, so it is worth assembling rather than asserting. `ε_j = −log p(h_bj)`, the self-information of the **target's own original block** under the cohort model, in **nats** (the code uses `np.log`; see §A.4). The forward algorithm computes `p(h_bj)` as a sum over every hidden path through the emission panel that could have emitted the observed block. Two measured facts get most of the way to the answer by hand:

- **82 of the 88 cohort haplotypes carry exactly the target's pattern** `[0,0,0,0,0]` at these five sites (measured in this project). Note the denominator is **88**, not 86: the panel the forward algorithm scores against **includes the target**, which is departure (c).
- The transitions are degenerate — see departure (b): total switch mass measured at **5.8 × 10⁻¹²** at a one-index gap — so essentially all the probability mass sits on the "stay with one donor throughout" paths, and the start distribution is uniform over the panel.

With switching effectively switched off, `p(h_bj)` collapses towards the fraction of panel haplotypes that match the target at all five anchors, and `docs/memory.md` (2026-09-24) records the resulting check:

> **Worked example** — the cross-check the ledger records: `−log(82/88) = 0.0706` nats **(derived)**, against the measured `ε_j` of **0.0711** nats.

Those agree to three decimal places, which is the ledger's stated basis for reading `ε_j` as "the surprisal of the target's block under the cohort". Read the number that *is* derivable: **0.0706 nats** says *"93.2% of the cohort carries this exact block (82 of 88, derived), so learning that the target carries it is worth almost nothing."* `ε_j` is a **surprisal**, and here the block is barely surprising at all. The small residual of 0.0005 nats is the size of the per-anchor additive term T·μ_mis = 5 × 10⁻⁴ with the mismatch probability μ_mis = 10⁻⁴ read from the code (**derived**; see §A.4.1).

> **Key idea** — the hand check above is **derived arithmetic that lands close to the measured value**, not a read-out of the code's internal quantities. It lands close only because the transitions are degenerate enough that the mosaic sum nearly collapses to a count of matching donors. Under working transition constants it would not collapse, and `ε_j` would have to be read from the forward pass rather than reconstructed at all. The closeness of the agreement is itself a symptom of departure (b).

> **Question** — *"Hold on. The scorer's panel is **88** and includes the target, but the sampler draws its start donor from **86** non-target haplotypes. Two different panels inside the same block — and if the scorer is counting the target as one of its own matches, isn't `0.0711` too small?"* Both halves of that are right, and the asymmetry is real: the forward algorithm scores against a panel that includes the target (`obfuscate.py:130,132`, measured in this project) while the sampler's draw is over the without-target panel. That is departure (c). The direction is the one you expect, but the **shape** is a ceiling rather than a general shrinkage: because the target always matches itself, `p(h_bj) ≥ 1/2N`, so `ε_j ≤ log(2N) = log(88) = 4.4773` nats (derived) however rare the block truly is. **Measured in this project** on 150 real HMM-path blocks for HG00438 (`docs/bugs.md`): shipped `ε_j` spans **0.071 – 4.508**, median **1.242**; re-scored with the target removed from the emission panel the median is **1.299**, a median ratio of **1.03×**, but the maximum rises from 4.508 to **78.1**, and for the **9** blocks sitting within 0.05 nats of the ceiling the leave-one-out value has median **12.06** and max **78.12** — understated by up to **17.4×**. For block 1077 specifically the answer is therefore *barely*, and this block is concrete enough to show why by hand. Of the **82 of 88** panel haplotypes carrying the target's pattern, two are the target's own two strands, so removing the target leaves **80 of 86** (derived): `−log(80/86) = 0.0723` nats against `−log(82/88) = 0.0706` nats (both derived), an increase of about 2.4% — in line with the measured 1.03× median. 1077's 0.0711 nats sits at the very bottom of that measured 0.071–4.508 range, nowhere near the ceiling. The blocks this defect distorts are the rarest ones, the opposite end of the distribution from this one. What has **not** been measured is whether the knapsack's *selection* changes when the ceiling is removed: the score distributions on both sides exist, the LP has not been re-solved. That is a recorded gap, and nothing about the paper's published frontier follows from any of it — we have reproduced none of their figures.

**What the scorer ignored.** Nothing. All five variants are anchors, so there is no non-anchor material for the anchor-only restriction to discard. This is the benign case. The malign case is the paragraph after next.

**The cost weight.** `support(v)` counts **haplotype slots with any called allele** — not slots carrying a particular allele — and it comes out at **87 of 88** for all five sites, so one haplotype somewhere in the cohort is `-1` at each. Each site therefore carries weight `1/87 = 0.01149`, and if all five alleles changed the block would cost `5/87 = 0.05747` (derived). Note that this weight is **flat in allele frequency**: a site where the target carries something almost nobody has would cost the same 0.01149, as long as the same number of haplotypes are called there. That asymmetry — value scaling with rarity, cost not — is what §A.5 means by the optimiser being pointed at rare material.

**What the sampler returned, and why that is the interesting part.** `sample_block_prior` picked a start donor uniformly among the 86 non-target haplotypes and walked the chain. Because the transitions are degenerate it did not recombine; it copied one donor across all five anchors. And because **82 of 88** donors carry `[0,0,0,0,0]` here, the donor it drew carried the target's own alleles. The candidate came back **identical to the input**. There is no rejection loop and no comparison to the original — the paper describes a candidate constrained to differ, the released code implements no such constraint (`sample_block_prior` is an unconditional prior draw) — so the identical candidate is carried forward as a legitimate move. The script's one guard (Step 14c) fires only if the candidate is identical to the original **everywhere on the chromosome**, so a per-block no-op passes it trivially.

**What the optimizer did with it.** `η_j = 0.00000`, `ε_j = 0.0711`. The LP's objective gains 0.0711 and its constraint gains nothing. Value per unit cost is unbounded, so there is no budget at which this move is not taken: it is selected on **both** strands. Multiply that logic across the chromosome and you get the Step 17 measurement — **14,526 of 47,070** selected moves changed nothing (**30.9%**), and because such moves concentrate on blocks with small `ε_j` (on the 3,000-move sample, median `ε_j` **0.3898** against **2.1904** for moves that changed something) they carry **7.0%** of the reported privacy credit, not 31% of it. Block 1077's `ε_j` of 0.0711 sits far below even that 0.3898 median, so it is a mild instance of a common phenomenon.

**What was released.** The stacker overwrote rows 34816–34820 on both strands with `[0,0,0,0,0]` — the values already there. Step 19 wrote `0|0` into HG00438's field on each of those five VCF data lines. **The released file is byte-identical to the input at these five records**, and the run's reported privacy total is 0.1422 nats (2 × 0.0711, derived) larger for it.

> **Question** — *"Why is `ε_j` computed from the **original** block at all? If the point is to price what gets released, you price the replacement, not the thing you are replacing."* Because `ε_j` is meant to measure the risk being **removed**, not the risk of what goes out. It is the surprisal of the material the move takes off the table, so it is a property of the input block and it is computed before any candidate exists: `ε_j` "is always positive and is computed from the target's ORIGINAL block regardless of what was sampled" (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24). Read charitably that is coherent — "how identifying is the thing I am hiding" is a reasonable thing to want to know. It is also the exact mechanical cause of what block 1077 does: pricing the input while charging for the output means the two sides of the ratio can be computed about **different objects**, and when the sampler hands back the input, the gap between those two objects is the entire move. Note the consequence for our own mechanism, which is stronger than "avoid this bug". `ε_j` is a function of the **private input**, so `docs/bugs.md` records the rule as *never use `ε_pmi` inside our mechanism*: a quantity that reads the target's own block cannot appear inside a bound that has to hold for every possible input (§4.2). It can still be computed **beside** the mechanism as a diagnostic, recomputed leave-one-out, provided nothing it produces feeds a decision.

> **Question** — *"So the privacy-savings-to-utility-cost ratio is wrong?"* The numerator is overstated and the denominator is not. `η_j = 0` is the **correct** cost of changing nothing, so the utility axis is honest. What is inflated is the privacy numerator — `pmi_gain`, reported as **115,315.84** on this run at capacity 0.1 — by about **7.0%**, and therefore the ratio. This is an accounting property of the released code, and no statement about the paper's published figures follows from it: `ε_j` enters the LP only through a **ranking**, and **we have not measured how much the selection changes** if no-ops are excluded or if the scoring ceiling of departure (c) is removed. That measurement does not exist, so the claim stops here.

**The malign case, for contrast: block 1083.** Block 1077 shows a no-op; it does not show the anchor-only blindness, because it has no non-anchors to be blind to. Block 1083 does. **Measured in this project** (`docs/bugs.md`, dated 2026-09-25): it holds **two common anchors at allele frequency 0.934** plus **one non-anchor at allele frequency 0.00385**. It has ≥ 2 anchors, so it takes the HMM branch, so the forward algorithm builds its observation vector and its emission panel from the **anchors only** and the non-anchor is not a position in the chain at all. The all-variant allele-frequency route would have scored the block **5.6956 nats**; the code returns **0.0349 nats** — a factor of **163**. The rarest thing in the block is the thing the score cannot see. Across **400** random HMM-branch blocks the median `ε_j` is **1.079** against an all-variant frequency sum of **2.769**, and in **32%** of blocks the ignored non-anchor rarity **exceeds the `ε_j` actually reported**. See §A.4.2 for the full treatment.

What `docs/` does **not** record for block 1083, and therefore what this primer will not state: its record range, its variant count beyond "two anchors plus one non-anchor", its `support(v)` values, its `η_j`, the replacement its sampler drew, whether the LP selected it, or what was released for it. The 163× comparison is a comparison of two scoring routes on the same block, not a full trace.

> **Question** — *"Both of these push the same way. The anchor-only restriction makes `ε_j` too small, and the self-inclusion ceiling makes the rarest `ε_j` too small. If every error shrinks the score of the most identifying blocks, is the ranking the LP uses worth anything? And is that a risk for us or an opportunity?"* The two do point the same way, and the honest answer to the first half is that **nobody has measured it**. What exists is the score distribution on each side of each defect. For the anchor restriction: over **400** random HMM-branch blocks, median `ε_j` **1.079** against an all-variant allele-frequency sum of **2.769**, with the ignored non-anchor rarity exceeding the reported `ε_j` in **32%** of them (measured in this project). For the ceiling: over **150** HMM-path blocks, median **1.242** shipped against **1.299** re-scored leave-one-out, with the **9** ceiling-bound blocks understated up to **17.4×** (measured in this project). What does not exist is a re-solved LP. `ε_j` enters Step 17 only through a **ranking**, and the optimizer has **not been re-run with either defect corrected**, so no statement is available about which moves would change hands — and none at all about the paper's published frontier, which we have not reproduced. For the second half: it is an opportunity only in a narrow sense. v1 does not contain this quantity. `ε_j` is a function of the private input, so it cannot sit inside our bound at all (see the Question on why `ε_j` prices the original, above), and v1 additionally deletes the anchor concept that causes the first defect, so there is no anchor-restricted score for the second defect to cap. The opportunity is not "we compute a better `ε_j`" — it is that our mechanism has no per-block score to get wrong. The price of that is the one the Note below names.

> **Note** — both traces bear on our own design in the same direction. It has **no knapsack and no moves**: every chain position is resampled from the tilted distribution, so there is no selection step in which a no-op can be booked as a gain and nothing is released verbatim by default (`A-IMP-v1-sampler` REPLACE item 5, `docs/plan.md`). And it **drops the anchor concept entirely** — under model C every variant *is* a chain position — so block 1083's non-anchor would be scored and sampled like any other position (REPLACE items 1 and 2). Note also the honest cost of that: making every variant a chain position raises mean missingness at chain positions from **0.26%** (PanMixer's anchors) to **11.31%** (measured in this project), with **7,555** positions over 25% missing — which is why `missing_policy` is a live, undecided question and not a detail (§A.10).

### Step 20 — Combine chromosomes
**File:** `tools/common/combine_vcfs.py` — `bcftools concat` of chr1…chr22 into `data/all/{row_id}/merged.vcf.gz`.
**Why it matters:** the re-identification attack and the read-mapping evaluation operate genome-wide or need a single index.


> **Question** — "Does anything check that the same subject was obfuscated on all 22 chromosomes before concatenating? A mixed merge would release one person partly sanitised."
>
> **Whether `combine_vcfs.py` validates that is not recorded in this project**, so the primer should name it as an operational hazard to verify rather than assert either way. `bcftools concat` itself will happily concatenate whatever files it is given, provided the sample lists match — and the sample list is identical in an obfuscated and an unobfuscated chromosome, because the release is the cohort VCF with one column rewritten. So the failure mode is invisible to the tool by construction.
>
> It is worth flagging for our own runs for a second reason: only **chr21** has been rebuilt against the build-matched 30x GRCh38 panel in this project — the repin for chr1–20 and chr22 is still in progress — so any genome-wide merge here would mix rebuilt and unrebuilt chromosomes, which is a different way to get a half-sanitised artefact.

### Step 21 — Downstream: the linkage (gap-score) attack
**Files:** `tools/downstream/privacy/gap_score.py` (single) and `diploid_gap_score.py` (all variants of the attack).
**Input:** the released `new_haplotypes.npy`, the masked attack database (`1000g_*_masked_posref.npy`), the biallelic SNP mask, allele frequencies.
**What happens:** restrict to sites that are biallelic SNPs *and* **polymorphic** in the attack panel (both alleles actually present — a site where every panel member is identical carries no information); binarise alleles to 0/1; compute each genotype as the sum of the two haplotypes (0, 1 or 2); model each genotype's probability as p², 2pq or q² under Hardy–Weinberg (see the box at Step 11); score a database entry by summing **−log(probability)** over the loci where its genotype *equals* the released genotype. Then compute self-score minus the maximum over the database. Four scoring variants are run: genotype, haplotype, both-haplotypes-equal, and unweighted match count.
**Output:** `genotypes_scores.npy` (one score per database entry), `genotypes_scores_self.npy`, and `gap_score_all.json` with `genotype_g_to_gstar`.
**Expect:** a negative `g_to_gstar` means the attack failed for that subject at that capacity. Sweeping capacity and finding where every subject goes negative is how ε_private is produced.
**Why it matters:** this is the actual empirical privacy measurement. The whole published privacy claim rests on this one function's behaviour, which is why departure (e) — the missing self-exclusion — is as consequential as it is. Note also the sign of published equation (5), carried forward from §A.7 because a re-implementer needs it: the sign is **settled**, and the paper has it right. The minus sign is typeset on **PDF page 10**, confirmed at **300 dpi** (**measured in this project**, `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26); the apparent discrepancy was text extraction dropping the glyph from a PDF with no text layer. So if you are re-implementing this scorer, **write L = − Σ log f_j** — minus the sum of the log frequencies over the matching loci — which is a positive score that grows as matches get rarer, and is what `gap_score.py` computes.


> **Question** — "Four scoring variants are run — genotype, haplotype, both-haplotypes-equal, unweighted count. Which one produces the published 0.002? Do the four agree, and if they disagree, which is the strongest attacker?"
>
> The key the pipeline writes for the headline quantity is `genotype_g_to_gstar` in `gap_score_all.json` (measured in this project, reading the code), so the genotype variant is the one the machinery treats as primary. **Whether the four variants agree, and which of them produced the published 0.002, has not been established in this project** — we have not run the capacity sweep, so there is no comparison to report, and the answer must say so rather than infer from the code which one the authors used.
>
> What can be said about strength without running it, as a structural matter: the haplotype variant is the sharper instrument in principle, because a phased haplotype match is far more identifying than an unordered genotype match (§1.1.2), and the unweighted match count is the weakest, because it throws away the rarity weighting that makes a handful of rare agreements decisive. For the head-to-head the decision already taken is to treat this attack as **our best comparison instrument** and to run it at **R ≥ 10 seeds per (subject, level) in both arms** — PanMixer's published frontier rests on one presampled draw reused across capacities (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).

> **Question** — "`np.max` over the entire database with no exclusion. If the target is in the database, the max *is* the target, so the gap score is driven negative mechanically. How many of the 44 are in the 30x panel?"
>
> **39 of 44**, including the target HG00438, are present in the 30x GRCh38 panel; **0 of 44** are present in Phase 3 (measured in this project). And the code does take a plain `np.max` over the whole attack database at `gap_score.py:241-242`, with no `i ≠ target` exclusion, although published equation (7) has one (measured in this project; quoted from the published paper). A partial mitigation exists — `tools/common/utils.py:357-361` does delete the self row — but only when the target's name is present in the loaded panel's sample list, so whether the exclusion happens depends on which panel is loaded.
>
> The conditional direction is the one to state: if the target's own entry can win the max, the gap score is driven **negative** for a reason that has nothing to do with obfuscation, and a negative gap score is what the pipeline reads as "the attack failed". So the operating point can be located by an attack that defeated itself.
>
> Now the framing rule, which bites hardest exactly here. **We cannot say how much that moves 0.002, and we are not claiming 0.002 is wrong.** We have not reproduced the published number and cannot say which code path or which database produced it. The claim is the narrow one: **the released code differs from published equation (7)**, and anyone re-running the released code should expect the exclusion question to change their numbers.

> **Question** — "Restricting to sites polymorphic *in the attack panel* means the attack cannot use any variant the panel has never seen — which is exactly the novel variants the frequency table calls rarest. Is the attack therefore blind to the most identifying material?"
>
> Structurally, yes, and it is the same shape as the blindness in the score. The attack restricts to biallelic SNPs that are also polymorphic in the panel, so its site set is the intersection of the mask (**274,458 records, 80.5%**, measured in this project; `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26) with the panel match set — and after both filters the gap score's biallelic path sees **238,052 records (69.8%)**, of which **zero** are non-SNP (measured in this project). Meanwhile the material the frequency table scores as rarest is by construction the material the panel does not contain: branch 3, the novel variants, is **56,889 chr21 variants (16.7%)**, with a median `−log f_v` of **5.41 nats** where the target carries a non-REF allele (measured in this project).
>
> So the strongest identifying signal on the chromosome is invisible to the empirical privacy measurement. Say it in the sanctioned form: **this metric measures something narrower than its name suggests.** It does not follow that the attack is useless — a re-identification attack that only uses common SNPs is a realistic attacker, and §2.3.2 shows that 34 to 61 ordinary common variants already make a haplotype expected-unique on Earth (derived). It follows that "the attack failed" is a statement about that attacker on that site set, which is precisely why §A.7 insists on the difference between a failed attack and a proved bound.

### Step 22 — Downstream: membership inference
**File:** `tools/downstream/privacy/MIA_privacy.py`
**Input:** the released haplotypes, the original, the other 43 pangenome samples, and optionally the external panel.
**What happens:** counts matching haplotype alleles and matching genotypes between the release and each comparison set, at all sites and at panel-overlapping sites.
**Output:** `MIA_privacy.npz` per (chromosome, row_id).
**Why it matters:** **membership inference** is a weaker but often easier attack than re-identification. The adversary already has the target's full genome and only wants to know *whether the target is in the cohort at all*. That alone can be sensitive — if cohort membership implies a diagnosis, then confirming membership discloses the diagnosis. This analysis appears in the **published** version of the paper and not in the superseded preprint.


> **Question** — "For our mechanism the target is external, so membership is 'no' by construction. But our output is a mosaic of *cohort* haplotypes. Could an attacker run membership inference and correctly conclude the donors are in the cohort? Does that leak anything about the target?"
>
> They could, and it leaks nothing, because the cohort is **public**. Which 44 individuals built the graph is published; an attacker who concludes from our release that HG00438 and 43 other named people are in the pangenome has learned something they could have read off the sample list. §4.2 lists the graph, the cohort and the cohort's paths as public, and only the target's own path and the private draw as secret.
>
> The genuinely open question is what the **right analogue** of membership inference is for us, and it is recorded as open rather than answered. The candidate is: *was this external person the target of a release?* — which is a different attack from the published one and is not yet defined precisely enough to implement. The proposal on the table is a held-out-target protocol, and it is marked a proposal, not a decision.
>
> What is **not** optional for us is the neighbouring measurement. A pure-prior draw — our τ = 0 — is a real cohort haplotype, so on any **cohort-level** utility axis it looks near-perfect while carrying **zero** information about the target. Two of the axes one would reach for first are unavailable here, and for a reason rather than an oversight: `af_loss` and `ld_loss` measure how much a release distorts the *cohort's* allele frequencies and LD, and our release never edits the cohort, so they are **not defined for our release object** and are not metrics this project computes (`docs/terms.md`). The hazard still stands on the axes that do apply, external read mapping among them. Any comparison must therefore carry a **target-fidelity** axis or our maximal-privacy point looks like a free lunch (project decision, `docs/memory.md`). Conveniently, the measurement already exists and is mechanism-agnostic: `MIA_privacy.py:80-83,140` computes match fractions of a release against the target's original haplotypes (measured in this project, reading the code). It has not been run for either arm.

> **Question** — "It compares against 'the other 43 pangenome samples, and optionally the external panel'. Optionally decided by what?"
>
> **Not recorded in this project.** The flag or code path that decides whether the panel comparison runs has not been read out and written down, so the primer should say "optionally, under a condition this primer has not established" rather than name a switch it has not verified. It is a one-line read away from being answerable, and it belongs in the same pass as the `optimizer.py` rounding line and the `AGGREGATION_DICTIONARY` weighting question at Step 27 — three small code reads that would close three honest holes in Part B.

### Step 23 — Downstream: allele-frequency loss
**File:** `tools/downstream/utility_in/af_loss.py`
**What happens:** recomputes allele frequencies over the cohort before and after, takes the L1 difference per site, and reports it overall and stratified by **minor allele frequency (MAF)** bands (<0.01, 0.01–0.05, 0.05–0.1, 0.1–0.5) and by SNP versus complex. MAF is the frequency of the *less* common allele at a site, so it always lies in [0, 0.5] and is the conventional axis for "how rare is this variant".
**The metric.** The paper reports a **Wasserstein divergence** between the before and after allele distributions. For alleles modelled as Bernoulli random variables, the published Methods state that this equals **two times the mean absolute difference** between allele frequencies, and they give the reason: *"The factor of two arises because each diploid individual carries two alleles per variant."* If you took the earlier, general "earth-mover's distance" description literally you would compute |p − q| and get half this; the factor of two is the Bernoulli-specific specialisation, and the two descriptions are consistent once you know that.
**Expect (published, at ε_private = 0.002), with labels attached:** Wasserstein divergence **0.006 for all alleles**, **0.006 for SNPs only**, **0.005 for rare SNPs (population MAF < 0.05)**. The paper's **"Removed" baseline** — the target excluded from the graph entirely — is **0.027**, close to 5× larger, and that contrast is the calibration that makes the 0.006 meaningful. (The superseded preprint's values were 0.004 / 0.004 / 0.002; do not quote them.)
**Why it matters:** the allele-frequency spectrum — the histogram of how many variants sit in each frequency band — is the single most-used summary in population genetics, and it is the main input to an **association study**, which tests variant by variant whether carrying one allele correlates with a trait or disease across a large sample. If PanMixer distorted it badly, the released graph would be useless for exactly the science it exists to serve.


> **Question** — "Rare SNPs come out at 0.005 and all alleles at 0.006, but rare variants are the ones the optimizer changes *first*. Why is the rare-SNP distortion *lower*?"
>
> Because the metric measures **absolute frequency change**, and a rare variant has almost no frequency to change. The published figures, all reported **at ε_private = 0.002**, are Wasserstein **0.006** for all alleles, **0.006** for SNPs and **0.005** for rare SNPs at population MAF < 0.05 (quoted from the published paper), and the quantity behind them is twice the mean absolute difference between the before and after allele frequencies.
>
> Work the bound. Changing one haplotype's allele at a site with support 88 moves that site's frequency by 1/88 = **0.01136** (derived). That is the *largest* per-site move a single edit can make, and it is the same number whether the allele was carried by 80 haplotypes or by 1. A variant at frequency 0.002 cannot contribute a frequency change bigger than about 0.002 by being removed, no matter how identifying it is. So the rare stratum is bounded below the common stratum almost by definition.
>
> This is §A.5's asymmetry read from the other end: **cost is flat in allele frequency while value scales with rarity.** The optimizer buys rare blocks first precisely because they are cheap on this axis — and the metric that scores the damage is built from the same axis, so it agrees that the damage was small. Both statements are true and they are about the same blind spot: nothing in this metric is sensitive to *how identifying* the changed material was, and nothing in it is sensitive to how much **sequence** changed either — **3,129,810 bp, 3.13 Mb, 6.7% of chr21** was rewritten on the capacity-0.1 run this figure would score (measured in this project).

> **Question** — "Wasserstein = 2 × mean absolute difference 'because each diploid individual carries two alleles'. Is that the same factor 2 as in the budget denominator `2 × Σ utility_loss`, or a different one?"
>
> Both come from diploidy and they are factors on different quantities, so they are not the same 2 and the coincidence should not be left to stand.
>
> In the metric, the 2 converts a **per-allele** mean absolute frequency difference into the Wasserstein divergence between two Bernoulli distributions over a diploid population — the published Methods state the specialisation and give the reason in those words (quoted from the published paper). It is a unit conversion on a distance.
>
> In Step 15, the 2 counts **both strands of the target**: the budget denominator is twice the sum of 1/support over sites because the target has two haplotypes, each of which could be edited at every site, giving **9,288.85** for HG00438 on our chr21 rebuild (measured in this project). It is a doubling of a budget, not of a distance.
>
> Getting this wrong in our own construction is the highest-severity error class in the project, which is why the two are worth separating explicitly: our diploid utility is the **mean** of the two per-haplotype utilities, `u_diploid = (u(p1,y1) + u(p2,y2)) / 2`, and using a **sum** instead leaves [0,1] and **doubles the effective tilt**, at which point the guarantee no longer holds (project definition, `docs/terms.md`).

> **Question** — "MAF < 0.05 defines 'rare' here. Population MAF from which panel?"
>
> From the **phased reference panel** of Step 3 — the same file that supplies the frequency table's branch 1 and the attack database. Which release that is changes the answer: Phase 3 with **2,504 samples** as shipped, or the **3,202-sample** 30x GRCh38 panel in our rebuild (measured in this project).
>
> It is worth naming because this particular metric is unusually fragile about it. The MAF strata are read from `pangenome_mask.npy`, the cross-build match set, so under the build mismatch of departure (a) **every stratum comes out `0/0`** and is reported as a legitimate value rather than raising (measured in this project). The metric is also sensitive to row count in a way that is easy to miss: comparing a 44-row cohort allele frequency against a 45-row one shifts every frequency by about 1/45 (derived). And both `af_loss` and `ld_loss` are **cohort-level** metrics, near-blind to target fidelity — never quote either as evidence that a release preserved the **target**.

### Step 24 — Downstream: LD loss
**File:** `tools/downstream/utility_in/ld_loss.py`
**What happens:** for each SNP, gathers the SNPs within the window — **5 kb** as the paper states it, **5 bp** as the shipped code executes it (see the second answer below) — computes **D = P(A and B) − P(A)·P(B)** — the covariance of allele co-occurrence, zero exactly when the two sites are independent — and then

> **r² = D² / [p(1−p)·q(1−q)]**

which is D rescaled to sit between 0 and 1: **the squared correlation between the two loci treated as 0/1 variables.** r² = 1 means knowing one allele tells you the other exactly; r² = 0 means it tells you nothing. Computed before and after obfuscation, with the L1 difference accumulated. (Here p and q are the two loci's allele frequencies; and again, this r² is unrelated to the recombination constant r = 1.26.)
**Expect (published, at ε_private):** the LD matrix was on average **0.003** off, against **0.017** for the complete-removal baseline.
**Why it matters:** this is the direct check that the block-level design worked. If PanMixer had edited variants individually rather than in whole LD blocks, r² would be destroyed, and this metric is precisely where you would see it.


> **Question** — "The window is 5 kb. But the hypervariable region's REF allele alone is 257,439 bp, its ALT alleles run to 514,768 bp, and it spans 370 blocks. A 5 kb window cannot see the long-range LD the block design exists to preserve. Is this metric measuring the thing it claims to measure?"
>
> Two separate answers, and the second one is worse than the question assumes.
>
> First, the scope limit is real. The metric accumulates an L1 difference in r² over SNP pairs within one window, so linkage spanning more than a window is outside its field of view by construction, and the structures that most need checking are exactly the long ones: the hypervariable region at `chr21:14569980` has a REF allele of **257,439 bp** and spans **370** overlapping plink blocks (measured in this project). Say it in the sanctioned form — **the metric measures something narrower than its name suggests** — and note that a snarl-tree segmentation would make that region **one** block rather than 370 (derived).
>
> Second, and this is a defect in the released code rather than a scope limit: the shipped `KB_WINDOW_SIZE = 5` is compared against **raw base-pair positions**, so the window as executed is **5 base pairs**, not 5 kb (measured in this project, reading `ld_loss.py`). At 5 bp almost no SNP has a partner in its window at all. Under the build mismatch the same function returns **`(0.0, 0)`** rather than raising (measured in this project) — a zero LD loss reported as a legitimate value. We have not reproduced the published **0.003** (reported at ε_private = 0.002), and nothing here says that figure is wrong; what we can say is that the released code as shipped does not compute the 5 kb window the published Methods describe.

> **Question** — "Work one r² for me: two sites, allele frequencies 0.3 and 0.4, co-occurrence 0.2. What is D, what is r², and what does an L1 change of 0.003 in that quantity actually feel like?"
>
> With p = P(A) = 0.3 at the first site, q = P(B) = 0.4 at the second, and P(A and B) = 0.2:
>
> **D = P(A and B) − P(A)·P(B) = 0.2 − 0.12 = 0.08** (derived) — positive, so A and B co-occur more often than independence would give.
>
> **r² = D² / [p(1−p)·q(1−q)] = 0.0064 / (0.3 × 0.7 × 0.4 × 0.6) = 0.0064 / 0.0504 = 0.127** (derived).
>
> So these two sites share about 13% of their variance: knowing one allele tells you a little about the other, well short of the r² = 0.64 worked in §2.4.4 where knowing A raises P(B) from 0.50 to 0.90.
>
> Now scale the published number. **0.003** at ε_private = 0.002 (quoted from the published paper) is a mean absolute change in a quantity that lives in [0, 1]. On this pair it would move r² from 0.127 to about 0.124 or 0.130 — a change you could not see by eye in a plot of r² against distance. For calibration the paper's own removal baseline on the same metric is **0.017** (quoted from the published paper), roughly six times larger, and that contrast is what makes 0.003 legible at all: on its own, an absolute change of 0.003 in a bounded quantity is uninterpretable without a comparison arm.

> **Question** — "0.003 versus 0.017 for the removal baseline. Is the 0.017 the same removal baseline as the 0.027 in Step 23, computed the same way?"
>
> Same **arm**, different **metric**. Both are the paper's "Removed" baseline — the target excluded from the graph entirely — and both are quoted from the published paper: **0.027** is that arm's Wasserstein allele-frequency divergence, and **0.017** is its L1 LD divergence. They are not comparable to each other and were never meant to be; each exists to calibrate the obfuscated number on its own axis (0.006 against 0.027; 0.003 against 0.017).
>
> For our own reporting the equivalent arm is already identified: our **τ = 0** point is an untilted draw from the baseline model, which ignores the target entirely, so it is the direct analogue of removal — and the PanMixer-side counterpart is the shipped `sampled_gap_score.py`, a full resample with no selection. The reporting contract requires the floor (τ = 0) and the ceiling (the target's own path, u = 1) to be **columns, not prose asides** (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).

### Step 25 — Downstream: Beagle reconstruction attack
**Files:** `tools/beagle/beagle_refinement.py`, `accuracy_stats.py`
**What happens:** runs **Beagle** — a standard **imputation** tool, which fills in or corrects genotypes by matching a target against a reference panel of haplotypes — on the released genotypes, using the external panel as reference with the targets excluded. Then compares the refined output to the *original* genotypes and to a naive "most frequent allele" baseline. The repository pins the jar **`beagle.27Feb25.75f.jar`** (downloaded by `external_tools/get_tools.sh`), i.e. the **27Feb25 build**; that is the version this project can verify. Note that the repo's `environment.yaml` ships no Java, so the jar has no declared runtime.
**Why it matters:** it tests whether obfuscation is *reversible*. If an off-the-shelf imputation tool can restore the original from the release, the privacy was cosmetic. The mosaic construction is what is supposed to prevent that: an imputer will happily snap the released haplotype onto *some* plausible reference haplotype, but the right one should no longer be distinguished. It is worth seeing that imputation is the **same Li–Stephens machinery as §A.3 run in the other direction** — there, sampling a plausible haplotype from a panel; here, inferring the most plausible haplotype given partial observations. That is why the attack is natural and why it is the right test of this particular mechanism.


> **Question** — "Beagle is Li–Stephens run in reverse, and our baseline R_D *is* a Li–Stephens model. So is Beagle the optimal attacker against us? And does our τ bound cover it, or does the fact that the attacker knows R_D exactly break something?"
>
> τ covers it, and knowing R_D is assumed rather than damaging. The bound is proved against an attacker who knows the graph, the cohort, the utility function, τ **and the full implementation** — everything except the private random draw (project definition, `docs/terms.md`; §4.2's Kerckhoffs paragraph). In the proof, target-independence of R_D is not a secrecy assumption at all: it is what makes R_D **cancel** in the likelihood ratio (§4.7, Step 3). An attacker who computes R_D exactly is computing a factor that has already cancelled.
>
> Then the nuance that makes the answer useful rather than smug. Beagle will still succeed at something: given a release, it can produce a **plausible completion** of it, and that completion may well look like a real human haplotype. What it cannot do is **distinguish which input produced the release** any better than (1 + τ)/2 — at τ = 0.10 that ceiling is **0.55** against a coin's 0.50 (derived). Reconstructing something plausible and identifying whose it was are different tasks, which is the same distinction §2.4.3 draws between rebuilding a path from nothing and matching a haplotype you already hold.
>
> One caveat in our own direction, not theirs: an attacker who knows R_D exactly is the right adversary to test the **implementation** against, because if our sampler is not actually drawing from the distribution we claim, an imputer built on the same model is the instrument most likely to notice. That is what exit gate 1 tests offline — sampler against exact enumeration, exact TV ≤ τ across a grid of τ, `log Z_p` inside [0, eta_tau] on every draw, and τ = 0 reproducing the baseline exactly (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).

> **Question** — "What does 'restore the original' even mean when the release is a mosaic of other people? Beagle will output *something* — how do we score whether it recovered the target?"
>
> The comparison is three-cornered, and the third corner is the part that makes it meaningful. `accuracy_stats.py` compares Beagle's refined output against (i) the target's **original** genotypes and (ii) a naive **most-frequent-allele** baseline (measured in this project, reading the code). Success for the attacker is beating the baseline: recovering the original better than you would by simply guessing the commonest allele at every site.
>
> That control is load-bearing. On a chromosome where most sites are overwhelmingly one allele, an imputer scores a high raw agreement rate with the original no matter what it was given, and quoting that raw rate would make any release look reversible. The only informative quantity is the **margin over the major-allele baseline**.
>
> For our arm there is a second control that must be reported beside it, for the reason given at Step 22: a τ = 0 release contains no target information at all, so whatever margin Beagle achieves against it is the **floor** — the amount of apparent reconstruction that comes from population structure rather than from leakage. Without that column, a reconstruction number is uninterpretable.

> **Question** — "`environment.yaml` ships no Java. So has anyone run this — us or them?"
>
> **This project has not run it.** The repository pins the jar `beagle.27Feb25.75f.jar`, downloaded by `external_tools/get_tools.sh`, and the shipped `environment.yaml` declares no Java runtime, so the jar has no declared way to execute inside the environment as distributed (measured in this project). Whether the authors ran it in some other environment is not something the release lets us determine, and nothing here asserts they did not.
>
> For our own run the decision is to **pin the repository's jar** rather than the current Beagle release, for comparability: the attack is only a comparison instrument if both arms are attacked by the same binary (project decision).

### Step 26 — Downstream: read mapping with vg giraffe
**Files:** `tools/downstream/utility_out/vg_prep.py` and `quick_align.py`
**What happens:** `vg_prep` normalises the released chr21 VCF (`bcftools view -c 1` to drop sites now carried by nobody, `norm -N -f REF`, `--trim-alt-alleles`, rename contigs, restrict to SNPs, sort), then `vg autoindex --workflow giraffe` builds the graph indexes. `quick_align` runs `vg giraffe` on chr21 **FASTQ** reads — FASTQ is the standard text format holding raw sequencing reads plus a per-base quality score — from five 1000 Genomes individuals **not in the graph**, then runs `vg stats -a` on the resulting **GAM** (Graph Alignment/Map) file.
**Output:** per-sample `*.giraffe.stats.txt`, parsed for percent of reads aligned perfectly, gapless (no insertions or deletions in the alignment), and at **MAPQ 60** — mapping quality 60 being the aligner's maximum-confidence value, meaning it considers the placement essentially unambiguous.
**Expect (published), with labels attached:** obfuscated graph **77.82% perfect / 95.61% gapless / 77.01% MAPQ 60**, against the baseline graph's **77.83% / 95.62% / 77.01%**. The differences are in the second decimal place.
**Why it matters:** this is the *external* utility test, and it is the one that matters most for the pangenome's reason to exist. A pangenome exists to reduce **reference bias** — the tendency of an aligner to place reads better when they happen to resemble the single reference, which systematically disadvantages people genetically distant from whoever the reference was built from. If obfuscation degraded mapping for outside individuals, the graph would have lost the thing it was built for.

**What this project has and has not run here, stated precisely, because an earlier version of this primer said flatly that the evaluation had not been run and that was wrong.** The **baseline** arm has been run. **Measured in this project** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-18 `A-EVL-readmapping`; logs `logs/pm_vg.log`, `logs/pm_vg2.log`): chr21 reads for the paper's own five external donors (HG00138 EUR, HG00635 EAS, HG01112 AMR, HG02698 SAS, NA18853 AFR) were extracted from the 1000G 30x CRAMs, the graph was built per `vg_prep.py`, alignment ran single-end per `quick_align.py` with vg 1.68.0, and `vg stats -a` scored it.

| metric | published baseline | ours |
|---|---|---|
| perfect | 77.83% | **79.05%** |
| gapless | 95.62% | **95.96%** |
| MAPQ 60 | 77.01% | **79.23%** |

> **Caution** — **do not describe the read-mapping baseline as reproduced.** We land in the right regime with the right internal structure — 10.9–11.4 M reads aligned per donor, and NA18853 (AFR) lowest on every metric as reference bias predicts — but the residual **+1.22 to +2.22** point offset (derived from the two columns above) is **unexplained**. One candidate explanation was tested and **refuted**, and it is worth stating as a retraction rather than as a dead end, because the reason it failed is the instructive part (`docs/memory.md`, **Correction 6**).
>
> **What was claimed.** That the offset came from the reference build: the chr21 used here was taken from the 1000G GRCh38 **analysis set**, which hard-masks **2.05 Mb** more of chr21 than the UCSC hg38 chr21 that `clean_fa.py` implies — **8,671,409 N against 6,621,364 N** (**measured in this project**) — and masking the false-duplication regions removes mapping ambiguity, which should inflate perfect alignment and especially MAPQ 60.
>
> **That explanation is withdrawn.** **What was measured instead:** the entire graph was rebuilt against UCSC hg38 chr21 and all five donors were re-aligned. The result is essentially identical — **79.05 / 95.96 / 79.23** (UCSC) against **79.03 / 95.96 / 79.26** (analysis set), a shift of **at most 0.03 points** (**measured in this project**) — against an offset of 1.22 to 2.22 points. **Note what survives:** the N-count difference is a real measurement and it stands; only the **causal claim** built on it was withdrawn.
>
> **Why the original reasoning failed**, and it should have been predicted: the reads were extracted from **CRAMs that were themselves aligned to the analysis set**, so **no read originates in the extra-masked regions under either graph**. The masking difference was real, and could not matter. Nothing here says the published numbers are wrong; an offset of this size is consistent with an undocumented difference in read extraction, in the exact graph VCF the baseline was built from, or in the tool versions actually used.

The **obfuscated** arm has not been run, so the published 77.82 / 95.61 / 77.01 remains unchecked here. The FASTQs and reference FASTAs are not downloadable **from the repository** and no script in the checkout produces them — we obtained them ourselves, and `docs/data.md` records both `external/PanMixer/read_fastqs` and `external/PanMixer/starting_data/references` as state `OK`.

**Two things a reader needs in order to line our run up against the paper's.**

> **Caution** — **the paper's two stated `vg` versions cannot be run together, and this makes one candidate explanation untestable rather than closing it.** The published Methods specify alignment with Giraffe **v1.68.0** and analysis of the resulting GAM files with `vg stats` **v1.36.0**. That combination **cannot execute**: the GAM serialization changed between those releases, and running the real v1.36.0 binary on a v1.68.0 GAM aborts with `obsolete, invalid, or corrupt protobuf input`, then `Signal 6 ... VG has crashed` (**measured in this project**, by downloading the v1.36.0 binary and trying it; `docs/bugs.md`). So the published numbers cannot have come from that combination exactly as written.
>
> Read the consequence narrowly, because the tempting reading is the wrong one. This does **not** rule "tool versions" out as a candidate cause of our baseline offset — it makes that candidate **untestable**, because the versions actually used are unknown and the stated pair is not a pair anyone could have run. In `docs/bugs.md`'s own words, it **"removes a route to explaining the residual offset"**. Nothing more than that follows: it is a statement about the version numbers printed in the Methods, not about the results they report. The practical rule it leaves behind is to use **one** version for both steps and **state which**, since metric definitions may differ across versions.

> **Note** — **the African donor is named differently in the paper and in the code, and this project follows the paper.** The published paper (**PDF page 10**) names the five read-mapping donors as HG00138, HG00635, HG01112, HG02698 and **NA18853** (**quoted from the published paper**; the page is confirmed in `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). The shipped `constants.py:42` instead lists `READ_SUBJECTS = ["HG00138", "HG00635", "HG01112", "HG01600", "HG02698"]` — **HG01600 substituted for NA18853** (**measured in this project**), and the repository's plotting helpers label HG01600 as **EAS** while NA18853 is **AFR**, so the substitution changes which population is represented, not just a name. This project **follows the paper** and extracted reads for NA18853 (`docs/data.md`). Reconcile any donor list you see against that: a five-donor table containing HG01600 came from the code, and one containing NA18853 came from the paper.


With those two reconciliations in hand, the questions this metric actually raises are about how much a difference in the second decimal place can carry.

> **Question** — "77.82% versus 77.83% is one part in ten thousand. What is the run-to-run variation of this measurement? If it is bigger than 0.01, the comparison says nothing."
>
> The objection is the right one and the number that would settle it does not exist. **The run-to-run noise floor of these statistics has not been measured in this project**, and the published values are single figures with no dispersion reported (quoted from the published paper). So the honest statement is that a difference in the second decimal place is **uninterpretable without a dispersion estimate**, and establishing one is cheap: re-run the same alignment several times and report the spread.
>
> That is a limitation of what can be concluded, not a criticism of the paper. And it cuts both ways: the same missing noise floor is why our own baseline offset above cannot be waved away — offsets of +1.22 to +2.22 points are one to two orders of magnitude larger than the published obfuscated-versus-baseline difference the metric is supposed to resolve.

> **Question** — "We could not run this because the FASTQs are not obtainable. Since our deliverable *is* a mapped path, this is the most important utility metric we have. What exactly do we need to acquire?"
>
> First a correction to the premise, which the paragraph above now carries: the **baseline arm was run in this project**. What is **not** downloadable from the repository is the input — no script in the checkout produces the read FASTQs or the reference FASTAs, and we obtained them ourselves.
>
> So the shopping list is: five 1000 Genomes read sets for individuals **not** in the graph, and the matching reference FASTAs — and then the obfuscated graph itself, which requires the release from a completed run at the operating point being evaluated.
>
> Two things that make the metric harder to use than it looks. `vg_prep` **restricts to SNPs**, dropping **52,804** indel and structural records and keeping **288,020 of 340,824 (84.5%)** (measured in this project) — so the graph this metric scores excludes the records that carry the largest edits by base pairs. And the published tool versions cannot be used as stated: the Methods specify alignment with Giraffe **v1.68.0** and analysis with `vg stats` **v1.36.0** (quoted from the published paper), and running the real v1.36.0 binary on a v1.68.0 GAM **aborts** with "obsolete, invalid, or corrupt protobuf input" (measured in this project). The stated combination cannot have been used as written; that is a statement about the version numbers in the Methods, not about the results.

> **Question** — "MAPQ 60 is the maximum-confidence value. Is 77.01% of reads at MAPQ 60 good or bad? What would a single linear reference give?"
>
> The number is only meaningful as a **difference between graphs**, and the comparison the metric exists for is graph-versus-graph: baseline graph against obfuscated graph, to check that obfuscation did not degrade mapping for people outside the cohort. **We do not have a linear-reference number**, so the primer cannot tell you how much the graph buys over a single reference, and it should not imply one.
>
> What the measured data does support is a sanity check on direction. Across the five donors the per-donor ordering is biologically sensible — **NA18853 (AFR) is lowest on every metric** (measured in this project), which is what reference bias against a GRCh38-backboned graph predicts — and the aligned-read volumes, **10.9 to 11.4 million per donor**, match the scale in the authors' own preserved sample output, whose comments report 11,521,845 total aligned reads working out to 77.68% perfect and 95.82% gapless (measured in this project). That sample also shows `Total paired: 0`, confirming the authors mapped **single-end**, as we did. Those agreements are why the installation is judged sound even though the baseline is not reproduced.

### Step 27 — `gather_results`
**File:** `tools/common/gather_results.py` — walks every `(chromosome, row_id)` directory, collects each JSON, and aggregates according to the `AGGREGATION_DICTIONARY` in `constants.py`: privacy and utility summed across chromosomes, rate-like metrics averaged.
**Why it matters:** privacy and utility are budgeted per chromosome (Step 15) but the claim is genome-wide, so the sums have to be reassembled. Summing a budget that was applied chromosome by chromosome is only meaningful because the blocks are treated as independent; that independence is an assumption, not a measurement.


> **Question** — "Privacy is budgeted per chromosome but summed genome-wide, and that only works if blocks are independent. Is the same true for our τ? Do we get 22 τ and a composition problem?"
>
> Two different problems, and only one of them is ours.
>
> Theirs is an **accounting** assumption: `gather_results` sums privacy and utility across chromosomes, and summing a budget applied chromosome by chromosome presumes the blocks are independent contributors to one total. That independence has not been measured, and the measured geometry argues against taking it for granted — **31,487 chr21 records (9.2%)** have their start position inside an earlier record's span, **15,540 of 100,757 blocks (15.4%)** contain such a record, and **78,937 variants (23.2%)** live in an affected block (measured in this project). Block disjointness fails broadly, not locally.
>
> Ours is a **scope** question with a proved answer inside one scope and an open answer across scopes. Within a chromosome the question is now closed by construction: **model C** is one chain over the whole chromosome, so Theorem 1 applies to the whole path directly and no factorisation argument is needed (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). The argument is still the thing that would license a block-unit fallback: the product of per-block tilted samplers **equals** the global tilted distribution, provided the baseline factorises over blocks, the `beta_t` sum to 1 and every `phi_t` lies in [0, 1] — so per-block sampling would be a scope choice, not a weaker guarantee (project argument, `docs/plan.md`). Across 22 chromosomes, if each chromosome is released under its own τ then the only statement available is the **subadditive** bound: k releases give at most k·τ, an upper bound and nothing sharper (project status).
