Whether a genome-wide release should be one chain with weights summing to 1 across the whole genome, or 22 releases with a composition argument, is part of the **reserved** T decision — open question 8, deferred to your PI. This primer does not settle it.

> **Question** — "'Rate-like metrics averaged' — averaged weighted by chromosome size, or unweighted?"
>
> **Not recorded in this project.** What `docs/` states is the split — privacy and utility **summed** across chromosomes, rate-like metrics **averaged**, according to `AGGREGATION_DICTIONARY` in `constants.py` — and it does not record whether that average carries a weight. Do not guess: an unweighted mean over chromosomes and a record-count-weighted mean differ substantially when chr21 and chr2 are in the same average, and which one is in use decides whether a genome-wide rate is a rate at all.
>
> This is the third of the three small code reads Part B owes (with the `optimizer.py` rounding direction at Step 17 and the panel-comparison switch at Step 22). All three are one line each and all three currently force this primer to say "we have not established that".

---

## Where the implementation departs from the paper

Ten findings, each established by reading the cited code and by executing it. **Every one of them is a statement about the released code, read against the published Methods — never a statement that the published results are wrong.** We have **not** reproduced the paper's figures — and the repository ships an *unwired* GRCh38 script and contains code branches that prefer 3,202-sample artifacts, which is consistent with the authors having run a path the release does not wire in. That is an inference, not a measurement. Each finding is included because it teaches something real about how the method is supposed to work. All ten are recorded with evidence in `docs/bugs.md`, in `docs/memory.md`, or in both, with the supporting measurements dated between 2026-09-18 and 2026-09-25. A word on the shapes a finding here can take, because they are different claims and I will not let them blur: most of the ten are *"the released code does something the published Methods do not describe"*; one of them — (h) — is *"the metric is computed exactly as described, and measures something narrower than its name suggests"*. Finding (i) has something of both characters, and the entry says so where it arises: the code computes exactly what it was written to compute, but what it was written to compute is not the published definition. Finding (j) is a third thing again — a consequence of the data model that the published Methods, as far as anything recorded here goes, never take up — and its entry opens by saying so. The naming kind is not a bug at all in the ordinary sense, and it is the most dangerous of them, because the number it produces is perfectly reproducible. A closing section, which follows the ten and is **not** one of them, applies the same standard to **our own** pipeline and finds it in breach: "The same standard, applied to our own pipeline".

### (a) The LD blocks and mappings are computed against the wrong genome build

`get_1000g_phased.sh` downloads the 1000 Genomes **Phase 3** release `20130502`, which is in **GRCh37** coordinates. The pangenome is **GRCh38**. Steps 8 and 10 then intersect them by base-pair coordinate.

**Measured in this project** on chr21: the strict (POS, REF, ALT) intersection gives **1,025** matches (0.30% of 340,824 records) against the GRCh37 Phase 3 panel, versus **258,610** (75.88%) against the same-build 30x GRCh38 panel — a **252× gap**. Two independent routes agree: PanMixer's own `get_mappings` and a direct `bcftools` comparison. The published Data availability section pins the same GRCh37 URL while the Methods state a GRCh38 backbone three times and the full published text contains zero mentions of GRCh37, liftover, 30x, high-coverage or NYGC — so the inconsistency is in the paper as well as the code, and the published version sharpens it rather than resolving it (the preprint had no Data availability section at all).

**What it teaches.** Genome coordinates are only meaningful relative to a named build. GRCh37 and GRCh38 differ by insertions, deletions and rearrangements, so the same base-pair number denotes different DNA. Joining two datasets on coordinate without checking the build is the genomics equivalent of joining two tables on an integer key that means different things in each — and, crucially, it fails *silently*, producing a small valid-looking join rather than an error. Concretely, it means block **boundaries** are placed using positions that do not correspond to the pangenome's sites, and the **attack database** and the **AF/LD utility site sets** are built on a near-empty intersection. A useful canary: print `len(pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased)` after `get_mappings`; near-zero confirms it.


> **Question** — "An earlier spec said INHERIT the plink blocks unchanged. But the blocks in the shipped pipeline were computed on GRCh37 positions. So which blocks are we inheriting — theirs, or ones we recomputed on the 30x GRCh38 panel? If it's the latter, 'inherit unchanged' is the wrong phrase."
>
> Two answers, and the second has overtaken the first. **Wherever blocks are used, it is the latter**, and the phrase to use is: *we inherit the method and recompute the artefact.* That keeps plink's block-finding procedure and the by-position site-to-block assignment, and it does **not** keep the shipped run's intervals, which were computed on GRCh37 coordinates that do not correspond to the graph: **1,025** exact (POS, REF, ALT) matches against the pangenome under Phase 3, against **258,610** under the build-matched 30x GRCh38 panel — a **252×** gap, confirmed by two independent routes (measured in this project).
>
> **But the mechanism no longer uses blocks at all.** Under the decided chain architecture, **model C**, the chain unit is the **individual variant**: one chain over the whole chromosome, no LD blocks, no plink run, and no external panel in the mechanism's input set (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). Blocks are retained for the model-B fallback and for parity runs against PanMixer, and it is in those places that "inherit the method, recompute the artefact" still has work to do. So the wording ambiguity is worth fixing wherever it survives — §4.13's preprocessing table, §4.14's item 1, and the Part B step table — with the scope attached: not "inherit unchanged", but "recomputed from the build-matched rebuild, **and used only in the comparison arm**".

> **Question** — "The 252× gap is the diagnostic. Is there a one-line canary we should put in our own pipeline so this can never happen to us?"
>
> Yes, and it is already named: after `get_mappings`, print `len(pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased)`. A near-zero length confirms a coordinate mismatch immediately. Expect **258,610** strict and **268,851** relaxed matches on chr21 under the build-matched panel, against **1,025** and **2,176** under Phase 3 (measured in this project).
>
> The reason a canary is the right instrument rather than a test is that nothing in the mismatch path **errors**. `pangenome_mask.npy` comes out tiny; `ld_loss` returns `(0.0, 0)`; every `af_loss` MAF stratum is `0/0`; and all of it is reported as a legitimate value (measured in this project). A silent coordinate failure produces numbers, which makes it the most dangerous class of defect in this pipeline — the lesson to carry rather than the finding itself.

> **Question** — "If the Methods say GRCh38 three times and the Data availability section pins a GRCh37 URL, which one did the published results come from? Can we tell from the numbers?"
>
> We cannot tell, and this is where the framing rule matters most. The inconsistency is in the **published paper**, not only in the code: the Methods pin `V_1000G` to *"the Phase 3 phased 1000 Genomes dataset"* as a subset of the **GRCh38** backbone (quoted from the published paper, page 8 lines 941-945), the Data availability section pins the exact **GRCh37** EBI URL for release 20130502 (quoted from the published paper, page 11), the backbone is stated as GRCh38 **three times**, and the full text contains **zero** mentions of GRCh37, liftover, 30x, high-coverage or NYGC (measured in this project, searching the PDF). The preprint had no Data availability section, so the published version **sharpens** the inconsistency rather than resolving it.
>
> There is also a reason no clean upstream fix exists: the official GRCh38 liftover of Phase 3 was **withdrawn in 2021**, with 1000 Genomes redirecting users to the 30x GRCh38 collection — which is the panel we substituted and the one the repository's own **unwired** `get_blocks_grch38.sh` downloads.
>
> What may be inferred, marked as inference: the repository shipping an unwired GRCh38 script and containing branches that prefer 3,202-sample artifacts is **consistent with** the authors having run a path the release does not wire in. That is not a measurement. The claim this project makes is the narrow one: **the released pipeline as documented does not reproduce the paper.** Resolving which panel is faithful may require asking the authors, and it is recorded as an open question, not a finding.

### (b) The Li–Stephens sampler never recombines, so the mosaic is inert

Four things compound in `starting_data/scripts/src/hmm.py` (and identically in the standalone `tools/panmixer/obfuscate.py`):

1. `effective_N = 1.0 / 10_000.0` — the **reciprocal** of the paper's stated N_e = 10,000. The published Fig. 6 gives d = Δx · N_e · r, with N_e in the numerator.
2. `get_anchor_snps` returns pairs `(snp, block[1][j])` where `snp` iterates over `block[1]` — the list of **VCF row indices**, not `block[0]`, the base-pair positions. So *both* columns of the returned array are row indices, and `get_transitions(pos_1, pos_2)` differences **row indices**. The paper specifies Δx in **centiMorgans**.
3. The scorer (`get_transitions`) computes `d = Δ/1e6 · effective_N · r` while the sampler (`get_transition_matrix_without_subject`) computes `d = Δ/1e6 · 4 · effective_N · r` — the two paths differ by a **factor of 4** that appears nowhere in the paper. Since Fig. 6 has no factor 4, the **sampler** is the deviation.
4. The non-anchor fill builds `anchor_pos_to_state` keyed on those row indices, then iterates `block_positions` (real base pairs) and tests `if pos in anchor_pos_to_state`. Comparing a base-pair coordinate against a row index, the guard **never fires**, so every site in the block — anchors included — falls through to the `np.argmin(np.abs(anchor_pos_array − pos))` fallback. With bp values on one side and row indices on the other, that argmin always selects the **last** anchor.

The arithmetic consequence: with row-index differences of order 1 to 10³, d lands around 10⁻¹⁰ to 10⁻⁷, so `exp(−d/86) ≈ 1`, so the switch probability q is on the order of 10⁻¹² per step. **The chain essentially never leaves its starting donor** — and even if it did, defect 4 would overwrite the whole block from a single donor anyway.

**Measured in this project (`docs/bugs.md`, `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18).** Executing the shipped `HaplotypeHMM` class directly: total switch mass **5.8 × 10⁻¹²** at a 1-index gap and 5.8 × 10⁻⁶ at a gap of 10⁶. With the paper's own Fig. 6 constants the switch mass would be **0.13–0.99**; that is a gap of ten to eleven orders of magnitude. And on **300 of 300 randomly chosen HMM-path blocks of real chr21 for subject HG00438**, the sampled block was **identical to a single donor haplotype copied verbatim**. (Blocks were chosen at random from the HMM-path set; the earlier synthetic-panel result reproduced on real data.)

**And there is a cost consequence, separate from the correctness one.** The two halves of this component have very different complexity, and an earlier characterisation of this project's own — that the per-target running time is **O(TK)** — was **retracted** for getting the sampler's half wrong (`docs/memory.md`, **Correction 1** of 2026-09-18).

> **Caution** — **the forward recursion is cheap; the sampler is not.** The **scorer's** forward recursion is genuinely **O(K) per step**, because the stay/switch form collapses the K×K matrix-vector product into a couple of vector operations plus one `logsumexp` (`obfuscate.py:140-152`, **measured in this project** by reading the pinned code). The **sampler** is **O(A·K²) in time and in memory**, where A is the number of anchors in the block, because `get_transition_probabilities_without_subject` **materialises every dense matrix for the block up front** rather than generating them as it walks (`obfuscate.py:117-124`, **measured in this project**). The loop that does it is four lines long, and it keeps every matrix it builds:
>
> `def get_transition_probabilities_without_subject(self, anchor_snps):` → `transitions = []`; `positions = anchor_snps[:, 0]`; `for i in range(len(positions) - 1): transitions.append(self.get_transition_matrix_without_subject(positions[i], positions[i + 1]))`; `return transitions`
>
> and each appended element is a full dense matrix built by `np.full((num_states_without_subject, num_states_without_subject), q)` with `p` written onto the diagonal (`obfuscate.py:99-108`, **measured in this project**). So a block with A anchors holds A − 1 matrices of K′ × K′ = 86 × 86 in memory at once, every entry of which takes one of **two** distinct values. The structure that makes the scorer O(K) is present in the sampler's matrices too and is simply not exploited. Keep this apart from the correctness findings above: the transition **constants** are wrong (points 1 to 4), and independently the transition **representation** is dense where it could be two scalars.

**What it teaches.** Everything Li–Stephens buys you comes from the *switching*. The mosaic is what makes the output a combination no individual possesses. Without switching, the released block is one other real person's real haplotype. That is not nothing — it does break the link to the target — but it is a categorically different object from a mosaic, and it means the released haplotype now carries a *different* individual's data verbatim, which is its own disclosure question. It is also a good lesson in why genetic distance is measured in centiMorgans rather than base pairs or row indices: recombination probability is a function of *genetic* map distance, and a row index is not even monotone in physical distance in any metric sense. The consequence for our own work is direct: "reuse PanMixer's cohort HMM" is not available as a plan. The log-space forward recursion is correct and O(K) per step and can be kept; the transition constants must be re-derived from Fig. 6 with a real genetic map.


> **Question** — "If the chain never switches, then their reported utility numbers are the numbers for 'copy one donor's block verbatim'. Does that make the published utility look better or worse than the method as designed? And when we fix the constants so it *does* recombine, should we expect our utility to get worse?"
>
> Both halves have honest answers and they point the same way, which is why the question is worth pre-empting.
>
> A verbatim donor block preserves **that donor's internal LD perfectly**, because it is a real haplotype that a real person carries. A true mosaic, by contrast, splices two donors mid-block and can create allele combinations that no single haplotype carries. So on a metric that measures LD fidelity, the degenerate sampler is **not** handicapped — if anything a verbatim-copying code path is advantaged on that axis relative to a recombining chain. Expect our LD numbers to be **worse**, not better, once the constants are corrected, and say so in advance rather than discovering it later.
>
> Frame the whole thing as released code versus published Methods: **we have not reproduced their figures**, so this is a prediction about what a corrected sampler should be expected to cost us, not a claim that their published utility is overstated. The measured basis for expecting the correction to bite at all: total switch mass **5.8e-12** at a one-index gap and **5.8e-6** at a gap of 10⁶ under the shipped constants, against **0.13–0.99** under the paper's own Fig. 6 constants — a gap of **10 to 11 orders of magnitude** — and **300 of 300** randomly chosen real chr21 HMM-path blocks returning a block identical to a single donor haplotype (measured in this project).

> **Question** — "Four compounding defects. Can you order them by how much each one alone would matter? If only the reciprocal N_e were wrong, would the chain still switch?"
>
> Ordered by how much each alone would do, from most to least decisive (all read from the pinned code, measured in this project):
>
> 1. **The non-anchor fill's guard never fires.** `anchor_pos_to_state` is keyed by row indices while the loop iterates base-pair positions, so the "is this an anchor" test never matches and `argmin` always selects the **last** anchor. This one alone overwrites the whole block from a single donor regardless of what the transitions do, so on the face of the code it should dominate the other three; **no measurement of the output with only this defect fixed exists in this project**, so treat the ordering as a reading of the code, not a measured ranking.
> 2. **Distance is a difference of VCF row indices.** `get_anchor_snps` returns `(snp, block[1][j])` with `snp == block[1][j]`, so the "distance" fed to the transition formula is a row-index difference where the published Methods specify **centiMorgans**. A row-index difference bears no fixed relation to genetic distance, so the transition probabilities are not on the right scale anywhere.
> 3. **`EFFECTIVE_N = 1.0/10_000.0`** is the **reciprocal** of N_e = 10,000. With d = Δx · N_e · r this understates d by a factor of **10⁸** (derived), which is most of the 10-to-11-order-of-magnitude gap. To answer the question directly: **fixing only this one would not be expected to make the chain switch** — defect 2 leaves the distance as a row-index difference, which is not a genetic distance at any scale, so correcting N_e alone does not put the transition probabilities on the scale the paper's Fig. 6 constants assume. **No measurement exists of the switch mass with one defect fixed and the others left in place**; the measured figures are for the shipped code as a whole (5.8e-12 at a one-index gap) and for the paper's Fig. 6 constants on a synthetic panel (0.13–0.99).
> 4. **A factor-4 divergence between scorer and sampler.** The forward pass and the sampler use d values a factor 4 apart; the paper's Fig. 6 has **no** factor 4, so the **sampler** is the deviation. On its own this is a factor of 4 on an already-dead quantity.
>
> Keep exactly one thing from this component: the **log-space forward recursion** (`obfuscate.py:140-152`) is correct and O(K) per step. The constants are not reusable; the algebra is.

The last two questions are about consequences rather than causes: what a verbatim donor block means for the donor, and what it takes to fix the constants at all.

> **Question** — "Releasing one other real person's haplotype verbatim — whose privacy problem is that? Have we just moved the disclosure from the target to a donor?"
>
> The question is fair and the answer is not fully settled, so treat it as an open question rather than a resolved one.
>
> What is clear: the donors are **public**. The cohort's membership and the cohort's haplotypes are published, so republishing a donor's block discloses no new sequence. What is less clear is the **pairing** — the released column says "this block of the target's genome looks exactly like donor j's", and the pairing of a named donor's block to a named target's release is not itself a published fact. Whether that constitutes a disclosure about the donor, about the target, or about neither is a question this project has not analysed.
>
> For our own mechanism the same issue exists in a weaker form and is worth stating as a design note rather than a defect: a tilted sampler that switches donors produces a mosaic, so no single donor's run of positions is republished intact, but the output is still built entirely from cohort material. The gate written against this requires precisely that a sampled segment be **demonstrably not a single donor copied verbatim** — the gate PanMixer's shipped sampler fails 300 times out of 300 (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).
>
> One place where our own chain came close to failing it is worth recording, because it is the reason the chr21 chain does not start at position 1. Below **12,968,320** bp, `np.interp`'s clamping plus the genetic map's own zero-cM centromeric plateau give **34,345 consecutive positions with identical cM** (measured in this project), so `P(switch) = 0` there and the sampler would emit one donor's real haplotype verbatim across **7.25 Mb** — departure (b) at roughly 200× the scale of the worst block. The decision (2026-09-28, closed at both ends 2026-09-29) is a **coordinate cut**: the chain runs over 12,968,320 < POS <= 46,680,243, keeping **305,887 of 340,824** chr21 variants (**89.75%**). That is flagged for revisit and is explicitly not a permanent answer (`docs/plan.md`).

> **Question** — "We need a GRCh38 genetic map to fix this and we do not have one. What is the interim, and does every number computed under the interim have to be labelled?"
>
> Yes, every number, without exception. The interim is **base-pair distance with a constant cM/Mb rate**, and the rule recorded with it is that the substitution must be **stated explicitly wherever a number computed under it is reported** (project decision, `docs/plan.md`).
>
> The acquisition gap is specific rather than vague. The map PanMixer's unwired script points at is the joepickrell `1000-genomes-genetic-maps` repository, offering `interpolated_from_hapmap` and `interpolated_OMNI` — both **GRCh37-era**. The likely correct source is the **SHAPEIT4 b38** maps, whose URL currently responds **302**; they are **not yet acquired or verified**, and this is an open task, not a decision.
>
> The consequence for the exit gates is worth naming: gate 3 requires a measured switch mass of order **0.1 to 1** at realistic separations. The **0.13–0.99** figure is what the paper's Fig. 6 constants *would* give, computed on a synthetic panel — not a measurement on chr21. ⚠ **Updated 2026-09-28.** The GRCh38 genetic map HAS since been acquired (`external/genetic_maps/chr21.b38.gmap`, 2026-09-27), and gate 3 is now recorded as **MIS-SPECIFIED** rather than merely unmeasurable: adjacent chain positions sit about 12 bp apart, where the biologically correct switch mass is the measured **0.0026**, so the gate as written would reject a correct model. `docs/plan.md` lists three candidate replacements and picks none.

### (c) The privacy scorer includes the target in its own reference panel

`forward_algorithm` uses `self.pangenome_haplotypes` — the concatenation of all 88 haplotypes, **including the target's own** — and `self.num_states` = 88, while normalising its transitions by the without-target count of 86. The sampler, by contrast, correctly uses `pangenome_haplotypes_without_subject` (86 states).

The consequence is a **ceiling**, and it needs to be stated carefully because the naive version of the statement is refuted by our own measurement. If the target's own haplotype is one of the states, then the model can always explain the observation by copying the target: pick that state (probability at least 1/2N = 1/88 under the uniform start) and match at every anchor (probability 1 − μ_mis per anchor, where **μ_mis = 10⁻⁴ is the HMM's per-anchor mismatch probability** — a fourth, unrelated use of the letter ε in the source, which is why I have renamed it). So

> p(h_bj) ≥ (1/88) · (1 − μ_mis)^n, where n is the number of anchors in the block,

and therefore

> ε_j = −log p(h_bj) ≲ log(88) + n·μ_mis = **4.4773 nats plus a small additive term**.

In words: **the block can never look much more surprising than log 88, no matter how rare it truly is, because the model always has the option of explaining the block as a copy of the target.** Note that this is a soft bound, not a hard cap: the additive per-anchor term means a measured value can legitimately sit slightly above 4.4773.

**Measured in this project** on **150 real HMM-path blocks for HG00438 on chr21** (`docs/bugs.md`; `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-18, Correction 3), by executing the shipped `HaplotypeHMM` class on real chr21 data: shipped ε_j spans **0.071 to 4.508**, median **1.242**, with only 9 of 150 within 0.05 nats of 4.4773. The maximum of **4.508 exceeds log(88) = 4.4773 by 0.031 nats**, which is exactly what the additive term predicts — 0.031 / 10⁻⁴ ≈ 300 anchors, an unremarkable size for a large block. So the measurement is *consistent with* the argument, not a counterexample to it. Re-scored with the target **removed** from the emission panel, the median moves only slightly, to **1.299**, but the **maximum rises to 78.1**. For the 9 ceiling-bound blocks the leave-one-out values have median 12.06 and maximum 78.12 — an understatement of up to **17.4×**.

An earlier version of this finding claimed ε_j was "pinned near log(2N) for essentially every block". That was **wrong** and is retracted; the retraction is recorded in `docs/memory.md` as Correction 3. The effect is a *ceiling*, not a *flattening*: typical blocks are barely affected (median ratio 1.03×) while the rarest are truncated hardest.

**What it teaches.** This is the most instructive defect in the set, because it is a *leave-one-out* error, and leave-one-out errors are endemic to privacy evaluation. The question "how surprising is this person's haplotype under a population model?" is only meaningful if the population model was not fitted on that person. Include them and you are instead asking "how surprising is this haplotype given that I already know someone has it", whose answer is "not very". Worse, the distortion is not uniform: it selectively flattens exactly the tail of the distribution that the whole mechanism exists to find. A knapsack that cannot see that block A is 17× more identifying than block B will not prioritise block A.

**What this does not establish.** Nothing about whether the *paper's* published numbers are affected. ε_j enters the LP only through a ranking, and we have not measured how much the selection changes when the ceiling is removed. The claim to make is the narrow one: the shipped scorer truncates rare blocks.


> **Question** — "Our target is external so this cannot happen — except we put 39 of 44 HPRC donors into branch-1 frequencies with the 30x repin. Is that the same error, and did we introduce it ourselves?"
>
> Yes to both, with one boundary drawn first, because the question runs two objects together. For the **deployed mechanism** the target really is external and this really cannot happen — no panel, frequency table or support count has a row for a person who is in no dataset. What the repin contaminated is the **benchmark**: the head-to-head on PanMixer's data, where a cohort member stands in for a target we do not have, and externality is *simulated* rather than given. The primer should say so in this section rather than leave any "target-independence comes nearly free" sentence unqualified — it is nearly free by construction in the deployed setting, and it is something that has to be actively maintained in the stand-in setting.
>
> The two are the same **shape** of error — an artefact used to score the target is computed from data that includes the target — arriving by different routes. Theirs is in the code: the forward panel is `self.pangenome_haplotypes`, all **88** haplotypes including the target, while transitions are normalised by the without-target count of **86** (measured in this project, `obfuscate.py:130,132`). Ours is in the data we substituted: **39 of 44** HPRC donors, including HG00438, are in the 30x panel that branch 1 of `get_af.py` counts from, against **0 of 44** in Phase 3 (measured in this project).
>
> The severity differs, though. For PanMixer it is a stronger version of a self-inclusion their code already has in branches 2 and 3. **For our benchmark it breaks the externality that arm exists to simulate**, and it is a break this project introduced. The fix, which is a comparison-arm fix, is that any leave-one-out cohort must drop the stand-in from the **1000G panel** as well as from the pangenome — and a full leave-one-out means recomputing the donor panel, the allele frequencies, the support counts, the site list **and** the block assignment without that individual. There is no visible symptom to catch it by: the frequencies look normal. Standing constraint 1 itself is not at risk in the deployed mechanism, where it holds by construction. The closing section, "The same standard, applied to our own pipeline", works the whole thing through.
>
> One hard limit to state in any write-up, because it bounds how far a leave-one-out can take us on their data: the PGGB graph **topology** was built from all HPRC assemblies, so even a full leave-one-out only **approximates** our external-target threat model on PanMixer's cohort. Never claim full externality on their data.

> **Question** — "The ceiling is log(88) = 4.4773 nats plus n·μ_mis. Show me the arithmetic for a block with 300 anchors, and show me why 4.508 is consistent rather than a counterexample."
>
> The ceiling comes from a probability floor. Because the target's own haplotype is in the emission panel, the forward algorithm can always explain the target's block by copying the target itself, which costs one uniform start-state choice, 1/88, times a per-anchor no-mismatch factor of (1 − μ_mis) at each of n anchors, with μ_mis = **1e-4** (measured in this project, reading the code). So
>
> **p(h_bj) ≥ (1/88)·(1 − 1e-4)^n**, hence **ε_j ≤ log 88 + n·1e-4** to first order (derived).
>
> For n = 300 anchors: log 88 = **4.4773** nats, and the additive term is 300 × 1e-4 = **0.03** nats, giving a ceiling of about **4.507** (derived).
>
> The measured maximum over 150 real HMM-path blocks is **4.508** nats, which exceeds log 88 by **0.031** (measured in this project). Divide that excess by the per-anchor term: 0.031 / 1e-4 ≈ **300 anchors** (derived) — exactly the kind of block the additive term predicts. So 4.508 is not a block that broke the ceiling; it is a block with about 300 anchors sitting **on** it. That is what makes the bound a soft ceiling rather than a hard one, and it is why the number has to be quoted as "log 88 plus a small additive term proportional to the number of anchors" rather than as 4.4773 flat.

> **Question** — "Leave-one-out moves the median from 1.242 to 1.299 but the maximum from 4.508 to 78.1. So the ranking changes only at the top. Does the knapsack's *selection* actually change? By how much?"
>
> **We have not measured that, and the honest answer is that we cannot say.** ε_j enters the LP only through a **ranking** — the solver buys in descending order of ε_j/η_j — so a distortion that leaves the ranking intact changes nothing about the release, and a distortion concentrated at the top of the ranking could change quite a lot. The measurements we have are of the scores, not of the solution: over 150 real HMM-path blocks the shipped ε_j runs **0.071 to 4.508** with median **1.242**, only **9 of 150** within 0.05 nats of the 4.4773 ceiling; with the target removed from the emission panel the median moves to **1.299** — a ratio of **1.03×** — while the maximum moves to **78.1 nats**, and for the nine ceiling-bound blocks the leave-one-out median and maximum are **12.06** and **78.12**, an understatement of up to **17.4×** (all measured in this project).
>
> That shape matters more than the magnitude: it is a **ceiling, not a flattening**. An earlier version of this finding claimed ε_j was "pinned near log(2N) for essentially every block", and that was wrong and is retracted (Correction 3). Typical blocks are barely affected; the rarest are truncated hardest, which means **the knapsack systematically under-values the highest-risk blocks**.
>
> Re-solving the LP with the ceiling removed is a measurement this project owes, and until it exists no claim can be made about the effect on anyone's frontier.

### (d) The "anchor" set is PanGenie, not 1000 Genomes

`hmm.py` loads `pangenome_to_thousand_g_alignments.pickle` into a variable named `self.snp_positions`, with the comment `#all alignments are snps`, and uses its keys as the anchor set. But `get_mappings.py` binds `thousand_g_alignments` to **`PG.vcf.gz`** — the PanGenie callset. So the variable named after 1000 Genomes is bound to PanGenie, while the paper defines anchors as **V₁₀₀₀G** — "Established variants from the Phase 3 phased 1000 Genomes dataset, used as trusted anchor points." The actual 1000G map is a different file, `pangenome_to_thousand_g_phased.pickle`, which `hmm.py` never opens.

There is an important bound on the blast radius here, and it corrects an inference this project itself got wrong once. PanGenie **is** GRCh38, so the anchor mapping is a same-build join and is **not** corrupted by departure (a). Consequently the **per-block routing decision** — HMM path versus allele-frequency path — is *not* degraded by the build mismatch. What departure (a) *does* corrupt is block **boundaries** (plink runs on the 1000G panel), the **attack database**, and the **AF/LD utility site sets**. Two different notions of "anchor" are in play; do not conflate them.

**Measured in this project (recorded in `docs/plan.md`'s comparison table):** **273,475 anchor variants** on our rebuilt chr21, with **10.4% of block-dictionary entries** reaching the ≥2-anchor threshold. Note that this count is not the same object as the 258,610 strict 1000G matches (a different callset) or the 384,720 PanGenie chr21 records (records in the source file, before intersecting with the pangenome), so the three figures are not expected to agree and comparing them is a category error.

**What it teaches.** Two things. The mundane one: **a variable name is not a type**, and a comment asserting a fact is not the fact. This one cost this project a wrong inference before it was caught. The more interesting one: "anchor" is a design choice with real consequences. Anchors determine which blocks are rich enough to model with an HMM and which fall back to independent allele-frequency sampling — and since the HMM path is the only path that respects LD, the anchor set decides where LD is preserved. Whether PanGenie or 1000 Genomes is the *better* anchor set is a genuinely open question and this project has not answered it. The defect is that the code does not do what the paper says.


> **Question** — "I still don't see why anchors exist. If we drop them and use every variant as a chain position, what do we actually lose?"
>
> The designer's reason first, because it is a real one. The published Methods justify the threshold in these words: *"dependent on the number of top-level SNPs that exist inside the LD block. If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM, and we can synthesize more accurate new haplotype blocks. If only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM"* (quoted from the published paper). The **≥ 2** part is mechanical: a Markov chain needs two positions before there is a transition between them. The **top-level SNP** part is about data quality, and the measurement supports it — on chr21, top-level SNPs are missing in a mean **2.25%** of haplotypes, top-level non-SNPs **3.11%**, and **nested** records **22.53%**, with 3,515 nested records more than half uncalled (measured in this project). Nested variants are about **ten times** more often uncalled. That is the real justification for restricting chain positions, and it is not the justification the code implements.
>
> Now the cost of dropping the restriction, which is the number that answers your question. The mean missingness at the chain positions PanMixer's HMM actually steps over — anchors inside blocks holding two or more anchors — is **0.26%**; across **every** variant, which is what v1 makes a chain position, it is **4.26%**, with **30,800** records over 25% missing. Worst is the class v1 adds *inside* blocks PanMixer already chains — the non-anchor records of multi-variant blocks — at **11.31%**, with **7,555** over 25% missing (all measured in this project; the per-class table is in `docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-26). **Each of those means is over a different set of positions; none of them is quotable without naming its set.**
>
> So what we lose is not accuracy in the abstract: we lose the guarantee that a chain position has data. That is precisely why `missing_policy` exists as a first-class decision, and why it is **reserved** rather than chosen — the recorded leaning is RENORMALISE with nothing decided. Two rationales the code might have offered do **not** hold up, incidentally: compute is not it (273,475 anchors against 340,824 variants is only **1.25×** more positions, 24.1M against 30.0M operations at K = 88), and multi-allelic emissions are not it either (the median ALT count is 1 even for non-SNP records). §A.8 is the full treatment.

> **Question** — "273,475 anchors, 258,610 strict 1000G matches, 384,720 PanGenie chr21 records — you say comparing them is a category error. Say it again with the denominators, because they look comparable."
>
> They are three counts of three different things, and here is each one with what it counts (all measured in this project):
>
> | count | what it counts | denominator it lives in |
> |---|---|---|
> | **273,475** | pangenome chr21 records that also appear in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset, matched on (POS, REF, whole ALT) — i.e. **anchors after intersecting with the pangenome** | 340,824 pangenome chr21 records |
> | **258,610** | pangenome chr21 records with an exact (POS, REF, ALT) match into the **1000 Genomes 30x** panel — a **different callset**, and the basis of `pangenome_mask.npy` | 340,824 pangenome chr21 records |
> | **384,720** | records in the **PanGenie source file** on chr21, **before** any intersection with the pangenome | the PanGenie callset itself |
>
> So the first two share a denominator and measure membership in two unrelated callsets; the third is a file size and shares no denominator with either. The comparison that *is* meaningful is the first against the paper's own definition: the paper's anchors are top-level SNPs, of which chr21 has **253,000**, and the code's 273,475 overlap them in **234,532** records — leaving **38,943** code anchors that are not top-level SNPs and **18,468** top-level SNPs that are not code anchors (measured in this project).

> **Question** — "We keep the anchor rule as a `missing_policy` option because it is the published behaviour. Does that option change T, or only the emission?"
>
> It changes **T**, which is why it is not merely an emission policy and why it has to be handled as a first-class option rather than a flag.
>
> The four options are RENORMALISE (drop a donor from the state distribution at a position where it has no call), WILDCARD (treat missing as matching whatever the target has), MISMATCH (treat missing as a difference), and RESTRICT (exclude ill-behaved positions from the chain altogether). The first three leave the chain's positions alone and change how a position is scored. **RESTRICT removes positions**, so the chain is shorter and T is different — and RESTRICT is, in effect, what PanMixer's anchor rule does. It is retained deliberately, because it is the published behaviour and therefore the only option that can be compared against published numbers.
>
> Two consequences follow. First, a run under RESTRICT is comparable only against another run under RESTRICT: changing T changes every position's share of the budget — `beta_t = 1/T` under the uniform weighting, or `beta_v = w_v / Σ_u w_u` under model C's support weighting — so it changes the per-position tilt as well as the chain. Second, RENORMALISE has its own cost, on the computational side rather than the comparability side: perturbing the per-position state set breaks the stay/switch collapse that makes the forward pass O(K) per step, because the transition matrix is no longer one value on the diagonal and one off it (derived; see §4.10.2). None of this decides the question — the policy is reserved, and the v1 exit gates require only that it be **chosen, implemented, and covered by a test that fails if the behaviour changes**.

### (e) The gap-score attack does not exclude the target from its own maximum

The published equation (7) is explicit: GapScore = L(g_target, g\*_target) − **max_{i ≠ target}** L(g_i, g\*_target). The code at `tools/downstream/privacy/gap_score.py` computes `highest_score_genotypes = np.max(scores_genotypes)` over the **entire** attack database, with no exclusion. The code is aware this can matter — `diploid_gap_score.py` contains a debug branch that checks whether `subject_name` appears in the attack database's sample list and prints its rank, and `tools/common/utils.py` does delete the self row, but only when the name is present in the panel's sample list, so which panel is loaded determines whether it happens at all — yet the exclusion is never applied to the score written to `gap_score_all.json`.

**What it teaches.** Whether this changes anything depends on whether the target is present in the panel being used as the attack database — and HPRC individuals largely do appear in the 1000 Genomes 30x release. If the target *is* in the database, the maximum can be the target's own entry, which mechanically drives the gap score negative and makes the attack look defeated when it was not. Since ε_private is *defined* as the threshold where the gap score goes negative for everyone, this is the quantity on which the entire empirical privacy claim is calibrated. The general lesson is the same as (c) from the other direction: **in privacy evaluation, who is and is not in the comparison set is not a detail, it is the definition.** Anyone attempting to reproduce the published 0.002 must exclude the target row before taking the maximum, and must pin which attack database was used — a denser panel is a strictly stronger attacker, so the choice moves the privacy axis.

A related operational hazard, recorded in `docs/bugs.md`: `apply_mask.sbatch` writes the masked attack database but is **not referenced** by `run_get_starting_data_pipeline.sh`, and `diploid_gap_score.py` prefers 30x-sample attack-database files that **no script in the repository produces**, while `constants.py` hardcodes `NUM_1000G_SUBJECTS = 3202` although the shipped pipeline downloads 2,504. Following the README end to end therefore either fails to find the attack database or silently falls back to a different, weaker one. This is the dangerous kind of defect: it still yields numbers.


> **Question** — "If the target can be its own maximum, the attack is *defeating itself*. And ε_private is defined as where the gap score goes negative. So the operating point is calibrated on an attack that can fail for a reason unrelated to obfuscation. Can we say how much that moves 0.002?"
>
> **No, and that limit is not a hedge — it is the whole discipline of this section.** We have not reproduced the published number, we cannot say which code path or which attack database produced it, and therefore nothing licenses a claim that 0.002 should be a different number.
>
> What can be stated has two parts. The **narrow finding**: the released code takes a plain `np.max` over the entire attack database at `gap_score.py:241-242`, while published equation (7) subtracts `max_{i ≠ target}` — so the released code differs from the published Methods (measured in this project; quoted from the published paper). The **conditional direction**: if the target's own database entry can win that max, the self-score minus the max goes **negative**, and negative is what the pipeline reads as "the attack failed" — so a self-max makes obfuscation look more effective than it was, at whatever capacity it happens at. That is a direction, not a magnitude, and it should be written as a direction.
>
> For our own head-to-head this is not optional to fix: the gap score is our best comparison instrument, and an attacker that can defeat itself is not an attacker we can calibrate two mechanisms against.
>
> One thing not to conclude from our target being external. It does **not** follow that the exclusion is moot on our side. A linkage evaluation is only defined if the attacker holds the target as a candidate at all — a gap score against a database that does not contain the person is not a weaker attack, it is a different question — so the attack database has to carry the target as a candidate whatever their relationship to the cohort. On that reading `max_{i ≠ target}` remains the right estimator for our arm as a property of **the metric**, not as a consequence of cohort membership. Read this as the weaker claim it is: the primer is not asserting that nothing is excluded on our side.

> **Question** — "`utils.py` deletes the self row but only if the name is in the panel's sample list. So whether the exclusion happens depends on which panel is loaded — and the panel the README downloads is not the one `diploid_gap_score.py` prefers. Does following the README produce numbers at all?"
>
> It produces numbers, and that is exactly what makes this the dangerous kind of defect. The pieces, all measured in this project by reading the pinned checkout: `tools/common/utils.py:357-361` **does** delete the self row, but only when the target's name is present in the loaded panel's sample list; `constants.py:38` hardcodes `NUM_1000G_SUBJECTS = 3202` while the shipped pipeline downloads **2,504**; `apply_mask.sbatch` is **not referenced** by `run_get_starting_data_pipeline.sh`; and the 30x attack-database `.npy` files that the diploid path prefers are produced by **no released script**.
>
> So reproducing ε_private = 0.002 is **blocked** in this project on attack-database files that do not exist and cannot be generated from the release. Following the README gives you a pipeline that runs, a gap score that is computed, and an exclusion that may or may not have happened depending on a name lookup — with no error either way. The canary-style discipline from departure (a) applies here too: assert that the self row was deleted, rather than assuming the lookup found it.

> **Question** — "We have to fix this before any head-to-head. Do we also re-run *their* arm with the correction, and do we report both corrected and as-published?"
>
> This was raised in the working session and **never resolved**, so it is an open decision, not a settled one, and the primer should say so.
>
> The recommendation on the table is: run **both arms leave-one-out**, and additionally report PanMixer's **as-published** numbers for continuity, so that the cost of the correction is visible rather than absorbed. The argument for it is that a corrected attack is the only instrument that measures the same thing in both arms, and the argument for keeping the as-published column is that readers will want to locate our numbers relative to the published ones.
>
> Two constraints on the design are already fixed and bear on it. The arms are aligned **only** on measured empirical attack success — `eps_pmi` and τ are formally incomparable and so are capacity and τ, with no conversion between them — and both frontiers are reported (project decisions, `docs/plan.md`). And every operating point gets **R ≥ 10 seeds per (subject, level) in both arms**, against PanMixer's published frontier resting on one presampled draw reused across capacities.

### (f) The scoring and sampling branches disagree about the anchor threshold

This is one of the smaller findings by consequence and the easiest to see, so it is a good one to read first.

**What the paper says.** There is *one* rule for each LD block, and the rule is a capability test. Quoted from the published Methods: *"dependent on the number of top-level SNPs that exist inside the LD block. If 2 or more exist, enough SNPs exist to build an HMM, and we can synthesize more accurate new haplotype blocks. If only one top-level SNP exists, we do not have enough useful information to build an HMM."* The reasoning is sound and worth spelling out because it is the whole justification for the threshold: a Markov chain is a statement about *transitions*, and a transition needs a **from** position and a **to** position. With one position there is no pair, so there is nothing for the transition matrix to act on and the chain degenerates to a single draw (derived). Hence: two or more positions, build the HMM; fewer, fall back to sampling each variant independently from its allele frequency.

One caution before going further, because `docs/terms.md` records conflating these two sets as one of this project's first misreadings: the **paper** counts **top-level SNPs** and the **code** counts **anchors**, and they are different sets — **38,943** of the code's **273,475** anchors are not top-level SNPs, and **18,468** top-level SNPs are not anchors (both measured in this project; see departure (d)). What carries over from the paper is the *rationale* for the threshold, not the set it is counted over. Everything below is about the code's threshold, on anchors.

**What the code does.** It applies that test **twice, with two different thresholds**, in two adjacent functions of the same file.

- **Scoring.** `get_support_and_pmi.py:87` sends a block to the HMM path when it has **two or more variants and at least one** anchor. A single-variant block, and a multi-variant block with no anchor at all, are summed from the per-site allele-frequency values instead — three branches, not two (measured in this project, read from the pinned source; the three branches are set out in Step 14b).
- **Sampling.** `get_support_and_pmi.py:159` guards the allele-frequency path with `len(block_indices) == 1 or len(anchor_snps_idx) <= 1`, which sends a block to the HMM only at **two or more** anchors (measured in this project, read from the pinned source).

The two guards are **not complements of each other**, and that phrasing matters, because the intuitive expectation when you read two `if` statements about the same quantity is that they partition the blocks the same way. They do not. Read as conditions on the anchor count *a* and the variant count *n*:

| metric | the scoring route | the sampling route |
|---|---|---|
| condition for the HMM path | `n >= 2` and `a >= 1` | `n >= 2` and `a >= 2` |
| condition for the allele-frequency path | `n == 1` or `a == 0` | `n == 1` or `a <= 1` |
| blocks on the HMM path, chr21 | **10,954** (measured in this project) | **10,502** (measured in this project) |
| blocks routed one way here and the other way there | — | **452** (measured in this project) |

All three block counts are measured on our rebuilt chr21, subject HG00438, and are properties of the block structure rather than of any particular run, so they do not depend on `capacity`. The 452 blocks are **0.45% of the 100,757 block-dictionary entries** and **4.1% of the 10,954 HMM-scored blocks** (both derived). The disagreeing set is exactly the blocks with two or more variants of which **exactly one** is an anchor.

> **Question** — *"Are those 452 blocks big or small? If they are all two-variant blocks it is a footnote; if they are the big ones it is not."* **Unmeasured, and the distinction you are drawing is the right one.** What this project has is the count — **452** of the **100,757** block-dictionary entries (measured in this project) — plus the structural fact that every member of the set has two or more variants of which exactly one is an anchor, so none of them can be a singleton (derived). Their variant counts and their base-pair spans have **not** been tabulated. What would settle it is one pass over the chr21 block dictionary, tabulating the number of variants and the `positions[]` span for exactly those 452 entries. Until that is run, read this finding as bounded by its **count** and not by its **mass** — the 4.1%-of-HMM-scored-blocks figure says how many decisions disagree, not how much sequence sits behind them.

> **Worked example** — take a hypothetical block with two variants, one of which is an anchor. On the **scoring** side, `a = 1 >= 1`, so `eps_j` comes from `forward_algorithm`, running over the anchor list only — which here is a chain of length one. A chain of length one uses the start distribution and the emissions and **never uses the transition matrix at all**, so the "HMM score" for this block is a mixture over donors at a single position (derived). On the **sampling** side, `a = 1 <= 1`, so the replacement for *both* variants is drawn independently from their own allele-frequency rows (derived). The released content for this block therefore comes from a model of independent sites, while the privacy credit booked for it comes from a copying model — and, because of finding (i) below, from a copying model that looked at one of the block's two variants and ignored the other.

**How it was measured.** Both thresholds were read directly out of the pinned checkout `external/PanMixer` at commit `c182c38d5bc8bb6f00f4b0b101207c4a009ca045`. The three block counts were obtained by re-deriving each guard's truth value over every entry of the chr21 block dictionary and tabulating the disagreement (measured in this project; `docs/bugs.md`, the entry recording the threshold disagreement). Severity is recorded there as **MEDIUM**: it is real, it is small, and it is a category error rather than an arithmetic one.

**What it means for anyone reusing the tool.** Two things.

First, if you are auditing a PanMixer run, **`eps_j` and the released alleles for a given block are not guaranteed to come from the same model.** For 452 chr21 blocks they demonstrably do not. Any statement of the form "this block's privacy score reflects the haplotype structure of what we released" is false for those blocks. This does not mean the score is *larger* or *smaller* than it should be — we have not measured a direction and it is not obvious there is a systematic one. It means the score and the release are answers to two different questions.

Second, for our own design the problem is removed by construction rather than fixed. The earlier specification (`A-IMP-v1-sampler`, `docs/plan.md`) routed **every** block through one sampler, treating a single-variant block as a chain with T = 1; the decided chain architecture, **model C**, goes further and has no blocks and no singleton case at all — one chain over the whole chromosome with the individual variant as the unit (**project decision**, `docs/plan.md`). One code path means one threshold means no disagreement. Note what that decision costs elsewhere, though: it is the same decision that makes every variant a chain position, which is what raises mean missingness at chain positions from **0.26%** to **11.31%** (both measured in this project) and is why `missing_policy` has to be decided at all — see §A.10, and `missing_policy` in `docs/terms.md`.

> **Question** — *"If the two guards disagree on only 452 of 100,757 blocks, why is this in the list at all?"* Because the *size* of a finding and the *kind* of a finding are independent, and this one is cheap to state and permanently useful as a diagnostic. If you ever see a PanMixer block whose `pmi_blocks` entry looks like a haplotype-model number while its released alleles look like independent draws, you now know why without having to re-derive it. It also makes a general point that recurs in (i): in this codebase, **the routing decision is made more than once, in more than one place, from more than one condition**, and the places do not agree. That is a maintenance property of the code, not a property of the method.

> **Question** — *"Which of the two thresholds is the one the paper meant?"* The sampling one, `a >= 2`. The published rationale is explicitly about having enough positions to build a chain, and a chain needs two (quoted from the published paper, plus the derived reading above). So the *scorer* is the deviation here. That is the opposite of departure (b), where the *sampler* was the deviation because the paper's Fig. 6 carries no factor of 4. Do not assume the same side is wrong twice.

### (g) Selected moves that change nothing still book their full privacy score

**What the paper says.** The published text (p. 10, lines 653–654) describes the candidate as constrained to **differ** from the target's own block — the replacement is supposed to be a different haplotype, not possibly the same one (quoted from the published paper).

**What the code does.** `sample_block_prior` is an **unconditional prior draw**. It walks the chain (or the allele-frequency rows), writes out a candidate, and **never compares the candidate to the target's original block**. There is no rejection loop and no re-draw (measured in this project, read from the pinned source; recorded in `docs/bugs.md`). Step 14c already notes this in passing; here is the measured consequence.

Now trace the arithmetic, because the interesting part is that the two axes behave differently.

- `eps_j` is computed from the target's **original** block, before any candidate exists (see §A.4). It therefore does not know, and cannot know, what the candidate turned out to be. A no-op candidate carries the block's full `eps_j`.
- `eta_j` is computed as `sum(utility_loss * (new != orig))` — a sum restricted to the sites where the candidate **actually differs**. For a candidate identical to the original that mask is all-false, so `eta_j` is **exactly 0.00000** (measured in this project).

So a no-op move is, in the optimizer's eyes, an item with **positive value and zero weight**. A 0–1 knapsack takes every such item unconditionally: it cannot be excluded by the budget, because it consumes none of it. No tie-breaking or rounding is involved; it falls straight out of the LP (derived).

> **Question** — *"A no-op is the extreme case. What about a move that changes one site out of five — does it get the block's whole `eps_j` for one site's worth of work?"* Yes, and that is the honest generalisation of this finding. `eps_j` is a **per-block** quantity charged in full the moment the block is selected (§A.4), while `eta_j` is a **per-site** sum restricted to the sites that actually differ (§A.5). The gap between credit booked and work done is therefore **continuous**, and the no-op is only its endpoint — the point where the charged weight reaches exactly zero and the item becomes unrefusable. What has been measured is the two ends: at **capacity 0.1**, seed 123, **32,544** selected moves changed something and **14,526** changed nothing (the first derived, the second measured in this project). The distribution *inside* the 32,544 — how many of a block's variants a typical changed move actually alters — is **not measured**. Getting it means one pass over `new_haplotypes.npy` against the original matrix, counting changed sites per selected block. Note that the direction of the bias is not in doubt even without that number: a partially-changing move is over-credited relative to its edit, never under-credited (derived).

**How it was measured, and the magnitude.** One run: subject HG00438, chr21, **capacity 0.1**, seed 123, on our 30x GRCh38 rebuild. The selected move set was compared element-wise against the original haplotypes to count moves whose released alleles were identical to the target's.

| metric | moves that changed something | moves that changed nothing |
|---|---|---|
| count (of 47,070 selected moves, capacity 0.1) | **32,544** (derived) | **14,526** (measured in this project) |
| share of selected moves | **69.1%** (derived) | **30.9%** (derived) |
| median `eps_j`, nats | **2.1904** (measured in this project) | **0.3898** (measured in this project) |
| mean `eps_j`, nats | **3.3103** (measured in this project) | **0.5964** (measured in this project) |
| `eta_j` charged | `Σ 1/support(v)` over changed sites | **exactly 0.00000** (measured in this project) |
| share of the run's total `eps` | **93.0%** (derived) | **7.0%** (measured in this project) |

The two `eps_j` distributions come from a random sample of **3,000** of the 47,070 selected moves — 2,112 that changed something and 888 that did not (measured in this project). The run's reported `pmi_gain` was **115,315.84** (measured in this project). All six numbers in the table are specific to **capacity 0.1**, and the no-op share has not been re-measured at any other capacity. The move set itself changes with capacity: at capacity 0.5 the same subject and chromosome spend **33.1%** of the budget denominator rather than the requested 50%, because the knapsack saturates — every move with positive value already fits (measured in this project; see §1.2.4 and Step 17). Nothing in the table above has been recomputed there.

> **Caution** — the two headline percentages here count **different things** and an earlier draft of this project's own notes conflated them. **30.9% is a share of moves. 7.0% is a share of privacy credit.** Writing "the privacy score is inflated by 31%" is **wrong**, and the correction is recorded as a follow-up under 2026-09-24 in `docs/memory.md`. The correct sentence is the pair: *30.9% of selected moves change nothing, and those moves carry 7.0% of the reported privacy credit.* The gap between the two figures is the finding's most informative feature, not a rounding difference — see the next paragraph.

**Why the two figures are so far apart.** Because no-ops are not drawn uniformly from the blocks. A candidate accidentally equals the original exactly when the donor the sampler happened to copy carries the same alleles the target carries — which is most likely at blocks whose allele pattern is *common*. Common patterns are, by the definition of `eps_j` as surprisal, precisely the **low-scoring** blocks (derived; the measured medians 0.3898 against 2.1904 nats confirm it). So the no-ops cluster at the cheap end of the value distribution: a third of the moves, but a fourteenth of the value.

> **Worked example** — **block 1077, the case traced end to end.** Rows **34816–34820** of our rebuilt chr21, spanning POS **13,000,333 – 13,000,621**: **5 variants, all 5 of them anchors**, so the block takes the HMM branch on both the scoring and the sampling side (all measured in this project). The target HG00438 carries `[0,0,0,0,0]` on both strands. Its `eps_j` is **0.0711 nats** per strand. `support(v)` is **87 of 88** at every one of the five variants, so each variant's utility weight is `1/87` = **0.01149**, and if all five alleles changed the block would cost `eta_j` = **0.05747** (measured in this project, except the last figure, which is derived). What the sampler actually returned was `[0,0,0,0,0]` — **identical to the target** — because the degenerate transitions of departure (b) copy one donor verbatim across the block and the donor drawn happened to carry the same common alleles. Changed variants: **0**. `eta_j`: **0.00000**. The LP took the move on both strands (`xsol[625] = 1` for both), and the released alleles at those five rows are the target's own, unchanged (measured in this project).
>
> The cross-check that makes `eps_j` legible: **82 of the 88 cohort haplotypes carry exactly the target's `[0,0,0,0,0]` pattern** (measured in this project), and `−log(82/88) = 0.0706` (derived), which matches the measured **0.0711** to within the HMM's per-anchor mismatch term. That agreement is the best single confirmation in this document that `eps_j` really does read as *the surprisal of the target's own block under the cohort model* — see §A.4.1. The full trace of this block through every step is the *Worked trace* after Step 19.

**What it means for anyone reusing the tool.** Three things, in increasing order of importance.

First, **the utility axis is honest and the privacy axis is not.** `eta_j = 0` for a no-op is arithmetically correct — nothing changed, so no allele frequency moved, so no utility was lost. It is `eps_j` that is charged for work not done. So the defect inflates the **numerator** of the privacy-per-utility ratio, and therefore the ratio, and therefore the apparent position of any frontier re-derived from these outputs. It does not inflate the reported utility loss. If you are re-deriving a frontier from PanMixer outputs, the utility column can be taken at face value and the privacy column cannot.

Second, this **compounds** departure (b) rather than being independent of it. The sampler's near-zero switch probability means every sampled block is one donor's block copied verbatim (measured in this project: 300 of 300 randomly chosen HMM-path blocks). The more often the sampler hands back a single common donor's alleles, the more often those alleles coincide with the target's, and the more free privacy credit the LP books. Fix the transition constants and this finding shrinks on its own — by how much is **not measured**.

Third, the fix for our own reporting is specific and cheap: **report the measured allele change, never `pmi_gain`, as the privacy numerator** (`docs/bugs.md`). A count of moves selected is not a count of moves that did anything, and the difference is 14,526 moves at capacity 0.1.

> **Question** — *"So is the published privacy–utility frontier overstated by 7%?"* We cannot say that, and it is worth being precise about why. `eps_j` is a per-block score that enters the LP objective; the published operating point `ε_private = 0.002` is a threshold located empirically on a **gap-score attack**, not a sum of `eps_j` values (quoted from the published paper, §A.7). The 7.0% figure is a statement about **the objective value of one run we executed ourselves**, at capacity 0.1 on our own rebuild with a substituted panel. It says the reported `pmi_gain` overstates the privacy work actually performed in that run. Connecting that to the published frontier would require reproducing the published figures, which we have not done.

> **Question** — *"Why does `eps_j` not just get recomputed after the candidate is drawn?"* Because of what `eps_j` is defined to be. It is `−log p(h_bj)` — the self-information of the **target's original block** — which is the thing being *removed* from the release, and that quantity genuinely does not depend on the replacement (see §A.4's two-case argument). The defect is not that the formula is evaluated on the wrong input; it is that a *credit* for removing information is booked for a move that removed none. The natural repair is not to change `eps_j` but to zero the whole item when the candidate equals the original — or, better, to stop generating no-op candidates, which is what the published Methods say happens.

> **Question** — *"Our sampler resamples everything, so it will sometimes hand back the target's own alleles too. Is that the same defect for us, or is it an opportunity?"* Neither, and the reason is worth stating precisely, because the two mechanisms book credit in completely different places. Our design has **no per-block privacy score and no knapsack** — under model C it has no blocks at all: every **chain position** is resampled from the tilted distribution and nothing is selected, so there is no item to charge an `eps_j` to and therefore no free credit to book (`docs/plan.md`, `A-IMP-v1-sampler`). A draw that happens to reproduce the target's alleles at a position is not an accounting error in our arm — it is an ordinary outcome of the sampler, and at any `tau` > 0 it is an outcome the tilt makes *deliberately more likely*, because the tilt rewards candidates that resemble the target (§4.6). Nor can we forbid it: a requirement that the drawn path differ from the target's path would be a target-dependent restriction of the candidate set, and that is precisely what our own first standing constraint rules out — see the closing section, "The same standard, applied to our own pipeline". What we do owe is the measurement, namely how often a draw reproduces the target's own alleles at each `tau`. **No `tau` has been run end to end**, so that number does not exist yet (`docs/plan.md`).

### (h) The per-record utility accounting cannot see the quantity of sequence rewritten

This finding is of the second kind named in the framing paragraph: **the code computes exactly what the paper describes.** Nothing here is an implementation error. The claim is about what the metric *measures*, and the reason to give it this much space is that the name invites a reading the quantity does not support.

**What the paper says.** The per-block utility cost is `η_j = Σ_{v ∈ Δ} 1/support(v)`, the sum taken over the variants the move alters, with `support(v)` the number of haplotypes with a called (non-missing) allele at *v*. Supplementary Remark 1 proves that this quantity equals the **induced L1 change in allele frequency** (quoted from the published paper; see §A.5).

**What the code does.** Precisely that. `obfuscate.py:207-211` builds the per-site weights as `1/not_missing`, where `not_missing = np.sum(pangenome != -1, axis=(0,2))`, and `obfuscate.py:279-290` sums them over the sites where the candidate differs (measured in this project, read from the pinned source). There is one small discrepancy worth noting for completeness — a legacy `2/support` variant survives at `get_support.py:25`, and a single-variant block charges its site's weight to both columns unconditionally whether or not the resampled allele differed (Step 14d) — but neither is the point of this finding.

**The point of this finding.** `1/support(v)` is a **per-record** weight. It contains no term for the length of the allele being replaced. A VCF record whose REF is 1 bp and a VCF record whose REF is 257,439 bp are charged the same `1/support(v)`, because they are one record each (derived, from the definition). That is not a mistake in the L1 sense — as an allele-frequency displacement the two edits really are comparable, one frequency count each. It becomes a problem only when `η_j` is read as *"how much of this genome did we rewrite"*, which is what the phrase "utility loss" invites.

**How it was measured, and the magnitude.** For the single run — HG00438, chr21, **capacity 0.1**, seed 123, on our 30x GRCh38 rebuild — we reconstructed the released VCF and summed the base-pair length of every allele that actually changed.

| metric | value, at **capacity 0.1**, seed 123 |
|---|---|
| sequence actually rewritten | **3,129,810 bp = 3.13 Mb = 6.7% of chr21** (measured in this project) |
| changed alleles longer than 1 kb | **222** (measured in this project) |
| largest single allele rewrite | **257,485 bp** (measured in this project) |
| what the tool reports for the same run | *"68,335 of 650,541 alleles changed (10.5%)"* (measured in this project — the tool's own output) |
| non-SNP records changed | **15,206**, a **23.3%** rate (measured in this project) |
| SNP records changed, same run | **14.9%** rate (measured in this project) |

So the release rewrote **3.13 Mb of chr21** and the accounting reported **a percentage of allele slots**. Nothing in `η_j` and — as the next table shows — nothing in the downstream metric suite is a function of that 3.13 Mb.

> **Caution** — **a retraction, because this project got it wrong first and the wrong version is the natural assumption.** An earlier note in this project implied that indel and structural records are *excluded* from the mechanism. **That is wrong** and the correction is recorded in `docs/memory.md` (2026-09-23). Indels and structural variants are **scored, selected and rewritten**, at a **higher** rate than SNPs: **23.3% against 14.9%** (measured in this project). The sentence to hold onto is: **the mechanism sees everything; only the evaluation filters.**

**Where the filtering happens.** Each downstream evaluator applies its own mask, and they are all narrower than the mechanism. Capacity 0.1, HG00438, chr21, all measured in this project:

| metric | mechanism (PMI + knapsack + stacker) | `af_loss` / `ld_loss` | gap score (posref mask) | gap score (biallelic-SNP mask) | read mapping (`bcftools view -v snps`) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| records seen | **340,824** | **258,610** | **268,851** | **238,052** | **288,020** |
| share of chr21 records | **100%** | **75.9%** | **78.9%** | **69.8%** | **84.5%** |
| non-SNP records seen | **65,176** | 21,550 | 29,965 | **0** | mixed records kept |
| `CONFLICT` records seen | **1,855** | 37 | 110 | 49 | **1,167** |

Read the first row against the last: **the largest edits by base pairs land on exactly the records the read-mapping metric cannot evaluate** (its graph is built SNP-only, and `bcftools view -v snps` drops 52,804 indel and structural records), while `af_loss` and `ld_loss` see 75.9% of records and the gap score's biallelic path sees **no non-SNP record at all** (all measured in this project). The verdict recorded in `docs/memory.md` is the flat one: **no metric in the suite is sensitive to the 3.13 Mb.**

> **Caution** — **a second retraction in the same area.** An intermediate figure used while investigating said that dropping non-SNP records removes about **84%** of `CONFLICT` records. That is wrong: `bcftools view -v snps` **keeps mixed multi-allelic records**, and conflicts are enriched in exactly those (median 4 alleles per conflict record), so **1,167 of 1,855 conflicts survive** the read-mapping filter — **37% are dropped, not 84%** (measured in this project; correction dated 2026-09-23). The error came from assuming `-v snps` implements a strict-SNP definition. It does not.

**Two further fragilities of those evaluators**, recorded in `docs/terms.md` and worth knowing before quoting any number from them. `af_loss` reads its MAF strata from `pangenome_mask.npy`, the cross-build match set, so under the build mismatch of departure (a) **every stratum is 0/0**; it is also sensitive to the row count, so comparing a 44-row cohort AF against a 45-row one shifts every frequency by roughly 1/45. `ld_loss` ships `KB_WINDOW_SIZE = 5` and compares it against **raw base pairs**, so the window is **5 base pairs**, not the paper's 5 kb; under the build mismatch it returns `(0.0, 0)` rather than raising. Both are **cohort-level** metrics and are near-blind to fidelity to the target: never quote either as evidence that a release preserved the individual.

**What it means for anyone reusing the tool.** The severity recorded in `docs/bugs.md` is **HIGH for interpreting any published utility number**, and that is the right scope for the claim — it is about interpretation, not correctness. Concretely:

- A utility figure from this suite is a statement about **allele-frequency displacement over a filtered site set**, not about how much of the genome was rewritten. Both are legitimate quantities. They are not interchangeable, and only one of them has been reported.
- For our own comparison, the consequence is a requirement rather than a repair: **we owe a base-pair-denominated axis** alongside the record-denominated one, and it must be defined for both arms. The integration seam is `new_haplotypes.npy` (§4.13) — **comparison-arm only and optional**, not a requirement of the mechanism (`docs/terms.md`) — which means that wherever we do opt into PanMixer's attack evaluators unchanged, **we inherit this blind spot exactly**. Adopting a suite means adopting what it cannot see.
- Keep the two measurement paths apart, as `docs/terms.md` insists: a figure computed over **all blocks** and a figure computed over **only the blocks where the donor differed** are different quantities, and the second is systematically lower. A Path-B number quoted over all blocks is not a more conservative result, it is the wrong result.

> **Question** — *"If adopting their evaluation suite means adopting a blind spot we just spent a page describing, why adopt it at all? Why not write our own metrics?"* Because for the **head-to-head** the alternative costs the comparison, which is the thing that arm exists for. Be careful how far the old scope wording is pushed, though. The early framing — change exactly **one** component, the sampler, and keep PanMixer's blocks, data model, donor panel and evaluation suite — has since been narrowed on three counts, all recorded in `docs/plan.md`. **The blocks are not kept:** under the decided chain architecture, model C, the chain unit is the individual variant, so the mechanism has no LD blocks, no plink run and no external panel in its input set at all. **The suite is not adopted whole:** its **attack** evaluators transfer and are worth having, but `af_loss` and `ld_loss` are cohort-distortion metrics — they score the change a release induces in the *cohort's* frequencies and LD — and our release never edits the cohort, so they are **not defined for our release object** and are not metrics this project computes (`docs/terms.md`). **And the emission is optional:** writing `new_haplotypes` in the shape their downstream code already reads (§4.13) is what lets the attack evaluators run unmodified, and it is comparison-arm-only, not a gate of the mechanism.
>
> With that scoping the original argument stands where it applies. A metric we invent produces a number that nothing published can be set beside. A metric we inherit produces a number that **both arms** have. So the inherited blind spot is best understood as a documented limit of a shared instrument, not as a reason to build a second instrument whose readings are incomparable. The recorded requirement is therefore **additive** rather than substitutive: keep the attack half of their suite for the comparison, and add a base-pair-denominated axis beside it, defined identically for both arms. One thing no amount of inheriting supplies, and it is worth stating here because this is the paragraph a reader will remember: the claim our guarantee actually makes — **two-target indistinguishability**, the empirical total variation between the release distributions of two different targets `X` and `Y`, checked against τ — is tested by **nothing** in PanMixer's suite, and is ours to write (`docs/plan.md`).

> **Question** — *"3.13 Mb out of chr21 is 6.7%, and the tool says 10.5% of allele slots changed. Those are close. Is the blindness actually costing anything?"* The closeness is a coincidence of this one run and does not generalise, because the two numbers have different denominators — **3,129,810 bp against the length of chr21** in one case, **68,335 changed allele slots against 650,541 slots** in the other (both measured in this project; the slot figures are the tool's own output). The thing the record count cannot express is the **concentration**: 222 changed alleles longer than 1 kb, one of them 257,485 bp, carry a large share of those 3.13 Mb while contributing 222 units to a count of 68,335. A metric that is linear in records cannot see a heavy tail in base pairs. Whether that matters depends entirely on the downstream use — for allele-frequency fidelity it genuinely may not; for anything sequence-level, including read mapping, it does.

The remaining two questions are about the base-pair axis itself — why the non-SNP rate is higher, and what a length-denominated metric would even be.

> **Question** — *"If indels and SVs are rewritten at 23.3% against SNPs at 14.9%, why is the non-SNP rate higher? Is the mechanism preferring them?"* We have not measured a cause and should not assert one.
