Two mechanisms are available in what we *have* measured and they point the same way, so the honest answer names both and stops. The first runs through missingness. Missingness is **22.53%** at nested records against **2.25%** at top-level SNPs (measured in this project), and higher missingness **lowers** `support(v)`, which **raises** `1/support(v)` — so wherever the two classes overlap that would make the affected records *more* expensive, not less, and it therefore cannot be the explanation on its own. The second is rarity: rarity raises `eps_j`, which is the objective. Both are hypotheses. Which dominates, and whether either does, is **unmeasured**, and the share of non-SNP records that are nested is not a number this project has measured either.

> **Question** — *"So what does the base-pair axis actually look like? Give me the definition."* There is no definition yet, and part of the reason is a decision this project has deliberately left open. The quantity is easy to state for a *release*: sum the base-pair length of every allele that actually changed, which is the **3,129,810 bp** figure above (measured in this project, at **capacity 0.1**, seed 123). Turning that into an **axis** requires two further choices, and only the first is merely bookkeeping. The bookkeeping choice is the denominator — the length of the chromosome, or the summed length of every allele that was at risk of being changed — and either can be reported as long as it is named. The choice that is genuinely open is whether the same length weighting should also enter the **mechanism**, as a length-weighted `phi_t`: `docs/plan.md` tracks "whether `phi_t` should be length-weighted when one block spans 257 kb" as an unsettled item under the hypervariable-sites question, together with how to avoid double-counting a top-level record against its **2,464** nested children (measured in this project). The evidence pulling one way is this entire finding — a per-record weight cannot see 3.13 Mb. The evidence pulling the other way is that a length-weighted `phi_t` would direct the utility budget at the longest records, which are also the most structurally uncertain and the most often `CONFLICT`-tagged ones (see (j)). Nothing in `docs/` chooses between them, so nothing here does either. What *is* settled is that the reporting axis is owed for both arms.

### (i) The privacy score reads only the block's anchor alleles

This is the largest finding in the section by consequence, and it has something of **both** shapes named in the framing paragraph. Like (h) it is a naming problem: the code computes exactly what it was written to compute, and the number it produces is named after the block while being a property of a **subset** of the block. Unlike (h) it is *also* a code-versus-Methods divergence, because what the code was written to compute is not the published definition — as the next paragraph shows, that definition carves out no subset at all.

**What the paper says.** `ε_j = −log p(h_bj)` is the self-information — the surprisal — of **the target's block** `h_bj` under the cohort model (quoted from the published paper; see §A.4). Read as English, "how surprising is this person's haplotype across this block". Nothing in that statement carves out a subset of the block's variants.

**What the code does.** On the HMM branch, `forward_algorithm` builds both the `observed` sequence and the emission panel from **`anchor_indices` only**. A non-anchor variant is therefore **not a chain position at all**: it is not emitted, it is not matched against any donor, and it contributes **nothing** to the likelihood `p(h_bj)` (measured in this project, read from `get_support_and_pmi.py`; recorded in `docs/bugs.md`). So on that branch,

> **Key idea** — `ε_j` is the self-information of the block's **anchor alleles**, not of its block.

That is the corrected reading, and it is the sentence now carried in `docs/terms.md` under `eps_pmi`. It matters because anchor membership has nothing to do with rarity. An anchor is a pangenome record that also appears in the PanGenie `bi_all` callset, matched on `(POS, REF, whole-ALT-string)` — see departure (d). Whether a variant is rare and whether a variant is in PanGenie are independent properties, and the code's value vector can see only the second.

**A distinction to hold, because two separate findings both push on the rare tail.** Departure **(c)** is a *ceiling*: including the target in its own emission panel means the model can always explain the block by copying the target, which caps `ε_j` near log(88) = 4.4773 nats. Finding **(i)** is an *exclusion*: rarity carried by a non-anchor variant is not merely capped, it is **never entered into the calculation**. They are different mechanisms with different fixes — (c) is repaired by removing the target from the panel, (i) by making every variant a chain position — and they happen to distort the same end of the distribution. Do not treat them as one finding.

**How it was measured, and the magnitude.** Two measurements, both on our rebuilt chr21 for HG00438.

The single clean case first. **Block 1083** contains two common anchors, each at allele frequency **0.934**, plus one non-anchor variant at allele frequency **0.00385** (measured in this project). The non-anchor is the rare thing in the block — that is the whole content of the example.

| metric | block 1083 |
|---|---|
| what the all-variant allele-frequency branch would return | **5.6956 nats** (measured in this project) |
| what the code actually returns | **0.0349 nats** (measured in this project) |
| ratio | **163×** (derived) |

Then the distributional version, over **400 randomly chosen HMM-branch blocks**:

| metric | value |
|---|---|
| median `eps_j` as shipped | **1.079 nats** (measured in this project) |
| median all-variant allele-frequency sum over the same blocks | **2.769 nats** (measured in this project) |
| share of blocks where the *ignored* non-anchor rarity alone exceeds `eps_j` itself | **32%** (measured in this project) |

That last row is the one to remember. In roughly a third of HMM-branch blocks, the rarity the score cannot see is larger than the rarity it can.

None of the `eps_j` figures in this finding depends on `capacity`. They are properties of the scorer, the cohort and the anchor set, computed in Step 14b before any run selects anything.

**The extreme case, and a mechanism correction attached to it.** The most identifying single record on our chr21 is the hypervariable site at **`grch38#chr21:14569980`**, graph bubble `>102270111>102277685`, VCF row **51791**, **LV = 0** (top level). It declares **90 alleles**, and the cohort carries **88 distinct ones across 88 haplotypes — every haplotype unique**. Its REF is **257,439 bp**; its ALTs run **257,124 – 514,768 bp**; the single VCF line is **27,983,949 bytes**, about **28 MB**. It has **2,464 nested child records**, so this one region occupies **2,485 matrix rows = 0.7% of chr21's 340,824** (all measured in this project; `docs/memory.md` 2026-09-22).

HG00438 carries alleles **89 and 88** there. Each gets allele frequency `f_v` = **1.540e-4** under the novel-variant denominator `2 × (3,202 + 44) = 6,492` on our 30x GRCh38 panel, which is **8.78 nats** of self-information per strand (measured in this project; the denominator and the nats figure are derived — and note that under the shipped Phase 3 panel the denominator would be 5,096 and the figure 8.54 nats, so always name the panel).

**That 8.78 nats is computed, stored, and never used.** The record is **not an anchor** (`r in align` is False). Its plink block, **block 1639**, has **92 records with 82 anchors**, so the block takes the **HMM branch** — where the forward algorithm scores the 82 anchors and does not look at row 51791 at all (all measured in this project). The most identifying record on the chromosome contributes **zero** to the block's privacy value.

> **Caution** — **this supersedes an earlier explanation of the same fact.** An earlier version of this project's notes explained the zero score by the `#FIX ME` path: an allele frequency of exactly 0 gives `−log 0 = +inf`, which the code detects as `pmi_subject[pmi_subject > 1e200] = 0` and sets to the **minimum** rather than the maximum, with a literal `#FIX ME` comment at `get_support_and_pmi.py:65-74`. **That code is real and that explanation of this site is wrong.** Across all **319,092** chr21 sites where HG00438 carries a called allele, **zero** have `f_v = 0`, so the branch **never executes** on our data (measured in this project; correction dated 2026-09-24 in `docs/memory.md`). It is a **latent** defect whose trigger is **panel-dependent**: the target's own allele is counted in every branch of the frequency table — through the pangenome counts in branches 2 and 3, and, under our 30x repin, through the panel itself in branch 1, because 39 of 44 HPRC donors including HG00438 are in the 30x panel. Under the shipped **Phase 3** panel, where **0 of 44** HPRC donors appear, a branch-1 variant whose allele no 1000 Genomes sample carries **would** give `f_v = 0` and would fire it (derived). **The conclusion survives; the mechanism changes.** The site scores zero by **non-anchor exclusion**, which is a completely different failure from `−log 0` zeroing. Step 14b and §A.4.3 both now carry the retraction in place.

**And the site was rewritten anyway.** Two separate measurements, kept separate because `docs/` does not join them and §A.4.3 forbids the join. First: at capacity 0.1 the release changed both strands at this record, **alleles [89, 88] → [35, 64]** (**measured in this project**). Second, and about the run rather than about this record: the **largest single allele rewrite anywhere on the chromosome** in that same run was **257,485 bp** (**measured in this project**). Nothing recorded in `docs/` attributes that particular rewrite to this record, so do not assume it is this one — and the point below does not need it to be. So the mechanism paid full price in sequence for an edit it valued at nothing — the two findings (h) and (i) meeting on one record. Its class is not a singleton either: chr21 carries **7** sites with 88 or more alleles, **155** with 44 or more, and **618** with 20 or more (measured in this project).

> **Question** — *"Block 1639 has 92 records and 82 anchors, so ten variants are being ignored and one of them is worth 8.78 nats on its own. What is that block's `eps_j` as shipped, and what would it be with all 92 positions in the chain?"* **Neither number has been computed for block 1639, and that is a real gap in this entry.** What is measured for that block is its composition — **92 records, 82 anchors**, so it takes the HMM branch, with VCF row **51791** among the ten excluded (all measured in this project) — and the per-strand self-information of the target's allele at row 51791 alone: **8.78 nats** under the **3,202-sample 30x GRCh38** panel, or **8.54 nats** under the shipped **2,504-sample Phase 3** panel (both derived, from allele frequencies measured in this project; the panel must be named every time, because the denominator differs — 6,492 against 5,096). The paired before-and-after figures this project *does* have are for the small clean case, **block 1083**: **0.0349 nats** as shipped against **5.6956 nats** on the all-variant allele-frequency branch (both measured in this project), a ratio of **163×** (derived). Producing the same pair for block 1639 means running the forward algorithm over that block twice — once on its 82 anchors, once with all 92 variants as chain positions — and it is the single measurement that would make this finding's extreme case as concrete as its clean one.

**One thing this finding is *not*.** It is not a claim that the ignored rarity is unmeasurable or absent from the data. **10,460 non-anchor variants still receive real 1000 Genomes frequencies** (measured in this project), so "is an anchor" and "has population-frequency data" are **different properties**, and a block with zero anchors can be fully populated with genuine frequencies. The rarity that (i) drops is, in most cases, **already computed and sitting in `allele_frequencies.npy`**. It is discarded at the routing step, not missing from the inputs.

**What it means for anyone reusing the tool.** `docs/bugs.md` records the severity as **HIGH and silent**, and "silent" is the operative word: the number `eps_j` produces is plausible, non-negative, monotone in the anchors' rarity, and inside a sensible range (measured across 150 real HMM-path blocks: **0.071 to 4.508 nats**, median **1.242**). Nothing about it looks wrong. There is no assertion to trip, no warning printed, and no downstream consumer that would notice. Practically:

- **Do not read `eps_j` as a block-level rarity.** If you need a per-block rarity, either restrict to anchors deliberately and say so, or compute it over every variant.
- **The optimizer's value vector inherits the blindness.** `eps_j` is the objective's only coefficient — `eta_j` appears only in the budget constraint (§A.6) — so a block whose entire identifying content sits on non-anchor variants enters the optimization as if it had none. This is the measured answer to the question of whether ignoring non-anchor variants matters for accounting: in 32% of HMM-branch blocks, yes, and by more than the score itself.
- **For v1 the repair is the same single decision as in (f):** every variant becomes a chain position, so nothing is excluded from the likelihood. And the same cost applies — chain-position missingness rises from **0.26%** to **4.26%**, and the class v1 adds inside already-chained blocks runs **11.31%** with **7,555** over 25% missing (measured in this project), which is exactly why `missing_policy` must be decided rather than defaulted.

> **Question** — *"Does removing the anchor restriction change which blocks the knapsack selects, and by how much?"* **Unmeasured, and we owe it.** `eps_j` enters the LP only through a **ranking** — the fractional knapsack takes items in descending order of density `eps_j/eta_j` (§A.6) — so a uniform *multiplicative* inflation of every score would leave that order untouched and change nothing at all; only a *re-ordering* changes the selection. The measurements we have — 163× on block 1083, medians of 1.079 against 2.769 over 400 blocks, 32% of blocks where the ignored rarity dominates — establish that the re-ordering would be substantial, but **we have not run the selection both ways and compared the chosen sets.** The same gap is recorded for departure (c). It is the single most useful missing measurement in this section, and it is the reason nothing here says anything about the published figures.

Two questions about repairing it follow, and both repairs are worse than they look.

> **Question** — *"Why not just add the non-anchor allele frequencies to the HMM score as an extra term?"* Because that is the double-counting the forward algorithm exists to avoid (§A.4). A product, or equivalently a sum of `−log f_v` values, treats the variants as independent; the whole reason a copying model is used is that a block whose alleles are individually uncommon but *jointly* a well-known haplotype should get a modest score, and the forward pass gets that right by summing over donor paths in O(T·K). Bolting an independent-sites term onto a copying-model likelihood would produce a quantity that is neither. The principled version is to make the non-anchors **emitting positions of the same chain**, which is what v1 does — and which immediately raises the missingness question, because the justification the *paper* gives for restricting chain positions is that the excluded class is about **10×** more often uncalled (nested records **22.53%** missing against **2.25%** at top-level SNPs, measured in this project). Note that the *code's* criterion is not that one: it admits **13,436** nested variants as anchors while dropping **18,468** top-level SNPs that qualify (measured in this project; see departure (d)), so the missingness rationale does not actually describe the set the code uses.

> **Question** — *"We decided to keep the anchor rule available as an option because it is the thing that was already published. Does keeping it mean keeping this defect on purpose?"* It means keeping it **measurable**, which is a different thing. `docs/terms.md` records the anchor rule as the `RESTRICT` setting of `missing_policy` — "exclude ill-behaved positions from the chain altogether, which is what PanMixer's anchor rule does in effect", and "worth keeping as a baseline precisely because it is the published behaviour, so a v1 run can be compared against it" (quoted from `docs/terms.md`, recording a 2026-09-25 decision). Two consequences follow and both matter. First, `RESTRICT` **removes chain positions**, so it changes **T** — it is not merely a rule about what the emission does — and `docs/terms.md` is explicit that a run under one `missing_policy` is valid only against a run under the **same** policy; never across policies. Second, a `RESTRICT` arm reproduces this finding by construction, because excluding non-anchor variants from the likelihood is exactly what the setting *is*. So the option exists to give the published behaviour a lane in our own harness, not because the exclusion has been judged defensible. Which policy v1 actually ships under is **not decided**: `docs/plan.md` records the leaning as renormalise and explicitly records that nothing has been chosen, and the evidence either way is the missingness cost — **0.26%** mean missingness with anchors as the only chain positions, against **11.31%** once every variant is a position, with **7,555** positions over 25% missing (all measured in this project).

### (j) Records flagged `CONFLICT` become missing genotypes, and are therefore released verbatim

**What the paper says.** Nothing that this project has recorded. `CONFLICT` is an INFO-field tag emitted by `vg deconstruct`, upstream of PanMixer, and no treatment of it appears in any note we hold on the published Methods — though we have not run a check for its absence from the full text, and `docs/bugs.md` carries a standing rule against concluding a string is absent from a PDF on the strength of a silent search. This entry is therefore not a paper-versus-code discrepancy in the strict sense; it is a consequence of the data model that the design does not appear to have considered, and it lands on the worst possible cell of the value/weight table. It is included because it is the clearest example in the section of a **structural** leak — one that is a property of the mechanism's inputs rather than of any line of its code.

**What the code does, in three steps.**

1. **Upstream.** When a sample has multiple graph paths through a bubble that disagree about the allele, `vg` cannot emit a single genotype, so it writes that sample's `GT` as `.|.` and tags the record `CONFLICT=<sample>` in INFO.
2. **At conversion.** `tools/common/VCFtoNP.py` keeps only `fields[1]` (**POS**) and `fields[9+i].split(':')[0]` (the **GT** subfield). Everything else is discarded — the **entire INFO column**, so `LV`, `PS`, `CONFLICT` and `AT` all vanish, along with **REF, ALT and the record ID** (measured in this project, read from the pinned source; `docs/bugs.md`). The `.|.` becomes **`-1`** in the allele matrix, indistinguishable from the other four causes of a missing entry (see Step 9).
3. **Downstream.** Two independent consequences follow from that `-1`, and they point the same way.
   - **Privacy → 0.** `get_support_and_pmi.py` zeroes the score wherever the *target's* haplotype is `-1` (`pmi_1[haplotypes[:,0] == -1] = 0`). Note the precision: the zeroing fires for the strand on which **the target itself** is missing, not merely because the record is tagged. At a `CONFLICT` record the target may or may not be one of the missing haplotypes, so the effect is per-strand, not per-record.
   - **Cost → up.** `support(v)` counts non-missing entries, and a `CONFLICT` record has many missing entries, so `1/support(v)` **rises**.

**How it was measured, and the magnitude.** All on our rebuilt chr21, and none of it depends on `capacity`, because these are properties of the data rather than of a run.

| metric | `CONFLICT` records | all other records |
|---|---|---|
| record count | **1,855 = 0.54% of 340,824** (measured in this project) | 338,969 (derived) |
| mean haplotypes missing at such a site | **29.4 of 88** (measured in this project) | **3.6 of 88** (measured in this project) |
| median utility weight `1/support(v)` | **0.01818** (measured in this project) | **0.01136** (measured in this project) |
| relative cost to change | **1.60× more expensive** (derived) | 1× (baseline) |
| share at nesting level LV = 0 | **59.1%** (measured in this project) | **90.8%** (measured in this project) |
| share at LV = 1 | **37.5%** (measured in this project) | **8.2%** (measured in this project) |
| share at LV = 2 | **3.4%** (measured in this project) | **0.9%** (measured in this project) |
| median size (max of REF/ALT) | **4 bp** (mean 602, max 311,406) (measured in this project) | mostly 1 bp (measured in this project) |
| median alleles per record | **4** (max 81) (measured in this project) | **2** (measured in this project) |

The LV = 1 row is the striking one: `CONFLICT` records are **4.6× enriched** among nested variants relative to the chromosome baseline (derived). They are not scattered uniformly — they concentrate in structurally complex regions, which is also where the target's own structure is most unusual.

Two further measured facts about where they sit. The 1,855 records occupy **1,634 blocks, of which 1,469 are singletons**, and only **1.8%** of `CONFLICT` records are on the HMM path against a chr21 baseline of **10.4%** (measured in this project). So they are mostly **alone in their own block** and mostly handled by the **independent per-variant allele-frequency sampler** rather than by the haplotype model (derived).

**The consequence, stated carefully.** A `CONFLICT` record on a strand where the target is missing is worth **exactly zero privacy** and costs **1.60× the median** to change. In a knapsack that maximises `Σ eps_j x_j` subject to `Σ eta_j x_j ≤ ΔU`, an item of zero value and positive weight cannot improve the objective and does consume budget, so the optimizer has no reason to take it (derived). The conclusion recorded in `docs/memory.md` is correspondingly that the knapsack "will essentially never select them", and that those records are therefore **released verbatim** — the target's own data, unchanged, published. The hedge is doing real work: a `CONFLICT` record that shares a block with ordinary records rides on that block's value, and 165 of the 1,634 blocks holding one are not singletons (derived). And they are precisely the records where the target's structure is unusual enough that `vg` could not resolve it.

> **Question** — *"'The optimizer has no reason to take it' is an argument, not a measurement. How many of the 1,855 `CONFLICT` records were actually released verbatim in the capacity-0.1 run?"* **That was not measured, and the claim should be read at exactly the strength of its evidence — which is why the wording above is hedged.** What is measured is the two inputs to the argument: a `CONFLICT` record contributes zero to the score on any strand where the target's own allele is missing, and its median utility weight is **0.01818** against **0.01136** elsewhere (both measured in this project), a factor of **1.60×** (derived). What is measured about where they sit is that the 1,855 records occupy **1,634 blocks, of which 1,469 are singletons**, and that only **1.8%** of them lie on the HMM path against a chr21 baseline of **10.4%** (measured in this project). The step from those facts to "released verbatim" is a zero-value-positive-weight argument about the knapsack (derived), and `docs/memory.md` states it in the hedged form on purpose: the knapsack "will essentially never select them". Settling it is a small piece of work — intersect `xsol.npy` from the capacity-0.1, seed-123 run with the block ids of those 1,634 blocks and count how many were selected on each strand. Until that has been run, the defensible claim is the structural one, not a rate.

This is the exact mirror image of finding (g). A no-op move has positive value at zero weight and is taken unconditionally; a `CONFLICT` record alone in its own block has zero value at above-median weight and the optimizer has no reason to take it. Both are the optimizer behaving correctly on inputs that misdescribe the situation.

> **Note** — **what causes `CONFLICT`, and what does not.** The intuitive hypothesis is repeats: a traversal revisiting a bubble gives that sample two passes that can disagree. **Measured, that is not the dominant cause.** chr21 has **37** records whose own `AT` field contains a revisiting traversal and **1,855** records tagged `CONFLICT`; **11** are both, **26** revisit without conflicting, and **1,844 conflict without any revisit** (measured in this project). `CONFLICT` also names **all 45 samples**, at roughly **200–470 records each** (measured in this project) — far too widespread for tandem duplications. The most plausible dominant cause is **assembly fragmentation**: each HPRC haplotype is many contigs, and two contigs of one sample traversing the same region give that sample multiple paths. **That hypothesis has not been verified and cannot be settled from the VCF alone** — it is recorded as a hypothesis in `docs/memory.md`, not a finding.
>
> There is a real asymmetry worth understanding, though, because it explains why the two counts fail to line up. The 257 kb hypervariable parent of finding (i) **does** have a revisiting allele — allele 58 walks **10,008 node steps over 5,014 distinct nodes**, so roughly **4,994 revisits**, a whole-region tandem duplication — and it is **not** tagged `CONFLICT`, because at the parent level HG01891 carries exactly **one** allele, number 58, whose walk happens to loop. The conflict materialises only at the **child** records, where that single parent allele corresponds to **two passes that may disagree**: the two copies are not identical (4,994 nodes appear exactly twice, 20 appear once, i.e. about 10 interior positions where the copies diverge), and `CONFLICT=HG01891` duly appears at **POS 14577263** (all measured in this project). A revisit at the parent becomes a conflict at the child.

**What it means for anyone reusing the tool.**

- **There is a structural leak here that no amount of tuning `capacity` will close**, because the leak is at zero value in the objective. Raising the budget cannot buy an item worth nothing. The only repairs are upstream of the optimizer.
- **Masking does not remove them from the evaluation either.** `bcftools view -v snps` keeps mixed multi-allelic records, and conflicts are enriched in those, so **1,167 of 1,855 survive** into the read-mapping evaluation — 37% dropped, not 84% (measured in this project; see the retraction in (h)).
- **The recommendation is specific and has a deadline.** All live causes of a `-1` — not applicable, inherited, assembly gap, conflict (**four** on chr21; the fifth candidate, haploid genotype, does not occur here) — are **distinguishable at the moment of conversion** and **none is recoverable afterwards**, because `LV`, `PS`, `CONFLICT` and the run-length context are all discarded there. So: **emit an auxiliary reason array beside the allele matrix during conversion.** The phrase recorded in `docs/memory.md` is the right one — *cheap now, unrecoverable later.* Without it, `missing_policy` cannot be split by cause, and splitting by cause is the whole reason to want it: "Not applicable" argues for RENORMALISE; an assembly gap arguably argues for something closer to a wildcard, since the donor almost certainly has an allele we merely do not know. ⚠ **DECIDED 2026-09-28: the policy is RENORMALISE**, with the cost to poorly-assembled donors accepted deliberately. The reason array is still wanted, because it keeps a cause-aware refinement available later.

> **Question** — *"If `CONFLICT` records are worth zero privacy anyway, are they actually identifying? Maybe the score is right."* They are identifying, and the score is not measuring their identifying content — it is measuring nothing, because the target's allele there is **unknown**, not common. That distinction is the finding. `−log f_v` is undefined for an allele we do not have, and the code resolves the undefined case to **zero**, which is the value reserved for *"this allele is universal and therefore uninformative"*. An unknown is being scored as an uninformative. Worse, the *fact* of the conflict is itself information: a record where the target could not be resolved is a record where the target's local structure is unusual, and that is published in the release as a `.` — see Step 19, where `-1` is written back out. We have **not** measured how much an attacker gains from the missingness pattern alone, and it should not be asserted without measurement.

> **Question** — *"If we emit the REASON array, do we then owe four different missing-data policies? That sounds like four times the work for a decision I have not made."* The array commits us to nothing; it only keeps the choice available, and that is the entire argument for doing it now. `docs/plan.md` records it as a v1 preprocessing prerequisite in exactly those terms: emitting an auxiliary reason array beside the allele matrix "keeps a cause-aware policy available without committing to one now. Cheap now, unrecoverable later" (quoted from `docs/plan.md`). The asymmetry is what makes it urgent rather than optional: the five causes are distinguishable at conversion from `LV`/`PS`, run-length, the `CONFLICT` tag and `GT` arity, and **none** of them is recoverable once `VCFtoNP.py` has kept only position and genotype (measured in this project). On the policy itself, the decision is reserved and this document does not make it. Its **shape** is a choice among the settings recorded under `missing_policy` in `docs/terms.md` — `RENORMALISE`, `WILDCARD`, `MISMATCH`, `RESTRICT` — applied either uniformly or per cause. The evidence on the table, all measured in this project on chr21: the causes are very unevenly common, with **66.1%** of nested missingness being "the parent allele does not contain this bubble", **12.1%** inherited from a missing parent call, **349** runs of 100 or more consecutive missing positions covering **448,283** entries as assembly gaps, **1,855** `CONFLICT` records, and **3.135%** of sample `GT` fields haploid; and a single policy applied to all five necessarily treats "the DNA does not exist on this chromosome" and "we failed to assemble it" as the same event, which are opposite situations for a copying model. `docs/plan.md` records the leaning as renormalise and records that nothing has been decided. One policy for all five remains fully available — the array is only what keeps the alternative available too.

> **Question** — *"Does this affect us, or is it a PanMixer-only problem?"* It affects us, and the v1 spec currently marks the VCF-to-matrix stage **REUSE AS IS** (§4.13). Reusing `VCFtoNP.py` unchanged means reusing the conversion that destroys the five-cause distinction. Our mechanism has no knapsack, so it has no "never selected" failure — every block is resampled — but it does have a `missing_policy`, and the policy cannot be chosen per cause if the cause was thrown away at conversion. This is the one place where "reuse their data loading" is not free.

## The same standard, applied to our own pipeline

This closing section applies the identical test to **the artifacts this project built**, and it is not one of the ten departures. They fail that test — in the same family of ways as departure (c), for a reason we introduced while fixing something else: our repin put `HG00438` inside the allele frequencies computed about it.

**Read the whole section with one distinction held in mind**, because everything in it turns on which of two objects is being discussed.

- **The deployed mechanism** releases an **external** target's path. That person is not in `G`, not in `D`, not in any panel and not in the attack database. Target-independence therefore holds **by construction**: `K = 88`, nothing is held out, and there is no quantity to recompute.
- **The benchmark** — the head-to-head on PanMixer's data — has no external target available, so a **cohort member stands in** and externality has to be *simulated*. That is where a leave-one-out is required, and it is the only place it is required.

Everything measured below is a real defect of **the benchmark as we ran it**. None of it is a property of the mechanism's design.

**Our own first standing constraint**, quoted from this project's `CLAUDE.md`:

> **Caution** — this project's standing constraint 1, quoted in full: **the output support `Y_D` and the baseline `R_D` are built from `G` and `D` only — never from the target.** Any target-dependent pruning of the HMM state space voids Theorem 1. This is the easiest way to buy utility and silently lose the guarantee; it is the single rule most likely to be broken by accident. **For us this holds by construction** — the target is external, so `K = 88` and nothing is held out. On PanMixer's data target inclusion is the **default**, which is correct there and is not a requirement on us; leave-one-out belongs to the head-to-head arm alone.

The second half of that constraint is the one this section exercises. On PanMixer's data the target is a cohort member, so its allele frequencies, support counts and scoring panel all include it — correctly, for their construction. The benchmark inherits that default, and what follows is what our own repin then did to it.

**What happened.** `get_af.py` builds the allele-frequency table by a three-way priority (Step 13). **Branch 1** — the branch that covers **258,610 chr21 variants, 75.9% of all records** (measured in this project) — draws its counts from the **1000 Genomes panel alone**, deliberately excluding the pangenome. The code comment states the reason: *"None of the pangenome subjects appear in 1000g_phased."* Given that, the branch-1 frequencies would be target-independent by construction.

The comment is true of the panel the shipped pipeline downloads and **false of the panel we substituted**.

| metric | shipped: 1000 Genomes Phase 3 | ours: 1000 Genomes 30x |
|---|---|---|
| build | GRCh37 | GRCh38 |
| samples in the panel | **2,504** (measured in this project — what the shipped `get_1000g_phased.sh` downloads) | **3,202** (measured in this project) |
| HPRC pangenome donors present in the panel | **0 of 44** (measured in this project) | **39 of 44** (measured in this project) |
| the target `HG00438` present | **no** (measured in this project) | **yes** (measured in this project) |
| branch-1 frequencies target-independent | **yes** (derived) | **no** (derived) |
| status of the `#FIX ME` zeroing path of (i) | can fire (derived) | cannot fire — 0 of 319,092 sites have `f_v = 0` (measured in this project) |
| the code comment asserting no overlap | true | **false** |

**The overlap measurement is the whole finding: 0 of 44 before the repin, 39 of 44 after — including the target.** We substituted the 30x GRCh38 panel to fix departure (a), the build mismatch, which it does: the strict `(POS, REF, ALT)` intersection goes from **1,025** matches to **258,610**, a **252×** improvement (measured in this project, §1.2.1). The same substitution silently placed the target inside the population from which its own rarity is computed.

**Why it breaks the benchmark and is merely a compounding for them.** For PanMixer the target is a cohort member by construction, and its scoring panel already includes the target (departure (c)); our repin makes an existing self-inclusion stronger, and nothing in their guarantee — which is empirical and attacker-specific (§A.7) — was resting on the absence. Our guarantee is a **worst-case pairwise bound** whose proof requires `R_D` to be a function of the graph and the donor panel only. The deployed mechanism satisfies that requirement with nothing to check: the target is external, so no panel, frequency table or support count can have seen it. The **benchmark** is where the requirement has to be *enforced*, because there a cohort member is standing in — and a frequency table that has seen the stand-in is not a function of `G` and `D` alone. **That is what the repin broke, and we introduced it** (recorded in `docs/bugs.md`). It is a defect of the simulated externality, not evidence that the design can violate constraint 1.

**Symptom visibility: none.** The frequencies look entirely normal. Nothing is out of range, nothing is zero that should not be, no assertion fires. In fact the repin *removes* one visible symptom, because with the target in the panel its allele is always counted and `f_v` is never 0, which is why the `#FIX ME` path of finding (i) cannot fire on our data at all (measured in this project: 0 of 319,092 sites). A self-inclusion error that suppresses a warning is the worst combination available.

**The correction required, in both arms of the benchmark.** Not a patch to one file. The decided meaning of a leave-one-out cohort in this project (`docs/terms.md`, `loo_cohort`) is a **recomputation of the donor panel, the allele frequencies, the support counts, the site list *and* the block assignment**, all without the stand-in. *"Recomputing only the donor panel — what PanMixer does — is not a `loo_cohort`."* Specifically for this finding: **any leave-one-out must drop the stand-in from the 1000 Genomes panel as well as from the pangenome** (`docs/memory.md`, 2026-09-24). Note the scope: `loo_cohort` is **comparison-arm only** and is *"not a concept our mechanism needs"* (quoted from `docs/terms.md`). Where the target is genuinely external there is nothing to hold out, and `K = 88` unconditionally; the `K = 2(N−1) = 86` panel belongs to the head-to-head arm alone.

And it has to happen in **both arms of the head-to-head**. `docs/plan.md` settles this as an **exit gate** under `A-EVL-headtohead`, not as an open question: the `loo_cohort`, with "donor panel, allele frequencies, support counts, site list and block assignment all recomputed without the target", is to be "applied to BOTH arms, with PanMixer's as-published numbers also reported so the cost of the correction is visible" (quoted from `docs/plan.md`). The reason it cannot be left to a default is that a leave-one-out arm compared against a target-inclusive arm is not a comparison of mechanisms.

**How much does the correction move?** We have one partial measurement and it is a warning, not an answer. Removing the target from the **emission panel only** — not from the frequency table, not from the support counts, not from the block assignment — over **150 real HMM-path blocks** moves the median `eps_j` from **1.242** to **1.299** nats and the **maximum** from **4.508** to **78.1** nats, with the nine ceiling-bound blocks going to a median of 12.06 and a maximum of 78.12, an understatement of up to **17.4×** (all measured in this project; see departure (c)). That is a single component of the leave-one-out, and it already moves the tail by more than an order of magnitude. What a **full** leave-one-out does to either arm is **not measured**.

> **Caution** — **a hard limit on what any corrected number can claim.** The PGGB graph **topology** was built from all HPRC assemblies, including the target's. So even a complete leave-one-out recomputation of panel, frequencies, supports, site list and blocks **only approximates** our external-target threat model when run on PanMixer's data. The graph itself still has the target in it. The standing rule is: **never claim full externality on PanMixer's data** (`docs/memory.md`, `docs/terms.md` `loo_cohort`).

> **Question** — *"Every number in this section was measured on the repinned panel. Does that mean the findings above are all measured on contaminated data?"* They are measured on the pipeline **as we ran it**, and that is both the honest description and the useful one, because that pipeline is the object the section is about. Two distinctions keep it from being worse than that, and they are worth separating. Most of the numbers here do not touch the frequency table at all: the two anchor thresholds and the **452** disagreeing blocks are properties of the block structure and the anchor set; the **1,855** `CONFLICT` records, their **29.4 of 88** mean missingness and their **1.60×** relative cost are properties of the VCF; the **3,129,810 bp** rewritten is a property of one run's output (all measured in this project, except the 1.60×, which is derived). What *is* affected is every quantity computed from `f_v` or from the emission panel — `eps_j` itself, the **0.0349**-against-**5.6956** pair at block 1083, the **8.78** nats at row 51791, the **32%** of HMM-branch blocks where the ignored rarity dominates. For those the correct reading is that they describe a **target-inclusive** scoring path, which is the path the released code takes on our data. The one partial measurement of what removing the target does is the emission-panel-only leave-one-out over **150** HMM-path blocks: median `eps_j` **1.242** → **1.299** nats, maximum **4.508** → **78.1** nats (all measured in this project). A **full** `loo_cohort` — donor panel, allele frequencies, support counts, site list and block assignment all recomputed without the target — has **not** been run, so no figure in this section has a leave-one-out counterpart. That is the measurement we owe, and it is another reason nothing here is stated against the published figures.

**Why this is in this section and not in a footnote.** Because the ten findings above are only worth reading if the same test is applied here, and because the failure mode is instructive in exactly the way departure (c) is instructive: **leave-one-out errors are not caught by looking at the outputs.** They are caught by asking, for each input the mechanism consumes, "was this computed from data that includes the person we are protecting?" — and then checking the answer against the data actually in use rather than against the comment in the code. The comment in `get_af.py` was correct when it was written. The panel changed underneath it. Nothing in the pipeline noticed, and nothing would have.

> **Question** — *"We substituted the panel to fix departure (a). Did the fix make things worse overall?"* No, and the two effects are on different axes. The substitution fixed a **correctness** failure — a 252× join gap that left block boundaries, the attack database and the AF/LD site sets built on a near-empty intersection (measured in this project). It created a failure of the benchmark's **simulated externality** that is real but repairable by recomputation, and repairable without giving the build fix back. The two are independent: a build-matched panel with the stand-in's own row removed satisfies both. Note the blast radius is the benchmark only — the deployed mechanism's target has no row in the panel to remove, and under model C the mechanism does not read an external panel at all. The lesson is not "do not substitute the panel"; it is that **changing a data source invalidates every comment and assumption written against the old one**, and this project's own standing constraint is one of the things that had to be re-checked.

> **Question** — *"Is a leave-one-out even well defined when 39 of 44 donors are in the panel? Do we drop all 39?"* No — constraint 1 is about **the target**, not about the cohort. The donor panel `D` is public and is supposed to be in `R_D`; that is what `R_D` *is*. What must be absent is the individual being protected. So the leave-one-out drops **HG00438** from the 1000 Genomes panel, from the pangenome haplotypes, from the support counts and from the site list, and leaves the other 38 co-present donors exactly where they are. The 39-of-44 figure matters because the target is measured to be one of the 39 — `docs/memory.md` names `HG00438` among them explicitly, so its presence is measured, not inferred from the overlap rate. The fix is about that one row.

> **Question** — *"Is branch 1 really the only place the stand-in got in? What about the other two frequency branches, and the support counts?"* Branch 1 is where the **repin changed the answer**, but it is not the only route by which the stand-in's own data reaches a quantity computed about it — and this is exactly why the correction is a recomputation rather than a patch to one file. All four routes below are properties of **the benchmark as run**, where a cohort member plays the target; in the deployed mechanism the target is external to `G`, `D` and every panel, so none of the four has an entrance. Four separate routes, all recorded: the stand-in's alleles are counted through the pangenome in the other two branches of `get_af.py` (Step 13), so those branches were target-inclusive **before** the repin and remain so; `support(v)` counts non-missing entries of the pangenome matrix, which for a cohort-member stand-in includes its two haplotypes, so every `1/support(v)` weight has seen it; the HMM's emission panel includes it, which is departure (c); and the plink block boundaries are computed on the 1000 Genomes panel, which after the repin contains that individual's row (measured in this project — HG00438 is one of the 39 of 44).
>
> **The fourth route is the one that does not transfer at all, and it is worth being exact about why.** plink's boundaries are a property of the panel — "computed with no knowledge that a 44-person pangenome exists" (Step 12). They became a weak function of *a cohort member's* genotypes only because the repin put that member's row into the panel. They are **not** a function of an external target's genotypes by any route, because an external target has no row in any panel to begin with. And under **model C** the point is moot twice over: the chain unit is the individual variant, so there are no LD blocks in the mechanism, no plink run, and no external panel in the mechanism's input set at all (project decision, `docs/plan.md`). The external panel survives only as the attack database in the evaluation.
>
> That is precisely why `docs/terms.md` defines a `loo_cohort` as recomputing **all** of it — "donor panel, allele frequencies, support counts, the site list, and the block assignment" — and states flatly that "recomputing only the donor panel — what PanMixer does — is NOT a `loo_cohort`" (quoted from `docs/terms.md`). Note what that entry also says: `loo_cohort` is **comparison-arm only**, not a concept the mechanism needs, because with a genuinely external target there is nothing to hold out. The repin's own contribution is the narrow, measured one: **0 of 44** HPRC donors in the Phase 3 panel against **39 of 44** in the 30x panel (measured in this project). It extended an existing self-inclusion to one more input, and it did so at the one input whose code comment asserted it was clean.

---

## The pipeline at a glance

| Step | Input | What happens | Output | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1–3. Download | URLs (S3, Zenodo, EBI FTP) | Fetch HPRC v1.0 PGGB VCF, PanGenie callset, phased reference panel (shipped: Phase 3 / GRCh37 / 2,504 samples; this project substituted 30x / GRCh38 / 3,202 samples) | `pangenome.vcf.gz` (45 samples), `PG.vcf.gz` (368 samples), `1000g_phased.vcf.gz` | Supplies the data to protect, plus two external models of "what is common in humans" |
| 4–6. Clean & split | `pangenome.vcf.gz` | Drop chrX (sex-dependent ploidy), drop `chm13` (a human cell line used as a reference assembly, not a cohort donor), split per chromosome | `chr{N}/pangenome.vcf.gz`, 44 samples = 88 haplotypes; chr21 = 340,824 records | Establishes the uniform "everyone has exactly 2 haplotypes" invariant everything else assumes |
| 7. Allele counts | per-chr pangenome VCF | Count `len(ALT)+1` per record | `num_alleles.npy` (340,824,) | Bounds the allele-index space; used as a consistency assertion later |
| 8. LD blocks | reference panel VCF | `plink --make-bed` then `plink --blocks` | `blocks.blocks.det` — 14,137 blocks on our chr21 / 30x GRCh38 | Defines the unit of editing: variants inherited together must be edited together |
| 9. VCF → numpy | 3 per-chr VCFs | Two-pass parse into `int16[samples, sites, 2]`; `-1` = missing | `*.npy`, `*_positions.npy`, `*_subjects.npy` | Turns text into arrays; the `-1` convention keeps "missing" distinct from "reference" |
| 10. Mappings | 3 VCFs | Join on (POS, REF, ALT) and relaxed (POS, REF) | mapping pickles + index masks — 258,610 strict on our GRCh38 rebuild, 1,025 with the shipped GRCh37 panel | The join keys for frequencies, anchors and the attack; where the build mismatch bites |
| 11. SNP mask | per-chr pangenome VCF | Flag records that are single-ALT single-base SNPs | `biallelic_snp_mask.npy` — 274,458 = 80.5% of the 340,824 records on our chr21 (measured in this project) | The attack's Hardy–Weinberg model is only valid on biallelic sites |
| 12. Block dict | plink intervals + positions | Assign each variant to a block; variants in no interval get their own | `blocks_dict.json` — 100,757 entries, of which 89,087 (88.4% **of entries**) hold one variant = 26.1% **of variants** | The index everything iterates over; most entries are trivial but most variants are not |
| 13. Allele freqs | pangenome/PG/panel arrays + mappings | 3-way priority (panel → PanGenie + graph → graph with padded denominator 2×(N_panel+N_pangenome) = 6,492 on our rebuild) | `allele_frequencies.npy` (340,824 × 90), rows sum to 1 | The population model. A novel allele gets a very small `f_v`, but that only reaches the score on the frequency route: on the HMM route non-anchor records are never read at all (departure (i)) |
| 14. Privacy + utility + candidates | everything above, one subject | Self-information per block (per-site −log AF, or HMM forward for ≥1 anchor); one sampled replacement per block per haplotype (HMM only at ≥2 anchors); cost = Σ 1/support over changed sites | `pmi_blocks.npy` (k,2), `support_blocks.npy` (k,2), `utility_loss.npy`, `new_haplotypes.npy` | Converts biology into a value vector and a weight vector — the whole trade-off in two arrays |
| 15. Total utility | all subjects' utility vectors | Sum 2 × Σ utility_loss per subject, genome-wide | `utility_loss.json` | A reporting aggregate; the LP's actual **budget denominator** is recomputed per chromosome in Step 17 |
| 16. Experiment start | subjects × capacities | Build row table and directory skeleton | `data.csv`, `exp_N/data/chr*/row_id/` | `row_id` is the key for the whole run |
| 17. Optimizer | `support_blocks` (weights), `pmi_blocks` (values), capacity | LP relaxation of 0–1 knapsack via OR-Tools GLOP, then round | `xsol.npy` (k,2) binary. HG00438/chr21: cap 0.1 → 47,070 moves at exactly 10.000% (928.92/9,288.85); cap 0.5 → 177,865 moves at 33.1% (3,077.60/9,288.85, saturates) — all measured in this project | The only place the trade-off is actually decided |
| 18. Stacker | `xsol`, candidates, original | Overwrite selected blocks; **leave unselected blocks verbatim** | `new_haplotypes.npy` (340,824, 2); 68,335 of 650,541 called alleles changed at cap 0.1 | Executes the decision — and fixes the guarantee structure: verbatim blocks ⇒ no worst-case pairwise bound |
| 19–20. Write & merge | stacked array + template VCF | Rewrite one sample column; bgzip; index; `bcftools concat` across chromosomes | `new_haplotypes.vcf.gz`, `merged.vcf.gz` | **The released artefact**: the cohort VCF with one column rewritten in place; graph topology untouched |
| 21. Gap score | release + attack DB + AF | Hardy–Weinberg genotype probabilities; rarity-weighted matching; self-score minus the maximum over the database, which the released code takes without excluding the target (departure (e)) | `gap_score_all.json`; negative = attack defeated | Produces ε_private = 0.002 (published) — the entire empirical privacy claim |
| 22. Membership inference | release, original, cohort, panel | Count matching alleles/genotypes against each set | `MIA_privacy.npz` | Tests the weaker "is this person in the cohort at all" attack; new in the published version |
| 23–24. AF / LD loss | before and after cohort arrays | Wasserstein (= 2 × mean abs AF difference) and L1 change in r² over windows the paper states as 5 kb, executed as 5 bp (see Step 24) | per-row JSON | Published: AF 0.006 all alleles / 0.006 SNPs / 0.005 rare SNPs (MAF<0.05) vs 0.027 removal baseline; LD 0.003 vs 0.017 |
| 25. Beagle | release + reference panel (targets excluded) | Imputation-based reconstruction with the pinned `beagle.27Feb25.75f.jar`; compare to original and to a most-frequent-allele baseline | refined VCF + accuracy stats | Tests whether obfuscation is reversible by off-the-shelf tools |
| 26. vg giraffe | release chr21 → graph indexes; FASTQ reads from 5 non-cohort individuals | `vg autoindex --workflow giraffe`, `vg giraffe`, `vg stats -a` | perfect / gapless / MAPQ-60 rates — published 77.82 / 95.61 / 77.01 vs baseline 77.83 / 95.62 / 77.01 | The external utility test: does the graph still reduce reference bias for outsiders? Baseline arm run here: 79.05 / 95.96 / 79.23 (measured in this project); not an exact reproduction, offset unexplained. Obfuscated arm not run. |
| 27. Gather | all per-(chr, row) JSONs | Sum privacy/utility, average rates | aggregated tables | Privacy is budgeted per chromosome but claimed genome-wide |


> **Question** — "Every number in this table needs its denominator and its panel in the cell itself. I'm going to read this table without the surrounding text."
>
> Agreed, and this is the checklist for it. Any cell quoting a **share** says what it is a share of: **88.4% of dictionary entries**, never of variants; **26.1% of variants**; **10.4% of entries** holding **71.4% of variants**; **80.5% of the 340,824 records** for the biallelic SNP mask. Any cell quoting a **match count** says which panel: **258,610** on the 30x GRCh38 rebuild against **1,025** with the shipped GRCh37 Phase 3 panel. Any cell quoting a **run** says the operating point and the subject: capacity 0.1, HG00438, chr21, seed 123 — that applies to 47,070 moves, 928.92 spent, 68,335 of 650,541 slots changed, and 3.13 Mb rewritten. Any cell quoting a **published** figure says so, names the operating point it was reported at, and does not sit in the same cell as a measured one: ε_private 0.002, and — at ε_private = 0.002 — utility loss 0.28, 0.006 / 0.006 / 0.005, 0.003, 77.82 / 95.61 / 77.01.
>
> The one denominator error worth guarding hardest is the entries-versus-variants one, because it is wrong by more than threefold in a direction that sounds plausible: writing "88.4% of variants are singletons" contradicts the measured 71.4%-of-variants figure in the same table.

> **Question** — "Can one row carry a single named block all the way down the table, so I can follow one object through all 27 steps?"
>
> Yes, and **block 1077** is the block to use because every stage of it is measured (all measured in this project unless marked; capacity 0.1, HG00438, chr21, seed 123):
>
> | stage | what happens to block 1077 |
> |---|---|
> | Step 6 / Step 9 | its five variants land at rows **34816–34820**, POS **13,000,333–13,000,621** |
> | Step 8 / Step 12 | all five positions fall in one plink interval, so they form one dictionary entry, id **1077** |
> | Step 13 | each of the five gets a frequency row; the target's alleles are the common ones |
> | Step 14a | support is **87 of 88** at every variant, so each site's weight is **1/87 = 0.01149** (derived) |
> | Step 14b | 5 variants, **all 5 anchors**, so the HMM branch: **ε_j = 0.0711 nats** per strand |
> | Step 14c | the sampled replacement is `[0,0,0,0,0]` — **identical to the target**, because the degenerate transitions copy one donor verbatim and that donor carries the same common alleles |
> | Step 14d | nothing differs, so **η_j = 0.00000** (it would be **0.05747** if all five changed, derived) |
> | Step 17 | the LP takes it unconditionally — infinite value per unit cost. `xsol[625]` = 1 on **both** strands |
> | Step 18 / Step 19 | the released alleles are **unchanged**; the release records the target's true block |
> | Steps 21–27 | the run books **ε_j = 0.0711** of privacy credit for a block it did not change |
>
> The cross-check that makes the object worth following: **82 of 88** donor haplotypes carry the target's exact pattern, and −log(82/88) = **0.0706** (derived), which matches the scorer's 0.0711. That confirms ε_j reads as the surprisal of the target's block under the cohort — and it shows, on one numbered object, how **14,526 of 47,070** selected moves come to change nothing while carrying **7.0%** of the reported privacy credit. The full step-by-step version is the *Worked trace* after Step 19.

---

## In plain words — Section 3

PanMixer takes a pangenome graph containing 44 real people, picks one of them, and rewrites that person's data in the released file so they can no longer be matched to an outside database — while leaving the file still useful to everyone else. It gets there in two halves.

The slow half, run once and taking (per the repository's README, line 78) "about 1–2 days to run end-to-end", converts the problem into arithmetic. It downloads the graph and two external reference datasets; strips chrX and the CHM13 sample — a human cell line derived from a complete hydatidiform mole, used as a reference assembly rather than contributed by a cohort donor — down to 44 individuals and 88 haplotypes; uses plink on a large external panel to find LD blocks, which are runs of variants inherited as a unit; turns every VCF into an integer array where −1 means "missing", *not* "reference"; builds allele frequencies with a three-way fallback that deliberately scores unseen variants as very rare; and then, for every block, computes three things — how surprising the target's real block is under the population model (the privacy value ε_j — though, as §A.4.2 shows, the released code evaluates that over the block's **anchor** variants only), one sampled plausible replacement block, and how much allele-frequency distortion installing that replacement would cause (the utility cost η_j).

The fast half runs many times. An LP-relaxed knapsack picks the best set of replacements within a per-chromosome utility budget; a stacker installs exactly those and copies everything else through untouched; and one column of the cohort VCF is rewritten. A suite of downstream evaluations then measures what was bought and what was lost: a rarity-weighted linkage attack whose failure point defines ε_private = 0.002 (published), a membership-inference check, allele-frequency and LD distortion (published: Wasserstein 0.006 for all alleles, 0.006 for SNPs only, 0.005 for rare SNPs with MAF < 0.05, against a 0.027 removal baseline; LD 0.003 against 0.017), a Beagle imputation attack testing reversibility, and `vg giraffe` read mapping testing whether the graph still serves outsiders (published: 77.82% perfect / 95.61% gapless / 77.01% MAPQ 60, against a baseline of 77.83 / 95.62 / 77.01). This project has run the **baseline** arm of that read-mapping evaluation and measured **79.05 / 95.96 / 79.23**, an offset of +1.22 to +2.22 points that remains unexplained after one candidate explanation was tested and refuted — so the baseline is **not** reproduced — and the obfuscated arm has not been run at all (both measured in this project; Step 26).

On the block structure, the number to keep straight is this: on our rebuilt chr21 there are 100,757 block-dictionary entries, of which 89,087 — **88.4% of entries** — hold a single variant, and those account for **26.1% of variants**. The 10.4% of entries with two or more anchors hold 71.4% of variants. Never say "88.4% of variants".

Reading the released code against the published methods turns up ten discrepancies, all recorded with evidence in this project's `docs/bugs.md`, in `docs/memory.md`, or in both. An earlier version of this summary said "five discrepancies"; that count was correct when it was written and is **retracted** now, because **five further departures, (f) to (j), were registered on 2026-09-25**, and a **closing section was added at the same time** that applies the same test to our own pipeline and is **not one of the ten**. The first five are these. The LD blocks and variant joins are computed against a GRCh37 panel while the graph is GRCh38, giving 1,025 chr21 matches instead of 258,610 — a 252× gap. The Li–Stephens sampler's distances are row-index differences rather than centiMorgans, with a reciprocal N_e, a stray factor of 4, and a non-anchor fill that compares base pairs against row indices; the switch probability collapses to about 10⁻¹² per step, and on 300 of 300 real chr21 blocks for HG00438 each "mosaic" was one donor haplotype copied whole. The privacy scorer includes the target in its own reference panel, so the model can always explain the block as a copy of the target, bounding the score by about log(88) = 4.4773 nats plus a small per-anchor term from the 10⁻⁴ mismatch probability — which is why a measured maximum of 4.508 sits just above 4.4773 without contradicting the argument — and on 150 real blocks the leave-one-out maximum rises from 4.508 to 78.1, truncating the rarest and most identifying blocks by up to 17×. The HMM's anchor set is bound to the PanGenie callset rather than the 1000 Genomes set the paper defines, though since PanGenie is GRCh38 this one is *not* compounded by the build mismatch. And the gap-score attack takes its maximum over the whole database without the self-exclusion the published equation (7) requires.

The five later items are these. **(f)** The same anchor count is tested with two different thresholds in two adjacent functions — the score takes the haplotype route at one anchor, the sampler at two — so 452 of the 100,757 chr21 block-dictionary entries are priced under one model and filled under another. **(g)** A move whose sampled replacement comes back identical to the original is charged η_j = exactly 0 while keeping its full ε_j, which is infinite value per unit cost, so the LP buys it ahead of every real edit: at capacity 0.1 on our one measured run, 14,526 of 47,070 selected moves changed nothing — 30.9% of *moves*, carrying 7.0% of the reported privacy *credit*, and those two denominators must never be merged into one figure. **(h)** The utility weight is charged per record and carries no term for length, so the same run that reports "68,335 of 650,541 alleles changed (10.5%)" rewrote 3,129,810 bp — 3.13 Mb, 6.7% of chr21 — and no metric in the suite is sensitive to those megabases; the related assumption that indels and structural variants are left alone is **retracted**, because they are rewritten at a higher rate than SNPs, 23.3% against 14.9%. **(i)** On the haplotype route the forward algorithm builds its chain from the block's anchors alone, so a non-anchor variant contributes nothing however rare: one measured block returns 0.0349 nats where the all-variant sum gives 5.6956, a factor of 163, and across 400 measured blocks the ignored material is individually more identifying than the reported score in 32% of cases. The most identifying record on chr21, worth 8.78 nats on our 30x GRCh38 panel, scores nothing — by **exclusion, not by the `−log 0` clamp**, whose earlier explanation is **retracted** because that branch never fires on this data (0 of 319,092 called sites have frequency 0). **(j)** Records tagged `CONFLICT` by `vg deconstruct` arrive as plain `-1`s, are therefore worth zero privacy where the target's own allele is one of them, and cost 1.60× the median record to change, so the knapsack has no reason to buy them and the 1,855 such chr21 records are released verbatim — concentrated 4.6× in nested variants, which is exactly where the target's structure is most unusual. And the closing section — which is not one of the ten — turns the same test on us: our own substitution of the 30x GRCh38 panel, made to fix (a), put 39 of the 44 cohort donors — including the target — inside the frequencies used to score the target, which is a violation of this project's own first standing constraint and one we introduced ourselves.

**Seven of those ten — (b), (c), (d), (f), (g), (h) and (i) — carry a retraction** of something this primer or this project previously asserted, and every retraction is kept in place rather than deleted. Counting by the criterion *"a departure against which a withdrawn claim is registered somewhere in this primer"*, they are: **(c)** ε_j is **not** "pinned near log(2N) for essentially every block" — the effect is a ceiling on the rarest blocks, not a flattening of all of them; **(d)** the build mismatch does **not** degrade the HMM-versus-frequency routing, because the anchor join is a same-build join against PanGenie; **(f)** "nothing depends on" the two anchor thresholds disagreeing was wrong, and the 452 blocks are the measurement that makes it a finding; **(g)** "the privacy total is inflated by ≈31%" was wrong, and the correct statement is the pair *30.9% of moves, 7.0% of the credit*; **(h)** "utility loss and amount of the genome rewritten track each other" is false in base pairs, and the assumption that indels and structural variants are left alone is false too — they are rewritten at a higher rate than SNPs; and **(i)** the `−log 0` clamp does not fire on this data, so the most identifying record scores zero by **exclusion, not by clamping**. Keeping the wrong version visible is what makes the reasoning legible.

These are places where the released code departs from the published Methods, or where a metric is computed exactly as described but measures something narrower than its name suggests, established by reading and running it. We have not reproduced the paper's figures and make no claim that the published results are wrong. Each is pedagogically useful in its own right, and together they are a compact tour of the three things that most often go wrong in this field: **coordinate systems, genetic distance, and who is and is not in the comparison set.**

What this section sets up for Section 4 is a single structural fact, not a list of bugs. PanMixer's guarantee is an *empirical threshold against one measured attacker*, and the "leave unselected blocks verbatim" design makes a worst-case distributional guarantee impossible in principle rather than merely absent in practice. Closing that gap — moving from a measured threshold to a bound that holds against every attacker at once — is what our own mechanism is for. What carries over from PanMixer is its **data**, its **block structure**, the algebra of its **forward recursion**, and its **evaluation suite**; what does not carry over is its **sampler**, its **privacy score** and its **release rule**. A rough sense of proportion, offered as an **estimate with no line-by-line counting method** rather than a measurement: most of the preprocessing and evaluation code is reusable, and almost none of the mechanism is.

**One last question you are likely to have here, answered.**

> **Question** — "The summary says PanMixer 'rewrites that person's data so they can no longer be matched to an outside database'. That's the *goal*, not the measured result. Can the summary say which part is demonstrated and which is claimed?"
>
> It can and it should, and the split is one clause long. What is **demonstrated in the published paper** is that ε_private = **0.002** is the largest privacy-risk setting at which **one** linkage attack, scored against **one** external database, went negative for **every** target in that cohort (quoted from the published paper). What is **not** demonstrated — by anyone, including us — is that the released person cannot be matched: a different attacker, a denser database, or a site set that included the material this attack filters out is a different experiment, and the paper says as much itself when it notes that ε_private "is not universal".
>
> Then the second honesty, which belongs in the same paragraph: **this project has reproduced none of the published figures**, and everything in the departures list is a statement about the released code relative to the published Methods. The section's job is to explain a method well enough to build on and to be precise about which of its numbers are load-bearing — not to adjudicate the paper.
